The Seven Tragedies

From the Greek: all seven surviving plays, complete — the spoken scenes as modern prose, the sung odes as verse

by Sophocles

c. 442-401 BC 2026

This is an AI modernization of The Seven Tragedies into contemporary English. The original is available from Perseus Digital Library. Also available as an epub.

Ajax (c. 442 BC)

Ajax

(Before Ajax's hut, at the far end of the Greek camp at Troy. Night is just over. Odysseus is studying the ground. Athena is present, but only he can hear her, and he cannot see her.)

Athena. Son of Laertes, I have always found you out on the prowl, looking for some opening against your enemies. And here you are again, at Ajax's hut down by the ships, where his post is at the edge of the camp. I have been watching you a while now, hunting, measuring his tracks — they are fresh — to work out whether he is inside or not. Your nose has brought you straight to the door, like a good Spartan hound. The man is in there. He went in just now, his face running with sweat, and his hands too, from the killing. You can stop peering through the doorway. Tell me instead what you are so set on, and hear it from someone who knows.

Odysseus. Athena — your voice, of all the gods the one I love best. I cannot see you, and still I know you at once. I catch what you say and hold it, the way you catch the note of a bronze-mouthed trumpet. You are right: I am circling the track of a man who hates me, Ajax with the great shield. It is him I have been following, and nobody else. Something happened last night that we cannot make sense of — if in fact he did it. We know nothing for certain. We are guessing. So I put myself forward for the job.

We found the cattle, all of them, dead. Our own plunder, butchered by somebody's hand, and the herdsmen with them. Everyone is laying it at his door. And I have a scout who says he saw him, alone, running across the plain with a sword still wet, and told me so. I went straight onto the trail. Some of the signs I can read. Some of them baffle me, and I cannot tell whose they are. You have come at the right moment. In everything, before and after, it is your hand that steers me.

Athena. I know, Odysseus. I have been out on the road a while, keeping watch, on your side of the hunt.

Odysseus. Then, dear mistress, am I working to any purpose?

Athena. The man did it. These are his deeds.

Odysseus. And what possessed him to raise his hand like that?

Athena. He was weighed down with rage over the arms of Achilles.

Odysseus. Then why go at the flocks?

Athena. He thought the blood he was getting on his hands was yours.

Odysseus. So this was a plot? Against the Greeks?

Athena. And he would have carried it off, if I had not been paying attention.

Odysseus. What made him so bold? What put that in his head?

Athena. He came at you in the night, on his own, in secret.

Odysseus. How close did he get? Did he reach us?

Athena. He was at the doors of the two generals.

Odysseus. Then how was his hand held back, with murder in it?

Athena. I held him back. I threw a weight across his eyes — ideas about a joy that would have finished him — and turned him aside onto the sheep, and the mixed droves under the herdsmen's guard, the spoil you had not yet divided up. He fell on them and went round in a circle cutting a slaughter through the horned animals, breaking their spines. One moment he thought he had the two sons of Atreus in his hands and was killing them himself; the next it was some other commander he was falling on. And the man ran about in a sickness of madness, and I drove him on. I kept throwing him further into the net.

Then, when he had worn himself out at it, he tied up the oxen that were still alive, and all the sheep, and brought them home — as though he had men, not a catch of cattle. And now he has them roped together indoors, and he is torturing them.

I will show you the madness too, with your own eyes, so you can go and tell every Greek what you saw. Stand your ground and do not be afraid. He will not be your ruin. I will turn the beams of his eyes aside and keep them off your face.

(She calls towards the hut.)

You there — you with your prisoners' arms strapped behind them — I am calling you. Come here. Ajax, I am calling you. Come out in front of the house.

Odysseus. Athena, what are you doing? Do not call him out.

Athena. Be quiet. Do you want a name for cowardice?

Odysseus. For the gods' sake, no. Let him stay inside. That is enough.

Athena. Afraid of what? Was he not a man before?

Odysseus. He was. My enemy then, and still is.

Athena. And is there any laughter sweeter than laughing at an enemy?

Odysseus. It is enough for me that he stays in his hut.

Athena. You are afraid to look at a madman in plain sight?

Odysseus. If he were sane I would not step out of his way.

Athena. He will not see you now, even standing beside him.

Odysseus. How, if he is looking out of the same eyes?

Athena. I will darken them. He can be staring straight at you.

Odysseus. True enough. Anything is possible when a god arranges it.

Athena. Then stand still and say nothing. Stay exactly where you are.

Odysseus. I will stay. I would rather be somewhere else.

Athena. Ajax. You. I am calling you a second time. Why so little respect for the ally at your side?

(Ajax comes out. There is blood on him, and a whip in his hand.)

Ajax. Athena — welcome. Child of Zeus, welcome. You stood by me magnificently. I will crown you with solid gold out of this catch, to thank you for it.

Athena. Well said. But tell me one thing. Did you dip your sword properly in the Greek army?

Ajax. I can boast about it. I am not going to deny it.

Athena. And did you get your armed hand as far as the sons of Atreus?

Ajax. So far that they will never dishonour Ajax again.

Athena. The men are dead — that is what you are telling me.

Ajax. Dead. Now let them try taking my arms off me.

Athena. Good. And the son of Laertes — where does he stand with you? Or has he got away?

Ajax. That damned fox. You are asking me where he is?

Athena. I am. Odysseus. The one who stands against you.

Ajax. He is inside, mistress, sitting down. My favourite prisoner. I do not want him dead yet.

Athena. Not until you do what? What more do you get out of it?

Ajax. Not until he is tied to the pillar under my roof —

Athena. And what harm will you do the poor wretch then?

Ajax. — with his back whipped red. Then he can die.

Athena. Do not torture the poor wretch like that.

Ajax. Athena, in everything else, take what pleasure you like. But he pays that price and no other.

Athena. Well, since doing it is what gives you pleasure — use your hand. Hold back from nothing you have in mind.

Ajax. I am going to the work. And I want this from you: stand by me always as you have stood by me today.

(He goes back inside.)

Athena. Do you see, Odysseus, how great the strength of the gods is? Who could you have found more level-headed than that man, or better at doing what the moment needed?

Odysseus. Nobody that I know of. And I pity him for all that, wretched as he is, enemy of mine though he is, because he is yoked under a ruin that is not to be undone. I am not thinking of his case any more than my own. I see that all of us who are alive are nothing but phantoms, or a weightless shadow.

Athena. Then, since you can see that much, never say an arrogant word yourself against the gods, and take on no swagger, if you outweigh another man in the strength of your hand or in the depth of your money. A single day tips all human things down, and lifts them up again. The gods love the man who keeps himself in hand, and they detest the bad.

(Athena is gone. The Chorus comes in: sailors from Salamis, Ajax's own men.)

Chorus.
	Son of Telamon, you who hold
	the seat of sea-washed Salamis
	where the water comes right up —
	when things go well for you I am glad.
	But when the stroke of Zeus falls,
	or a rumour comes raging out of the Greek camp
	with all their bad tongues in it,
	I am afraid. A great dread takes me,
	and I flinch like the eye of a dove.
	So it was with the night now over:
	a loud noise has settled on us,
	and it is our disgrace —
	that you went into the meadow
	where the horses run mad
	and destroyed the Greeks' animals,
	the plunder that was left,
	the spear-won share not yet divided,
	killing them with the flashing iron.
	These are the whispers Odysseus shapes
	and breathes into everybody's ear,
	and they believe him. What he says
	about you now is easy to believe,
	and every man who hears it
	is more delighted than the man who told it,
	and tramples on your suffering.
	Aim at a great spirit and you cannot miss.
	Say the same things about me
	and nobody would credit it.
	It is the man who has something that envy crawls towards.
	And yet the small without the great
	are a shaky tower to shelter behind.
	The lowly stand up best beside the great,
	and the great are held upright by lesser men.
	But there is no teaching this to fools
	before they need to know it.
	Those are the men making the noise against you,
	and we have no strength to fight it off
	without you, my lord.
	Once they have got out from under your eye
	they chatter like flocks of birds.
	But if you appeared — with the great vulture over them —
	they would drop down suddenly,
	silent, and not a sound out of them.
Chorus.
	Was it Artemis, the bull-driver, daughter of Zeus —
	terrible rumour,
	mother of my shame —
	who drove you against the herds that belong to us all?
	Some victory she got no thanks for, perhaps,
	or cheated of glorious spoils,
	or a stag brought down and no gift given?
	Or has the Lord of War in his bronze breastplate
	held some grudge over a spear shared,
	and paid back the insult
	with contrivances in the night?
Chorus.
	Never out of your own mind, son of Telamon,
	would you have gone so far wrong
	as to fall upon the flocks.
	When madness comes from the gods it arrives.
	But let Zeus and Apollo turn aside
	this evil talk of the Greeks.
	And if it is the great kings
	putting the story about behind their hands,
	or the man out of the worthless line of Sisyphus —
	then do not, my lord, do not go on sitting
	in the hut by the sea with your face hidden,
	and let them make an evil name for you.
Chorus.
	Get up from where you sit. Wherever you have settled
	into this long standstill, out of the fighting,
	you are letting the ruin flare up to heaven.
	Your enemies' outrage runs about unafraid
	in the glens where the wind blows,
	and every tongue in the army is cackling,
	and it is heavy to bear.
	The pain in me has come to a stop and will not move.

(Tecmessa comes out of the hut.)

Tecmessa.
	Crew of Ajax's ship,
	men of the line that sprang from the earth of Athens,
	we have cause to cry out, we who care
	for the house of Telamon far away.
	The terrible one, the great one, the man of raw force,
	Ajax, is lying sick
	under a storm that has muddied him.
Chorus.
	What change has this night brought,
	and what weight, after the day before?
	Daughter of Teleutas the Phrygian, speak.
	Ajax took you in war and holds to you and loves you,
	so you can tell us something,
	you are not without knowledge.
Tecmessa.
	How am I to say a thing that cannot be said?
	What you are going to hear weighs as much as a death.
	Madness took him in the night —
	our famous Ajax — and has left him shamed.
	Go inside the hut and you can see it:
	butchered animals soaking in blood,
	sacrifices, and his hand made every one.
Chorus.
	What news you have shown us of the man of fire,
	not to be borne, and not to be escaped either,
	spread abroad by the great Greek lords,
	and their loud telling of it keeps growing.
	I am afraid of what is coming. He will die
	in front of everybody, that man,
	with his hand knocked off its aim,
	having killed with the dark sword the beasts
	and the herdsmen who ride with them.
Tecmessa.
	So that was it. That was where he came from,
	bringing the flock in ropes behind him.
	Some of them he cut the throats of, there on the floor;
	some he struck at the ribs and tore open.
	And he picked up two white-footed rams,
	and sheared off the head of one, and the tip of its tongue,
	and threw them away; and the other
	he tied upright to a pillar,
	and took a heavy harness strap
	and beat it with the doubled lash, whistling,
	cursing it in language
	that a god taught him and no man.
Chorus.
	It is time for each of us to cover his head
	and steal away on foot,
	or take his seat at the fast bench of the oars
	and let the ship go out to sea.
	Such are the threats the two kings, the sons of Atreus,
	are driving at us. I am afraid of being stoned,
	beaten down beside this man,
	whom a fate you cannot go near has hold of.
Tecmessa.
	Not any more. Like a south wind
	that comes up hard and then drops, with no lightning in it,
	he is quiet. And now he is in his right mind
	he has a new pain. Because to look at your own suffering,
	when no other hand was in it,
	lays a sharp ache under you.

Chorus. If he has stopped, I think we may do well enough. Once the trouble is gone it counts for less.

Tecmessa. And if you were given the choice, which would you take: to hurt your friends and be happy yourself, or to share the grief and be one of the grieving?

Chorus. The double sorrow is the greater evil, lady.

Tecmessa. Then we are ruined now, and the sickness is over.

Chorus. What do you mean? I do not follow you.

Tecmessa. That man, while the sickness was on him, took pleasure in the horrors he was held by, though being near him was misery for us, who were sane. Now that it has let go of him and he can breathe, he is driven from end to end by a wretched grief, and we are no better off than we were before. Is that not two sorrows out of one?

Chorus. I agree, and I am afraid a stroke has come from a god. How else, if he is no happier now it has released him than he was when he was sick?

Tecmessa. That is how it stands. You had better know it.

Chorus. How did the trouble first swoop down on him? Tell us. We share the pain of it.

Tecmessa. You are in it with us, so you will hear all of it.

In the dead of night, when the evening lamps had burned out, he took up a two-edged sword and started feeling his way to the door on some errand that led nowhere. I went at him and said, "What are you doing, Ajax? Nobody has summoned you, no messenger came for you, you have heard no trumpet — why are you setting out? The whole army is asleep." And he said what he always says, the same few words he has worn out: "Woman, silence is what makes a woman lovely." I understood him, and I stopped, and he went out alone.

What happened out there I cannot tell you. He came back in with them roped together — bulls, herd dogs, the woolly catch. Some he took by the neck, some he turned face up and cut their throats and broke their backs; and some, still tied, he tortured as though they were men, going at cattle. At the end he darted out through the door and hauled up words at some shadow — some of it against the sons of Atreus, some about Odysseus — laughing a great deal at how much outrage he had gone and paid them back for.

Then he rushed back into the house, and slowly, somehow, over time, he came to himself. He looked round the room, and it was full of his own ruin, and he struck his head and howled. He went down in the wreckage, among the wreck of the slaughtered sheep, and sat there with his nails dug into his hair and his fist closed on it.

For a long while he sat and said nothing. Then he began threatening me with terrible things if I did not tell him everything that had happened, and he demanded to know what business it was that he had found himself in. And I was frightened, friends, of what he had done, and I told him all of it, as much as I knew for certain.

He cried out at once, and the sound was wretched. I had never heard him make it before. He had always taught that wailing like that belonged to cowards and low-hearted men; he used to grieve quietly, no loud weeping, and groan low like a bull. Now he is down in this miserable luck of his, no food, no drink, sitting still where he fell among the cattle the iron killed. And it is plain that he means to do something terrible. His words say so, and so do his laments.

Friends — this is the errand I came on — go in and help him, if there is anything you can do. Men like him can be won over by what their friends say.

Chorus. Tecmessa, daughter of Teleutas, this is terrible news you bring us: our man driven out of his mind by his own suffering.

Ajax. (inside) Oh — oh no. Oh no.

Tecmessa. And it will get worse, by the sound of it. Did you not hear Ajax? That cry he just gave?

Ajax. (inside) Oh — oh no. Oh no.

Chorus. Either the man is sick again, or he is in pain at the sight of what the sickness left behind.

Ajax. (inside) My son. My son.

Tecmessa. Oh, this is misery. Eurysaces — it is you he is calling. What is he thinking of? Where are you? Oh, this is misery.

Ajax. (inside) Teucer. Where is Teucer? Is he going to be off raiding for ever, while I am destroyed?

Chorus. He sounds sane. Open the doors. Perhaps if he looks at me he will pull himself together.

Tecmessa. There. It is open. Now you can look at what he has done, and at the state he is in himself.

(The doors are opened. Ajax is sitting among the slaughtered animals.)

Ajax.
	Ah — my sailors, my friends,
	the only friends I have left,
	the only ones still keeping faith,
	look at me. Look what a wave has just gone round me
	and broken, out of a storm of blood.
Chorus.
	It seems your evidence was all too good, lady.
	What he does shows there is no mind in him.
Ajax.
	Ah — you men who know the sea,
	who came aboard and pulled the blade through the salt water,
	you and nobody else, you and nobody else,
	I see as a defence against what is happening to me.
	Come and kill me on top of these.
Chorus.
	Say better words than that. Do not cure a bad thing
	with a bad thing, and make the hurt of the ruin worse.
Ajax.
	You see the bold one, the man with the great heart,
	the one who never shook in a battle with the enemy —
	you see me, terrible-handed, against animals that could not fight back.
	The laughter. The outrage they have done me.
Tecmessa.
	Ajax, my lord, I beg you, do not say these things.
Ajax.
	Get out. Take yourself back out of here.
	Ah. Ah.
Chorus.
	In the gods' name, give way. Get some sense.
Ajax.
	I am cursed. I let them go out of my hand,
	the men who were asking to be punished,
	and I fell on cattle with curled horns and fine goats
	and made their dark blood run.
Chorus.
	Why grieve over what is done? It cannot be
	other than it is.
Ajax.
	You who see everything, you tool
	that every crime is done with, son of Laertes,
	the filthiest thing in the whole army —
	how you must be laughing, and enjoying it.
Chorus.
	It is as a god allows that any man laughs or mourns.
Ajax.
	Let me see him even so, shattered as I am.
	Oh. Oh no.
Chorus.
	Say nothing big. Can you not see what trouble you are in?
Ajax.
	Zeus, father of my fathers,
	let me destroy that smooth-tongued creature, that hated thing,
	and the two kings who share the command,
	and then die myself.
Tecmessa.
	When you pray for that, pray that I die with you.
	What reason have I to live, once you are dead?
Ajax.
	Ah —
	Darkness, you are my light.
	Blackness under the earth, the brightest thing my eyes can find,
	take me. Take me to live with you.
	Take me. I am not fit any longer to look for help
	from the family of the gods, or for anything good
	from men, who live a day at a time.
	The daughter of Zeus, the strong goddess,
	is grinding me down to nothing.
	Where is a man to run?
	Where can I go and stay?
	If what I have done is ruined, friends,
	and these things lying beside me are what I have,
	and I have set myself to a fool's hunting —
	then the whole army would come at me with both hands
	and kill me.
Tecmessa.
	This is misery, that a man who was worth so much
	should say the kind of thing he could never once have borne to say.
Ajax.
	Ah —
	you channels of the sounding sea,
	you caves along the shore, you pasture at the water's edge,
	long, long, far too long
	you have held me here at Troy. But not any more.
	Not while I still have breath in me.
	Let anyone with sense understand that.
	Scamander, you streams nearby,
	you were good to the Greeks;
	you will not look at this man again.
	I will say a large thing:
	Troy never saw his equal come out of the land of Greece
	in all the army. And now
	I lie here, stripped of honour.
Chorus.
	I do not know how to hold you back,
	nor how to let you go on speaking,
	fallen into troubles like these.

Ajax. Aiai. Who would ever have thought my own name would fit my sorrows so exactly? Aias — aiai. I can cry it twice now, and three times over. These are the troubles I have run into.

My father took the first prize for courage out of this same country of Ida, the fairest in the army, and carried every glory home. I am his son. I came to the same ground at Troy with no less strength in me, and what my hand did was no smaller — and I am destroyed here, dishonoured by the Greeks.

Though I know one thing for certain: if Achilles were alive and were awarding his own arms to the best man, nobody would have taken them before me. As it is, the sons of Atreus have got them into the hands of a man who will do anything, and shoved the victories of Ajax aside. And if these eyes and this mind had not swerved off what I intended, they would never have voted a verdict like that against another man again.

Instead the daughter of Zeus, the grim-eyed goddess nobody masters, tripped me at the very moment I was fitting my hand to them, and threw a raving sickness into me, so that I could bloody my hands on livestock. And they have got away and are laughing about it — no thanks to me. But when a god does the harm, even a worthless man can slip past a better one.

So what now? What am I to do? The gods obviously hate me, the Greek army loathes me, all Troy hates me and so does this whole plain. Do I go home across the Aegean, and leave my station at the ships and the sons of Atreus to themselves? And then what face do I show my father Telamon when I get there? How is he going to bear the sight of me, standing in front of him with nothing, no prize for courage, when he himself won the great crown of it? No. I could not do it.

Well then — go at the Trojan wall, take them on alone, do something worth doing, and die at the end of it? But that, I think, would only make the sons of Atreus happy. No.

Some enterprise has to be found that will show my old father he did not father a son with nothing inside him. It is a disgrace for a man to want a long life when nothing changes in his troubles. What pleasure is there in one day after another, moving you up to the edge of death and back again? I would not give anything for the man who warms his hands at empty hopes. A man of good birth has two choices and no more: live well, or die well. That is all of it. You have heard me out.

Chorus. Nobody will ever say that was a borrowed speech, Ajax. That came out of your own mind. All the same — stop. Let your friends have charge of your thinking, and put these ideas down.

Tecmessa. Ajax, my lord — there is no greater evil for a human being than the luck they are forced to live with. My father was a free man, as strong in wealth as any in Phrygia. Now I am a slave. Somehow that is what the gods decided, and above all it was your hand that did it. And since I came into your bed, I want what is good for you, and I am begging you, by Zeus of our hearth and by the bed you and I came together in: do not leave me to the bitter talk of your enemies, do not leave me to some stranger's hand.

The day you die and widow me by dying, you can be sure that on that same day the Greeks will take me by force, and your son with me, and I will be fed as a slave is fed. And one of my masters will say something cutting, shooting words at me: "Look at the woman who shared Ajax's bed, the greatest man in the army. Look at the work she does now, after a life other women envied." That is what they will say. Fate will drive me, and the shame of the words will stain you and your family.

Have some respect for your father, whom you are leaving in a miserable old age; have some respect for your mother, with her long share of years, who prays to the gods so often that you will come home alive. And pity your son, my king. If he is robbed of the care he should be growing up in, and has to get through his days without you, in the hands of guardians who are neither family nor friends — think what you will be leaving to him and to me when you die.

There is nothing left for me to look at except you. You are the reason. Your spear made nothing of my country. Another fate took my mother and the man who fathered me and gave them a house in the world of the dead. So what homeland could I have except you? What money? Everything that keeps me safe is in you.

Remember me too. A man ought to hold on to what he remembers, if he has ever taken any pleasure anywhere. It is kindness that gives birth to kindness, always. The man who lets the memory of a good turn run out of him was never a man of any quality.

Chorus. Ajax, I wish pity would touch you the way it touches me. Then you would think well of what she says.

Ajax. She will have my good opinion, if she can bring herself to do what she is told and do it properly.

Tecmessa. Dear Ajax, I will do anything you say.

Ajax. Then bring me my son. I want to see him.

Tecmessa. I was frightened. I sent him away.

Ajax. Because of this trouble of mine? Is that what you mean?

Tecmessa. In case the poor child came across you and died of it.

Ajax. That would have suited my luck exactly.

Tecmessa. Well, I took care that it should not happen.

Ajax. I approve. That was a good thought and you were right to have it.

Tecmessa. Then how can I help you now, as things are?

Ajax. Let me speak to him. Let me see his face.

Tecmessa. He is close by. The servants are watching him.

Ajax. Then why is he not here?

Tecmessa. Child, your father is calling you. Bring him here — whichever of you has him by the hand.

Ajax. Is the man coming, or did your voice not reach him?

Tecmessa. Here he is now, one of the servants, bringing him.

(A servant brings in Eurysaces, a small boy.)

Ajax. Lift him. Lift him up here. He will not flinch at the sight of this fresh blood — not if he is really mine, his father's son. He has to be broken in early to his father's raw ways, and shaped to the same nature.

Boy, I hope you have better luck than your father, and are like him in everything else. Then you will not turn out badly. Even now there is one thing about you I could envy: you have no idea of any of this. Life is sweetest when you cannot think yet. Not being able to think is an affliction with no pain in it — until you learn what joy is, and what hurt. When you get that far, you will have to show my enemies what you are made of, and whose son you are.

Meanwhile feed on light air. Grow, and be your mother's happiness. No Greek is going to lay a hand on you, I am certain of that, or insult you, even with me gone. I will leave Teucer at your gate to keep watch, and he will not slacken in looking after you, though at the moment he is far off, hunting enemies.

My shield-men, my companions at sea — I lay this on you as I lay it on him, a service you all share. Tell Teucer my orders. He is to take this child home and show him to Telamon, and to my mother — Eriboea, I mean — so that he can be the comfort of their old age from now on, until they reach the deep places of the god below. And my arms are not to be set up as a prize for the Greeks by any master of the games, nor by the man who destroyed me.

That much you take for me, son — the broad shield you are named for. Hold it. Swing it by the strong grip. Seven layers of hide, and no spear has gone through it. The rest of my gear will be buried with me.

Now take the child, quickly. Shut the doors. And no mourning outside the house. God, women are a weepy business. Shut it, and be quick. It is no clever doctor who chants spells over a wound that wants the knife.

Chorus. I am afraid, hearing you in such a hurry. I do not like the edge on your tongue.

Tecmessa. Ajax, my lord — what is in your mind?

Ajax. Do not judge me. Do not question me. Keeping yourself in hand is a fine thing.

Tecmessa. I have no heart left. By your child, by the gods, I beg you — do not betray us.

Ajax. You are irritating me. Do you not understand that I owe the gods nothing any more?

Tecmessa. Say better words than that.

Ajax. Say them to somebody who is listening.

Tecmessa. Will you not hear me?

Ajax. You have said too much already.

Tecmessa. Because I am afraid, my king.

Ajax. Shut the doors. Now.

Tecmessa. In the gods' name, soften.

Ajax. You are a fool, I think, if you have only now decided to start training my temper.

Chorus.
	Famous Salamis, you sit
	out among the beating waves, and you are happy,
	and every man can see you where you are.
	And I, wretched, have been here a long time,
	making my bed month after month
	on the fields of Ida and not counting them,
	worn down by the time,
	with the bad expectation still in front of me
	that I shall get through at last
	to Hades, whom nobody wants and nobody sees.
Chorus.
	And now here is Ajax to sit down beside me,
	past curing, and no relief in it,
	sharing his tent with a madness sent by a god.
	Once you sent him out from home
	and he was master in the rush of war.
	Now he grazes his mind in country of his own
	and he has been found a great grief to his friends.
	What his hands did before, the highest courage there is,
	has fallen unloved before the unloving,
	fallen, fallen, before the miserable sons of Atreus.
Chorus.
	And his mother, who has lived with the old days,
	who is white with age — when she hears
	that his mind has been destroyed,
	she will not cry the way the nightingale cries,
	that pitiful bird.
	She will sing sharp songs over him,
	and the beat of hands will fall on her breast,
	and grey hair will come out in her fingers.
Chorus.
	Better hidden in the world below, the man sick with a madness
	that came to nothing, who by his father's line
	was the best of the Greeks who have endured so much,
	and is no longer steady in the temper he was born with
	but has gone outside himself and lives out there.
	Unhappy father — what news is waiting for you,
	the ruin that has fallen on your son,
	which no life in the house of Aeacus
	has carried before him.

(Ajax comes out with the sword in his hand. Tecmessa follows.)

Ajax. Everything is brought out of the dark by the long, uncountable years, and everything in the light is buried by them. There is nothing a man should not expect. The most terrible oath is beaten. So is the will that would not bend.

Even I — who was so hard, then, so like iron off the anvil — I have been softened at the mouth by this woman's words. I feel the pity of leaving her a widow and the boy an orphan among my enemies.

I am going to the washing places, to the meadows at the water's edge, so that I can clean off what is on me and get out from under the goddess's heavy anger. I will find some untrodden spot, and bury this sword of mine, the most hateful of weapons, dig it into the ground where nobody will see it. Let Night and the world below keep it down there.

From the day I took it into my hand, a present from Hector, my greatest enemy, I have had nothing good from the Greeks. The proverb men have is true: an enemy's gifts are no gifts, and no good comes of them.

So from now on I will know how to give way to the gods, and I will learn to respect the sons of Atreus. They are in command. One gives way. Of course one does. The most fearsome and unbreakable things give way to office. Winter, with its paths under snow, stands aside for summer and the fruit. Night's long circle steps back and lets day light her fire with her white horses in front. The blast of terrible winds lets the groaning sea lie down. And Sleep, who is stronger than anything, ties a man up and then unties him, and does not keep him for ever.

And are we not going to learn to keep ourselves in hand? I am. I have just found out that an enemy is to be hated only so far as suits a man who is going to be his friend later on; and that with a friend I will want to do just as much good and no more, since he is not going to stay one. Most people find the harbour of comradeship untrustworthy.

But all of that will be well enough. You, wife, go inside and pray to the gods that what my heart wants may be finished, right to the end.

(Tecmessa goes in.)

And you, my friends, honour what I have asked, exactly as she does. Tell Teucer, when he comes, to take care of us, and to be good to you at the same time. I am going where I have to go. Do as I say, and before long, perhaps — badly off as I am now — you will hear that I have found rest.

(He goes out.)

Chorus.
	I shudder with longing. I am up and flying with joy.
	Pan, Pan,
	Pan the sea-wanderer, come to us
	from the stony ridge of Cyllene where the snow is beaten flat.
	King, dance-maker to the gods, appear,
	and join us, and set us going in the Nysian steps,
	the Cnosian steps, the ones you taught yourself.
	Now I want to dance.
	And let Apollo come over the Icarian water,
	the lord of Delos, in a shape I will know,
	and be with me, kindly, all the way through.
Chorus.
	Ares has lifted the terrible ache off our eyes.
	Now, now, Zeus,
	now the white light of a good day can come near
	our fast ships that run on the sea,
	because Ajax has forgotten his pain again,
	and has carried out every offering the gods are owed,
	keeping the rule, with the greatest respect.
	Great time makes everything fade.
	And I would not call anything unsayable,
	now that Ajax, against all our hope,
	has been talked round out of his anger
	and out of his great quarrel with the sons of Atreus.

(A messenger comes in from the camp.)

Messenger. Friends, here is my news first. Teucer is back, just now, from the heights of Mysia. He came to the generals' quarters in the middle of the camp and the whole Greek army is shouting at him. They recognised him a long way off and closed round him, and then they pelted him with insults from every side — not one of them held off — calling him the brother of the madman, the man who plotted against the army, saying he was not going to get out of it without being torn to pieces under their stones. It went so far that swords were pulled out of scabbards and drawn. Then the quarrel, having run nearly the whole way, was stopped by the older men talking them round.

But where do I find Ajax? I have to tell him. Everything has to be reported to the man in charge.

Chorus. He is not inside. He went out a moment ago. He has yoked a new purpose to a new mood.

Messenger. No. No. Then the man who sent me on this errand sent me too late — or I came too slowly.

Chorus. What is urgent about it? What part of it has been neglected?

Messenger. Teucer said flatly that Ajax was not to be let out of the hut until he got here himself.

Chorus. Well, he is gone. I tell you again. He turned his mind to the best thing he could do: making his peace with the gods over his anger.

Messenger. Those words are full of foolishness, if there is anything in what Calchas prophesies.

Chorus. What does he prophesy? What do you know about this?

Messenger. This much. I was there and saw it. Calchas got up out of the circle of chiefs sitting in council, went off by himself away from the sons of Atreus, put his right hand into Teucer's hand in a friendly way, and told him — laid it on him — to use every method there is to keep Ajax inside his hut for the length of this one day that is shining on us now, and not to let him move, if he ever wanted to see him alive.

Because it is this day alone, he said, that the anger of the goddess Athena will drive at him. Lives that have grown outsized and stopped being any use, the prophet went on, come down under heavy trouble from the gods — especially when a man born to a man's nature stops thinking like one.

And Ajax, from the moment he first set out from home, showed himself a fool when his father was giving him good advice. Telamon said to him, "Son, want to win with the spear — but always want to win with a god beside you." And he answered, high and stupid: "Father, with the gods even a nobody could win. I mean to drag that glory in without them."

That is the size of the boast he made. Then a second time, when the goddess Athena was urging him on and telling him to turn a killing hand on the enemy, he answered her with something terrible, a thing that should not be said: "Queen, go and stand by the other Greeks. Where we are, the line will never break."

Words like that are how he earned the goddess's anger, which does not soften — because he was thinking thoughts too big for a man. But if he is still alive today, we may find some way, with a god's help, to save him. That is what the prophet said.

Teucer got up from his seat at once and sent me to you with these orders. But if we have missed our chance, the man is dead — unless Calchas knows nothing.

Chorus. Tecmessa, born to suffer — come out and hear what this man is saying. The razor is against the skin, and it will take all our happiness off.

(Tecmessa comes out, with Eurysaces.)

Tecmessa. Why do you get me up again, when I had only just found some rest from troubles that never let go?

Chorus. Listen to this man. He has come with news of Ajax that hurt me to hear.

Tecmessa. Oh — what are you saying? Are we destroyed?

Messenger. I know nothing about your position. About Ajax I know this: if he is out of doors, I have no confidence.

Tecmessa. He is out of doors. I am in agony to know what you mean.

Messenger. Teucer gives strict orders to keep him under the roof of his hut, and not to let him out alone.

Tecmessa. Where is Teucer? Why does he say this?

Messenger. He has only just come back. And he thinks this going out is going to kill Ajax.

Tecmessa. Oh, this is misery. Who did he learn it from?

Messenger. From the son of Thestor, the prophet. It is about today: today decides whether the man lives or dies.

Tecmessa. Friends — stand in front of me. This is a fate I cannot get round. Hurry. Some of you go and make Teucer come faster. Some of you take the western bays and some the eastern, and go looking for the way the man went out, which is a bad way.

I know now that my husband has deceived me and that I am thrown out of the favour I used to have with him.

Oh, child — what am I to do? I must not sit here. I will go too, as far as I have strength for it. Move. Quickly. This is no time to be sitting, if we want to save a man who is in a hurry to die.

Chorus. I am ready to go, and I will show it in more than words. Speed of foot and speed of doing will come together.

(A lonely place by the shore. Ajax is alone, with the sword fixed in the ground.)

Ajax. The killer is standing where it will cut deepest — if a man has leisure even to work that out. It is Hector's gift, and of all the guests I ever had he was the one I hated most and least wanted to look at. It is planted in enemy ground, in Troy, and freshly sharpened on the stone that eats iron. And I have set it firmly, and packed the earth round it myself, so that it will be as kind to me as it can and kill me quickly.

So we are properly equipped.

Now, Zeus, you first — as is right — do this much for me. It is not a large prize I am asking. Send somebody with the bad news to Teucer, so that he is the first to lift me when I have fallen on this sword and made it wet, and I am not spotted by one of my enemies first and thrown out for the dogs and the birds to pick at.

That is all I ask of you, Zeus. And I call on Hermes, who takes men below, to put me to sleep properly, with one quick jump and no struggling, when I have broken my side open on this blade.

And I call for help on the maidens who are always there and always watching everything that men suffer, the dread Furies with their long stride, to see how I am being destroyed, wretched, by the sons of Atreus. And let them take those bad men in the worst possible way and finish them off entirely. As they see me here, fallen and killed by my own hand, so let those two be killed by their own, at the hands of their own dearest children.

Go, Furies. You are quick and you do not forgive. Feed. Spare nobody in the whole army.

And you, Sun, driving your chariot up the steep sky — when you see the country my fathers came from, pull in the gold-backed rein and tell my ruin and the way I died to my old father, and to the unhappy woman who nursed me. When she hears it, poor woman, I think she will send up a great cry through the whole city.

But there is no use weeping about it like this. The thing has to be started, and quickly.

Death — Death, come and look at me now. Though I will speak to you again where I am going, and be with you there.

And you, light of the day that is shining now — you, and the Sun in his chariot — I say goodbye to you. This is the last time, and there will be no other. Light. Sacred ground of my own Salamis, the firm floor of my father's hearth. Famous Athens, and the people who are my own kin. Springs and rivers here — and I say goodbye to the plains of Troy as well — all of you who fed me, goodbye. This is the last word Ajax says to you. The rest I will say to the dead, down below.

(He throws himself on the sword. The Chorus comes back in two groups, from opposite sides.)

First Half-Chorus.
	Work brings work and work again.
	Where — where
	have I not tramped?
	And no place will say it knows anything.
	Listen.
	I hear something. A thud.
Second Half-Chorus.
	Us. Your shipmates, off the same voyage.
First Half-Chorus.
	Well then?
Second Half-Chorus.
	The whole western side of the ships has been gone over.
First Half-Chorus.
	And?
Second Half-Chorus.
	A great deal of work, and nothing more to see.
First Half-Chorus.
	And on the road that faces the morning sun
	the man has not shown himself either.
Chorus.
	Who can point me the way — some hard-working
	fisherman out at his sleepless catch,
	or one of the goddesses of Olympus,
	or of the streams that run down to the Bosporus —
	if anywhere she has seen
	the fierce-hearted man wandering,
	and could call out? It is cruel
	that I have gone so far and worked so hard
	and cannot come near him with a fair wind,
	cannot see where the failing man is.
Tecmessa.
	Oh. Oh no.
Chorus.
	Whose cry came out of the trees just there?
Tecmessa.
	Oh, this is misery.
Chorus.
	It is the poor woman his spear won,
	Tecmessa. I see her, and she is dissolved in it.
Tecmessa.
	I am gone. Destroyed. Levelled to the ground, friends.
Chorus.
	What is it?
Tecmessa.
	Ajax. Here. Newly killed,
	lying folded round the sword, with the sword inside him.
Chorus.
	Then there is no going home.
	My king, you have killed me,
	the man who sailed with you.
	And her. Poor broken woman.
Tecmessa.
	The state he is in, there is nothing to say but aiai.
Chorus.
	By whose hand did he do it, the unlucky man?
Tecmessa.
	His own. It is obvious. The sword he planted in the earth
	and fell on gives the evidence against him.
Chorus.
	What blindness in me. You bled alone,
	then, and none of your friends was guarding you.
	And I took no care of it, deaf as I was, knowing nothing.
	Where — where is he lying,
	the man who would not bend, whose name is grief?
Tecmessa.
	He is not to be looked at. I will cover him
	with this cloak, right over, wrapping him,
	because no one who loved him could bear to see
	the black blood driven up through his nostrils
	out of the wound he made himself.
	What am I to do? Which of his friends will lift him?
	Where is Teucer? He would come at the right moment,
	if he came now, to lay his brother out.
	Ajax, unlucky man — such as you were, and lying like this.
	Even your enemies owe you mourning.
Chorus.
	You were bound to, poor man. With that stiff heart
	you were bound in the end to work your way through
	to a hard fate, and to troubles without number.
	Such things you groaned out at me
	all night and into the daylight,
	savage-hearted, hating the sons of Atreus,
	with a deadly passion in it.
	That was the great beginning of these sorrows,
	the hour when the arms were put up
	as the prize for a contest in courage.
Tecmessa.
	Oh. Oh no.
Chorus.
	It goes to the liver, I know. That grief is a true one.
Tecmessa.
	Oh. Oh no.
Chorus.
	I do not wonder that you cry out twice over, lady,
	having just now lost a man you loved so much.
Tecmessa.
	You can work my troubles out. I have to feel them.
Chorus.
	That is true.
Tecmessa.
	Oh, my son — what a yoke of slavery
	we are walking into. What overseers are standing over us.
Chorus.
	The things you name, in the middle of this grief —
	they would be unspeakable, from those two
	hard men, the sons of Atreus.
	May a god hold them off.
Tecmessa.
	None of what you see would have turned out this way
	if the gods had not been in it.
Chorus.
	Yes. They have loaded on more weight than can be carried.
Tecmessa.
	And this is the kind of suffering the terrible goddess,
	Pallas, daughter of Zeus, grows for Odysseus' sake.
Chorus.
	No doubt the man who endures everything
	is exulting in his dark heart, laughing loud
	at these mad sorrows — shame on it —
	and the two royal brothers laughing with him
	when they hear.

Tecmessa. Then let them laugh, and enjoy this man's ruin. Perhaps, though they did not want him while he was alive, they will mourn him dead, when the fighting goes badly for them. Men with crooked judgement do not know what good thing they are holding until they have thrown it away.

His death is more bitter to me than it is sweet to them, and to himself it is a pleasure, because he got hold of everything he wanted: the death he wanted. So what have they got to exult over? He died before the gods. Not before them. No.

Let Odysseus go on being insulting to no purpose. For them Ajax is no longer there. For me he is gone, and he has left me my grief and my mourning, and gone.

Teucer. Oh. Oh no.

Chorus. Be quiet. I think I hear Teucer's voice, striking the note that fits this disaster.

(Teucer comes in.)

Teucer. Ajax, dearest of men, brother, the face I loved to look at — is it true what you have done, as the rumour says it is?

Chorus. He is dead, Teucer. Take it as a fact.

Teucer. Then my luck is too heavy to carry.

Chorus. Things being as they are —

Teucer. This is misery. Misery.

Chorus. You have cause to mourn.

Teucer. It happened so fast.

Chorus. Far too fast, Teucer.

Teucer. This is misery. And the man's child — where in all Troy am I to find him?

Chorus. Alone, by the hut.

Teucer. Then bring him here at once, before one of our enemies snatches him away the way a hunter takes a cub from a lioness and leaves her with nothing. Go. Hurry. Help me. Men everywhere like to stand over the dead and laugh at them, once they are only bodies on the ground.

Chorus. While he was still alive, Teucer, he gave you orders to look after the child, and you are doing it.

Teucer. Of everything my eyes have ever looked at, this is the most painful. And of all the roads I have gone down, this one I have just come along, tracking you, hunting out your footsteps once I heard what had happened to you, dearest Ajax — this is the one that has hurt me most.

A rumour about you went through the whole Greek army as fast as if a god had sent it: that you were dead and gone. I was still far off when I heard it, and I groaned quietly. Now I am looking at it, and it destroys me.

Uncover him. Let me see the worst of it.

(The body is uncovered.)

That face — it is hard to look at, and there is a bitter courage in it. What a crop of griefs you have sown for me by dying. Where can I go? What people will take me in, when I was no use to you in your trouble?

I suppose Telamon — your father, and mine — will greet me with a smile and a kind word when I come home without you. Naturally he will. He is a man who cannot manage a pleasant look even when things go well for him. What will he leave unsaid? What insult will he spare the bastard, got on a woman his spear won — the coward, the man too soft to be a man, who betrayed you, dear Ajax; or better still, who betrayed you on purpose, so as to have your power and your house once you were dead. That is what he will say. He is a man with a short temper and old age has made him heavy, and his anger picks quarrels out of nothing.

And in the end I shall be pushed out of the country and thrown away, called a slave instead of a free man by his own mouth.

That is what waits for me at home. Here at Troy I have many enemies and very little that helps me. All of it I have got out of your death.

What am I to do? How am I to draw you off the sharp tooth of this shining sword, the murderer you breathed your last against? Do you see how, in time, Hector was going to destroy you after all, even dead?

In the gods' name, look at the luck of these two men. Hector was lashed to the chariot rail with the very belt Ajax gave him, and shredded along the ground until his life was gone out of him. And Ajax had this present from Hector, and it is on this that he has died, in a fall that killed him.

Was it not a Fury who forged this blade? Was that belt not made by Hades, the grim workman? For my part I would say that these things, and all things ever, are contrived for men by the gods. If anyone does not care for that idea, let him keep his own, and I will keep mine.

Chorus. Do not go on at length. Think how you will bury him, and what you are going to say — and soon. I can see an enemy coming, and a man who means harm is likely to come laughing at our troubles.

Teucer. Which of the army do you see?

Chorus. Menelaus. The man this whole voyage was fitted out for.

Teucer. I see him. He is not hard to make out at this distance.

(Menelaus comes in.)

Menelaus. You there. I am telling you not to lift that body for burial. Leave it where it is.

Teucer. Why did you spend so many words on that?

Menelaus. Because it is my decision, and the decision of the man who commands the army.

Teucer. And would you tell us what reason you are putting forward?

Menelaus. This. We hoped when we brought him from home that we had an ally for the Greeks and a friend, and on closer inspection we found him worse than a Trojan. He planned the murder of the entire army and marched against us in the night to take us with the spear. And if some god had not put that attempt out, we would have drawn the lot he has drawn, and we would be lying dead by the most shameful death there is, and he would be alive. As it is, a god turned his outrage aside so that it fell on sheep and cattle.

For which reason there is no man strong enough to put his body in a tomb. He will be thrown out somewhere on the pale sand and become food for the birds along the shore.

So do not raise any terrible force of feeling about it. If we could not master him alive, at any rate we shall rule him dead, however much you object, and keep him in order with our hands. While he lived there was never a moment when he would listen to a word from me.

It is the mark of a low nature when a man in the ranks does not think it right to obey the men set over him. The laws will never run well in a city where there is nothing to be afraid of, and no camp can be governed sensibly if it has no fear and no shame in front of it as a defence. A man may grow a great body, but he should expect to fall, even from a small hurt. Where a man has fear in him and shame with it, you may be sure he has found his safety; but where there is licence to be outrageous and do as one likes, be certain that such a state will in time run before a fair wind and then go straight to the bottom.

Let there be fear, I say, standing where fear belongs. And let us not imagine we can do what we enjoy and pay nothing back in pain later. These things come round in turn. He used to be the hot one, doing the outrage; now it is my turn to think well of myself.

So I am warning you not to bury him. Bury him and you may fall into a grave yourself.

Chorus. Menelaus — do not lay down wise principles and then commit an outrage on the dead yourself.

Teucer. I shall never again be surprised, men, when somebody born a nobody does wrong, if men who are supposed to be well born make errors like that in what they argue.

Come — say it again from the beginning. Do you really claim you brought this man here as an ally, and that you recruited him? Did he not sail of his own accord, as his own master? Where are you his commander? Where do you get the right to be king over the men he brought from home? You came here as king of Sparta, not as our master. Nowhere in what you are lawfully allowed to do was it laid down that you should give him orders any more than he should give them to you.

You sailed here under other men's command, not as the supreme general with authority over Ajax at any time. Command the troops you command, and use your solemn language to discipline them. This man I will lay in the grave as justice requires, whether you forbid it or the other general does, and I am not afraid of your mouth.

He did not go on this expedition for the sake of your wife, like those worn-out drudges. He went for the sake of an oath he had sworn, and not for you at all — he never thought much of nobodies.

So come back with more heralds, and bring the commander-in-chief with you. I would not turn round for your noise as long as you are what you are.

Chorus. Again — I do not like that tone in trouble like this. Hard words sting, however right they are.

Menelaus. The archer seems to think rather well of himself.

Teucer. Yes. It is no menial skill I have.

Menelaus. How you would boast, if you had a shield.

Teucer. Even without one I would be a match for you in full armour.

Menelaus. That tongue of yours feeds a fearful temper.

Teucer. When a man has right on his side he may think in a large way.

Menelaus. Is it right that my murderer should prosper?

Teucer. Your murderer? That is a strange thing to say, if you are alive after being killed.

Menelaus. A god saved me. As far as he is concerned, I am dead.

Teucer. Then do not dishonour the gods, when it is the gods who saved you.

Menelaus. Would I find fault with the law of heaven?

Teucer. You would, if you stand here and stop the dead being buried.

Menelaus. I stop my own enemies being buried. That would not be right.

Teucer. Did Ajax ever stand out in the open and make war on you?

Menelaus. He hated me and I hated him. You knew that too.

Teucer. He hated you because you were caught rigging the votes to rob him.

Menelaus. He lost that at the hands of the judges, not mine.

Teucer. You could make a great many stealthy crimes look handsome.

Menelaus. That remark is going to bring somebody pain.

Teucer. No more, I think, than the pain we shall be giving.

Menelaus. I will tell you once. There is no burial for him.

Teucer. And you will hear the answer. He will be buried at once.

Menelaus. I saw a bold-tongued man once who urged sailors to put to sea in winter. And when the worst of the storm was on him you could not have found a voice in him at all. He got under his cloak and let the crew walk over him as they liked. So with you and that raging mouth: perhaps some great storm out of a small cloud will blow up and put your shouting out.

Teucer. And I have seen a man stuffed with foolishness who mocked at his neighbours' misfortunes. And a man rather like me, of the same temper, looked at him and said: "You. Do not do wrong to the dead. Do it and be sure you will suffer for it." That is how he warned the poor fool to his face. And I can see him now, I think, and he is nobody but you. Have I been speaking in riddles?

Menelaus. I am going. It would be a disgrace if it got out that I argue when I have the power to use force.

Teucer. Then crawl off. It is the worst disgrace to me to stand listening to an empty man talking nonsense.

(Menelaus goes.)

Chorus.
	There is going to be a trial of this,
	and a great quarrel. Teucer, be quick,
	as quick as you can manage, and find
	some hollow grave for him, where he will keep
	his damp tomb, and men will remember it always.

(Tecmessa comes back, with Eurysaces.)

Teucer. Here they are, and in good time — the man's son and his wife, come to arrange the burial of this poor body.

Come here, child. Stand close, and take hold of your father as a suppliant, the man who fathered you. Kneel here and beg, and hold in your hands locks of hair — mine, and hers, and yours as the third. That is all a suppliant has.

And if any soldier of the army drags you off this body by force, then for his wickedness let him be thrown out of his country wickedly and get no burial, and let his whole family be cut off at the root, just as I cut this lock.

Take it, child. Keep it safe. Let nobody move you. Kneel down and hold on to him.

And you — do not stand about beside him like women instead of men. Defend us until I get back, when I have seen to a grave for him, though the whole world forbids it.

(He goes.)

Chorus.
	Which will be the last year? When will the count
	of all these wandering years come to an end,
	and stop bringing on me the endless ruin
	of the work of the spear,
	over the wide fields of Troy,
	the sorrow and the shame of Greece?
Chorus.
	He should have gone up into the great sky first,
	or down to Hades, where everyone goes,
	that man who showed the Greeks
	how to make common war with hateful weapons.
	Those labours of his, the parents of all our labours.
	He is the man who ruined the human race.
Chorus.
	He gave me no share in garlands,
	none in the deep wine-cups,
	no sweet noise of the pipes,
	nothing, the miserable man,
	no lying down at night for pleasure.
	And from love — from love — he has shut me out entirely.
	Here I lie with nobody caring,
	and the heavy dew keeps soaking my hair,
	to remind me of Troy, where there is no joy.
Chorus.
	Before this, bold Ajax was always
	my defence against the terror in the night
	and against the flying spears.
	Now he has been handed over
	to a hateful power. What is left for me?
	What pleasure is there now?
	Let me be where the wooded headland
	fences off the sea, under the cliff
	of Sunium, so that we could speak
	to holy Athens as we passed.

(Teucer comes back.)

Teucer. Here I am — I hurried when I saw the commander-in-chief, Agamemnon, coming this way at speed. It is obvious he means to let that clumsy mouth of his go.

(Agamemnon comes in.)

Agamemnon. So it is you, they tell me, who dared to open your mouth wide and make these terrible threats against us — and nothing has happened to you yet. You. I mean you, the son of the captive woman. No doubt if you had been born of a well-bred mother your talk would have gone up to the sky and you would have walked about on tiptoe. As it is, you are a nobody and you have stood up for another nobody lying here, and sworn that we came out with no authority, either as admirals or as generals over the Greeks or over you: Ajax sailed as his own master, you say.

Is it not a great injury to have to hear this from the mouths of slaves? What sort of man is it you shout about so arrogantly? Where did he go, or where did he stand, that I did not? Have the Greeks no men except him?

It looks as though we did ourselves harm when we announced the contest for the arms of Achilles, if we are to be called corrupt on every side because of Teucer — if it will never be enough for you people, even beaten, to accept the verdict that satisfied most of the judges, and you will always be shooting slander at us, or knifing us behind our backs when you finish behind us in the race.

Where those habits get in, no law could ever be settled at all, if we are going to push the rightful winners aside and bring the men at the back up to the front. That has to be stopped. It is not the thick, broad-shouldered men who are the steadiest. It is the men who think well who win everywhere. A broad-backed ox is kept straight on the road by a small whip.

And I can see that medicine coming your way before long, unless you acquire some sense. The man no longer exists — he is a shadow already — and still you insult us boldly and give your tongue too much freedom.

Will you not restrain yourself? Remember your birth, and what you are. Bring somebody else here, a free man, who can plead your case in front of me instead of you. When you speak I no longer follow it. I do not know your foreign language.

Chorus. I wish you both had the sense to hold yourselves in. I have no better advice to give the two of you.

Teucer. How quickly gratitude to the dead drains out of men and is found to have gone over to the other side. This man no longer holds even the smallest word of remembrance for you, Ajax — for you, who so often worked yourself to the bone on his account and put your own life in front of the spear for him. It is all gone. Thrown away.

You there, with all those words and no sense in them: do you not remember anything of the time you were shut inside your own defences, already down to nothing, when the battle turned, and he came alone and saved you? The fire was already burning at the stern decks of the ships and Hector was leaping high over the ditch at the hulls. Who stopped that? Was it not this man — the man who, you say, never set foot anywhere you had not been first? Do you grant that he did his duty by you then?

And the other time, when he went out alone against Hector, alone against alone, by the fall of the lot, and nobody ordered him to? The lot he put in was not the kind that runs away. It was no lump of wet earth. It was the one that would come first and lightly out of the crested helmet. He is the man who did those things, and I was beside him — the slave, the son of the foreign mother.

Teucer. You poor creature. Where are you looking when you talk like that? Do you not know that your father's father, Pelops, was a foreigner, a Phrygian, long ago? That Atreus, who fathered you, set before his own brother a meal made out of his brother's children — the most unholy thing that has been done? And that you yourself were born of a Cretan mother, and her own father caught a stranger on top of her and handed her over to the voiceless fish to be destroyed?

Being what you are, do you reproach a man like me with his breeding? I am the son of Telamon, who won my mother as his bedfellow for being the bravest in the army; and she was a king's daughter by birth, the child of Laomedon, and Heracles gave her to him as the choice gift out of the spoil.

So, born the best from two of the best — could I disgrace my own blood-relation? Him, lying here under such a weight of trouble, whom you would push out unburied, and say so without any shame at all?

Understand this clearly. If you throw him out anywhere, you throw the three of us out with him, lying together. It is a fine thing for me to die in the open, working for him, rather than for your wife — or should I say your brother's? So do not think about my safety. Think about your own. If you cause me any trouble at all, you will soon wish you had been a coward with me rather than bold.

(Odysseus comes in.)

Chorus. Lord Odysseus, you have come at the right moment, if you are here to untie this and not to pull it tighter.

Odysseus. What is the trouble, friends? I heard the sons of Atreus shouting some way off, over this brave man's body.

Agamemnon. And is that not because we have just been hearing the most shameful language from this man, Lord Odysseus?

Odysseus. What language? I can forgive a man for throwing abuse back when he has had abuse thrown at him.

Agamemnon. He heard shameful things because he was doing shameful things to me.

Odysseus. What did he do to you that harmed you?

Agamemnon. He says he will not leave this body without its burial. He will bury it whether I like it or not.

Odysseus. Then may a friend tell you the truth, and go on pulling the oar with you exactly as before?

Agamemnon. Speak. I would be out of my mind otherwise. I count you my greatest friend among the Greeks.

Odysseus. Then listen. In the gods' name, do not bring yourself to throw this man out unburied so hard-heartedly. Do not let the violence in you win so completely that you trample justice underfoot.

He was once the most hostile man in the army to me, too — from the day I won the arms of Achilles against him. But hostile as he was, I would not dishonour him in return, or refuse to say that of all of us Greeks who came to Troy he was the best and bravest, except for Achilles.

So it would not be just for you to dishonour him. It is not him you would be damaging but the laws the gods laid down. There is no justice in doing harm to a good man once he is dead, not even if you hate him.

Agamemnon. You, Odysseus — you are fighting for him against me?

Odysseus. I am. I hated him when it was honourable to hate him.

Agamemnon. And should you not put your heel on him now he is dead?

Odysseus. Do not take pleasure, son of Atreus, in an advantage that does you no credit.

Agamemnon. It is not easy for a man with absolute power to be scrupulous.

Odysseus. But it is easy to allow something to friends who advise you well.

Agamemnon. A good man ought to listen to those in office.

Odysseus. Stop. You win when you give way to your friends.

Agamemnon. Remember what sort of man you are doing this favour for.

Odysseus. He was my enemy. But he was a man of quality.

Agamemnon. What are you doing? Have you so much respect for an enemy's corpse?

Odysseus. His excellence weighs far more with me than his hostility did.

Agamemnon. Men who behave like this are the unsteady kind.

Odysseus. A great many men are friendly at one time and bitter at another.

Agamemnon. And these are the friends you recommend acquiring?

Odysseus. It is not my habit to recommend a rigid spirit.

Agamemnon. You will make us look like cowards today.

Odysseus. On the contrary. We shall look like just men to every Greek.

Agamemnon. So you really are telling me to let the body be buried?

Odysseus. I am. I shall come to the same necessity myself.

Agamemnon. How true it is that every man works for himself in everything.

Odysseus. And who should I work for more than myself?

Agamemnon. Then it will be called your doing, and not mine.

Odysseus. However you do it, you will come out of it well.

Agamemnon. Then be quite certain of this: to you I would grant a larger favour than this one. But that man, here or below, will be my worst enemy just the same. You may do what you like.

(He goes.)

Chorus. Odysseus, anyone who denies you were born with a wise judgement, after this, is a fool.

Odysseus. And now I say to Teucer that from this moment I am as much his friend as I was once his enemy. I would like to join in burying your dead and share the work, and leave out no service that men should perform for the best and bravest of us.

Teucer. Odysseus, you are the best of men, and I have nothing but praise for what you have said. You have proved my fears completely wrong. Of all the Greeks you were his deadliest enemy, and you alone stood by him with your hands ready. You did not come here, alive, and outrage the dead — unlike the general who came here out of his senses, who wanted, he and his brother, to throw the man out mutilated and unburied.

So may the Father who is first on Olympus, and the Fury who forgets nothing, and Justice who completes what she begins, destroy those bad men badly, exactly as they wanted to throw this man out, insulted and undeserving.

But you, child of old Laertes — I hesitate to let you touch the body in the burial, in case I offend the dead by it. In everything else be our partner. And if you want to bring any soldier of the army with you, he is welcome. I will get everything else ready. And you should know that to us you have been a good man.

Odysseus. It was my wish to help. But if it does not please you that I should, I accept what you say, and I will go.

(He goes.)

Teucer.
	Enough. This has gone on a long time already.
	Some of you be quick, dig the hollow grave.
	Others set the high stand with the cauldron
	round with fire, ready for the washing.
	Let another party bring from the hut
	the gear he wore under his shield.
	Child, you too — with what strength you have,
	put a loving hand on your father here
	and help me lift him. The channels
	are still warm, and still driving up
	the dark force in him.
	Come on, everyone who says he is a friend.
	Move. Get going. Do the work
	for this man, who was good in every way —
	and no better man has ever had it done for him.
	I mean Ajax, when he was alive.
Chorus.
	There is a great deal a man can know once he has seen it.
	Before he sees it, nobody can prophesy
	what is coming, or how he will come out of it.

Antigone (c. 441 BC)

Antigone

(Thebes, in front of the royal house. It is just before dawn. Antigone has brought Ismene out through the courtyard gates.)

Antigone. Ismene — my sister, my own blood, the same mother and the same father. Is there one evil, out of everything Oedipus left behind him, that Zeus is not going to bring down on the two of us while we are still alive? There is nothing — no pain, no ruin, no shame, no dishonour — that I have not already seen among your troubles and mine. And now this. What is this order they say the general has just given to the whole city? Do you know anything? Have you heard? Or has it slipped past you that what our enemies do to their enemies is on its way to the people we love?

Ismene. Not one word about the people we love has reached me, Antigone, good or bad, since the day we lost our two brothers — the two of them, on one day, each by the other's hand. And since the Argive army ran during the night just gone, I know nothing beyond that. I am no better off than I was, and no worse.

Antigone. I knew it. That is why I brought you out here past the courtyard gates: so that you could hear it alone.

Ismene. Hear what? Something is turning you dark. I can see it.

Antigone. Yes. Creon, and the burial of our two brothers: one he has honoured and one he has thrown away. Eteocles, they say, he has laid in the ground as justice and custom require, so that he has his honour among the dead below. But Polyneices — the body of Polyneices, who died so miserably — the word has gone out to the citizens that nobody is to cover him with a grave, or so much as cry over him. He is to be left unwept and unburied, a sweet find for the birds, who are watching him already and looking forward to their meal. That, they say, is what the good Creon has proclaimed for you and for me — yes, for me too — and he is coming here now to say it out loud to anyone who has not heard, and he does not treat it as a small matter. Whoever does any of these things is to die for it: stoned to death by the people, in the middle of the city. So that is how things stand for you. And very soon you will show what you are — whether you were born of good blood, or whether you are the bad daughter of a good line.

Ismene. Poor sister. If that is how things are, what use could I be, either way — pulling the knot tighter, or picking it loose?

Antigone. Decide whether you will share the work and the risk with me.

Ismene. What risk? What are you thinking of?

Antigone. Whether you will help these hands of mine lift his body.

Ismene. You mean to bury him? When the city has forbidden it?

Antigone. He is my brother. And yours, whether you like it or not. Nobody will ever catch me betraying him.

Ismene. You reckless thing — with Creon standing against it?

Antigone. Creon has no right to keep me from my own.

Ismene. Oh, sister. Think. Think how our father died, hated and disgraced, after he found out his own crimes for himself and struck out both his eyes with his own hand. Then his mother, who was also his wife — two names for one woman — twisted a rope and ended her life. And third, our two brothers, on a single day, killed each other, the poor fools, each with a hand raised against the other, and made one death between them. Now look at us, the two who are left, alone. Think how much worse we will die if we break the law and go against the ruler's decree and the ruler's power. We have to remember what we are. We were born women; we are not made for fighting men. And we are ruled by people stronger than we are, so we must obey in this, and in things that hurt more than this. So I will ask the dead below to forgive me, because I am forced into it, and I will obey the men in charge. To do more than you can is madness — it makes no sense at all.

Antigone. I would not ask you now. And if you changed your mind later and wanted to do it, I would not be glad to have you with me. Be whatever you have decided to be. I will bury him. If I die doing it, that is a fine way to die. I will lie beside him, his own beside her own — a criminal, and holy. There is more time that I have to please the dead than the living: I will lie there forever. But you, if that is what you choose, go on dishonouring what the gods hold in honour.

Ismene. I dishonour nothing. But to act against the whole city — I was not made for it. I cannot.

Antigone. Use that as your excuse. I am going to heap up a grave over the brother I love best.

Ismene. Oh, you poor thing. I am so afraid for you.

Antigone. Do not be afraid for me. Get your own life straight.

Ismene. Then at least tell nobody what you are doing. Keep it hidden. I will do the same.

Antigone. No — shout it. I will hate you far more if you keep quiet and do not announce it to everyone.

Ismene. You have a hot heart for cold work.

Antigone. I know I am pleasing the people I most need to please.

Ismene. If you can. But you are in love with what cannot be done.

Antigone. Then when my strength gives out, I will stop.

Ismene. You should not go hunting the impossible in the first place.

Antigone. If you go on like this you will have my hatred, and the dead man will hate you too, and he will be right to. Leave me alone — me and my bad judgment — to suffer this terrible thing. Nothing I suffer will be so terrible that it stops me dying well.

Ismene. Go, then, if you have decided. And know this: you are going out of your mind, but to the people you love, you are truly loving.

(Antigone goes out towards the plain. Ismene goes back into the house. The Chorus comes in: the old men of Thebes.)

Chorus.
	Ray of the sun, the finest light
	that ever shone on seven-gated Thebes,
	you appeared at last, eye of the golden day,
	coming up over the streams of Dirce,
	and the man with the white shield, who came out of Argos
	in full armour — you set him running,
	headlong, with a sharper bit in his mouth.
Chorus.
	He rose up against our land
	because of Polyneices and his quarrelsome claims,
	screaming shrilly,
	and flew over into our country like an eagle,
	roofed with a wing of white snow,
	with a mass of weapons
	and helmets with horsehair crests.
Chorus.
	He stood over our houses, his mouth gaping wide
	around our seven gates, with spears that wanted blood,
	and he went away again
	before his jaws could fill with our blood,
	before the pine-fed fire of Hephaestus took our crown of towers.
	That is how loud the crash of War rose about his back —
	too hard a hold for a man wrestling the dragon's own children.
Chorus.
	For Zeus hates the boasts of a big tongue
	above everything, and when he saw them coming
	in a great flood, arrogant
	with the clang of their gold,
	he threw his fire at the man
	on the top of our walls, at the finish line,
	just as he was starting to shout victory.
Chorus.
	He swung and fell on the hard ground,
	the torch still in his hand — the man who a moment ago
	was raging at us in a frenzy, like a drunk god,
	with the blasts of the most hateful winds.
	But it went otherwise for him,
	and to the others great War dealt out other things, smashing them down,
	the strong horse on the right of our team.
Chorus.
	Seven captains at seven gates,
	posted equal against equal,
	left their bronze arms behind for Zeus who turns the battle —
	all but the two hated ones, who, born of one father
	and one mother, set their two
	conquering spears against each other
	and took an equal share of one death.
Chorus.
	But Victory with the great name has come
	and smiled back at Thebes of the many chariots.
	So after this war let us
	make ourselves forget,
	and go to all the temples of the gods
	with dancing all night long, and let Bacchus,
	who shakes the ground of Thebes, lead the way.
Chorus.
	But here is the king of the country,
	Creon, son of Menoeceus, our new ruler
	by the new turn the gods have given things.
	What plan is he rowing towards,
	that he has called this special council of old men
	and summoned us
	with one proclamation for all?

(Creon comes in, with attendants.)

Creon. Men. As for the city: the gods shook it with a heavy sea, and they have set it upright again, safe. I sent messengers to bring you here, you apart from everyone else, because I know that you always respected the power of the throne of Laius; and again, when Oedipus set the city straight; and when he was destroyed, you stayed loyal in your hearts to his sons. So now that those two have died on one day by a double fate — each striking and each struck, with the pollution of a brother's blood on their own hands — I hold all the power and the throne, as the nearest kin of the dead.

Now, there is no way to know a man fully — his spirit, his thinking, his judgment — until he has shown himself in office, with the laws in his hands. For my part, a man who steers the whole city and does not hold to the best advice, but locks his tongue out of some fear — that man I think the worst of men, and I have always thought so. And a man who rates one of his own people above his own country — I count him nowhere. I myself — let Zeus, who sees everything, always, be my witness — would never keep silent if I saw ruin marching on the citizens instead of safety; and I would never make a friend of a man who was an enemy of this land. Because I know this: it is the country that keeps us safe, and only while she sails upright can we make any friends at all.

Those are the laws I use to make this city great. And in keeping with them I have now proclaimed this to the citizens, about the sons of Oedipus. Eteocles, who died fighting for this city and was the best of them all with a spear — he is to be buried, with every rite that goes down to the noblest of the dead. But his brother — Polyneices, I mean — who came back from exile wanting to burn his fathers' land and his family's gods to the ground, wanting to drink the blood of his own people and lead the rest away as slaves — the city has been told: nobody is to give him a grave, nobody is to mourn him. He is to be left unburied, his body a thing for the birds and the dogs to eat, mangled where anyone can see it. That is my thinking. Bad men will never, through me, stand ahead of the just in honour. But whoever means well by this city — I will honour him dead and alive alike.

Chorus. That is your decision, Creon, son of Menoeceus, for the man who hates this city and the man who loves it. And you have the power, we suppose, to use whatever law you like — over the dead, and over all of us who are alive.

Creon. Then see to it that you keep watch over what I have said.

Chorus. Put that on some younger man's shoulders.

Creon. That is not what I meant. There are men watching the body already.

Chorus. Then what else are you ordering us to do?

Creon. Not to take the side of anyone who disobeys.
