Chorus. Nobody is fool enough to want to die.

Creon. And that is the price, exactly. But hope of profit has destroyed many men before now.

(A Guard comes in from the plain.)

Guard. My lord, I am not going to tell you that I have run here, or that I am out of breath from getting my feet up. I stopped a lot of times to think, and I turned round in the road more than once to go back. My mind kept talking to me. "You poor fool, why are you going somewhere you will be punished the moment you arrive?" And then: "You idiot, are you standing still again? If Creon hears this from somebody else, how do you think you will feel?" Turning it over like that, I got here slowly, in my own time, and that is how a short road turns into a long one. In the end, though, coming to you won. And even if what I have to say is nothing, I will say it anyway, because I have come holding onto one hope: that nothing can happen to me except what was going to happen anyway.

Creon. What is it that has you so downhearted?

Guard. I want to tell you about myself first. I did not do it, and I did not see who did it, and it would not be fair for me to come to any harm.

Creon. You aim carefully, and you fence the thing off all round you. Plainly you have some news.

Guard. Bad news makes a man hang back a long time.

Creon. Will you say it, and then get out of here?

Guard. All right, I am telling you. Somebody has just buried the body and gone. They sprinkled dry dust over the flesh and did the rites that have to be done.

Creon. What are you saying? What man dared do this?

Guard. I do not know. There was no mark of a pickaxe there, no earth thrown up by a spade. The ground was hard and dry, unbroken, no wheel-tracks across it. Whoever did the work left no sign. When the first man on the day watch showed us, everybody was amazed, and none of us liked it. The body had vanished — not buried, no, but there was a thin layer of dust over it, the way someone does it who is trying to get clear of a curse. And there was no trace of any animal, any dog, coming near it or tearing at it. Then the ugly words started flying, guard accusing guard, and it would have ended in blows, and there was nobody there to stop it. Every one of us was the man who did it, and none of us was caught doing it; everybody said he knew nothing about it. We were ready to pick up red-hot iron in our hands, to walk through fire, to swear by the gods that we had not done it and did not know who had planned it or done it. In the end, when searching got us nowhere, one man said something that made every one of us bow his head to the ground in fear, because we could not argue with him, and we could not see how it would go well for us if we did what he said. What he said was that this had to be reported to you, not hidden. That won. And the lot fell on me — lucky me — to win this prize. So here I am. I did not want to come, and you do not want me here, I know that. Nobody loves the man who brings bad news.

Chorus. My lord, something in us has been asking for a while now whether this might be the gods' doing.

Creon. Stop, before what you are saying fills me up with anger too, and you turn out to be a fool as well as an old man. What you are saying is unbearable — that the gods take thought for that corpse. What, were they burying him as a benefactor, out of the honour they hold him in? A man who came to burn their pillared temples and the offerings in them, and their land, and to scatter their laws? Or do you see the gods honouring bad men? It does not happen. No. From the beginning there have been men in this city who could hardly bear this order and were muttering against me, shaking their heads where I could not see, and would not keep their necks under the yoke properly, as they should if they were content with me. It was those men — I am certain of it — who paid somebody, and led them on, to do this.

Money. Nothing that ever grew among human beings is as evil as money. It sacks cities. It drives men out of their homes. It teaches honest minds and twists them round to stand for shameful things. It showed men how to be villains, and how to know every kind of impiety there is. But the men who did this for hire have made sure that sooner or later they pay for it. And if Zeus still has any respect from me, know this for certain, and I say it on my oath: if you do not find the very hand that did this burial and bring him before my eyes, then a plain death will not be enough for you. Not until you have been hung up alive, and shown everyone what this outrage is — so that from now on you will know where to get your profit when you go thieving, and learn that you should not love money from every source there is. You will find that dirty earnings have brought more men to ruin than to safety.

Guard. Will you let me say something? Or do I just turn round and go?

Creon. Do you not know, even now, how much your voice annoys me?

Guard. Does it bite you in the ears, or in the heart?

Creon. Why are you working out where my pain is?

Guard. The man who did it hurts your heart. I only hurt your ears.

Creon. God. What a born chatterer you are.

Guard. Maybe. But not the man who did this. Never.

Creon. Yes, and you sold your life for silver to do it.

Guard. Oh. It is a terrible thing when a judge judges, and judges wrong.

Creon. Be as clever about judging as you like. But if you do not show me the men who did this, you will find out that dirty profits bring misery.

(Creon goes into the house.)

Guard. Well, I hope he is found. That would be best. But caught or not — and luck will decide that — there is no chance that you will see me coming here again. I have got out of this alive, when I never hoped to or expected to, and I owe the gods a great deal of thanks.

(The Guard goes out.)

Chorus.
	There are many wonders, and none more wonderful than man.
	He crosses the grey sea in the winter south wind,
	going through under swells
	that roar all round him.
	And the oldest of the gods, Earth,
	who never dies and never tires, he wears away,
	his ploughs going round year after year,
	turning her over with the horse's breed.
Chorus.
	The light-headed tribe of birds he snares and carries off,
	and the nations of wild beasts, and the salt brood of the sea,
	in the coils of his woven nets —
	the man who thinks of everything.
	With his devices he masters the beast
	that lives in the open and walks the hills; he puts the yoke
	on the neck of the shaggy-maned horse
	and on the tireless mountain bull.
Chorus.
	Speech, and thought quick as the wind, and the temper
	that builds a city — he taught himself all of these, and how to escape
	the arrows of frost under the open sky, and the arrows of the rain.
	He has a way through everything. Nothing that is coming
	finds him without a way. From Hades alone he will get no escape,
	but from diseases that had no cure he has thought out ways out.
Chorus.
	Clever beyond all hope, this inventing skill of his,
	and it carries him now to evil, now to good.
	When he honours the laws of the land and the justice of the gods he swore to,
	his city stands high. But the man who lets what is not right
	live with him for daring's sake has no city at all. May he never share my hearth,
	never think my thoughts — the man who does such things.

(The Guard comes back from the plain, leading Antigone.)

Chorus.
	This is a sign from the gods, and I cannot make sense of it.
	I know her. How can I argue
	that this is not the girl Antigone?
	Unhappy child
	of an unhappy father, Oedipus —
	what is this? Surely they are not bringing you in
	for breaking the king's laws,
	caught doing something mad?

Guard. This is her. This is the one who did it. We caught her burying him. Where is Creon?

Chorus. Here he comes, back out of the house, just when he is needed.

(Creon comes out.)

Creon. What is it? What have I come out in time for?

Guard. My lord, there is nothing a man should swear he will never do. Second thoughts make a liar of the first. I could have sworn I would not be back here in a hurry, after the way your threats had me shaking. But a joy you never hoped for is bigger than any other pleasure, and so I have come, oath or no oath, bringing this girl. She was caught arranging the grave. No lots were drawn this time. This piece of luck is mine, and nobody else's. And now, my lord, take her yourself, as you see fit — question her, convict her. And I am free of it. I have a right to be, and clear of this whole trouble.

Creon. This girl you are bringing — how did you take her, and where?

Guard. She was burying the man. You know all of it now.

Creon. Do you understand what you are saying? Are you telling it straight?

Guard. I saw her burying the body you gave orders about. Is that clear and plain enough?

Creon. And how was she seen? How was she caught in the act?

Guard. It went like this. When we got back, with those terrible threats of yours hanging over us, we swept away all the dust that had been covering the body and stripped the corpse properly bare — it was going soft by then. Then we sat down on the top of the ridge, upwind, to keep the smell of him from hitting us, every man keeping his neighbour awake with a stream of abuse if he looked like slacking off. That went on until the bright disc of the sun stood in the middle of the sky and the heat was burning. Then, all at once, a whirlwind lifted a pillar of dust off the ground — a trouble that reached the sky — and filled the plain, and tore the leaves off every tree on the flat, and the whole great air was choked with it. We shut our eyes and put up with it, this sickness from the gods.

And when it had passed, a long while later, there was the girl. She was crying out, the sharp cry of a bird in bitter grief, the way a bird cries when she looks into her nest and finds it empty and the young ones gone. That is how she was when she saw the body bare: she wailed out loud, and she called down evil curses on the men who had done it. Then straight away she brought dry dust in her hands, and from a fine bronze jug held up high she crowned the dead man with a triple pouring of drink-offerings. We saw it and went for her, and closed round her at once, and she was not frightened at all. We charged her with what she had done before and what she had done just now, and she denied none of it — which was sweet to me, and painful at the same time. Getting out of trouble yourself is the sweetest thing there is. Bringing trouble on people you like is painful. But all of that counts for less with me than my own skin.

Creon. You. Yes, you, with your head bowed to the ground. Do you admit that you did this, or do you deny it?

Antigone. I did it. I say so, and I do not deny it.

Creon. You can take yourself off wherever you like. You are clear of a heavy charge.

(The guard goes out.)

Now you. Tell me — not at length, briefly — did you know that a proclamation had forbidden this?

Antigone. I knew. How could I not? It was public.

Creon. And you still dared to step over these laws?

Antigone. Yes. Because it was not Zeus who made that proclamation to me. And Justice, who lives with the gods below, laid down no such laws among human beings. I did not think your orders had so much strength in them that a man — a mortal — could outrun the unwritten and unshakable laws of the gods. Those are not laws of today or yesterday. They live for ever, and nobody knows when they first appeared. I was not going to pay the penalty for breaking them, in front of the gods, because I was afraid of what some man thinks.

I knew I was going to die — of course I knew — even if you had never proclaimed a word. And if I die before my time, I call that a gain. Anyone who lives the way I live, in the middle of so much evil — how is dying not a gain for them? So to meet this death is a pain that counts for nothing. But if I had let my own mother's son lie dead and unburied, that would have hurt. This does not hurt me. And if what I am doing looks like folly to you, then it may be a fool who is charging me with folly.

Chorus. The girl shows the raw stock she comes from — a raw father's raw daughter. She does not know how to bend to trouble.

Creon. Then let her know this: it is the stiffest spirits that fall hardest. The toughest iron, baked in the fire until it is brittle, is the iron you see snap and shatter most often. And I have seen horses with big tempers brought to order by a small bit. There is no room for big thoughts in anyone who is the slave of the people next door.

This girl already knew all about outrage when she stepped over the laws that had been laid down. And now that she has done it, here is a second outrage: to boast of it, and to laugh at having done it. Well then, I am not the man now — she is the man — if she gets this victory and pays nothing for it. No. Whether she is my sister's child, or closer to me by blood than anyone who worships Zeus at the altar of my house, she and her sister will not escape the worst of deaths. Yes, her sister too. I charge her with an equal share in planning this burial. Call her out. I saw her inside just now, raving, not in possession of her wits. The mind of people plotting something crooked in the dark usually gets caught as a thief before the act. But what I hate most is when someone is caught in a crime and then wants to make it look beautiful.

Antigone. Do you want anything more than to take me and kill me?

Creon. Nothing. With that, I have everything.

Antigone. Then what are you waiting for? Nothing you say pleases me, and I hope nothing ever will; and everything I do is just as hateful to you. Still, where could I have won a brighter glory than by putting my own brother in a grave? Every one of these men would say they approved of it — if fear had not locked their tongues. But a tyrant is lucky in a great many ways, and one of them is that he can do and say whatever he likes.

Creon. You are the only one of these Thebans who sees it that way.

Antigone. They see it too. They keep their mouths shut for you.

Creon. And you are not ashamed to think differently from them?

Antigone. There is no shame in honouring my own flesh and blood.

Creon. Was the man who died on the other side not your blood too?

Antigone. My blood. Same mother, same father.

Creon. Then why pay an honour that is an insult to him?

Antigone. The dead man will not say so.

Creon. He will, if you honour him no higher than the traitor.

Antigone. It was not a slave who died. It was his brother.

Creon. Attacking this country. The other died defending it.

Antigone. Hades wants these laws kept all the same.

Creon. But the good man does not get an equal share with the bad.

Antigone. Who knows what counts as clean down there?

Creon. An enemy is never one of your own. Not even when he is dead.

Antigone. I was not born to share in hating. I was born to share in loving.

Creon. Then go down there and love them, if you must love someone. While I am alive, no woman is going to rule.

(Ismene is brought out of the house by two attendants.)

Chorus.
	Look — here is Ismene at the doors,
	weeping a sister's tears;
	a cloud over her brow
	spoils her flushed face
	and wets her lovely cheek.
Creon.
	And you — you who slid about my house like a viper
	and drank my blood while I never saw it,
	never knew I was rearing two ruins,
	two risings against my throne —
	come, tell me. Do you say you had a share
	in this burial too? Or will you swear you knew nothing?
Ismene.
	I did it — if she agrees.
	I take my share of the blame, and I carry it.
Antigone.
	No. Justice will not let you.
	You did not want to, and I did not let you in.
Ismene.
	But with you in trouble like this, I am not ashamed
	to make myself your shipmate in the suffering.
Antigone.
	Whose deed it was, Hades and the dead below know.
	One of my own who loves me in words — I do not love her.
Ismene.
	Sister, do not shut me out of the honour —
	let me die with you, and make the dead man clean.
Antigone.
	Do not die with me. Do not claim what you never touched.
	It is enough that I die.
Ismene.
	What life is there for me, once I have lost you?
Antigone.
	Ask Creon. He is the one you look after.
Ismene.
	Why hurt me like this? It does you no good.
Antigone.
	If I am laughing at you, it hurts me to do it.
Ismene.
	Then what can I do for you, even now?
Antigone.
	Save yourself. I do not grudge you your escape.
Ismene.
	Oh, this is misery. Am I to miss your death as well?
Antigone.
	You chose to live. I chose to die.
Ismene.
	Not without my saying what I had to say.
Antigone.
	One side thought you were right. The other thought I was.
Ismene.
	And yet the fault is the same for both of us.
Antigone.
	Take heart. You are alive. My life died long ago,
	so that I could be of use to the dead.
Creon.
	One of these two girls, I tell you, has just now shown
	she is a fool. The other has been one since the day she was born.
Ismene.
	Whatever sense we are born with, my king,
	does not stay with people in trouble. It leaves them.
Creon.
	Yours left you, when you chose to do evil with the evil.
Ismene.
	What life is there for me alone, without her?
Creon.
	Do not say "her." She no longer exists.
Ismene.
	You will kill your own son's bride?
Creon.
	There are other fields for him to plough.
Ismene.
	None fitted to him the way she was.
Creon.
	I hate a bad wife for a son of mine.
Antigone.
	Haemon — dearest — how your father dishonours you.
Creon.
	You are wearing me out, you and your marriage.
Chorus.
	Will you really take her from your own son?
Creon.
	Hades is the one who will stop this wedding.
Chorus.
	Then it is settled, it seems. She dies.
Creon.
	Settled — for you, and by me. No more delay.
	Take them inside, slaves. From now on
	they must be women, and not left loose.
	Even the bold run for it
	when they see Hades closing in on their life.

(The attendants take Antigone and Ismene into the house. Creon stays.)

Chorus.
	Lucky are those whose lives have never tasted evil.
	For once a house is shaken by the gods
	no ruin is missing from it; it creeps down
	over the whole crowd of the family —
	like the swell of the sea, when Thracian gales
	blow hard across the dark water under the surface,
	and it rolls up black sand from the bottom,
	and the headlands that take the blows of the wind
	roar back with a groan.
Chorus.
	I see the old sorrows of the house of Labdacus
	falling on the sorrows of the dead.
	One generation does not free the next;
	some god drives it down, and there is no release.
	Just now a light had been spread
	over the last root in the house of Oedipus,
	and now the bloody dust of the gods below
	mows it down in its turn — that,
	and folly in speech, and a fury in the mind.
Chorus.
	Zeus, what overstepping by men
	could hold your power in check?
	Sleep, who catches everything, never takes it;
	the tireless months of the gods do not;
	ageless through time, you hold in your power
	the marble glitter of Olympus.
	For what comes next, and what is far off,
	and what went before, this law will hold:
	nothing enormous enters the life of mortals
	without ruin.
Chorus.
	Hope goes wandering wide, and to many men
	it is a comfort; to many it is a cheat,
	a trap of light-headed desires.
	It creeps up on a man who knows nothing
	until he sets his foot on hot fire.
	Somebody wise once made a saying famous:
	evil looks like good, sooner or later,
	to the man whose mind a god
	is leading towards ruin —
Chorus.
	and he lives only the briefest time clear of ruin.

(Haemon comes in.)

Chorus.
	But here is Haemon, the last born
	of your children. Does he come grieving
	for Antigone, the bride he was promised —
	sick at heart over the cheat of his marriage?

Creon. We shall know soon enough, better than any prophet could tell us. My son — you have heard the final vote on the girl you were going to marry. Have you come here raging at your father? Or are we still your own, whatever we do?

Haemon. Father, I am yours. You have good judgement, and you set me straight by it, and I will follow it. No marriage will count for more with me than your good guidance.

Creon. Yes, my boy. That is how to carry it in your heart: stand behind your father's judgement in everything. That is why men pray to father obedient children and keep them in their homes — so that they pay back their father's enemy with harm and honour his friend exactly as their father does. And the man who fathers useless children — what has he done, would you say, except breed trouble for himself and a great deal of laughter for his enemies?

So do not, my boy, ever throw your good sense away for pleasure, because of a woman. Know that it turns into a cold thing to hold in your arms — a bad woman sharing your bed and your house. What wound could go deeper than a false friend? No. Spit her out like an enemy, and let her go and marry somebody in Hades. I caught her — the one person in the whole city — in open disobedience, and I will not make a liar of myself in front of the city. I will kill her. Let her sing her hymns to Zeus of the blood-tie. If I rear my own family to be disorderly, the people outside the family will certainly go the same way. A man who is straight in his own household will be seen to be just in the city too. But anyone who oversteps and does violence to the laws, or thinks he can give orders to those in power, gets no praise from me. Whoever the city sets up, that man must be obeyed — in small things and in just things and in their opposites. That is the man I would trust to rule well, and to be willing to be ruled well, and to hold the post he was given in a storm of spears, a just and good man at your side in the line.

There is no worse evil than anarchy. It destroys cities; it turns houses upside down; it breaks the allied ranks and sends them running. But where men stand firm, it is obedience that saves most of their lives. So we must defend good order, and never, in any way, let ourselves be beaten by a woman. If we must fall, better to fall to a man. Then nobody could call us weaker than women.

Chorus. To us — unless age has robbed us of our wits — you seem to speak sensibly about what you are speaking of.

Haemon. Father, the gods plant reason in human beings, and of everything we own it is the highest thing. To say that you are wrong in what you have said — I could not do it, and I hope I never learn how. But another man might have something right to say too. And it is my nature to watch, on your behalf, everything people say or do or find to blame. Your eye is a frightening thing to an ordinary citizen, when it comes to words you would not enjoy hearing. But I can hear these things, in the dark: how the city is grieving for this girl — how of all women she least deserves to die so shamefully, for deeds so glorious. Her own brother had fallen in the slaughter, and she would not leave him unburied, to be destroyed by wild dogs or by some bird. Does she not deserve golden honour? That is the dark talk that goes quietly round.

For me, father, nothing I own is worth more than your doing well. What greater pride is there for children than a father's flourishing good name — or for a father than his children's? So do not carry one way of thinking in you and one only — that what you say, and nothing else, is right. A man who thinks he alone has sense, or a tongue or a soul that nobody else has — when you open men like that up, they turn out empty. Even a wise man is not shamed by learning many things, and by not pulling too tight. You see how, beside a winter torrent, the trees that yield keep their branches, and the ones that stand against it are torn out root and all. And the sailor who keeps the sheet taut and never slackens it turns his boat over, and finishes his voyage with the benches underneath him.

Give way. Let your anger shift. If I, a younger man, can contribute one judgement, it is this: by far the best thing is for a man to be born full of knowledge. But since that seldom tips the right way, it is good to learn from those who speak well.

Chorus. My king, if he says something to the point, it is right that you should learn from him — and you, Haemon, from your father. There has been good speaking on both sides.

Creon. Men of my age are to be taught sense by a man of his?

Haemon. Nothing that is not just. If I am young, look at what I do, not at my years.

Creon. Is honouring the disorderly something to do?

Haemon. I would never tell anyone to respect the bad.

Creon. And is she not sick with exactly that disease?

Haemon. The whole people of this city of Thebes say no.

Creon. Is the city going to tell me how to rule?

Haemon. Do you hear how young that sounds?

Creon. Am I to rule this land for someone other than myself?

Haemon. There is no city that belongs to one man.

Creon. Is the city not held to belong to the man in power?

Haemon. You would rule a desert beautifully. Alone.

Creon. This one, it seems, is fighting on the woman's side.

Haemon. If you are a woman. It is you I care about.

Creon. You filthy thing — taking your father to court?

Haemon. Because I see you going wrong, against justice.

Creon. I go wrong by respecting my own authority?

Haemon. You do not respect it. You are trampling the honours of the gods.

Creon. Foul character — and coming second to a woman.

Haemon. You will never catch me giving in to what is shameful.

Creon. Every word you say is for her.

Haemon. And for you, and for me, and for the gods below.

Creon. You will never marry her while she is alive.

Haemon. Then she will die, and dying she will destroy someone.

Creon. So you have got as far as threats now? That bold?

Haemon. What threat is there in speaking against empty judgements?

Creon. You will pay in tears for teaching sense, with no sense in you.

Haemon. If you were not my father, I would say you were out of your mind.

Creon. A woman's slave. Do not try to wheedle me.

Haemon. Do you want to talk, and talk, and hear nothing back?

Creon. Is that so? By this Olympus above us — you can be sure of it — you will not go on abusing me and get any joy of it. Bring out that hateful thing. She dies now, this minute, here in front of his eyes, at her bridegroom's side.

Haemon. Not at my side. Never think it. She will not die beside me, and you will never set eyes on my face again. Go mad with whichever of your friends can stand it.

(He goes.)

Chorus. The man is gone, my king, fast, in a rage. A mind that young is heavy when it is hurt.

Creon. Let him do it. Let him go and think bigger thoughts than a man should. He will not save those two girls from their death.

Chorus. Both of them? You mean to kill them both?

Creon. Not the one who did not touch it. You are right.

Chorus. And what death do you have in mind for the other?

Creon. I will take her where the path is empty of people and hide her alive in a hollow of rock, with just as much food set out as keeps off pollution, so that the whole city escapes the stain. And there she can pray to Hades — the only god she worships — and perhaps she will win herself a way out of dying. Or else she will learn, at last, that worshipping what is in Hades is wasted labour.

(He goes in.)

Chorus.
	Love, unbeaten in battle — Love,
	who falls on what men own,
	who keeps the night watch on a girl's soft cheek,
	you cross the sea, you go into the huts in the wild fields;
	not one of the immortals can escape you,
	nor any of us who live for a day.
	Whoever has you goes mad.
Chorus.
	You pull the minds of just men off the line
	into injustice, to their ruin;
	you have stirred up this quarrel too,
	between men of one blood.
	And the bright desire in the eyes of the bride
	made for the bed — that wins.
	It sits beside the great laws as a fellow ruler.
	The goddess Aphrodite plays with us,
	and there is no fighting her.

(Antigone is brought out of the house under guard.)

Chorus.
	And now I too am carried outside the laws
	as I look at this. I cannot hold back
	the springs of my tears any longer,
	when I see Antigone going
	to the bridal chamber where everyone sleeps.
Antigone.
	Look at me, citizens of my father's land,
	walking the last road,
	looking at the last light of the sun,
	and never again. Hades, who puts everyone to bed,
	is leading me alive to the shore of Acheron.
	No wedding song was my share; no bridal hymn
	was ever sung for me. I will be married to Acheron.
Chorus.
	But you go famous, and with praise,
	down into this hiding place of the dead,
	not struck by wasting sickness,
	not paid the wages of the sword.
	You go by your own law, alive —
	the only mortal who ever did —
	down into Hades.
Antigone.
	I have heard how the Phrygian stranger died
	the most pitiful of deaths — Niobe, the daughter of Tantalus,
	on the peak of Sipylus, whom the rock grew over
	like clinging ivy, and mastered her; and the rains,
	so people say, never leave her as she wastes away,
	nor the snow; and under her weeping brows
	she wets the ridges of the hill.
	The god is putting me to sleep like her. Exactly like her.
Chorus.
	But she was a goddess, and born of gods,
	and we are mortals, born to die.
	Still, for a woman who is dead it is a great thing to hear
	that she drew the same lot as the godlike —
	in her life, and afterwards in her death.
Antigone.
	Oh — I am being laughed at. By the gods of our fathers,
	why not wait until I am gone to mock me,
	and not do it to my face?
	City — rich men of my city —
	springs of Dirce, sacred grove of Thebes and her chariots —
	you at least I call as witnesses
	how unwept by anyone I love, and by what laws,
	I go to the heaped rock that shuts in
	my strange new tomb.
	Oh, I am wretched. I have no home among the living
	and none among the dead —
	not with those who live, not with those who have died.
Chorus.
	You went to the far edge of daring
	and struck the high seat of Justice,
	child, and fell hard.
	You are paying off some debt of your father's.
Antigone.
	You have touched the thought that hurts me most:
	my father's grief, told three times over,
	and the whole doom of us,
	the famous house of Labdacus.
	Oh, the ruin of my mother's bed,
	my poor mother sleeping with her own son,
	my father —
	and I was born of them, wretched.
	To them I go, cursed, unmarried,
	to live with them.
	Brother — your marriage was doomed;
	you died, and dying you killed me while I still lived.
Chorus.
	There is a kind of reverence in showing reverence;
	but power, to the man whose care is power,
	can never be stepped over.
	Your own self-willed temper destroyed you.
Antigone.
	Unwept, unloved, no wedding song,
	I am led, wretched, down the road that waits.
	I may not look any longer, poor girl,
	at this holy eye of light.
	No tear falls for my fate.
	Not one of my own is grieving.

Creon. Do you not realise that nobody would ever stop singing and wailing before they died, if wailing did any good? Take her away. Now. Wall her up in the roofed tomb the way I ordered, and leave her there alone, whether she wants to die or to go on living in a house like that. Our hands are clean as far as this girl is concerned. But one way or the other, she loses her home up here.

Antigone. Tomb. Bridal room. Deep-dug house that will keep me for ever. I am on my way to my own people, and Persephone has already taken in so many of them, dead among the dead. I go down last of all, and by far the worst, before my share of life has run out. And yet I go with a strong hope in me: that I will be welcome to my father when I come, and dear to you, Mother, and dear to you, my brother. Because when each of you died it was my own hands that washed you and laid you out and poured the offerings at your graves. And now, Polyneices, this is what I get for tending your body.

And yet I was right to honour you. Anyone with sense would say so. I would never have taken this on against the will of the city for children, if I had been a mother, or for a husband, if he had lain dead and rotting. What law am I appealing to when I say that? If my husband died, I could have had another. If I lost a child, I could have had another, by another man. But with my mother and my father both hidden in Hades, no brother could ever grow for me again. That was the law I honoured you by, above all the others, and for it Creon decided that I was a criminal and had dared something terrible, my brother.

And now he has hold of me and is taking me away like this: no marriage bed, no wedding song, no share of marriage, no bringing up of children. Stripped of everyone who owes me anything, in misery, I go alive into the hollow places of the dead. What justice of the gods have I broken? Why should I look to the gods any more, wretched as I am? Who is there to call on for help? I did what was holy, and holiness has got me convicted of the opposite. Well, if this is what the gods approve of, then once I have suffered I will admit that I was at fault. But if the fault is these men's, I wish them nothing worse than what they are doing, unjustly, to me.

Chorus.
	The same winds still,
	the same gusts of the spirit, have hold of her.
Creon.
	Then the men taking her
	will have reason to cry over their slowness.
Antigone.
	Oh. That word
	has come as close to death as a word can come.
Chorus.
	I cannot tell you to take heart.
	It is going to end the way he says.
Antigone.
	City of my fathers, land of Thebes,
	and you gods who were here before us,
	they are taking me, and there is no more waiting.
	Look at me, you lords of Thebes:
	the last daughter left of the house of your kings.
	Look what I suffer, and at whose hands,
	for holding holy what is holy.

(Antigone is led away.)

Chorus.
	Danae too had to give up the light of the sky
	for a room with walls of bronze.
	Her father shut her in it, because a prophecy
	had said her son would kill him,
	and she was hidden in a bedroom that was a tomb,
	and put under the yoke.
	And yet she came of an honoured line, my child, my child,
	and she kept in her body the seed of Zeus,
	who came to her as a shower of gold.
	But whatever the power is that hands out our portion,
	it is terrible.
	Not wealth escapes it, not war,
	not a walled tower, not the black ships
	that beat against the sea.
Chorus.
	And the son of Dryas, quick to anger,
	king of the Edonians, was put under the yoke too,
	for his mocking rages:
	Dionysus shut him up in a prison of rock.
	There the terrible, flowering force of his madness
	drained out of him drop by drop,
	and he came to know the god
	he had struck at in his frenzy with a mocking tongue.
	He had tried to stop the women the god possessed,
	and the fire of the god's cry,
	and he had angered the Muses who love the flute.
Chorus.
	And beside the Dark Rocks, where two seas meet,
	are the shores of the Bosporus
	and Salmydessus in Thrace, that welcomes no stranger,
	where Ares, who lives close to that city,
	saw the cursed wound
	dealt to the two sons of Phineus by his savage second wife:
	darkness struck into the circles of their eyes,
	crying out for vengeance,
	eyes put out not by a blade
	but by bloody hands and the sharp point of a weaver's shuttle.
Chorus.
	They wasted away, poor boys, and wept
	for their miserable suffering,
	born of a mother whose marriage had been torn from her.
	And she was of the ancient blood of the Erechtheids,
	the kings of Athens,
	and in far-off caves she was reared
	among her father's storms:
	the daughter of Boreas, the North Wind,
	running with the horses over the steep hills,
	a child of gods. And yet on her too
	the long-lived Fates laid their hands, my child.

(Teiresias comes in, blind, led by a boy.)

Teiresias. Lords of Thebes, we have come here by one road, two people seeing with one pair of eyes. That is how the blind travel: somebody goes in front.

Creon. What is it, old Teiresias? What news?

Teiresias. I will tell you. And you, do what the prophet says.

Creon. I have never yet stood apart from your judgement.

Teiresias. That is why you have steered this city on a straight course.

Creon. I can testify to it. I have had the benefit.

Teiresias. Then understand this: you are standing on a razor's edge again.

Creon. What do you mean? Your mouth makes me shiver.

Teiresias. You will know, when you hear what my art has shown me.

I was sitting in my old place for reading the birds, where every kind of bird comes in to me as into a harbour, and I heard a sound out of them I had never heard: they were screaming in some ugly frenzy, and it had turned to gibberish. And I could tell they were tearing at each other with their claws, murderously. The beating of their wings was clear enough. I was afraid at once, and I tried the burnt offering on an altar with the fire well lit. But the god of fire would not blaze up out of the meat. Instead a wet ooze sweated out of the thigh-bones onto the embers and smoked and spat; the gall burst and scattered into the air; and the thighs lay there streaming, bare of the fat that had been wrapped round them. That is what this boy told me: the rites failing, the signs giving nothing. He is my guide, as I am the guide of others.

And the city is sick with this because of you, because of your decision. Every altar in our city, every hearth, is fouled with what the birds and the dogs have carried there off the poor fallen son of Oedipus. And so the gods will no longer take our prayers at the sacrifice, or the flame from the thigh-meat; and no bird screams out a sign we can read, because they have eaten the fat of a slaughtered man's blood.

Think about it, my son. Everybody makes mistakes. But when a man has made one, he is not stupid, and not doomed either, if he sets about mending the harm he has fallen into instead of standing there immovable. Stubbornness gets a man convicted of stupidity. Give way to the dead. Do not keep stabbing at a man who is finished. What kind of strength is it, to kill the dead a second time? I mean you well, and what I am telling you is good for you. And the sweetest thing there is, is to learn from good advice, when the advice is to your profit.

Creon. Old man, every one of you shoots at me like archers at a target, and now I am not even safe from your prophet's trade. Your tribe has been buying and selling me for years; I have been loaded and shipped like cargo. Go on, make your profit; trade in the white gold of Sardis and the gold of India, if you like. But you will not put that man in a grave. Not even if the eagles of Zeus want to snatch him up for food and carry him to the throne of Zeus himself. Not even then will I be frightened by that pollution into allowing him a burial. I know very well that no human being has the strength to pollute the gods. And even the cleverest of men, old Teiresias, take a shameful fall when they dress shameful words up handsomely for the sake of money.

Teiresias. Ah. Does any man know, does any man stop to think,

Creon. What? What is this great truth for everyone that you are announcing?

Teiresias. How much the best thing anyone can own is good judgement?

Creon. As much, I suppose, as having no sense is the worst thing.

Teiresias. And that is the sickness you are full of.

Creon. I do not want to answer a prophet with insults.

Teiresias. You are doing it, when you say my prophecies are lies.

Creon. The whole tribe of prophets loves money.

Teiresias. And the tribe of tyrants loves dirty profit.

Creon. Do you know that you are saying that about your king?

Teiresias. I know. It was through me that you saved this city.

Creon. You are a skilled prophet. You just like doing wrong.

Teiresias. You will make me say what should stay unspoken in my mind.

Creon. Say it. Only not for money.

Teiresias. Is that what you think I have been doing, where you are concerned?

Creon. Understand this: you are not going to buy and sell my judgement.

Teiresias. Then understand this, and understand it well. The sun's chariot will not run many more laps before you pay back, out of your own body, one corpse for the corpses: a dead child for the dead. Because you have taken one who belonged up here and thrown her down below; you have lodged a living soul in a tomb, with no honour. And you keep up here what belongs to the gods below: a dead body, with no share of the rites, no burial, unholy. None of that is any business of yours, or of the gods above; you are forcing it on them. And for that the destroyers who come late are lying in wait for you, the Furies of Hades and of the gods, and you will be caught in these same evils yourself.

Now look and see whether I say this because somebody has silvered my palm. Not much time will pass before this house of yours is loud with the wailing of men and of women. And every city is stirring up in hatred against you: every city whose dead have had their funeral rites performed by dogs, or wild beasts, or some bird of the air, carrying the unholy stink home to the hearths of the city.

You provoked me, so I have shot these at you like an archer, in anger, straight from the heart, and they fly true. You will not outrun their sting.

Boy, take me home. Let him spend his temper on younger men, and learn to keep a quieter tongue in his head, and a better mind than the one he is carrying now.

(Teiresias is led away.)

Chorus. The man is gone, my king, and he has left terrible prophecies behind him. And we know one thing: in all the time since this hair of ours turned from black to white, he has never once told this city a lie.

Creon. I know it myself. My mind is in confusion. To give way is terrible. To stand and resist, and have my will smashed by ruin, that is terrible too.

Chorus. You need to take good advice, son of Menoeceus.

Creon. Then what should I do? Tell me. I will do it.

Chorus. Go and let the girl out of her underground room. And build a tomb for the man who is lying there.

Creon. That is your advice? You think I should give in?

Chorus. Yes, my king, and as fast as you can. The harm the gods send has swift feet, and it cuts off men whose thinking has gone wrong.

Creon. Oh, it is hard. I am going against my heart, but I will do it. There is no use fighting necessity.

Chorus. Then go and do it yourself. Do not leave it to other people.

Creon. I will go as I am. Come, come, servants, all of you, those here and those not here. Get axes in your hands and run to that place over there, the one in view. My judgement has turned this way, and I tied her up, so I will be there to untie her myself. I am afraid, now, that the best thing is to keep the laws that have been laid down, right to the end of your life.

(Creon goes out with his men.)

Chorus.
	God of many names, glory of Semele, the bride of Cadmus,
	and child of Zeus who thunders deep,
	you who watch over famous Italy
	and rule in the folds of the valley of Eleusis,
	Demeter's, where everyone is welcome:
	Bacchus, who lives in Thebes,
	the mother-city of your Bacchants,
	beside the wet streams of the Ismenus,
	on the ground where the savage dragon's teeth were sown.
Chorus.
	You are seen through the flaring smoke of torches
	above the twin-peaked rock of Parnassus,
	where the nymphs of the Corycian cave
	walk in your train,
	and Castalia's spring sees you too.
	The ivy-covered slopes of the hills of Nysa
	and the green shore heavy with grapes
	send you here,
	while immortal voices cry your cry,
	to watch over the streets of Thebes.
Chorus.
	Thebes you honour above every city,
	you and your mother whom the lightning struck.
	And now, when the whole city and all its people
	are held in the grip of a violent sickness,
	come with cleansing feet over the slope of Parnassus,
	or across the groaning strait.
Chorus.
	You who lead the dance of the stars that breathe fire,
	who watch over the voices in the night,
	child of Zeus, show yourself,
	my lord, with the women who go round you,
	the Thyiads, who dance for you all night in their madness
	and call you Iacchus, the giver.

(A Messenger comes in.)

Messenger. You who live beside the house of Cadmus and Amphion: there is no human life, however it stands, that I would ever praise or blame as settled. Luck sets a man on his feet and luck knocks him down, lucky and unlucky alike, every day; and nobody can prophesy to men how things will stand. Creon was a man to envy once, in my opinion. He had saved this land of Cadmus from its enemies, taken sole and complete command of the country, and steered it straight, and he was flourishing in a noble crop of children. And now all of it is gone. When a man has thrown away what gave him pleasure, I do not count him as living. He is a breathing corpse. Pile up wealth in your house if you like; live with a king's pomp. If the joy is missing from it, I would not give the shadow of smoke for the rest, set against joy.

Chorus. What is this new weight of grief for the royal house that you have brought us?

Messenger. They are dead. And the living are to blame for their dying.

Chorus. Who killed? Who is lying dead? Tell us.

Messenger. Haemon is dead. And the hand that shed his blood was his own.

Chorus. His own? Or his father's?

Messenger. His own, in rage at his father over the killing.

Chorus. Prophet, how true your word has come.

Messenger. That is how things are. The rest is for you to decide.

Chorus. Wait. Here is Eurydice, poor woman, Creon's wife. Either she has heard about her son, or it is chance that brings her out of the house.

(Eurydice comes out.)

Eurydice. People of Thebes, all of you: I heard what you were saying as I was going out to the gate to pray to the goddess Pallas. I was just drawing back the bolts of the door to open it, when the sound of a disaster in my own house struck through my ears. I fell back terrified into my slaves' arms, and everything went dark. But tell me again, whatever the news was. Tell it again. I am no stranger to bad news. I can hear it.

Messenger. Dear lady, I was there, and I will tell you, and I will leave out not one word of the truth. Why should I soften it for you, and be shown up afterwards as a liar? The truth is always the straight road.

I went with your husband as his guide to the far edge of the plain, where the body of Polyneices was still lying, unpitied, torn by dogs. We prayed to the goddess of the crossroads and to Pluto to hold back their anger and be kind. We washed him with the holy washing, and with fresh-cut branches we burned what was left of him, and heaped up a high mound of his own earth over it. Then we turned to go into the girl's hollow bridal room of Hades, with its floor of stone.

While we were still some way off, one of the men heard the sound of loud, high wailing from the direction of that bridal room where no rites had been paid, and came and told his master Creon. As Creon crept closer the confused sounds of a wretched cry came round him, and he groaned, and let out a word of misery. "Oh. Am I a prophet? Is this the unluckiest road I have ever walked? It is my son's voice calling me. Servants, go closer, quickly. Stand by the tomb, go in through the gap where the stones have been torn out of the mound, right up to the mouth of it, and look. Is it Haemon's voice I hear, or are the gods deceiving me?"

Messenger. We looked, as our despairing master ordered. And in the furthest part of the tomb we saw her, hanging by the neck, in a noose of fine linen, and him, with his arms thrown round her waist, holding her, crying out for his bride lost to the world below, and for what his father had done, and for his wretched marriage.

When his father saw him he gave a terrible groan and went in to him and called to him, wailing: "Poor boy, what have you done? What was in your mind? What disaster has destroyed your reason? Come out, my son. I am begging you on my knees." But the boy glared at him with wild eyes, spat in his face, said nothing in reply, and drew his two-edged sword. His father ran out and he missed. Then, poor wretch, he turned his anger on himself, and just as he stood, leaned on the sword and drove it half its length into his side. Still conscious, he took the girl in the loose curve of his arm and held her, and gasping, sent a sharp jet of blood down her white cheek, drop by drop.

Dead, he lies holding the dead. He got his marriage in the end, poor boy, in the house of Hades. And he has shown the world how badly a man is served by bad judgement, the greatest evil that can lie on him.

(Eurydice goes back into the house.)

Chorus. What do you make of that? The woman has gone back in without a word, good or bad.

Messenger. It startled me too. But I live on the hope that she has heard her son's fate and does not think it proper to cry out in front of the city: that she will go inside and set her slaves to mourn the household's grief. She has too much judgement to do anything wrong.

Chorus. We do not know. To us, too much silence is as heavy a sign as a lot of useless shouting.

Messenger. Well, we will find out whether she is hiding some purpose in the dark of a heart worked up to fury. I will go into the house. You are right. Too much silence can weigh as much.

(He goes in. Creon comes in carrying the body of Haemon.)

Chorus.
	And here is the king himself,
	with a memorial in his arms that speaks for itself:
	if it is right to say it, the ruin of no stranger's making,
	but his own mistake.
Creon.
	Oh,
	the mistakes of a mind that would not think,
	stubborn, deadly.
	Look at us: the killer and the killed,
	one blood.
	Oh, the misery of my decisions.
	My son. So young, and dead so young,
	aiai, aiai,
	you are dead, you are gone,
	by my folly, not yours.
Chorus.
	Oh. You seem to have seen justice too late.
Creon.
	Oh, misery.
	I have learned. I am wretched, and I have learned.
	A god, then, it was then, with a great weight in his hand
	struck me on the head and shook me down wild roads,
	oh, and trampled my joy underfoot.
	Ah. Ah. The pains men take, and the pain of them.
Messenger.
	Master, you have your hands full, and more in store.
	One grief you carry in your arms;
	the other is in the house, and you will see it soon.
Creon.
	What? What can be worse, after this?
Messenger.
	Your wife is dead. The mother of this dead boy,
	all mother to him, poor woman, by wounds cut a moment ago.
Creon.
	Oh.
	Oh harbour of Hades, that no washing can clean,
	why are you killing me? Why?
	You who bring me the worst news,
	what is it you are saying?
	Aiai. You have killed a dead man over again.
	What are you telling me, boy? What new thing,
	aiai, aiai,
	a woman's death, slaughtered,
	heaped on the death that was here already?
Chorus.
	You can see for yourself. It is not hidden inside any more.

(The doors are opened. Eurydice's body lies inside.)

Creon.
	Oh,
	a second evil. I see it, wretched as I am.
	What fate is still waiting for me? What more?
	I have just had my son in my arms,
	and now, in front of me, another corpse.
	Ah, ah, poor mother. Ah, my son.
Messenger.
	She stood at the altar with a sharp-whetted sword
	and let the dark come down over her eyes.
	First she cried for the glorious death of Megareus, who died before,
	then for this boy, and last of all
	she called down evil on you, the killer of her sons.
Creon.
	Aiai, aiai.
	I am shaking with fear. Why does nobody
	strike me full in the chest with a two-edged sword?
	I am wretched, aiai,
	and I am drowning in wretched misery.
Messenger.
	Yes. She laid the blame for both deaths, this one and the other,
	on you, this dead woman, before she died.
Creon.
	And how did she die? How did the blood come?
Messenger.
	By her own hand. A blow under the heart,
	when she heard what had happened to her son, and the crying.
Creon.
	Oh, oh. This will never fit another man.
	This is mine. I did it.
	I killed you, I did, it was me, I say it,
	and it is true. Servants,
	take me away. Quickly. Take me out of the way.
	I am nothing now. I am less than nobody.
Chorus.
	What you ask is a gain, if there is any gain in evil.
	When trouble lies at your feet, the shortest way is best.
Creon.
	Let it come, let it come,
	the best death of all, the one that brings me
	my last day. Let it come, let it come,
	so that I never see another.
Chorus.
	That is for later. There is work here in front of us.
	Those things are in the hands of the ones whose business they are.
Creon.
	Everything I want, I put into that prayer.
Chorus.
	Then pray for nothing more. There is no escape for a human being
	from the suffering that is allotted.
Creon.
	Take me away, out of the way, a useless man,
	who killed you, my son, without meaning to,
	and you too, my wife. Oh, misery. There is nowhere I can look,
	nothing I can lean on. Everything in my hands
	has gone crooked, and on my head
	a fate has jumped that I cannot carry.

(Creon is led into the house.)

Chorus.
	Good sense is the first part of happiness,
	by a long way.
	And do nothing the gods would count as unholy.
	Great words from boasting men
	are paid for with great blows,
	and in old age they teach good sense.

Oedipus the King (c. 429 BC)

Oedipus the King

(Before the palace at Thebes. People of every age sit on the altar steps holding suppliants' branches. Oedipus comes out.)

Oedipus. My children — the newest growth on old Cadmus' tree — why are you sitting here in front of me like this, crowded round the altars with your branches wound in wool? The whole city is full of incense, and full at the same time of hymns for healing and of groaning. I did not think it right, children, to hear about it from other people's messengers, so I have come myself: Oedipus, the man whose name everybody knows. Now, old man — you speak, since it is your place to speak for the rest of them. What has you sitting here? Is it something you are afraid of, or something you want? You can count on me to help in every way I can. I would be a hard man indeed if I could look at a gathering like this and feel no pity.

Priest. Oedipus, ruler of my country, you can see for yourself the ages of those of us sitting at your altars: some not yet strong enough to fly far, some heavy with years — priests, I am the priest of Zeus — and these here, picked from the young men. The rest of the people sit wreathed in the market-place, at the two temples of Pallas, and beside the ash of Ismenus, where the fire gives its answers. Because the city, as you see yourself, is pitching in a heavy sea now, and can no longer lift her head up out of the trough of the bloody swell. She is dying in the fruit-buds of her soil; dying in the herds at pasture; dying in the labour of her women, who bring nothing to birth. And on top of it all the fire-bearing god has swooped down and is driving the city before him — the plague, the most hated thing there is. By him the house of Cadmus is emptied, and black Hades grows rich in groans and weeping.

It is not that we rank you with the gods, I and these children, that we are sitting at your hearth. It is that we judge you the first of men, both in the ordinary chances of life and in dealings with the powers above. It was you who came to the city of Cadmus and freed us from the tax we were paying to the cruel singer, the Sphinx — and you did it knowing no more than the rest of us, with nobody to teach you. A god was at your side, people say, and believe it; and it was you who set our lives straight. So now, Oedipus, mightiest of all in our eyes, every one of us here is begging you: find us some defence. Whether you have heard a voice from one of the gods or know something from a man — because I notice that with men of experience, even the outcome of their plans tends to come out alive. Come, best of mortals, set the city on its feet again. Come, take care: this land calls you its saviour now, for the good will you showed us before. Do not let us remember your reign as the time we were stood up straight and then fell down. Set the city upright, and make it safe. The luck you brought us then came under a good omen; be the same man now. If you are going to rule this land, as you do now, it is better to rule it with men in it than empty. A wall is nothing, and a ship is nothing, with no men living inside them together.

Oedipus. My poor children. I know what you have come wanting; it is no mystery to me. I know very well that you are all sick — and sick as you are, there is not one of you as sick as I am. Your pain comes to each of you singly, to himself and to nobody else; but my heart groans for the city, and for myself, and for you, all at once. So you are not waking a man who was asleep. Understand that I have wept, a great deal, and have gone down many roads in the wanderings of my thought. And the one cure I could find, looking hard, I have already acted on: I sent Creon, the son of Menoeceus, my wife's brother, to Apollo's house at Pytho, to learn what I must do or say to save this city. And now that I count the days against the time, it worries me what he is doing. He has been gone longer than is reasonable — longer than the journey takes. But when he comes, I would be a poor sort of man if I did not do everything the god makes plain.

Priest. You have timed that well. These men are signalling to me that Creon is coming up now.

Oedipus. Lord Apollo — let him come with some saving luck, as bright as his face is.

Priest. To guess from the look of him, it is good news. He would not be coming with his head wreathed so thick in bay, all in berry.

Oedipus. We will know in a moment; he is close enough to hear. My lord, my kinsman, son of Menoeceus — what word from the god have you brought us?

Creon. Good. I say that even hard things, if they happen to come out straight, can all turn out well.

Oedipus. But what is the word itself? What you have said so far leaves me neither bold nor frightened.

Creon. If you want to hear it with these people standing close, I am ready to speak. Or we can go inside.

Oedipus. Speak to all of them. I carry the grief for these people more than for my own life.

Creon. Then I will tell you what I heard from the god. Lord Apollo commands us, in plain terms, to drive out a pollution of this country — one that has been nursed in this soil — and not to go on feeding it until it cannot be healed.

Oedipus. Drive it out with what kind of cleansing? What sort of trouble is this?

Creon. By banishing a man, or by paying blood back with blood. It is that blood which is storming the city.

Oedipus. And what man's fate is he pointing to?

Creon. We had a leader once in this country, my lord — Laius — before you took the steering of the city.

Oedipus. I know it, from hearing. I never saw him.

Creon. He was killed. And now the god tells us clearly to punish his killers, whoever they are, by force.

Oedipus. And where on earth are they? Where is anyone going to find the track of an old crime, so hard to read?

Creon. In this country, he said. What is looked for can be caught. What is neglected gets away.

Oedipus. Was it at home, or in the fields, or on some other soil that Laius met this death?

Creon. He left the country, he told us, to consult the oracle. And once he had set out he never came home again.

Oedipus. And no messenger, no companion on the road, saw it — nobody whose account could have been learned from and used?

Creon. They died — all but one, who ran away in terror, and of what he saw he could say for certain only one thing.

Oedipus. What thing? One thing can lead to the discovery of many, if we could get hold of even a short beginning for hope.

Creon. He said robbers fell in with them and killed him — not one man's strength, but a crowd of hands.

Oedipus. Then how would a robber have come to that pitch of daring, unless something was being arranged from here, with silver?

Creon. That was thought at the time. But with Laius dead, nobody came forward to help, in the troubles we were in.

Oedipus. What trouble stood in the way? A throne had fallen. What stopped you finding out?

Creon. The Sphinx, with her shifting riddle-song. She made us drop what was out of sight and look at what was under our feet.

Oedipus. Then I will go back to the beginning and bring it to light again myself. Apollo has done right, and you have done right, to take up this care for the dead man. So you will see me too, and justly, standing with you as an ally, taking vengeance for this country and for the god together. Not for some far-off friend's sake — for my own sake I will scatter this pollution. Whoever killed that man might well want to take the same hand to me. So in helping him I am helping myself. Now quickly, children — up from the steps, and take your suppliants' branches with you. And let somebody gather the people of Cadmus here, since I mean to do everything there is to do. With the god's help we will be shown lucky — or fallen.

Priest. Children, let us stand up. What we came here for, this man has promised us of his own accord. And may Apollo, who sent these oracles, come himself — to save us, and to stop the sickness.

(They go. The Chorus, elders of Thebes, comes in.)

Chorus.
	Sweet-spoken word of Zeus,
	what are you, that have come from Pytho, rich in gold,
	to shining Thebes?
	I am stretched on the rack; my frightened heart is shaking.
	Healer of Delos, to whom we cry —
	I stand in awe of you. What will you bring to pass for me?
	Something new, or a debt come round again with the turning seasons?
	Tell me, deathless Voice, child of golden Hope.
Chorus.
	First I call on you, daughter of Zeus, deathless Athena,
	and on your sister, who holds the land,
	Artemis, seated on her famous throne at the circle of the market,
	and on Apollo, who shoots from far away —
	come, you three who turn death aside, show yourselves to me.
	If ever before, when ruin rose up over the city,
	you drove the flame of the plague out of our borders, come now too.
Chorus.
	Oh, the pain — the sorrows I carry have no number.
	The whole people is sick, and thought has no spear
	that anyone can fight it off with.
	Nothing grows from the famous soil,
	and the women do not come up out of the screaming labour of birth.
	One after another you can watch them go,
	like a bird on strong wings,
	faster than fire that nobody can fight,
	to the shore of the god of evening.
Chorus.
	And of those deaths the city dies without number.
	Her children lie on the ground, unpitied, carrying death,
	with no one to weep for them;
	and among them wives, and grey-haired mothers with them,
	at the altar's edge, from here and from there,
	cry out begging for an end to their bitter pains.
	The hymn to the Healer rings out, and with it the sound of groaning.
	For all this, golden daughter of Zeus,
	send us the bright face of your help.
Chorus.
	And the raging god — Ares — who now with no bronze shield
	burns me up, coming at me with a shout —
	I beg him to turn his back and run out of my country
	with a fair wind behind him,
	either into the great bedchamber of Amphitrite
	or into the Thracian surf where there is no harbour.
	Because whatever the night leaves undone
	the day comes and finishes.
	Him — father Zeus, you who hold the power
	of the fire-bearing lightning —
	destroy him under your thunderbolt.
Chorus.
	Lycian lord, Apollo — I would have your arrows too,
	from the gold-twisted string of your bow,
	dealt out unconquerable, set to guard us,
	and the fire-bearing torches of Artemis
	with which she races over the hills of Lycia.
	And I call on the god with the golden headband,
	who carries this land's name,
	wine-faced Bacchus, whom the Bacchants cry to,
	who goes with the Maenads —
	come near with your blazing pine-torch
	against the god who has no honour among the gods.

(Oedipus comes out of the palace.)

Oedipus. You pray. And what you pray for — if you are willing to hear what I say and take it in, and do the sickness's own work against it — you may get: help, and relief from your troubles. I say this as a stranger to the story, and a stranger to the deed. I could not have tracked it far on my own, without some clue to go on. But as it is — since I was made a citizen among citizens only after the event — I proclaim this to all of you, Cadmus' people:

Whoever among you knows at whose hand Laius the son of Labdacus died, I order him to tell me everything. And if he is afraid, let him take the charge out of his own way by laying it against himself. He will suffer nothing unpleasant beyond this: he will leave the country, unharmed. If instead someone knows the killer to be another man, from another land, let him not keep silent. I will pay the reward, and my thanks will go with it.

But if you keep silent — if any man, out of fear for a friend or for himself, pushes this order of mine aside — then hear what I will do about that. I forbid anyone in this land, where I hold the power and the throne, to take that man in, whoever he is, or to speak to him, or to let him share in prayers to the gods or in sacrifices, or to give him a share of the holy water. Drive him from your houses, all of you. He is our pollution — the Pythian oracle of the god has just shown me this. That is the kind of ally I am to the god and to the dead man.

And I curse the one who did it, whether he is one man who has kept hidden or one of several: may he wear out his miserable life, badly, as the bad man he is. And I pray one thing more. If he should turn out to be a member of my own household, at my own hearth, with my knowledge — may I suffer everything I have just called down on them. All this I charge you to carry out, for my sake, and for the god's, and for this country's, wasted as it is, fruitless and godless.

Oedipus. Even if the god had not pushed this matter on us, it was not right to leave it uncleansed as you did. The best of men had died, and your king. You should have hunted it down. As it is, I hold the power that he held before me; I hold his bed, and his wife, who had borne his children; and if his hope of a family had not failed him, we would have had children in common, born of one mother. Instead, fate came down on his head. For all these reasons I will fight for him as I would for my own father, and I will go to every length to catch the man whose hand did the murder — for the son of Labdacus, of Polydorus before him, of Cadmus before him, and of Agenor of old.

And on those who do not do this, I pray the gods send them no harvest from the earth and no children from their women, but let them die of the doom that is on us now, or of one worse. As for the rest of you, Cadmus' people, who are content with this — may Justice, our ally, and all the gods be with you for ever.

Chorus. Since you have bound me under your curse, my lord, I will speak on those terms. I did not kill him, and I cannot show you the man who did. As for the search — it was for Apollo, who sent the order, to tell us who did it.

Oedipus. That is fairly said. But no man alive can force the gods to do what they do not want to.

Chorus. Then I will say what seems to me the second-best thing after that.

Oedipus. If there is a third, do not leave that out either.

Chorus. Lord Teiresias — I know he sees the same things that Lord Apollo sees. From him, my king, a man searching into this could learn about it most clearly.

Oedipus. That is not something I have let sit idle either. On Creon's advice I have sent two messengers already. And I have been wondering for a while why he is not here.

Chorus. Apart from him, the other talk is faint and old.

Oedipus. What talk? I am going into every story.

Chorus. He was said to have been killed by some travellers.

Oedipus. I heard that too. But nobody sees the one who saw it.

Chorus. Well, if he has a scrap of fear in him, he will not stand still once he hears the curses you have laid.

Oedipus. A man who was not afraid of doing it will not be afraid of a word.

Chorus. But here is the one who will convict him. They are bringing the prophet of the god at last — the only man in whom the truth is born.

(Teiresias is led in by a boy.)

Oedipus. Teiresias, you who take everything in — what can be taught and what cannot be spoken, what is in heaven and what walks on the earth — you cannot see, but you understand all the same what sickness the city lives with. And against it, lord, we find you our one protector and saviour. Apollo — in case you have not already heard it from the messengers — sent back this answer to our question: the only release from this sickness would come if we found out for certain who killed Laius, and killed them, or sent them out of the land in exile. So do not grudge us anything — a voice from the birds, or any other road of prophecy you have. Save yourself, save the city, save me, save everything the dead man's pollution has touched. We are in your hands. And there is no finer work for a man than to help others with what he has and what he can do.

Teiresias. Ah. How terrible it is to understand, when understanding does the man who has it no good. I knew that well, and I let it slip — or I would not have come here.

Oedipus. What is it? You have come in with no heart at all.

Teiresias. Let me go home. You will bear what is yours most easily, and I what is mine, if you take my advice.

Oedipus. That is no lawful thing you are saying, and no loving one, towards the city that reared you — to refuse her this answer.

Teiresias. Because I see that your words are not going where they should either. So, in case the same thing happens to me —

Oedipus. In the gods' name, if you know something, do not turn away. All of us here are bowing to you, begging.

Teiresias. Because none of you understands. I will never bring out what is mine — I will not call it yours — my troubles.

Oedipus. What are you saying? You know, and you will not tell? You mean to betray us and destroy the city?

Teiresias. I will not hurt myself, and I will not hurt you. Why cross-examine me for nothing? You will not get it from me.

Oedipus. Nothing? You worst of the worst — you would put a stone into a rage — will you never speak out? Are you going to stand there, unmoved, and never come to the end of it?

Teiresias. You find fault with my temper. You have not looked at your own, living in the same house with you. You blame me instead.

Oedipus. Who would not lose his temper, hearing words like that — the way you are treating this city, with contempt?

Teiresias. It will come of itself, whether I cover it in silence or not.

Oedipus. Then what is going to come, you must tell me.

Teiresias. I will say nothing more. Rage at that, if you like, with the wildest anger you have in you.

Oedipus. I will, and I will leave nothing out, angry as I am, of what I have understood. Know this: it looks to me as if you helped to plant this deed, and did it — everything short of killing with your own hands. If you happened to have eyes, I would say the deed was yours alone.

Teiresias. Would you. Then I tell you: stand by the proclamation you made. From this day forward do not speak to these men, or to me. You are the unholy polluter of this land.

Oedipus. You have the gall to fling that out? And where do you think you are going to get away from it?

Teiresias. I have got away already. The truth I carry is strong.

Oedipus. Taught by whom? Not by your art, at any rate.

Teiresias. By you. You pushed me into saying it, against my will.

Oedipus. Saying what? Say it again, so I can learn it better.

Teiresias. Did you not understand it the first time? Or are you testing me, making me talk?

Oedipus. Not so well that I could call it known. Say it again.

Teiresias. I say you are the murderer of the man whose murderer you are looking for.

Oedipus. You will not be glad you said that twice — that piece of misery.

Teiresias. Shall I tell you something else, then, to make you angrier?

Oedipus. As much as you want. It will be said for nothing.

Teiresias. I say you have been living, without knowing it, in the most shameful closeness with those nearest to you — and you do not see how deep in evil you stand.

Oedipus. And you think you can go on saying things like that and enjoy it?

Teiresias. Yes — if there is any strength in the truth.

Oedipus. There is. But not for you. You have none of it, because you are blind — in your ears, in your mind, in your eyes.

Teiresias. And you are a wretched man, throwing those insults — which every man here will soon be throwing at you.

Oedipus. You are fed by one long night. You could never hurt me, or any man who sees the light.

Teiresias. No. It is not your fate to fall by me. Apollo is enough for that, and it is his business to bring it about.

Oedipus. Is this Creon's invention, or yours?

Teiresias. Creon is no harm to you. You are your own harm.

Oedipus. Wealth, and kingship, and the skill that outdoes every other skill in this life that everybody envies — how much jealousy you keep stored up against yourselves, if for the sake of this power, which the city put into my hands as a gift — I never asked for it — Creon the faithful, Creon my friend from the beginning, has crept up on me in secret and wants to throw me out, and has put up this scheming wizard, this tricky beggar-priest, who has sharp eyes for profit only and was born blind in his art.

Come, tell me: where have you ever shown yourself a true prophet? When the singing bitch was here, why did you say nothing to set these citizens free? And that was a riddle not for the first man who came along to read; it needed prophecy. And you were shown to have none — nothing from the birds, nothing learned from any god. I came along instead, Oedipus who knew nothing, and I stopped her. I hit the answer by my own wits, with nothing learned from birds. And this is the man you are trying to throw out, hoping to stand close beside Creon's throne. I think you will be sorry, you and the man who put this together, that you tried to purge the land. If you did not look like an old man, you would have learned by suffering what your ideas were worth.

Chorus. To our way of judging, Oedipus, his words were spoken in anger, and so were yours. And we do not need that. What we need is to think how best we can carry out the god's oracles.

Teiresias. You may be king. But the right to answer, at least, has to be held equal, word for word — I am master of that much. I do not live as your slave. I live as Apollo's. So I will not be put on the roll as Creon's client. And since you have thrown my blindness in my face, I tell you this: you have your eyes and you do not see the evil you stand in — not where you live, not whom you live with. Do you know where you come from? You have been, without knowing it, the enemy of your own people, under the earth and on it. And the curse of your mother and your father — a double lash, a terrible-footed thing — will one day drive you out of this land, seeing straight now, and after that seeing darkness. Where will there be a harbour for your cries? What corner of Cithaeron will not echo them, soon, when you understand the marriage you sailed into in that house — no harbour there — after such a fair voyage? And there is a crowd of other evils you have no sense of, that will bring you level with what you really are, and level with your own children. Now — spit on Creon, and on this mouth of mine. There is no mortal alive who will be ground down more miserably than you.

Oedipus. Am I to stand here and listen to that from him? Damn you — out, and quickly. Turn round and get away from this house, back where you came from.

Teiresias. I would not have come if you had not called me.

Oedipus. I did not know you would talk like a fool. Otherwise I would have taken my time about summoning you to my house.

Teiresias. That is what I am — a fool, as you think. But to the parents who bred you, I had sense.

Oedipus. What parents? Wait. Who among mortals bred me?

Teiresias. This day will breed you, and destroy you.

Oedipus. Everything you say is riddles and darkness.

Teiresias. And are you not the best man born at solving them?

Oedipus. Go on, throw that at me. It is where you will find I am great.

Teiresias. And yet that is the luck that has destroyed you.

Oedipus. If I saved this city, I do not care.

Teiresias. Then I am going. Boy — take me away.

Oedipus. Yes, let him take you. Standing here you are in the way; you are a nuisance. Once you are gone you cannot hurt me any more.

Teiresias. I am going — after I have said what I came to say. I am not afraid of your face. There is no way you can destroy me. I tell you this: the man you have been looking for all this time, with your threats and your proclamations about the murder of Laius — that man is here. A stranger, supposedly, living among us; but he will be found a Theban, born here, and he will take no joy in the discovery. Blind, who now sees; a beggar, who now is rich; he will go into a foreign land feeling the ground in front of him with a stick. And he will be found to be, to his own children, both brother and father; to the woman he came from, both son and husband; and to his father, the sharer of one wife, and the killer. Go inside and work that out. And if you catch me out in a lie, then say I know nothing about prophecy.

(The boy leads him away. Oedipus goes into the palace.)

Chorus.
	Who is he? The god-speaking rock of Delphi
	has spoken of a man who has done, with bloody hands,
	unspeakable things past all speaking.
	Time for him to run —
	to set his feet moving faster than storm-horses.
	Because the son of Zeus is leaping on him, armed
	with fire and lightning,
	and behind him come the terrible ones,
	the death-spirits that never miss.
Chorus.
	It has flashed out, just now, from snowy Parnassus:
	the voice that says track down the unknown man, everyone.
	He is wandering under the wild wood,
	through the caves and over the rocks,
	like a bull, wretched, on wretched feet, alone,
	trying to get clear of the oracles
	from the navel of the earth.
	And they are alive for ever, and they fly round him.
Chorus.
	Terrible — the clever reader of birds
	has stirred up terrible things in me,
	and I can neither believe him nor deny him. I do not know what to say.
	I hover on hopes, and see nothing clearly, here or behind me.
	What quarrel there was between the house of Labdacus
	and the son of Polybus, I never learned, then or now —
	nothing I could use as a test
	to go against the public name of Oedipus
	and take the side of Labdacus' house in unsolved deaths.
Chorus.
	Zeus and Apollo understand; they know what men do.
	But that a human prophet sees more than I do —
	there is no true way to judge that.
	One man may pass another in skill.
	But never — not until I saw the words come true —
	would I side with the men who blame him.
	Because the winged girl came at him once, in plain sight,
	and he was seen to be wise, and passed the test,
	and the city loved him.
	So in my heart he will never be found guilty of evil.

(Creon comes in.)

Creon. Men of this city — I have heard that Oedipus the king is making terrible charges against me, and I am here because I will not put up with it. If, in the trouble we are in now, he thinks he has suffered anything at my hands, in word or in action, that was meant to harm him, then I have no wish for a long life, carrying a name like that. The damage this story does me is not a small thing. It is the biggest there is — if I am to be called a bad man in the city, and a bad man by you and by my friends.

Chorus. But that reproach may well have been forced out of him by anger, not by any judgement of his mind.

Creon. And was it said out loud — that the prophet was put up to his lies by me, on my advice?

Chorus. That was said. What he meant by it, I do not know.

Creon. And when he laid this charge against me — were his eyes steady? Was his mind?

Chorus. I do not know. What the men in power do, I do not see. But here he comes now, out of the house himself.

(Oedipus comes out of the palace.)
