Oedipus. You. How did you get here? Have you really got the face for it — the nerve to come to my house, when you are plainly the murderer of the man who owns it, and the barefaced thief of my throne? Come on, tell me, in the gods' name: was it cowardice you saw in me, or stupidity, that made you plan this? Did you think I would not notice this work of yours creeping up on me in the dark, or that once I had seen it I would not fight it off? Is the attempt itself not stupid — to go hunting a throne without a following and without friends, when a throne is a thing you take with a following and with money?

Creon. Do you know what you should do? Hear as much back as you have said, and then judge for yourself, once you know.

Oedipus. You are a clever talker, and I am a poor learner from you. I have found you my enemy, and a heavy weight on me.

Creon. On that very point, hear first how I will put it.

Oedipus. On that very point, do not tell me that you are not a bad man.

Creon. If you think stubbornness with no sense in it is something worth having, you are not thinking straight.

Oedipus. If you think you can do a kinsman wrong and not answer for it in justice, you are not thinking well.

Creon. I agree — that is justly said. Now teach me what this injury is that you say you have suffered from me.

Oedipus. Did you or did you not persuade me that I ought to send someone for that holy prophet?

Creon. I did. And I am still of the same mind.

Oedipus. How long ago is it now, then, that Laius —

Creon. Did what? I do not follow you.

Oedipus. — vanished from sight, by a deadly stroke?

Creon. You would be measuring long years, and old ones.

Oedipus. And was this prophet in his trade back then?

Creon. As skilled as he is now, and honoured just the same.

Oedipus. Did he say anything about me, at that time?

Creon. Never — not while I was standing anywhere near.

Oedipus. But did you not hold an inquiry into the killer?

Creon. We did, of course we did. And we heard nothing.

Oedipus. Then how is it this clever man did not say all this then?

Creon. I do not know. Where I do not understand, I like to keep my mouth shut.

Oedipus. This much you do know, and could say — if you were in your right mind.

Creon. What? If I know it, I will not deny it.

Oedipus. That if he had not put his head together with yours, he would never have said this killing of Laius was mine.

Creon. If that is what he says, you know best. But I think it only fair that I learn from you what you have just learned from me.

Oedipus. Learn all you like. I will not be caught as a murderer.

Creon. Well then. You are married to my sister?

Oedipus. There is no denying what you ask.

Creon. And you rule this land as she does, sharing it equally?

Oedipus. Whatever she wants, she gets from me.

Creon. And am I not the third of you, equal to the two of you?

Oedipus. That is exactly where you show yourself a false friend.

Creon. Not if you reasoned with yourself the way I do. Consider this first: do you think anyone would choose to rule with fear at his back, rather than sleep untroubled — if he was going to have the same power either way? I was never born wanting to be king, rather than to do what a king does; nor was any other man who knows how to keep himself in hand. As it is, I have everything from you and nothing to fear. If I ruled myself, there would be a great deal I had to do against my will. So how could a throne be sweeter to me than power without pain, and influence? I am not yet so far deceived that I want anything beyond what is good and profitable too. As it is, everyone greets me; everyone welcomes me; everyone who wants something from you calls me out to talk, because their whole chance of getting it lies with me. How would I give up all this and take the other? A mind that thinks well would not turn bad. No — I am no lover of that idea, and I could never bear to act with anyone else who was.

And here is your proof. First, go to Delphi and ask whether I reported the oracle to you accurately. Then, if you catch me having plotted anything at all with the seer, take me and kill me — not by one vote but by two, yours and my own. But do not accuse me on your own, on a suspicion you cannot prove. It is not just to think bad men good at random, or good men bad. To throw away a good friend, I tell you, is the same as throwing away the life in your own body, the thing you love most. In time you will know all this for certain. Time alone shows a man to be just. A bad one you can know in a single day.

Chorus. He has spoken well, my lord, for a man who is careful not to fall. Those who are quick to judge are not safe.

Oedipus. When a man plotting in secret moves fast, I have to be fast in planning against him. If I sit quiet and wait, his business will be done and mine will have failed.

Creon. What do you want, then? To throw me out of the country?

Oedipus. Far from it. I want you dead, not exiled — so that you can show the world what envy comes to.

Creon. So you will not give way, and you will not believe me?

Creon. Because I can see you are not in your right mind.

Oedipus. In my own interest, I am.

Creon. You should be in mine just as much.

Oedipus. But you were born bad.

Creon. And if you understand nothing?

Oedipus. I must rule all the same.

Creon. Not if you rule badly.

Oedipus. City! Oh, my city!

Creon. I have a share in the city too. It is not yours alone.

Chorus. Stop, my lords. I see Jocasta coming out of the house, and in good time. With her help you must settle this quarrel that stands between you.

(Jocasta comes out.)

Jocasta. You poor fools, why have you started up this senseless brawl of tongues? Are you not ashamed — with the land as sick as this — to be stirring up troubles of your own? Go into the house, you; and you, Creon, go to yours. And stop making so much of a grief that is nothing.

Creon. Sister, your husband Oedipus thinks it right to do a terrible thing to me. He has picked one of two evils: either to drive me from my fathers' land, or to take me and kill me.

Oedipus. I do. I have caught him, wife, working evil against my person with an evil craft.

Creon. May I get no good of my life — may I die under a curse — if I have done any of the things you charge me with.

Jocasta. In the gods' name, Oedipus, believe him. Believe this oath before the gods most of all, and then believe me, and these men standing here before you.

Chorus.
	Give way. Be willing. Think, my lord. I beg you.
Oedipus.
	What is it you want me to give you?
Chorus.
	Respect a man who was never a fool before,
	and who now is bound by a great oath.
Oedipus.
	Do you know what you are asking?
Chorus.
	I know.
Oedipus.
	Then say what you mean.
Chorus.
	A friend who has put himself under a curse —
	never cast him out in dishonour
	on a charge from a story nobody can prove.
Oedipus.
	Then understand this. When you ask that,
	you are asking for my death, or my exile from this land.
Chorus.
	No — by the Sun, the first of all the gods.
	Godless, friendless, by the worst death there is,
	let me die, if that thought is in me.
	But the land is dying, and it wears my heart out,
	unlucky as I am —
	if now, on the old troubles,
	new troubles are to be piled from the two of you.
Oedipus.
	Then let him go. Even if it means I must die,
	or be driven out of this land by force, dishonoured.
	It is your mouth I pity, not his —
	your pleading. Him, wherever he goes, I will hate.
Creon.
	You are sullen in giving way, that much is plain,
	as you are heavy when your anger runs past the mark.
	Natures like that are hardest, and rightly,
	on themselves.
Oedipus.
	Will you not leave me alone and get out?
Creon.
	I am going.
	You have not known me. To these men I am what I was.

(Creon goes.)

Chorus.
	Lady, why do you wait to take this man inside?
Jocasta.
	When I have learned what happened.
Chorus.
	Blind suspicion came, out of talk.
	And even an unjust word bites.
Jocasta.
	From both of them?
Chorus.
	Yes.
Jocasta.
	And what was the story?
Chorus.
	Enough, for me — enough, with the land already in pain.
	Let it stay where it stopped.
Oedipus.
	Do you see where you have got to, good judge that you are,
	letting my cause go slack and blunting my heart?
Chorus.
	My lord, I have said it more than once.
	Know this: I would be shown a madman,
	lost to all sense, if I turned away from you —
	you who set my beloved country on a straight course
	when it was wandering in trouble,
	and who now, if you can, will be its good pilot again.
Jocasta.
	In the gods' name, my lord, tell me too:
	what is this thing that has set such anger in you?

Oedipus. I will, wife, for I honour you above these men. It is Creon, and the plot he has laid against me.

Jocasta. Tell me, then — if you can say clearly what the quarrel is that you charge him with.

Oedipus. He says I am the murderer of Laius.

Jocasta. On his own knowledge, or on hearsay from somebody else?

Oedipus. Oh, he sent in a villain of a prophet to say it for him. As for his own mouth, he keeps that entirely clean.

Jocasta. Then let go of the things you are talking about. Listen to me, and take this to heart: no human being has any share in the prophet's art. I will show you the proof, and briefly.

An oracle came to Laius once — I will not say from Apollo himself, but from his servants — that his fate would be to die at the hands of a child born to him and to me. And Laius, so the story goes, was killed one day by foreign robbers, at a place where three roads meet. As for the child, three days had not passed since its birth when Laius pinned its ankles together and had it thrown away, by other hands, on a mountain where no one goes.

So there Apollo did not bring it about that the child became his father's killer, or that Laius suffered the terrible thing he was afraid of and died at his child's hands. That is how the voices of prophecy had marked out the future. Pay them no attention. Whatever the god needs, he will easily bring to light himself.

Oedipus. What a wandering of the soul, wife, what an upheaval of the mind has taken hold of me, just now, hearing you.

Jocasta. What worry has turned you round, that you say this?

Oedipus. I thought I heard you say that Laius was cut down at a place where three roads meet.

Jocasta. That was what was said, and the story has never stopped.

Oedipus. And where is this place, where the thing happened?

Jocasta. The country is called Phocis. The road forks there, and one branch comes to that spot from Delphi and the other from Daulia.

Oedipus. And how much time has gone by since this happened?

Jocasta. It was announced to the city a little before you appeared and took power over this land.

Oedipus. Zeus. What have you decided to do with me?

Jocasta. What is it, Oedipus? Why does this weigh on your mind?

Oedipus. Do not ask me yet. Laius — tell me what he looked like, what build he had, and how far on he was in life.

Jocasta. He was tall. His hair was just beginning to go white. And in shape he was not so very unlike you.

Oedipus. Oh, this is wretched. I think I have just laid myself under terrible curses, without knowing it.

Jocasta. What do you mean? I am frightened to look at you, my lord.

Oedipus. I am terribly afraid the prophet can see. But you will show me better, if you tell me one thing more.

Jocasta. I am frightened too. But ask, and what I know I will tell you.

Oedipus. Was he travelling with a small party, or with a lot of armed men around him, the way a ruler does?

Jocasta. There were five of them in all, and one of them was a herald. And one carriage, carrying Laius.

Oedipus. Oh — now it is all coming clear. Who was it that told you this story, wife?

Jocasta. A servant of the house, the only one who came back alive.

Oedipus. And does he happen to be in the house now?

Jocasta. No. When he got back from there and saw you holding the power and Laius dead, he took my hand and begged me to send him out to the fields, to the pastures with the flocks, so that he could be as far out of sight of this city as possible. And I sent him. For a slave, he deserved that favour and a bigger one.

Oedipus. Can he be brought back to us quickly?

Jocasta. He can. But why do you want that?

Oedipus. I am afraid for myself, wife — afraid I have said far too much. That is why I want to see him.

Jocasta. Well, he will come. But I think I have a right, too, to learn what weighs so heavily on you, my lord.

Oedipus. You will not be kept from it, now that my fears have come this far. Who has a better claim than you to hear it, when I am walking through luck like this?

My father was Polybus of Corinth; my mother was Merope, a Dorian. I was counted the greatest man in that city until a thing happened to me — a thing worth wondering at, though not worth the weight I gave it. At a dinner a man who had drunk too much, in his cups, threw it at me that I was not my father's true son but a changeling. That day I held myself in, though it was hard. The next day I went to my mother and father and questioned them, and they were furious at the man who had let the insult fly. So on their side I was comforted; and still the thing kept scratching at me, because it had spread a long way.

So without telling my mother or my father I went to Delphi. And Apollo sent me away without an answer to what I had come for, but he said other things, and showed me miseries and terrors and griefs: that I was to sleep with my mother, and show the world a breed of children that men could not bear to look at, and be the killer of the father who made me. When I heard that, I fled the land of Corinth, and from then on I measured my distance from it by the stars, running for some place where I would never see the shame of those evil oracles come true. And on my way I came to this very country, where you say this king of yours was killed.

Oedipus. And I will tell you the truth, wife. When I was on the road and close to this place where the three ways meet, there a herald came towards me, and a man riding in a carriage drawn by young horses, just as you describe. And the man in front, and the old man himself, tried to force me off the road. I was angry, and I struck the one who was shoving me aside, the driver. And when the old man saw that, he watched for the moment I was passing the carriage, and brought his double goad down on the middle of my head.

He did not pay an equal price. He paid more. In one stroke from the staff in this hand he went over backwards and rolled straight out of the middle of the carriage. And I killed them. Every one of them.

But if this stranger had any tie of blood with Laius, then who is more wretched than the man in front of you? What man could the gods hate more? No stranger and no citizen may take him into their house; no one may speak to him; everyone must drive him from their doors. And it was nobody else who laid these curses on me — I did it, myself. And I defile the dead man's bed with the very hands that killed him. Was I born bad? Am I not wholly unclean? — if I must go into exile, and in exile never see my own people or set foot in my own country, or else be yoked in marriage to my mother and kill my father Polybus, who fathered me and brought me up. If a man judged that all this came from some savage power, would he not be right about Oedipus?

No. No — by the holy majesty of the gods, let me never see that day. Let me go out of the sight of men, vanished, before I see a stain like that come down on me.

Chorus. To us, my lord, this is frightening. But until you have learned it all from the man who was there, keep hoping.

Oedipus. That is exactly how much hope I have left: to wait for the man, the herdsman, and nothing else.

Jocasta. And when he has appeared, what is it you want from him?

Oedipus. I will tell you. If he is found to say the same as you, then I have escaped this disaster.

Jocasta. And what did you hear from me that matters so much?

Oedipus. You said he told of robbers — that it was men, plural, who killed him. If he still says the same number, then I did not kill him: one man cannot be the same as many. But if he speaks of one man travelling alone, then the deed is plain, and it tips onto me.

Jocasta. Well, be sure that is how the story came out, and he cannot take it back again. The city heard it, not I alone. And even if he does turn aside from what he said before, he can never, my lord, show that the killing of Laius squares with the prophecy — for Apollo said outright that he was to die at the hand of a child of mine. And that poor creature never killed him; it died first itself. So as far as prophecy goes, from now on I would not look this way for it or that.

Oedipus. You are right. But all the same, send somebody to fetch the farm hand. Do not let it drop.

Jocasta. I will send at once. But let us go into the house. I will do nothing that is not what you want.

(Oedipus and Jocasta go inside.)

Chorus.
	May it be my portion to carry
	a holy purity in every word and act,
	under the laws that are set on high,
	walking the heights, born through the bright air of heaven,
	whose only father is Olympus.
	No mortal nature of men gave them birth,
	and forgetting will never lay them asleep.
	A great god is in them,
	and he does not grow old.
Chorus.
	Outrage breeds the tyrant.
	Outrage, once it has gorged itself for nothing
	on all that is out of season and does no good,
	climbs to the topmost crag
	and plunges down a sheer drop into necessity,
	where no foot can be of any use.
	But the wrestling that is good for the city —
	I pray the god never to end it.
	The god is my protector, and I will not let him go.
Chorus.
	If a man walks in arrogance, in what he does or says,
	with no fear of Justice, no reverence for the seats of the gods,
	let an evil portion take him
	for his ill-starred pride of living —
	if he will not win his gain justly
	and keep his hands from what is unholy,
	but reaches out, in his folly, for what must not be touched.
	If that goes on, what man will ever again
	pray to keep the gods' arrows off his soul?
	If deeds like these are honoured,
	what is my dancing for?
Chorus.
	I will go no more in reverence to the untouched navel of the earth,
	nor to the temple at Abae, nor to Olympia,
	unless these things are made to fit together
	so that every mortal can point to them.
	But you who rule — if it is right to call you that —
	Zeus, lord of all: let this not slip past you,
	or past your deathless, everlasting power.
	The old prophecies about Laius are fading;
	already men are setting them aside,
	and nowhere is Apollo plain in his honours.
	The things of the gods are going.

(Jocasta comes out, carrying a wreathed branch and incense.)

Jocasta. Lords of this land, it has come into my mind to visit the temples of the gods, with these garlands in my hands and this incense. Oedipus is letting his spirit run too high, on every kind of grief. He does not judge the new by the old, the way a man in his senses would; he belongs to whoever is talking, if the talk is of terrors. So, since I do no good by advising him, I have come to you, Apollo — Lycean Apollo, whose altar is nearest — as a suppliant with these offerings of prayer, that you may find us some clean way out of this. For now we are all afraid, seeing him struck out of his wits, as if he were the helmsman of our ship.

(A Messenger enters, an old man.)

Messenger. Could you tell me, strangers, where the house of King Oedipus is? Or better still, tell me where he is himself, if you know.

Chorus. This is the house, stranger, and he is inside. And this lady is his wife, the mother of his children.

Messenger. Then may she be blessed, and always among the blessed, since she is his wife in every sense.

Jocasta. And the same to you, stranger. You deserve it, for your kind words. But tell me what you have come wanting, and what you wish to say.

Messenger. Good news, lady, for your house and for your husband.

Jocasta. What good news? And who has sent you?

Messenger. I come from Corinth. And the word I am about to say — you will be glad of it, how could you not be? — but perhaps you will grieve too.

Jocasta. What is it? What is this double power it has?

Messenger. The people of the Isthmus are going to set him up as their king. That is what was being said there.

Jocasta. What? Is old Polybus no longer in power?

Messenger. No. Death holds him in his tomb.

Jocasta. What did you say? Polybus is dead, old man?

Messenger. If I am not telling the truth, I deserve to die.

Jocasta. You, girl — go in and tell your master this, quickly.

(A servant goes in.)

Oracles of the gods, where are you now? This is the man Oedipus was afraid of all these years; this is the man he fled, so as not to kill him. And now he is dead — by chance, and not by Oedipus.

(Oedipus comes out.)

Oedipus. Jocasta, dearest wife, why have you sent for me out here from the house?

Jocasta. Listen to this man. And as you listen, think where the god's solemn oracles have got to.

Oedipus. Who is he, and what has he got to tell me?

Jocasta. He is from Corinth, with news of your father Polybus: that he is no more. He is dead.

Oedipus. What are you saying, stranger? Tell it to me yourself.

Messenger. If that is what I must make plain first — then know it for certain: he has gone down to his death.

Oedipus. By treachery, or by a visit of sickness?

Messenger. A small tilt of the scale puts an old body to sleep.

Oedipus. Sickness, then. Poor man — that is what finished him, it seems.

Messenger. That, and the long years he had lived, measured out in full.

Oedipus. Ah. Ah, wife — why would anyone look to the prophet's hearth at Delphi now, or to the birds screaming over our heads, who guided me to this: that I was going to kill my father? He is dead. He is hidden under the earth, and here I am, and I never laid a hand on a spear — unless he died of longing for me. That way, yes, he would have died because of me. But as it stands, Polybus has gathered up the oracles we had and taken them with him, and lies in Hades. They are worth nothing.

Jocasta. Did I not tell you this long ago?

Oedipus. You did. But I was led astray by my fear.

Jocasta. Then from now on, do not take any of it to heart.

Oedipus. And how am I not to fear my mother's bed?

Jocasta. What should a man be afraid of, when chance rules his life and there is no clear foresight of anything? Best to live at random, however you can. And as for marrying your mother — do not be afraid of that. Many men before now have slept with their mothers, in their dreams. The man who counts such things as nothing carries his life most easily.

Oedipus. All that would be well said, if the woman who bore me were not alive. As it is, since she is alive, I have to be afraid, however well you argue.

Jocasta. And yet your father's grave is a great light to see by.

Oedipus. Great — I know it. But the fear is of the woman who is living.

Messenger. And who is this woman you are so afraid about?

Oedipus. Merope, old man. The woman Polybus lived with.

Messenger. And what is there about her that frightens you?

Oedipus. A terrible oracle, stranger, sent by the gods.

Messenger. Can it be spoken? Or is it not lawful for another man to know?

Oedipus. It can. Apollo said once that I must sleep with my own mother, and take my father's blood with my own hands. That is why I have kept Corinth far away from me all these years. Happily enough — and yet there is nothing sweeter than to look into the eyes of your parents.

Messenger. And was it really for fear of this that you kept away from the city?

Oedipus. And because I did not want to be my father's killer, old man.

Messenger. Then why, my lord, since I came here meaning well, have I not set you free of this fear?

Oedipus. Do that, and you would have a reward from me worth having.

Messenger. And that is mainly why I came — so that when you came home, I would do well out of it.

Oedipus. But I will never go where my parents are.

Messenger. My boy, it is plain as day you do not know what you are doing.

Oedipus. How, old man? In the gods' name, teach me.

Messenger. If it is because of all this that you will not go home.

Oedipus. I am afraid that Apollo will come out true for me.

Messenger. Afraid of taking some pollution from your parents?

Oedipus. That very thing, old man. That is what frightens me, always.

Messenger. Then do you know that you are trembling at nothing, and rightly nothing?

Oedipus. How can it be nothing, if I was born the child of those two?

Messenger. Because Polybus was nothing to you by blood.

Oedipus. What did you say? Polybus did not father me?

Messenger. No more than the man talking to you now. Exactly as much.

Oedipus. And how can the man who fathered me count for nothing?

Messenger. Because he did not father you. No more than I did.

Oedipus. Then why did he call me his son?

Messenger. You should know this: he had you as a gift. Out of my hands.

Oedipus. And he loved me that much — a child from another man's hands?

Messenger. He had no children before. That is what won him over.

Oedipus. And you — had you bought me, or found me, when you gave me to him?

Messenger. Found you. In the wooded folds of Cithaeron.

Oedipus. What were you doing, travelling in that country?

Messenger. I was in charge of the mountain flocks there.

Oedipus. A shepherd. A hired hand, wandering about for wages?

Messenger. And the man who saved your life, child, at the time.

Oedipus. Saved me from what? What was wrong with me when you took me in your arms?

Messenger. The joints of your ankles could tell you that.

Oedipus. Oh — why do you bring that up? That old injury?

Messenger. I set you free. Your feet had been pinned through at the ankles.

Oedipus. A terrible mark of shame to carry out of the cradle.

Messenger. So terrible that it gave you your name — Oedipus, the swollen foot. That piece of luck is what you are called after.

Oedipus. In the gods' name — who did it? My mother or my father? Tell me.

Messenger. I do not know. The man who gave you to me knows more about that than I do.

Oedipus. You got me from somebody else? You did not find me yourself?

Messenger. No. Another shepherd handed you over to me.

Oedipus. Who? Can you say who he was?

Messenger. He was called one of Laius's men, I think.

Oedipus. Laius — the king who ruled this country, years ago?

Messenger. That is the one. The man was a herdsman of his.

Oedipus. Is he still alive? Could I see him?

Messenger. You people who live here would know that best.

Oedipus. Is there anyone here — any of you standing near me — who knows the herdsman he is talking about? Who has seen him, out in the fields or here in the town? Speak up. The moment has come for this to be found out.

Chorus. I think it is nobody but the man from the fields, the one you were already asking to see. But Jocasta here could tell you that as well as anyone.

Oedipus. Wife — you know the man we sent for just now? Is that who he means?

Jocasta. What does it matter who he means? Pay no attention. What has been said — do not waste a thought on it. It is nothing.

Oedipus. Impossible. I have clues like these in my hands, and I am not going to bring my birth into the open?

Jocasta. In the gods' name, if you care at all for your own life, stop looking. I am sick enough already.

Oedipus. Take heart. Even if I turn out to be a slave, born of a slave, and her mother a slave before her — three generations of it — you are not shown to be low-born.

Jocasta. Even so, listen to me. I am begging you. Do not do this.

Oedipus. I will not listen. Not until I know the whole thing for certain.

Jocasta. I know what I am saying. I am telling you what is best for you.

Oedipus. Then this "best" of yours has been torturing me for a long time.

Jocasta. Oh, you unlucky man. I hope you never find out who you are.

Oedipus. Somebody go and bring the herdsman here to me. And let her go on enjoying her rich family.

Jocasta. Oh — oh, you poor wretched man. That is all I can call you. That, and nothing else, ever again.

(She goes into the palace.)

Chorus. Why has your wife gone, Oedipus, rushing off like that, in that wild grief? I am afraid something terrible is going to break out of that silence.

Oedipus. Let it break out. Whatever it likes. My own seed — I want to see what it is, however small it turns out to be. She, perhaps — she has a woman's big pride — is ashamed of my low birth. But I count myself the child of Fortune, the Fortune who gives good things, and I will not be dishonoured. She is the mother I was born from. And the months, my brothers, have marked me out small and then great. That is what I am, and I could never come out of it a different man. So I will find out where I come from.

Chorus.
	If I am any prophet, if I know anything at all,
	then, Cithaeron — by Olympus —
	you will not get through tomorrow's full moon
	without our praising you as Oedipus's countryman,
	his nurse and his mother,
	and dancing for you,
	because you did our king a kindness.
	Apollo, god we cry to — let this please you.
Chorus.
	Who was it, child, who was it bore you?
	One of the long-lived nymphs, lying with Pan,
	the mountain-walker, your father?
	Or some bride of Apollo? —
	every upland pasture is dear to him.
	Or was it the lord of Cyllene, Hermes,
	or the god of the Bacchants who lives on the high peaks,
	who took you as a gift, a find,
	from one of the nymphs of Helicon
	he plays with most?

Oedipus. Elders — if I may make a guess about a man I have never dealt with, I think I see the herdsman we have been looking for all this time. His long old age is a match for this stranger's years; and besides, I recognise the men bringing him as servants of my own. But you are probably ahead of me in knowledge, if you have seen the herdsman before.

Chorus. I know him, be sure of it. He was Laius's man — as trusty a shepherd as any.

(The old herdsman is brought in.)

Oedipus. I ask you first, Corinthian stranger — is this the man you mean?

Messenger. That is him. The one you are looking at.

Oedipus. You. Old man. Look at me and answer what I ask you. Were you Laius's man, once?

Servant. I was. A slave — not bought, brought up in the house.

Oedipus. Doing what work? What kind of life?

Servant. Most of my life I went with the flocks.

Oedipus. Where, mostly? What country did you keep them in?

Servant. Cithaeron, some of the time. And the ground next to it.

Oedipus. This man — do you know him? Did you ever meet him up there?

Servant. Doing what? Which man do you mean?

Oedipus. This one, here. Have you ever had anything to do with him?

Servant. Not that I could say straight off, from memory.

Messenger. No wonder, master. But I will remind him clearly of what he has forgotten. I know very well he remembers when we were up in the Cithaeron country, he with two flocks and I with one, and I was alongside this man three whole seasons, six months each, from spring until Arcturus rose. Then, for the winter, I used to drive mine down to my own folds, and he took his to Laius's pens. Is any of this true, or is it not? Did it happen as I say?

Servant. It is true. Though it was a long time ago.

Messenger. Come on, then. Tell me. Do you remember giving me a child, back then, to bring up as my own?

Servant. What is this? Why are you asking that?

Messenger. This is him, my friend. The one who was a baby then.

Servant. Damn you. Will you shut your mouth?

Oedipus. Do not scold him, old man. Your words need a scolding a great deal more than his.

Servant. And how have I done wrong, best of masters?

Oedipus. By not telling about the child he is asking after.

Servant. He is talking without knowing anything. He is wasting his effort.

Oedipus. You will not talk to oblige me. You will talk in pain.

Servant. No — in the gods' name, do not hurt an old man.

Oedipus. Somebody twist his arms behind him. Quickly.

Servant. Why? What have I done? What do you want to know?

Oedipus. The child he is asking about. Did you give it to him?

Servant. I did. I wish I had died that day.

Oedipus. You will come to that, if you do not tell me the truth.

Servant. If I tell it, I am destroyed far worse.

Oedipus. This man, it seems, is going to play for time.

Servant. No, I am not. I said just now that I gave it to him.

Oedipus. Where did you get it? Your own house, or somebody else's?

Servant. Not mine. I took it from somebody.

Oedipus. Which of these citizens? From what house?

Servant. In the gods' name, master, no — do not ask any more.

Oedipus. You are dead if I have to ask you again.

Servant. Well then. It was one of the children of Laius's house.

Oedipus. A slave's? Or one of his own blood?

Servant. Oh — I am right at the terrible thing now. The saying of it.

Oedipus. And I at the hearing of it. But it has to be heard.

Servant. It was called his own child. But your wife inside could tell you best how it stands.

Oedipus. She gave it to you?

Servant. Yes, my king.

Oedipus. For what?

Servant. So that I would get rid of it.

Oedipus. Her own child? The wretched woman.

Servant. She was afraid of the prophecies. Bad ones.

Oedipus. What prophecies?

Servant. That it would kill its parents. That was the story.

Oedipus. Then why did you let it go to this old man?

Servant. Out of pity, master. I thought he would carry it away to another country, the one he came from. And he saved it — for the worst of all. Because if you are the man he says you are, know that you were born to a terrible fate.

Oedipus. Oh. Oh. It has all come out. All of it, clear. Light — let this be the last time I look at you. I have been shown for what I am: born from those I should never have been born from, living with those I should never have lived with, and the killer of those I should never have killed.

(He goes into the palace.)

Chorus.
	Oh, generations of men,
	I count your lives as equal to nothing.
	Where is the man, where,
	who carries off more of happiness
	than just enough to seem to have it,
	and having seemed, to fall away?
	With your example before me,
	your fate, yours — Oedipus, you poor wretch —
	I call no mortal blessed.
Chorus.
	He drew his bow past everyone
	and won a fortune blessed in every way —
	O Zeus — he brought down
	the girl with the crooked claws,
	the singer of riddles, and rose up
	for my country as a tower against death.
	From that day you were called my king,
	and honoured above all men,
	ruling in great Thebes.
Chorus.
	And now — who is more wretched to hear of?
	Who lives closer to savage ruin, to pain,
	in the turning-over of a life?
	Oh, famous Oedipus,
	one harbour was enough —
	the same harbour, for you
	to put in at as son and as husband.
	How, how could the furrows your father ploughed
	bear you in silence, poor man, for so long?
Chorus.
	Time, who sees everything, has found you out
	against your will. He passes sentence
	on the marriage that was no marriage,
	where the one who begot was the one begotten.
	Oh, son of Laius,
	I wish, I wish I had never seen you.
	I mourn like a man pouring out a funeral song
	from his lips. To tell the plain truth:
	you gave me breath again,
	and you have closed my eyes in the dark.

(A second messenger comes out of the palace.)

Messenger. You who have always been the most honoured men in this land — what things you are going to hear, what things you will see, what grief you will carry, if you still care, as kin should, for the house of Labdacus. I do not think the Ister or the Phasis, the great rivers, could wash this house clean, so much is hidden in it — and more is about to come into the light, evils done on purpose, not by accident. And of all hurts, the ones that hurt most are the ones we are shown to have chosen ourselves.

Chorus. What we knew before was already more than heavy enough to groan under. What do you have to add to it?

Messenger. The quickest thing to say, and to take in: Jocasta is dead. Our queen.

Chorus. Poor, poor woman. What caused it?

Messenger. Her own hand. The worst of what happened you are spared, because you did not see it. Still, as far as my memory holds it, you will hear what that wretched woman suffered.

She came in through the porch in a frenzy and went straight for her marriage bed, tearing at her hair with the fingers of both hands. Once inside, she slammed the doors shut behind her and called on Laius, dead so long ago — remembering the child got years back, by whose hand he died himself, leaving the mother to breed with his own son a cursed brood. And she wept over the bed where, twice wretched, she had borne a husband from a husband and children from her child.

How she died after that I do not know. Oedipus burst in, shouting, and because of him we could not watch her misery through to the end. Our eyes were on him as he ran round the room. He went from one of us to the next demanding a sword, and asking where he could find the wife who was no wife — the mother's field, sown twice over, where he and his children had both been planted. And as he raved, some god pointed the way for him. It was none of us men who were standing near. With a terrible cry, as though somebody were leading him, he threw himself at the double doors, bent the bolts out of their sockets, and fell into the room.

And there we saw the woman hanging, her neck in a twisted noose of swinging cords. When he saw her he gave a terrible deep roar, poor man, and loosed the rope she hung from. And when she was lying on the ground, poor wretch, what came next was terrible to see. He tore the golden brooches from her dress — the pins she had fastened her clothes with — lifted them, and struck them into his own eyes, into the sockets, saying something like this: that they would not see him any more, not what he was suffering and not what he was doing; that from now on, in the dark, they would look at the people they should never have seen, and fail to know the people he had wanted to know.

Chanting that, over and over, not once, he raised his hand and struck his eyes again and again. And with every blow the bleeding eyeballs wet his beard — not oozing slow drops of gore, but all at once a black rain of blood, with hail in it, came pouring down.

This has broken out from two people, not one: evils mingled together for husband and wife. Their old happiness, before, was a happiness they had a right to. Now, on this one day: groaning, ruin, death, shame — every name there is for evil, and not one of them missing.

Chorus. And now? Is there any rest from the pain for the wretched man?

Messenger. He is shouting for someone to open the doors and show all Thebes the father-killer, the mother's — I cannot say the word; it is unholy — so that he can throw himself out of the land and not stay in the house under the curse he called down himself. But he needs strength, and someone to guide him. The sickness is more than a man can carry. He will show you himself. Look — the bolts of the doors are being drawn. You are about to see a sight that even a man who hated him would pity.

(Oedipus comes out, blind. The blood is still on his face.)

Chorus.
	Oh, terrible thing for people to see,
	most terrible of everything
	I have ever come upon. Poor man,
	what madness came over you? What god
	took a leap longer than the longest
	and landed on your unlucky life?
	Oh, oh, you wretched man —
	I cannot even look at you. There is so much
	I want to ask, so much to learn,
	so much to look at,
	and you make me shudder so.
Oedipus.
	Aiai, aiai, I am wretched.
	Where am I being carried, poor man? Where
	is my voice flying off to, on the wind?
	Oh, my fate — how far you leapt.
Chorus.
	Into something terrible. Not to be heard. Not to be looked at.
Oedipus.
	Oh, cloud of darkness,
	my own, come over me, unspeakable, driven on
	by a wind that cannot be turned aside —
	oh, no —
	oh, no, again — how they go into me together,
	the stab of these pins and the memory of what I did.
Chorus.
	No wonder. In pain like this
	you mourn twice and carry the evil twice.
Oedipus.
	Oh, my friend —
	you are still here, still my attendant, still standing by,
	still putting up with the blind man, and caring for him.
	Oh, oh.
	You cannot hide from me. I know you plainly,
	dark as I am — I know your voice.
Chorus.
	You have done a terrible thing. How could you bear
	to put out your own eyes? Which of the gods drove you?
Oedipus.
	It was Apollo, friends, Apollo
	who brought it to pass, this evil, evil suffering of mine.
	But no hand struck them except my own — mine, wretched as I am.
	Why should I have eyes,
	when there was nothing sweet left for them to see?
Chorus.
	It was just as you say.
Oedipus.
	Then what is there for me to see,
	or to love, or what greeting
	could still touch my ear with any pleasure, friends?
	Take me away, out of this country, as fast as you can,
	take me away, friends — the great destruction,
	the man most cursed, and more than that
	the man the gods hate most.
Chorus.
	Wretched in your mind and in your fortune, both alike —
	I wish I had never known you.
Oedipus.
	Death to him, whoever he was,
	who took the cruel pin out of my feet on the mountain
	and rescued me from death and saved me. He did me no kindness.
	If I had died then
	I would not be this grief to those who love me, or to myself.
Chorus.
	I would have wanted that too.
Oedipus.
	I would not have come here my father's killer,
	or been called by men the bridegroom
	of the woman I was born from.
	Now I am godless, the child of unholy parents,
	sharing the bed of the man who begot me, poor wretch.
	If there is an evil older than evil,
	Oedipus has drawn it.

Chorus. I cannot say you planned this well. You would have been better off dead than alive and blind.

Oedipus. Do not lecture me that this was not the best way to do it. Do not give me any more advice. If I had eyes, I do not know how I could have looked at my father with them when I went down to Hades, or at my poor mother — I have done things to those two that no hanging could pay for. Or the sight of my children — was that something I would have wanted to look at, born the way they were born? Never. Not with these eyes. Nor the city, nor its walls, nor the holy statues of the gods — all of which I, the most wretched of men, I who was brought up in Thebes the finest man in it, took from myself when I gave the order that everyone must drive out the impious man, the one the gods have shown to be unclean, and of the family of Laius. Having exposed a stain like that on myself, was I to look at these people with steady eyes? No. And if there were any way to dam up the spring of hearing through my ears, I would not have held back from shutting this wretched body up altogether, so that it was blind and heard nothing. It is sweet for the mind to live outside its troubles.

Cithaeron, why did you take me in? Why did you not kill me the moment you had me, so that I would never have shown men where I came from? Polybus, Corinth, and the old house I called my father's — what a beautiful thing you brought up in me, with evil festering under the skin. Now I am found evil, and born of evil. You three roads, and the hidden glen, the oak wood and the narrow place where the three ways met, that drank my father's blood from my own hands — do you still remember me? What I did in front of you, and what I went on to do after, when I came here? Marriage — marriage, you gave me life, and having given it you sent the same seed up again, and showed the world fathers, brothers, sons, one blood; brides, wives, mothers; every foulest thing that is done among human beings. But it is not right to speak of what it was not right to do. Quickly, in the gods' name, hide me somewhere outside the land, or kill me, or throw me into the sea, where you will never have to look at me again. Come — do not think it beneath you to touch a wretched man. Do as I ask, do not be afraid. No man can carry my evils except me.

Chorus. Here is Creon, at the right moment for what you ask — to act, and to decide. He is left the only guardian of the country in your place.

Oedipus. Oh — what am I going to say to him? What claim have I on his trust that would be just? Everything I did to him before has been shown to be wrong.

(Creon comes in.)

Creon. I have not come to laugh at you, Oedipus, or to throw any of the old wrongs in your face. But you men — if you have no shame left before the children of men, at least respect the flame of the lord Sun, who feeds everything, and do not show this pollution uncovered like this, a thing that neither the earth nor the holy rain nor the light will accept. Take him into the house as fast as you can. It is right that only family should see and hear a family's troubles.

Oedipus. In the gods' name — since you have torn away what I expected, and come, the best of men, to me, the worst — do one thing for me. I say it for your sake, not mine.

Creon. What is it you want from me so badly?

Oedipus. Throw me out of this land, as fast as you can, to a place where no human being will ever speak to me.

Creon. I would have done it, be sure of that, if I had not wanted first of all to learn from the god what should be done.

Oedipus. But his word came out in full: destroy the father-killer, the unholy one — me.

Creon. That is what was said. All the same, where we now stand, it is better to find out clearly what to do.

Oedipus. You will ask the god? For a wretch like me?

Creon. Yes. Even you will put your trust in the god now.

Oedipus. Yes. And I lay this charge on you. I beg it of you. The woman inside — bury her as you think right. You will do it properly; she is your own. But me — never let this city, my father's, be condemned to have me living in it. Let me live in the mountains, on the Cithaeron that is called mine, that my mother and father while they lived set aside as my proper tomb, so that I die by the will of the two who tried to kill me. And yet I know this much: no sickness will destroy me, and nothing else will either. I would never have been saved from dying, except for some terrible evil. Well, let my fate go where it will.

My children, Creon. Do not trouble yourself over the boys. They are men; wherever they are, they will never lack a living. But my two girls, poor pitiable things — my table was never set apart from them, never without me; whatever I touched, they always had a share in it — take care of those two for me. And above all let me touch them with my hands and weep over what has happened. Do it, my lord. Do it, you noble-born man. If I touched them with my hands I would think I had them, as I did when I could see.

What is this? In the gods' name — is that not my two dear ones I hear, crying? Has Creon pitied me and sent my children, the dearest of all my children? Am I right?

Creon. You are. I arranged it. I knew the joy you had in them — the joy you have had all along.

Oedipus. Then may good fortune come to you, and for this road may some god guard you better than he guarded me. Children, where are you? Come here. Come to these hands, your brother's hands — hands that did this to the eyes of the father who begot you, eyes that were bright once. He saw nothing, girls, and knew nothing, and became your father in the woman he was sown in himself. I weep for you both as well — I have no strength to look at you — when I think of the rest of the bitter life you will have to live among men. What gatherings of the citizens will you go to, what festivals, that you will not come home from in tears, instead of having watched the show? And when you reach the age for marriage — who will he be, girls, who will take the risk, taking on the disgrace that will wound my children and yours alike? What evil is missing? Your father killed his father. He ploughed the mother he was sown in, and got you from the same place he was born from. That is what they will throw at you. And then who will marry you? Nobody. There is nobody, children. You are going to wither, barren and unmarried. That is plain.

Son of Menoeceus — you are the only father left to them; the two of us who made them are gone, both of us. Do not look on while they go wandering, beggars, unmarried, without a home — they are your own blood — and do not put them on a level with my evils. Pity them. Look at them, so young, stripped of everything except what you can give. Nod to me, noble man. Give me your hand on it.

And you, children — if you were old enough to understand, I would have a great deal to tell you. As it is, pray this for me: to live wherever the moment allows, and to have a better life than the father who begot you.

Creon. Enough. You have wept enough. Go inside the house.

Oedipus. I must obey. Though nothing about it is sweet.

Creon. Everything is good in its season.

Oedipus. Do you know on what terms I go?

Creon. You will tell me, and then I will know.

Oedipus. That you send me out of this land, to live away from it.

Creon. What you ask is the god's to give.

Oedipus. But I have become the thing the gods hate most.

Creon. Then you will get it soon enough.

Oedipus. You agree?

Creon. I do not like to say things I do not mean. It is empty talk.

Oedipus. Then take me away from here. Now.

Creon. Come on, then. Let go of the children.

Oedipus. No — do not take these from me.

Creon. Do not try to be master of everything. What you did master has not stayed with you through your life.

(Oedipus is led inside.)

Chorus. People of Thebes, our own country, look: this is Oedipus, who knew the famous riddle and was the most powerful of men. Which citizen did not look at his fortune with envy? See what a sea of terrible trouble he has come into. So, while a mortal is still waiting to see that final day, count no one happy until he has crossed the boundary of his life without suffering pain.

The Women of Trachis (date unknown)

The Women of Trachis

(Trachis, in front of the house where Deianeira and her children live as guests. Deianeira comes out alone.)

Deianeira. There is an old saying among people: you cannot know whether a person's life has been good or bad until they are dead. But I know mine, before I ever go down to Hades. It is unlucky, and it is heavy.

I was still living in my father Oeneus's house at Pleuron when I came to dread marriage worse than any girl in Aetolia. Because my suitor was a river — Achelous, I mean — and he kept coming to my father to ask for me in three shapes. Sometimes he came as a bull, plain to see; sometimes as a snake, glittering and coiled; sometimes with the body of a man and the face of an ox, with springs of fresh water spraying out of his thick, shadowy beard. With a suitor like that waiting for me, I was wretched, and I prayed all the time to die before I ever came near that bed.

Then, late, but to my joy, the famous son of Zeus and Alcmena came, and went into a fight with him for me, and set me free. How the fight went I could not tell you. I do not know. Anyone who sat and watched it without shaking could describe it; I sat there stunned with fear that my beauty was going to find me pain in the end. But Zeus, who presides over contests, made it come out well — if it was well. Because since I was joined to Heracles as his chosen bride, I have been feeding one fear after another, always worrying over him. One night brings a trouble in, and the next night takes it away and hands on a new one.

We had children, and he saw them the way a farmer sees a field he has taken on somewhere far off: once at sowing, and once at harvest. That was the kind of life that kept sending the man out of the house and back to it, in service to one master or another.

And now, when he has come through those labours, now is when I am most afraid. Ever since he killed the strong man Iphitus, we have been living here in Trachis, uprooted, in the house of a foreign host; and where he has gone, nobody knows. All I know is that he went away and left me a sharp pain on his account. I am nearly certain some harm has come to him. It is not a short time — it is ten months now, and five more on top of that, and no word. And there is some terrible trouble in it: he left me a tablet when he went, and I keep praying to the gods that I did not take it to my sorrow.

(An old woman, a slave of the house, has come out and heard her.)

Servant. Deianeira, my mistress, I have watched you many times already, crying your eyes out and mourning Heracles going away. But now — if it is right for a slave's judgement to put a free woman straight, then I have to say what is best for you. How is it that you have so many sons, and you do not send one of them to look for your husband? Hyllus above all: he is the obvious one, if he cares at all whether his father is thought to be doing well. And here he is, coming up to the house at a run, on his own two feet. So if you think what I say is timely, you have the man and my advice both, ready to use.

(Hyllus arrives.)

Deianeira. My child, my son — so good words can fall from people with no birth to speak of. This woman is a slave, but what she has just said is a free woman's speech.

Hyllus. What did she say? Tell me, mother, if it is something I can be told.

Deianeira. That it is a disgrace to you: your father gone so long, out of the country, and you have not tried to find out where he is.

Hyllus. But I know where he is, if you can trust what people say.

Deianeira. And where in the world, my child, do they say he has settled?

Hyllus. All last year, they say, through the whole length of it, he was working as a slave for a Lydian woman.

Deianeira. If he put up with that, then you could believe anything.

Hyllus. But he is free of that now, from what I hear.

Deianeira. Then where do they report him now? Alive, or dead?

Hyllus. They say he is making war on Euboea, on the city of Eurytus — or is about to.

Deianeira. Do you know, my son, that he left me oracles I can trust about that very country?

Hyllus. What oracles, mother? I do not know what you mean.

Deianeira. That either he will meet the end of his life there, or, if he takes on that contest and comes through it, he will live happily for all the rest of his days. So when he stands on the edge like that, my child, will you not go and help him? Because either we are saved, if he saves his life, or we fall with your father when he is destroyed, and are lost together.

Hyllus. I will go, mother. If I had known what these prophecies said, I would have been at his side long ago. As it was, my father's usual luck did not let me be frightened for him ahead of time, or worry too much. Now that I understand, I will do everything, and leave nothing out, to learn the whole truth about this.

Deianeira. Then go, my son. Good news is worth having even for someone who hears it late, once he has heard it.

(Hyllus goes. The Servant goes back into the house. The Chorus comes in: young women of Trachis.)

Chorus.
	You whom the shimmering Night brings to birth
	as she is stripped and put to death,
	and lays to sleep again when you are all on fire —
	Sun, Sun, I ask you:
	tell me this. Where is Alcmena's son,
	where, where is he living now,
	you who blaze with the bright lightning?
	Is he down some channel of the sea,
	or leaning on one of the two continents?
	Tell me, you whose eye is stronger than any.
Chorus.
	Because I hear that Deianeira,
	the bride two suitors fought over,
	is always longing in her heart
	like some poor bird,
	and never puts the ache in her eyes to sleep
	long enough for them to be dry.
	She feeds a fear that will not let her forget
	her husband's road,
	and wears herself out on a bed
	that has a wife in it and no man,
	expecting, in her misery,
	a bad end.
Chorus.
	You could watch the waves go by
	on the wide sea, wave after wave,
	coming on and passing under
	the south wind that never tires, or the north;
	and that is how the trouble of his life,
	heavy as the Cretan sea,
	turns the Theban-born man over
	and lifts him up again.
	But some god keeps him always
	from going wrong, and away
	from the house of Hades.
Chorus.
	I blame you for this, with respect,
	but I will say the other side.
	I say you must not wear away
	the good hope in you.
	The king who accomplishes everything,
	the son of Cronus,
	has not laid on mortals a life without pain.
	Sorrow and joy come round to everyone,
	like the wheeling paths of the Bear.
Chorus.
	Nothing stays. Not the shimmering night
	for mortals, not the powers of death,
	not wealth. In a moment it is gone,
	and it comes to the next one in turn
	to be glad, and then to lose.
	So I tell you, our queen, to hold on
	to this in your hopes, always.
	Because who has ever seen Zeus
	so careless of his children?

Deianeira. You have heard about my trouble, I suppose, and that is why you are here. But how I am eating my heart out — may you never learn that by suffering it. You know nothing of it now. A young thing grows in its own sheltered places, and the god's heat does not shake it, nor rain, nor any wind; it lifts its life up in pleasure, with no labour, until the day a girl is called a wife instead of a girl, and takes on her share of thinking in the night, afraid for a husband or for children. Then, looking at her own situation, a woman might see the troubles that weigh me down.

I have wept over a great many things. But there is one like nothing before it, and I will tell you now. When my lord Heracles set out from home on this last journey of his, he left an old tablet in the house, with signs written on it that he had never before brought himself to explain to me, though he had gone out to many contests. He always went as a man going to do something, not a man going to die. This time, as if he were already no longer alive, he told me what I was to take as my widow's portion, and what share of their father's land he wanted divided among the children. And he fixed a time for it: when he had been out of the country a year and three months, then either he would have to die in that time, or he would get past the end of it and live the rest of his life untroubled. That, he said, was what the gods had settled as the end of the labours of Heracles — the old oak at Dodona had said so once, through the two doves who speak for it. And the truth of it is falling due now, at this present time, just as it has to be fulfilled. So I leap up out of sweet sleep in terror, my friends, afraid that I will be left behind, widowed of the best man of all.

Chorus. No more of that now. Keep quiet — I see a man coming, with a garland on his head as though he had good news.

(A man of Trachis arrives, garlanded.)

Messenger. Deianeira, my mistress, I will be the first messenger to set you free of your fear. Know this: Alcmena's son is alive, and he has won, and he is bringing the first-fruits of the battle to the gods of this country.

Deianeira. What is this you are telling me, old man?

Messenger. That soon your much-envied husband will come to your house, showing himself in the strength of his victory.

Deianeira. And who did you learn this from — a townsman, or a stranger?

Messenger. Out in the meadow where the oxen graze in summer, Lichas the herald is telling it to a crowd. I heard him and I ran off, so that I might be the first to bring it to you, and get something out of it from you, and win your thanks.

Deianeira. And why is he not here himself, if he has done so well?

Messenger. He is not finding it easy to move, lady. The whole people of Malis are standing round him in a ring asking questions, and he cannot get a step further. Every one of them wants to learn what he is longing to hear, and will not let the man go until he has heard it to his satisfaction. So there he is, not by his choice but by theirs. But you will see him yourself, any moment.

Deianeira. Zeus, who holds the meadow on Oeta where no scythe is allowed — you have given us joy at last, late as it is. Raise your voices, women, those of you inside the house and those outside the gate. This message has risen on me like a light I never hoped to see, and we are gathering the fruit of it now.

Chorus.
	Let the house give the cry,
	the girl who is soon to be a bride,
	with shouts of triumph at the hearth.
	And with her let the men's voice go up together,
	a shout for Apollo of the fine quiver,
	our protector.
	And at the same time, girls,
	raise the paean, the paean —
	cry out to his sister,
	Artemis of Ortygia, the shooter of deer,
	with a torch in each hand,
	and to the Nymphs, her neighbours.
	I am lifted up. I will not push away the flute —
	you master of my mind.
	Look, the ivy is whirling me,
	euoi! —
	turning me round now
	in the race of Bacchus.
	Oh, oh, Paean!
	Look, dear lady:
	this is coming straight at you,
	and you can see it, plain.
Deianeira.
	I see it, my friends. My eyes were keeping watch
	and they have not missed this procession.
	Welcome to the herald, I say, appearing after so long —
	if what you bring is anything to be glad of.

(Lichas the herald comes in with a group of captive women. One of them is young, and says nothing.)

Lichas.
	We are glad to be back, and glad of your greeting,
	lady, and it matches what we have done.
	When a man has done well
	he is bound to earn good words.
Deianeira.
	Dearest of men, first tell me the first thing I want:
	will I have Heracles back alive?
Lichas.
	I left him alive and strong,
	in full health, and no sickness on him.
Deianeira.
	Where? On his own soil, or in a foreign country? Tell me.
Lichas.
	There is a headland on Euboea, where he is marking out
	altars and the fruits of the ground for Zeus of Cenaeum.
Deianeira.
	To pay a vow, or because some oracle told him to?
Lichas.
	For a vow, made when he was taking by the spear
	the country of these women you see in front of you.
Deianeira.
	And these — in the gods' name, whose are they, and who are they?
	They are to be pitied, unless their trouble deceives me.
Lichas.
	When he sacked the city of Eurytus, he picked these out
	as a prize, for himself and for the gods.
Deianeira.
	And was it that city that kept him away
	all that time beyond reckoning, more days than I can count?
Lichas.
	No. Most of that time he was held in Lydia —
	not a free man, as he says himself, but sold.
	And there should be no resentment at the story, lady,
	when it turns out that Zeus was behind it.
	He was sold to Omphale, the foreign woman,
	and served out a full year, as he tells it himself.
	And he was so stung by the disgrace of it
	that he swore an oath, and bound himself with it,
	that one day he would make a slave of the man
	who had brought that suffering on him — the man, his wife and his child.
	He did not say it for nothing. Once he had been purified,
	he raised an army of hired men and went against
	the city of Eurytus. That man alone among mortals, he said,
	was to blame for what he had suffered.
	Heracles had come to his house and his hearth
	as a guest of long standing, and Eurytus
	shouted at him again and again, with a mind bent on ruin,
	saying that he had arrows in his hands that never miss,
	and still fell short of Eurytus's own sons in a contest of the bow;
	and that he was a slave, and a free man's slave, and broken;
	and at dinner, when his guest was full of wine,
	he threw him out of the house. Heracles was angry at that,
Lichas.
	and when Iphitus came afterwards to the hill of Tiryns,
	tracking horses that had strayed,
	he caught him with his eyes on one thing and his mind on another
	and hurled him from the top of the tower-high rock.
	For that deed the king, Olympian Zeus, father of all,
	was furious, and sent him away to be sold.
	He would not stand for it, because this once, and only this once,
	Heracles had killed a man by treachery.
	If he had taken his revenge in the open,
	Zeus would have forgiven him, since he would have won it with justice.
	The gods have no love for outrage either.
	So those men, who ran wild with their bad tongues,
	are all of them living in Hades now,
	and their city is a slave. And these women you are looking at,
	who have found a life nobody envies after a happy one,
	are coming to you. That was your husband's order,
	and I am his faithful man, and I carry it out.
	As for Heracles himself: once he has made
	clean sacrifice to Zeus of his fathers for taking the city,
	expect him to come. Of all this good news,
	well told and at length, that is the sweetest thing to hear.
Chorus.
	Queen, now your joy is plain to see —
	part of it in front of you, the rest in what you have heard.
Deianeira.
	How could I not be glad, with all my heart,
	hearing that my husband has done so well?
	My joy has to run alongside his success.
	And yet a person who looks carefully
	can feel fear for the one who is doing well, in case he stumbles.
	Because a terrible pity came over me, my friends,
	looking at these unlucky women,
	wandering in a strange country, with no home and no father.
	They were the daughters of free men, perhaps, before;
	now they live the life of slaves.
	Zeus, who turns the tide of battle,
	may I never see you come against my children like this —
	or if you are going to, not while I am still alive.
	That is how afraid I am, looking at them.
	You poor girl. Which of these young women are you?
	Are you unmarried, or a mother? By the look of you
	you know nothing of all that yet, but you are somebody well born.
	Lichas, whose is she, this stranger?
	Who is her mother? Who is the father who begot her?
	Tell me. She is the one I pitied most of all when I looked at them,
	because she is the only one who knows how to carry herself.
Lichas.
	How would I know? Why ask me? Maybe
	she is a child of one of the better families out there.
Deianeira.
	Not of the ruling house? Was she some child of Eurytus?
Lichas.
	I do not know. I did not ask many questions.
Deianeira.
	You did not even get her name from one of the others she travelled with?
Lichas.
	No. I did my job in silence.
Deianeira.
	Then tell us yourself, poor girl, out of your own mouth.
	It is a misfortune too, not to know who you are.
Lichas.
	If she opens her lips now it will be nothing like
	the time before. She has not brought out a word,
	big or small. She has been in labour
	with the weight of her misfortune the whole time, weeping,
	poor thing, since she left her windy country.
	Her luck is bad — for her — but it earns her some understanding.
Deianeira.
	Then let her be, and let her go in under the roof
	in whatever way is easiest for her. Let her not take
	another grief from me on top of the ones she has;
	what she has is enough. Now let us all go in,
	so you can hurry off wherever you want to go,
	and I can set the house in order.

(Lichas goes into the house with the captives. The Messenger stops Deianeira before she can follow.)

Messenger.
	Wait here first, a little, so that you can learn,
	with those people out of the way, who it is you are taking indoors,
	and find out what you need to know and have not heard a word of.
	I know the whole of it.
Deianeira.
	What is it? Why are you stopping me on my way?
Messenger.
	Stand there and listen. The last thing you heard from me
	was not a waste of your time, and I do not think this will be.
Deianeira.
	Should we call them back out here, then,
	or do you want to speak to me and these women?
Messenger.
	To you and these women there is nothing to stop me. Leave the others.
Deianeira.
	Well, they are gone. Let your story say what it means.
Messenger.
	Nothing this man told you just now
	was spoken straight by the rule of justice. Either he is a liar now,
	or he was no honest messenger before.
Deianeira.
	What are you saying? Tell me clearly everything you mean.
	What you have said so far I do not understand.
Messenger.
	I heard this man say, with many witnesses standing by,
	that it was for this girl's sake
	that Heracles brought down Eurytus and Oechalia with its high towers,
	and that Eros, the god of desire, and no other god,
	bewitched him into that fighting —
	not the labours in Lydia, not slaving under Omphale,
	not Iphitus hurled to his death.
	Now he has shoved Eros aside and tells the opposite story.
	When he could not persuade her father
	to give him the girl to keep in his bed in secret,
	he got up some small complaint as a pretext
	and marched on her country — the country where, as this man said,
	Eurytus held the throne — and killed her father the king,
	and sacked her city. And now, as you see, he has come home,
	and sends her to this house not carelessly, lady,
	and not to be a slave. Do not expect that.
	It is not likely, if he is burning with desire.
	So I decided to tell you the whole of what I happened
	to learn from this man, my mistress.
	And many people in the middle of the Trachinian market
	heard it exactly as I did, so they can prove him wrong.
	If what I say is not welcome, I am sorry.
	But it is the truth, and I have told it.
Deianeira.
	Oh no. Oh, I am wretched. Where do I stand now?
	What trouble have I taken in under my roof
	without knowing it? Oh, the misery of it. So she has no name —
	as the man who brought her swore?
Messenger.
	She is very bright, in her name and in her blood.
	She is the daughter of Eurytus by birth, and she was called
	Iole once. And he could tell you nothing of where she came from —
	because, of course, he never asked.
Chorus.
	Damnation — not on all bad men, but on the one
	who practises hidden wrongs that do not become him.
Deianeira.
	What am I to do, women? What I have just heard
	has knocked me stupid.
Chorus.
	Go and question the man. He might say something clear,
	if you were willing to press him hard and force it out of him.
Deianeira.
	I will go. What you say is not without sense.
Messenger.
	And we — should we wait here? Or what do you want done?
Deianeira.
	Stay. Here is the man coming out of the house —
	no messenger of mine fetched him; he comes of his own accord.

(Lichas comes out.)

Lichas.
	Lady, what am I to say to Heracles when I get there?
	Tell me. As you see, I am on my way.
Deianeira.
	You took your time coming, and how you rush off now —
	before we have even taken up the conversation again.
Lichas.
	Well, if there is anything you want to ask, I am here.
Deianeira.
	And will you give me the truth, and honestly?
Lichas.
	Great Zeus be my witness — in anything I know.
Deianeira.
	Then who is the woman you came here bringing?
Lichas.
	She is from Euboea. Who her parents are I cannot say.
Messenger.
	You there. Look at me. Who do you think you are talking to?
Lichas.
	And you — what are you asking me that for?
Messenger.
	Have the nerve to answer what I ask you, if you have any sense.
Lichas.
	To the lady in charge here, Deianeira, daughter of Oeneus,
	wife of Heracles, and — unless my eyes are playing tricks on me —
	my mistress.
Messenger.
	That is exactly what I wanted, exactly what I wanted to hear from you.
	You say this woman is your mistress?
Lichas.
	It is only right.
Messenger.
	Then what? What punishment do you think you deserve
	if you are found to have done her wrong?
Lichas.
	Wrong? What is this you have been weaving?
Messenger.
	Nothing. Though you are doing a great deal of it yourself.
Lichas.
	I am going. I was a fool to stand here listening to you this long.
Messenger.
	Not until you have answered one short question.
Lichas.
	Ask, if you want something. You are not the silent kind.
Messenger.
	The prisoner you sent into the house. You know the one I mean?
Lichas.
	I do. Why do you ask?
Messenger.
	This girl, then, whom you look at as if you had never seen her —
	did you not say she was Iole, Eurytus's daughter,
	and that you were bringing her here?
Lichas.
	Said it in front of whom? Who is going to come forward,
	and from where, to swear he heard that from me?
Messenger.
	Plenty of the townspeople. In the middle of the market square
	of Trachis a whole crowd heard exactly that from you.
Lichas.
	I said that was what I had heard. Repeating what people guess
	is not the same thing as giving an exact account.
Messenger.
	Guess? Did you not say, and swear to it,
	that the girl you were bringing was to be Heracles' wife?
Lichas.
	I said wife? For the gods' sake, dear mistress, tell me —
	who is this stranger?
Messenger.
	A man who stood there and heard you say
	that a whole city was brought down out of desire for this girl,
	that it was not the Lydian woman who destroyed it
	but the love for her that came into the open.
Lichas.
	Lady, send this person away. Trading nonsense with a sick man
	is not what a man in his right mind does.
Deianeira.
	No. By Zeus, whose lightning strikes down
	the high glens of Oeta, do not cheat me of the truth.
	You will not be telling it to a bad woman,
	or to one who does not know how people are:
	that what we take pleasure in does not stay the same for ever.
	Anyone who squares up to Love like a boxer,
	fists raised, has lost his senses.
	Love rules the gods themselves however he likes,
	and he rules me. So why not another woman, made as I am?
	If I blamed my husband for catching this sickness
	I would be out of my mind —
	or blamed this girl, who shares with him in a thing
	that is no disgrace to her and no harm to me.
	No. But if it was from him that you learned to lie,
	you have learned a lesson that does you no credit;
	and if you are teaching yourself, then when you set out to be kind
	you will turn out to be cruel.
	Tell me the whole truth. For a free man
	to be called a liar is an ugly mark to carry.
	And you will not get away with it, either:
	you told too many people, and they will tell me.
	If you are afraid, you are afraid of the wrong thing.
	Not being told: that is what would hurt me.
	What is so terrible about knowing? Has Heracles, one man,
	not been with more women than anyone could count?
	And not one of them ever had a hard word
	or a reproach from me. Nor will this girl,
	even if she is melting with love for him —
	because when I looked at her I pitied her more than any of them.
	Her beauty has destroyed her life,
	and, poor thing, without meaning to, she has wrecked
	her own country and made it a slave.
	Well, let all that run with the wind.
	But I am telling you: cheat other people if you like.
	With me, always tell the truth.
Chorus.
	Do what she says. It is good advice. You will not regret it
	with this lady later, and you will have my thanks as well.
Lichas.
	Dear mistress — now that I see you are a mortal
	who thinks as a mortal should, and not unreasonable,
	I will tell you the whole truth and hide nothing.
	It is as this man says.
	A terrible longing for this girl went through Heracles
	once; and it was for her sake that Oechalia,
	her father's city, was ruined, brought down by his spear.
	And — I must say what is on his side too —
	he never told me to hide it, and never denied it.
	It was I, lady, afraid of hurting you
	with news like this, who went wrong —
	if you count it as going wrong at all.
	But now you know the whole story:
	for his sake and for yours alike,
	put up with the woman, and let the words
	you spoke about her just now stand for good.
	Because he, who wins every other fight with his hands,
	has been beaten by his love for this girl, completely.
Deianeira.
	That is how I think too, and that is what I will do.
	I will not take on a second sickness of my own
	by fighting the gods, which nobody wins.
	Let us go inside, so that you can carry my messages,
	and the gifts that have to go back in return for gifts —
	take those with you too. It would not be right
	for you to go back empty-handed,
	after arriving with such a train behind you.

(They go into the house.)

Chorus.
	Great is the strength Aphrodite carries off in victory, always.
	What she did to the gods I pass over —
	how she fooled the son of Cronus, Zeus himself, I will not say,
	nor Hades of the dark, nor Poseidon who shakes the earth.
	But for this bride —
	who were the two great rivals who came down for her before the wedding?
	Who went out to the contest of blows and dust?
Chorus.
	One was the strength of a river, the shape of a bull,
	high-horned, four-legged: Achelous, from Oeniadae.
	The other came from Thebes, the city of Bacchus,
	shaking his springing bow, his spears, and his club —
	the son of Zeus. The two of them came together then
	into the middle, hungry for her bed;
	and Aphrodite alone, who makes the marriage, sat between them with the umpire's rod.
Chorus.
	Then there was the thud of fists and the twang of the bow
	and the bull's horns all mixed in together;
	there were legs locked and twisted,
	there were deadly blows from the foreheads,
	and groaning from both.
	And the girl, soft, lovely-eyed,
	sat on a hillside that could be seen far off,
	waiting for whichever would be her husband.
	The fight rages on, as I tell it;
	but the bride's face, the prize they fought for,
	waits, pitiable, for the end.
	And suddenly she has gone from her mother,
	like a calf left on its own.

(Deianeira comes out of the house alone.)

Deianeira. Friends — while our guest is inside saying goodbye to the captive girls, on the point of leaving, I have slipped out of the house to you without his knowing. Partly to tell you what my hands have been contriving. Partly to have you grieve with me over what I am going through.

I have taken in a girl — no, I think, not a girl any longer, but a woman who has been yoked to a man — the way a sailor takes on cargo: a piece of merchandise that will wreck my peace of mind. And now we are two women waiting under one blanket for one man's embrace. That is what Heracles, the man I called faithful and good, has sent home to me as payment for keeping his house all this long time.

I do not know how to be angry with him. He has caught this sickness badly, and it has happened many times. But to live in the same house with her, sharing the same marriage — what woman could do it? I can see her youth coming into flower while mine is fading; and a man's eye loves to snatch the flower, and turns its step away from what is faded. So I am afraid of this: that Heracles will be called my husband and be the younger woman's man.

But as I said, for a woman with any sense, anger is not becoming. So I will tell you, friends, the way I have found of getting release and relief.

I had an old gift, from a monster of the old days, hidden in a bronze jar. I was still a girl when I took it from the shaggy-chested centaur Nessus, as he lay dying, out of his own wound. He was the one who used to carry people in his arms, for a fee, across the deep-running river Evenus — no oars to row them over, no ship's sail. He carried me too, on his shoulders, the first time I went with Heracles as his wife, when my father sent me on my way; and when I was in midstream he put his hands on me where he had no business. I screamed, and Zeus's son turned round at once and let fly a feathered arrow from his hands. It went whistling through his chest into the lungs. And as he died, the monster said this much to me:

"Child of old Oeneus, you shall get this much good out of my ferrying, if you do what I say, since you are the last passenger I ever carried. Take the blood clotted round my wound in your hands, at the place where the arrow was dipped in the black gall of the Hydra, the creature of Lerna, and you will have a charm for Heracles' heart, so that he will never look at any woman and love her more than you."

I thought of this, friends — since his death it had been kept locked away safely in the house — and I dipped this robe in it, doing everything he told me while he was alive. And it is done. I hope I never know anything of wicked daring, never learn it; I hate the women who go in for it. But if there is any way I can get the better of this girl, with love-charms and spells laid on Heracles — that is the plan, and it is ready. Unless you think I am doing something foolish.
