If so, I will stop at once.

Chorus. If there is anything to trust in what has been done, we think you have not planned badly.

Deianeira. The trust goes this far: it looks as though it will work. But I have never put it to the test.

Chorus. You will have to act to find out. However sure you feel, you cannot know a thing until you have tried it.

Deianeira. Well, we shall know soon. There he is, already at the door; he will be gone in a moment. Only — keep this well covered for me. Do a shameful thing in the dark, and you will never fall into shame.

(Lichas comes out.)

Lichas. What are your orders? Tell me, daughter of Oeneus. I have stayed so long that I am late already.

Deianeira. That is exactly what I have been seeing to for you, Lichas, while you were talking to the foreign women inside: that you should carry this for me. A long-woven robe, a gift to my husband from my own hands. When you give it to him, tell him this. No one is to put it on his body before he does. It is not to be seen by the light of the sun, or inside a holy enclosure, or by the fire on a hearth, until he stands out in the open, in full view, and shows it to the gods on the day the bulls are sacrificed. That was my vow: if I ever saw him come home safe, or heard for certain that he had, I would dress him in this robe and show him to the gods as a new sacrificer in a new garment. And you will carry a token of it, which he will easily recognise: the mark inside this seal. Now go. And first, keep to the rule that a messenger does not take it upon himself to do more than he was sent to do; and second, see to it that his thanks and mine come together, so that you earn double instead of single.

Lichas. If I practise Hermes' craft of carrying messages at all soundly, I will never once slip in your service. I will carry this box and show it exactly as it is, and add the assurance of the words you have told me to go with it.

Deianeira. You can go now. You know very well how things stand here in the house.

Lichas. I know, and I will tell him that all is well.

Deianeira. And you know, from your own eyes, how I received the foreign girl — how warmly I welcomed her.

Lichas. So warmly it struck my heart with pleasure.

Deianeira. Then what else is there for you to tell him? I am afraid it would be too early to speak of my longing for him, before I know whether I am longed for over there.

(Lichas goes out along the road. Deianeira goes into the house.)

Chorus.
	You who live beside the harbour and the rocks
	and the hot springs, and the crags of Oeta;
	you who live along the inner shore of the Malian gulf
	and the beach of the girl with the golden shuttle, Artemis,
	where the Greeks hold their famous assemblies
	at the Gates, at Thermopylae:
Chorus.
	soon the flute with its lovely voice
	will rise up for you again, not with a harsh
	and grating clang, but with music like the lyre, fit for the gods.
	Because the son that Alcmena bore to Zeus
	is hurrying home,
	carrying the spoils of all his excellence.
Chorus.
	We had him out of the city altogether, somewhere,
	while we waited twelve months,
	out on the sea, knowing nothing.
	And his loving wife, poor woman,
	wretched, with a wretched heart,
	was wasting away, always in tears.
	But now Ares, stung to fury,
	has set her free from the days of toil.
Chorus.
	Let him come, let him come. Let the many-oared ship
	that carries him not stop
	before he reaches this town,
	leaving the island altar
	where they say he is sacrificing.
	Let him come from there all desire,
	steeped through with the charm of Persuasion,
	with the robe for its excuse.

(Deianeira comes out of the house.)

Deianeira. Women, I am afraid I may have gone too far in everything I was doing just now.

Chorus. What is it, Deianeira, daughter of Oeneus?

Deianeira. I do not know. But I am losing heart. I think I will soon turn out to have done a great wrong, out of a good hope.

Chorus. Surely not something to do with your gift to Heracles?

Deianeira. Exactly that. So much so that I would never advise anyone to be eager to act when they cannot see what the act will do.

Chorus. Tell us, if it can be told, what you are afraid of.

Deianeira. Something has happened, women, that if I tell it, you will hear as a wonder nobody could have expected. The thing I was using just now to anoint the festal robe — a white tuft of wool from a fleecy sheep — has vanished. Nothing in the house ate it. It ate itself, wasted away on its own, and crumbled to nothing on the top of a stone slab. So that you can know the whole of it, how this came about, I will tell it at more length.

Of the instructions the centaur gave me, the monster, while he was in agony from the bitter arrowhead in his side, I left out nothing. I kept them as if they were writing cut in a bronze tablet that no hand could wash away. This was what he told me, and this is what I did: keep this drug in the innermost part of the house, away from fire and never touched by a warm ray of sun, until I should spread it fresh on something and put it to use. And so I had done. And just now, when the time came to act, I anointed the robe secretly, indoors, with a tuft of wool pulled from one of our own sheep; then I folded my gift up and laid it, out of the sunlight, in its hollow chest, as you saw.

But as I am going back inside, I see a thing there are no words for, that no human mind could work out. Somehow I had happened to throw the tuft of wool I was anointing with into the full blaze of the sun's rays. As it warmed, it ran all together into nothing and crumbled away on the ground, looking most of all like the crumbs you would see left where a saw has cut through wood. That is how it lies where it fell. And out of the earth where it lay, clotted foam is seething up, like the thick juice of the blue fruit from Bacchus's vine when it is poured on the ground.

Deianeira. So I do not know, in my misery, which way to turn my mind. I only see that I have done a terrible thing. Why, in return for what, would the monster have wished me well as he died, when I was the reason he was dying? It is impossible. No — he wanted to destroy the man who shot him, and he was casting his spell on me to do it. And I am learning that now, too late, when it is no use any more. If my judgement is not wrong, I alone, wretched as I am, am going to destroy him. Because I know that the arrow that struck the centaur hurt even a god, Cheiron, and that it kills every kind of animal it touches. And this black poison of the blood, which came out through Nessus's wound from that same arrow — how can it fail to kill Heracles too? In my judgement, at least, it cannot. And yet I have decided: if he is to fall, I will die with him in the same fall, at the same moment. No woman who cares more than anything about not being evil by nature could bear to live and be called evil.

Chorus. When terrible things are done, fear is unavoidable. But you should not judge your hope before the outcome.

Deianeira. Where the plan is bad there is no hope — not the kind of hope that gives you any confidence.

Chorus. But with people whose mistake was not of their own choosing, anger softens. That is what should happen with you.

Deianeira. That is what someone would say who had no share in the harm — not someone with trouble of her own at home.

Chorus. It would be best to say nothing more, unless you mean to say it to your own son. He is here — the one who went off before to look for his father.

(Hyllus comes in.)

Hyllus. Mother. I wish I could pick one of three things for you: that you were no longer alive; or that if you were spared, you were somebody else's mother; or that from somewhere you could exchange the heart you have for a better one.

Deianeira. What is it, my son? What have I done that you hate?

Hyllus. Your husband — I mean my father — know that you have killed him. Today.

Deianeira. Oh. What is this you have brought out, child?

Hyllus. Something that cannot fail to be fulfilled. What has come into the light — who could make it not have happened?

Deianeira. What are you saying, my son? From whom did you learn that I have done a thing so far from anyone's envy, that you charge me with it?

Hyllus. From nobody. I saw my father's crushing misfortune with my own eyes. I did not hear it from anyone's tongue.

Deianeira. Where did you find him? Where were you at his side?

Hyllus. If you have to know, then I must tell all of it.

After he had sacked the famous city of Eurytus and was on his way with the trophies of victory and the first pick of the spoils — there is a headland of Euboea washed by the sea on both sides, Cape Cenaeum, where he was marking out altars and a leafy sacred precinct for Zeus, his father's god. That is where I first saw him, and I was glad; I had been longing for it. He was about to make a great sacrifice, with many victims, when his own herald, Lichas, arrived from home carrying your gift — the deadly robe. He put it on, exactly as you had instructed, and began by killing twelve bulls without a blemish, the first fruits of the plunder; but altogether he brought a hundred beasts of every kind to the altar.

And at first, poor wretch, he prayed with a calm mind, glad of the finery and the dress. But when the bloody flame began to blaze up from the solemn offerings and the resinous pine, sweat came up on his skin, and the robe clung to his sides at every joint, close-glued, as if a craftsman had fitted it. Then a biting pain came into his bones, and wrenched him; and then the poison began to eat him, like the venom of some deadly, hateful viper.

At that he shouted for Lichas, poor devil, who had no part in your crime, and demanded to know by what trick he had brought this robe. And Lichas, knowing nothing, poor wretch, said it was your gift and yours alone, exactly as it had been sent. When my father heard that, and a spasm of agony took hold of his lungs, he grabbed him by the foot, where the ankle turns in its socket, and hurled him at a rock washed by the sea. The white brain came spurting out through his hair, with the middle of his skull smashed open and the blood along with it.

Hyllus. And all the people cried out and wailed at the sight of them — the one in his sickness, the other destroyed — and nobody dared to go near the man. He was dragged to the ground and hauled into the air, shouting, screaming; and the rocks all round rang with it, the mountain headlands of Locris and the capes of Euboea.

When he had worn himself out with throwing himself on the ground, over and over, and howling, over and over, cursing the bed he had made with you, wretched woman, and the marriage into the house of Oeneus, which he said he had got himself as the ruin of his life — then, out of the smoke that hung round the altar, he lifted his rolling eyes and saw me, weeping, in the middle of the great crowd of soldiers. He looked straight at me and called me.

"My son, come here. Do not run away from my suffering, not even if you have to die with me as I die. Lift me and carry me out of here; best of all, put me somewhere no human being will see me. But if you have any pity, at least ferry me out of this country as fast as you can. Do not let me die here."

That was his order, and we laid him in the middle of a boat and brought him to this shore, only just, bellowing in his convulsions. And you will see him soon, either alive or just dead.

That, Mother, is what you have been caught planning against my father, and doing. May Justice that punishes, and the Fury, pay you back for it. If it is lawful to pray so, I pray it. And it is lawful, because you threw the law away first, when you killed the best man of all the men on earth. You will never look at his like again.

(Deianeira turns and goes towards the house without a word.)

Chorus. Why are you going off in silence? Do you not know that by saying nothing you are speaking on the side of the man who accuses you?

Hyllus. Let her go. Let a fair wind carry her far out of my sight. Why should she keep up the dignity of the name "mother" for nothing, when nothing she does is what a mother does? Let her go, and much good may it do her. And the pleasure she is giving my father — may she get the same herself.

(Deianeira goes into the house. Hyllus follows.)

Chorus.
	Look, girls, how suddenly it has come upon us,
	the god's word spoken long ago,
	the old prophecy which foresaw it all:
	that when the twelfth year of ploughing
	had run its full count of months, it would bring
	the end of the labours for Zeus's own son.
	And this is coming true, on a steady wind, exactly.
	For how could a man who no longer sees the light
	ever again have any painful labour to serve, once he is dead?
Chorus.
	If the deadly cloud of the centaur
	is smeared on his sides by a trap that had to close,
	with the poison melted into him
	that Death gave birth to and the glittering snake fed —
	how can he see any other sun than this one,
	fused to the Hydra's shape,
	the most terrible of all? And along with that,
	the murderous goads of black-haired Nessus,
	set on him by treacherous words, boil in him and torture him.
Chorus.
	And this poor woman, seeing the great harm
	rushing at her house from the new marriage,
	did not hold back: part of it she brought on herself,
	and part came from a stranger's mind,
	in a fatal exchange.
	Surely she groans in ruin now;
	surely she wets a pale dew
	of thick tears.
	And the fate that is coming shows ahead of it
	a treacherous, enormous ruin.
Chorus.
	The spring of tears has burst. The sickness has poured out — oh,
	such suffering as no enemy ever brought
	on glorious Heracles, to be pitied.
	Oh, dark point of the spear that led the fight,
	which brought that bride so swiftly then
	down from the heights of Oechalia at the spear's edge —
	and Aphrodite, silent, in attendance,
	stands revealed as the one who did all this.

(A cry from inside the house.)

First Half-Chorus. Am I imagining it, or did I just hear a cry of grief start up somewhere inside the house? What was that?

Second Half-Chorus. That is no vague sound. It is a wail, and it is a wail of disaster, from indoors. Something new has happened in that house.

Half-Chorus. And look. The old woman is coming out to us with her head down and her brows drawn together. She has something to tell.

(The Nurse comes out.)

Nurse. Children — so this is what it came to. The present she sent off to Heracles has started no small run of evils for us.

Chorus. What has happened, old woman? What is new?

Nurse. Deianeira has gone on the last journey of all her journeys, and never moved a foot.

Chorus. You cannot mean she is dead?

Nurse. You have heard it all.

Chorus. She is dead? The poor woman?

Nurse. That is the second time you have heard it.

Chorus. Poor lost woman. How did she die? Tell us how.

Nurse. In the cruellest way anyone could do it.

Chorus.
	Tell us, woman, what death
	she ran to meet.
Nurse.
	She destroyed herself.
Chorus.
	What passion, what sickness of the mind
	brought her down on the point of that evil weapon?
	How did she plan it, alone —
	death on top of death, and carried it through by herself?
Nurse.
	With the cut of the iron that makes people groan.
Chorus.
	You saw it, you poor helpless thing — this outrage?
Nurse.
	I saw it. I was standing right beside her.
Chorus.
	Who did it? How? Come, tell us.
Nurse.
	Herself. With her own hand. She made it happen.
Chorus.
	What are you saying?
Nurse.
	The plain truth.
Chorus.
	She has borne, she has borne a great Fury
	for this house — the new bride,
	the girl who only just arrived.

Nurse. Too great a one. And if you had been there beside her and seen what she did, you would have pitied her far more.

Chorus. And a woman's hand brought itself to do that?

Nurse. Yes, and terribly. You will hear it, and then you can bear me out.

When she came into the house alone and saw her son out in the courtyard spreading a hollow litter, to go back and meet his father with it, she hid herself where nobody could see her. She threw herself down at the altars and cried aloud that they were going to be left empty. She wept over every household thing she touched — the things she used to use, poor woman, in the old days. She went from one room to another, and if she caught sight of any of the servants she was fond of, she wept at the sight of them, the unhappy woman, calling out on her own fate and on a life that would have no children in it from now on.

When she had done with that, all at once I saw her rush into Heracles' bedroom. I kept my eye on her in secret, from the shadows, watching. And I saw the woman throwing coverings onto Heracles' bed and spreading them out. When she had finished that she jumped up onto it and sat down in the middle of the bed, and hot streams of tears broke out of her, and she said: "My bed, and my bridal room — goodbye now, for good. You will never take me in to lie in these sheets again."

She said that much and no more. Then with a hard, quick hand she undid her dress where the brooch of beaten gold sat over her breasts, and bared her whole left side and her arm. I ran, as fast as I had the strength to, and told her son what she was contriving. And in the time it took to go there and come back, we found her with a two-edged sword driven into her side, under the liver, into the heart.

The boy saw it and cried out. Because he understood, poor boy, that his anger had driven her to it. He had learned too late, from the people in the house, that she had done it without knowing what she did, because of the beast — the Centaur. And then there was no lament the poor boy left unmade, groaning over her, falling on her to kiss her mouth; he lay down side by side with her and groaned and groaned that he had thrown a false charge at her, and wept that now he was orphaned of both of them at once, his father and her.

So that is how things stand in there. And anyone who counts on two days, or on more than that, is a fool. There is no tomorrow until you have got safely through today.

(She goes back inside.)

Chorus.
	Which do I groan for first?
	Which sorrow is the heavier?
	I am wretched, and I cannot judge.
Chorus.
	One of them we can see, here in the house.
	The other we wait for, and dread.
	To have it and to wait for it come to the same.
Chorus.
	If only some wind would rise
	and blow fair, away from this hearth,
	and carry me off out of this place,
	so that I need not die of terror
	at one sight of the strong son of Zeus.
	They say he is coming to the house
	in pains that nothing will loosen,
	a horror past all telling.
Chorus.
	And he was near, not far —
	the thing I cried out for beforehand, like the shrill nightingale.
	Here is a procession of strangers.
	And how are they carrying him? Like someone they love
	and are caring for — a heavy, soundless step.
	Aiai. He is carried in, and makes no sound.
	Am I to think him dead,
	or only sleeping?

(An Old Man comes in with attendants, carrying Heracles on a litter. Hyllus comes out of the house.)

Hyllus.
	Oh, father — oh, this is misery,
	father, this is misery for me.
	What is to become of me? What am I to do? Oh.
Old Man.
	Quiet, child. Do not wake
	your father's savage pain. He is at the edge of it —
	alive, and barely. Bite your lip and hold it.
Hyllus.
	What are you saying, old man? He is alive?
Old Man.
	You must not wake him. Sleep has hold of him.
	You must not stir it, must not bring it back —
	the sickness that comes and goes, that terrible thing, child.
Hyllus.
	But the weight on me is more than I can carry.
	My mind is out of my hands.
Heracles.
	Zeus —
	what country have I come to? Who are these people
	I lie among, worn down by pains
	that never stop? Oh, I am wretched.
	It is biting again, the filthy thing. Ah.
Old Man.
	Did I not know how much we gained
	by keeping quiet, by not scattering
	the sleep off his head and off his eyes?
Hyllus.
	I cannot. I do not know how
	to look at this and bear it.
Heracles.
	Cenaeum, foundation of my altars,
	what thanks you have got me
	for my offerings there — Zeus.
	What ruin you have made of me. What ruin.
	I wish these eyes had never seen it,
	never looked on this flower of madness
	that nothing can charm away.
	What singer, what healer's hand
	will charm this ruin quiet, except Zeus?
	Show me one. I would be amazed to see him, even far off.
Heracles.
	Ah —
	let me be, let me be, I am done for,
	let me sleep my last sleep.
Heracles.
	Where — where are you touching me? Where are you laying me down?
	You are killing me. You are killing me.
	You have woken whatever had gone quiet.
	It has me — oh — here it comes again, crawling. Where are you from,
	you men, the most unjust in all Greece? I wore myself out
	clearing your seas and every forest of their monsters,
	I was destroyed doing it — and now, when I am sick,
	will not one of you bring fire, or a sword, and do me that kindness?
Heracles.
	Ah —
	will nobody come and hack the head
	off this hateful body? Ah. Ah.
Old Man.
	Son of this man, the work has grown beyond
	my strength. You take him. You have more in your hands
	to save him than I have.
Hyllus.
	I have hold of him.
	But I have nothing, in myself or from outside,
	that will make his life forget its pain. That is what Zeus deals out.
Heracles.
	Son, where are you? Here — take me here,
	lift me, take the weight. Ah. Oh, what has come to me.
Heracles.
	It leaps again, it leaps, the wretched thing,
	it is finishing me,
	the wild sickness nobody can go near.
	Pallas, Pallas — it is torturing me again. Son —
	pity your father. Draw the sword — nobody will blame you —
	and strike here, under my collarbone. Cure the pain
	your godless mother filled me with. Let me see her fall
	like this, exactly like this, the way she has destroyed me. Sweet Hades —
Heracles.
	brother of Zeus — put me to sleep, to sleep,
	finish me with a fast death. I am wretched.

Chorus. I shudder to hear it, friends — what our king suffers. A man like that, driven by pains like these.

Heracles. Oh — many a hot, hard thing, and not just in the telling, these hands have laboured through, and this back. But nothing like this was ever set me, not by Zeus's wife, not by hateful Eurystheus — nothing like what the daughter of Oeneus, with her lying face, has fastened on my shoulders: this woven net of the Furies I am dying in. It has glued itself to my sides and eaten the flesh right through to the bone; it lives inside me and sucks at the passages of my lungs; it has already drunk my fresh blood, and my whole body is destroyed, mastered by this chain that no one can describe.

No spear on a battlefield ever did this to me. Nor the earth-born army of the Giants, nor any wild beast's strength, nor Greece, nor any land with no Greek in it, nor any country I came to and cleared of its monsters. A woman did it. A female, born with nothing of a man's nature in her — she, alone, has brought me down without a sword.

Son — be my true son now, born of me, and do not set your mother's name above mine. Go into the house yourself and bring her out with your own two hands, and put the woman who bore you into my hand, so that I can know for certain which sight hurts you more: mine, or hers, when her body has been mutilated as justice demands.

Go, child. Have the stomach for it. And pity me, whom so many would pity — bellowing and crying like a girl. Nobody could say he had ever seen this man do that before. I followed my troubles without a groan, always. And now, from that, I have been found out a woman. I am wretched.

Heracles. Now come here and stand beside your father, and see what kind of misfortune has done this to me. I will show it to you. I will take off the coverings. Look — all of you, look at this miserable body. Look at the wretch I am. Look at the pitiful state I am in.

Aiai — oh, I am wretched. It has flared up again, just now, this spasm of ruin; it has shot through my sides. It will not leave me alone, it seems, this wretched, devouring sickness.

Lord Hades, take me. Fire of Zeus, strike. Hurl it, lord — send the bolt of your thunder down on me, father. It is feeding again; it has burst into flower; it has broken out.

Hands — my hands, my back, my chest, my own dear arms. Are you the same ones that once broke by force the thing that lived in Nemea, the herdsmen's curse, the lion, a creature nobody could approach or speak to? And the Hydra of Lerna; and that double-natured army of beasts that would mix with no one, that went on horses' legs — the Centaurs, violent, lawless, overbearing in their strength; and the boar of Erymanthus; and, under the earth, the three-headed dog of Hades, a monster nothing could fight, the whelp of the terrible Echidna; and the snake that guarded the golden apples at the ends of the earth. I tasted ten thousand other labours, and no man ever set up a trophy over these hands.

And now look at me — my joints unstrung, torn to rags, sacked and ruined by a ruin I could not see coming. I, who am called the son of the noblest of mothers. I, who am named the child of Zeus among the stars.

But know this one thing for certain: even if I am nothing, even if I cannot crawl a step, the woman who did this I will get into my hands, even from here. Only let her come near me, so that she can learn to tell the whole world that living or dead I punished the bad.

Chorus. Poor Greece — what mourning I see coming for her, if she loses this man.

Hyllus. Father, since you have paused and given me a chance to answer, be quiet a while and listen to me, sick as you are. I am going to ask you for what I have a right to. Give yourself to me — not as you are now, savage with the pain that is biting you. Otherwise you cannot know where you are wrong to want to take pleasure, and where you are wrong to hurt.

Heracles. Say what you want, and then stop. I am sick; I cannot follow any of these riddles you have been spinning.

Hyllus. I have come to tell you about my mother — the state she is in now, and how she went wrong without meaning to.

Heracles. You vile — you name her again, in my hearing? Your mother, who killed your father?

Hyllus. Things are such that silence would not be right.

Heracles. Not right — after what she did before?

Hyllus. You will not say so. Not after what she has done today.

Heracles. Speak. But take care you do not turn out a bad son.

Hyllus. I will speak. She is dead, just now, newly killed.

Heracles. By whose hand? That is a monstrous thing to prophesy, out of all this evil.

Hyllus. Her own. No one else's — no outsider's.

Heracles. Oh — before she could die as she should have, by my hand?

Hyllus. Even your anger would turn, if you knew the whole of it.

Heracles. That is a strange way to begin. Tell me what you mean.

Hyllus. The whole thing is this: she went wrong, meaning to do good.

Heracles. Good, you vile creature? She kills your father, and that is good?

Hyllus. She thought she was putting a love-charm on you, when she saw the marriage inside the house. She missed.

Heracles. And which of the Trachinians is that great a poisoner?

Hyllus. Nessus the Centaur. Long ago he persuaded her to madden your desire with a love-charm like that.

Heracles. Oh — oh, I am wretched. I am gone. Finished, finished — there is no light left for me. Oh. Now I understand where we stand.

Go, child. You have no father any more. Call all your brothers, the whole seed of my blood, and call poor Alcmena, Zeus's wife for nothing, so that you can hear my last word on the oracles — everything I know.

Hyllus. But your mother is not here. It happens that she has settled at Tiryns on the coast. Some of your children she has taken and is raising herself; others, you should know, are living in the city of Thebes. Those of us who are here, father — if anything needs doing, tell us, and we will do it.

Heracles. Then listen. Here is the work. You have come to where you will show what kind of man you are, who are called my son.

It was foretold me by my father long ago that I would die by nothing that draws breath, but by someone already dead, a dweller in Hades. So this beast, the Centaur, exactly as the god foretold it, has killed me alive, being dead.

And I will show you fresh oracles that match those, and agree with the old ones. I went into the grove of the Selli, the men who live on the mountain and sleep on the ground, and wrote them down from my father's oak, the oak that speaks with many tongues. It told me that at the time which is alive and here now, my release from the labours laid on me would be completed. And I thought that meant I would do well. It meant nothing but this: that I would die. The dead have no more labours.

So since all this is coming out clearly, child, you must be this man's ally again, and not wait until my tongue gets sharp; give way of your own accord and work with me. Find the finest law there is: obey your father.

Hyllus. Father — I am frightened to have come to this point in what we are saying. But I will do what you decide.

Heracles. First of all, put your right hand in mine.

Hyllus. Why do you press this pledge on me so hard?

Heracles. Give it. Now. Do not distrust me.

Hyllus. There. I hold it out. I will not refuse you anything.

Heracles. Now swear, by the head of Zeus who fathered me —

Hyllus. Swear to do what? Will you tell me that too?

Heracles. — to carry out for me the task I name.

Hyllus. I swear it. Zeus is my witness.

Heracles. And pray that if you break it, you suffer for it.

Hyllus. I will not suffer for it, because I will do it. But I pray it all the same.

Heracles. Do you know the peak of Oeta, the mountain of Zeus?

Hyllus. I know it. I have stood up there many times to sacrifice.

Heracles. Then you must lift my body and carry it there — with your own hands, and with whatever friends you want. Cut a great pile of wood from the deep-rooted oak, and cut down a great deal of the male wild olive with it, and lay my body on top, and take a flaming pine torch and set it alight. And let no tear of mourning come into it. Do it without a groan and without a tear, if you are this man's son. If you do not, I will be waiting for you, even from under the earth — a curse on you for ever, and a heavy one.

Hyllus. Oh, father — what have you said? What have you done to me?

Heracles. What has to be done. Otherwise, get yourself some other father, and never be called my son again.

Hyllus. Oh — again, oh. What are you calling on me to be, father? Your murderer, with your blood on my hands?

Heracles. Not that. The healer of what I have — the one doctor for my pain.

Hyllus. And how do I heal your body by setting fire to it?

Heracles. If you are afraid of that part, do the rest.

Hyllus. You will not be refused the carrying, at any rate.

Heracles. And building the pyre I have described?

Hyllus. So long as I do not touch it with my own hands. The rest I will do; my part will not fall short.

Heracles. That will be enough. Then add one small favour to the large ones.

Hyllus. Even if it is very large, it will be done.

Heracles. You know the girl, Eurytus's daughter?

Hyllus. You mean Iole, I would guess.

Heracles. You know her. This is what I lay on you, child. When I am dead — if you want to do what is right by the gods, remembering the oath you swore your father — take her as your wife. Do not refuse your father. Let no other man ever have her, who has lain at my side — no one but you. Make that marriage yours, my son. Do as I say. You have trusted me in great things; to distrust me in small ones wipes out the thanks you earned before.

Hyllus. Oh. It is wrong to be angry with a sick man. But who could bear to see him think like this?

Heracles. You talk as if you meant to do nothing I say.

Hyllus. Who would? She is the one to blame — the one cause of my mother's death, and of your being as you are. Who would choose that, unless he was sick with avenging spirits? Better that I die too, father, than live in the same house with the people I hate most.

Heracles. This man, it seems, will not give me what is due to a dying man. Well — the curse of the gods will wait for you, if you disobey what I say.

Hyllus. Oh — you will soon show how sick you are, it seems.

Heracles. Yes, because you are stirring up the evil that had gone to sleep.

Hyllus. I am wretched. There is no way out, anywhere I turn.

Heracles. Because you do not think it right to listen to the man who fathered you.

Hyllus. Then am I to be taught to offend the gods, father?

Heracles. It does not offend the gods, if it gladdens my heart.

Hyllus. So you order me to do this, as wholly just?

Heracles. I do. I call the gods to witness it.

Hyllus. Then I will do it. I will not refuse — and I show the gods that the deed is yours. I could never turn out bad by trusting you, father.

Heracles. You finish well. And add the favour quickly, my son: put me on the pyre before some spasm or sting comes over me again. Come — hurry — lift me. This is the rest from evils. This is the last end of this man.

Hyllus. Nothing stops it being done for you, since you command it and force it on me, father.

Heracles. Come, then — before you wake this sickness again, my hard soul, set a bit of steel in my mouth, stone fitted to stone, and stop my crying. You are finishing a task you did not want, and it is a joy.

Hyllus. Lift him, men. Grant me great forgiveness for this, and know the great unfeelingness of the gods in what is being done — they who beget children and are called fathers, and look on at suffering like this. Nobody sees what is to come. What is here now is pitiful for us, and shameful for them, and hardest of all men for the one who is bearing this ruin.

And you, girl — do not stay behind at the house either. You have seen great deaths, fresh ones, and many sufferings never suffered before, and there is nothing in any of it that is not Zeus.

Electra (c. 410 BC)

Electra

(Mycenae. Before the palace of the house of Pelops, at first light. The Tutor, an old man, comes in with Orestes and Pylades.)

Tutor. Son of Agamemnon, who once commanded the army at Troy — now you can look with your own eyes at what you have always longed for. This is the ancient Argos you were homesick for, the sacred ground of Io, Inachus' daughter, whom the gadfly stung and drove across the world. And this, Orestes, is the Lycean marketplace, named for Apollo the wolf-killer. There on the left is Hera's famous temple. And the place we have come to — call it Mycenae, rich in gold, that you are looking at, and this the house of Pelops' line, where so many have been killed. It was from here, when your father was murdered, that I took you from your sister, your own blood, and carried you off and saved you and raised you to the age you are now, to avenge your father's murder.

So now, Orestes — and you, Pylades, dearest of friends from abroad — we have to decide quickly what to do. Already the sun's bright light is stirring the birds into their clear morning songs, and the black night of stars is gone. Before any man comes out of that house, put your heads together. We are at the point where there is no more time for hesitating. It is the moment to act.

Orestes. Dearest of all the men who serve me — how clearly you show what a loyal man you are to our house. A thoroughbred horse, even when he is old, does not lose heart in danger; he pricks up his ears. That is you. You drive us on, and you follow in the front rank yourself. So I will tell you what I have decided. Listen sharply, and if I miss the mark anywhere, put me right.

When I went to Apollo's oracle at Delphi to learn how I should take justice for my father from the men who killed him, Apollo gave me this answer — you will hear it now: that I myself, with no armour and no army, should use cunning to steal the just killing that my hand owes. So, since that is the oracle we heard — you go into that house, when the moment lets you in, and find out everything that is going on in there, so that you can come back and tell us for certain. They will never know you, after so many years and at your age; nobody will suspect a man with hair as white as yours. Your story is this: you are a stranger from Phocis, sent by Phanoteus — he is the greatest ally they have. Tell them, and swear to it, that Orestes is dead — an accident he could not escape, thrown from a racing chariot at the Pythian games. Let that be the story, and let it stand.

Orestes. Meanwhile the two of us will go first to my father's tomb, as the god ordered, and crown it with poured wine and a cut lock of hair. Then we will come back here, carrying in our hands the bronze urn — you know the one; it is hidden in the bushes — so that we can bring them, by a lie, the sweet news that this body of mine is gone, burned already and turned to ash. What does it cost me? Dead in words, and in fact alive, and carrying off the glory. I think no word is a bad omen if it brings gain. I have seen it often enough: clever men who died in rumour, and then came home, and were honoured more than before. So I am confident that this report will see me alive as well, and blazing down on my enemies like a star.

Land of my fathers, and gods of this country: receive me, and let this journey go well. And you, house of my fathers — I come to cleanse you, in justice, sent on my way by the gods. Do not send me out of this land stripped of honour. Let me hold the wealth that was mine and set my house in order.

That is all I have to say. Old man, go now, and see to the task you have been given. The two of us will be off. It is the moment — and the moment is the greatest master of any work that men undertake.

(From inside the house, a cry.)

Electra. Oh — oh, misery.

Tutor. Listen, my boy. I think I heard one of the servant women moaning inside, behind the doors.

Orestes. Could it be Electra? Poor Electra. Do you want us to stay here and listen to her crying?

Tutor. No. Nothing before Apollo's orders. Let us set out to do those and make our beginning from them — pouring the offerings at your father's grave. That is what brings victory into our hands, and mastery over everything we do.

(The three go out, the Tutor one way and Orestes and Pylades the other. Electra comes out of the house.)

Electra.
	Pure light,
	and air, that shares the earth with the light —
	how many songs of grief you have heard from me,
	how many blows struck full on this breast
	until it bled,
	whenever the dark night lets go.
	And my hateful bed in that house of suffering
	knows my night-long vigils by now:
	how much I mourn my wretched father.
	In a foreign land the bloody god of war
	did not take him in as a guest.
	No — my mother, and the man who shares her bed,
	Aegisthus, split his head with a murdering axe,
	the way woodcutters split an oak.
	And for that no one grieves but me.
	No cry of pity from any lips but mine,
	for you, father, dead
	so shamefully, so pitifully.
Electra.
	But I will not stop —
	not the grieving, not the bitter crying —
	as long as I see the stars flash,
	as long as I see this daylight.
	Like the nightingale, who killed her own child,
	I will cry out in front of my father's doors
	so that everyone hears.
	House of Hades and Persephone,
	Hermes of the underworld, and lady Curse,
	and you dread Furies, daughters of the gods,
	who see the people unjustly killed,
	who see the marriage-beds stolen in secret —
	come. Help me. Take vengeance
	for my father's murder,
	and send me my brother.
	Alone I have no strength left
	to carry the weight of grief that drags me down.

(The Chorus comes in: women of Mycenae.)

Chorus.
	Electra — child of the most wretched of mothers —
	why are you always wasting away like this
	in a mourning that never has enough,
	for Agamemnon, caught long ago,
	godlessly, in your treacherous mother's snare,
	and betrayed to a wicked hand?
	May whoever did it die —
	if I may say such a thing without offending the gods.
Electra.
	Daughters of noble houses,
	you have come to comfort me in my trouble.
	I know it. I understand it. None of it escapes me.
	But I will not give this up.
	I will not stop mourning my poor father.
	You who return my love with every kind of love —
	let me go on raving like this.
	Aiai. I beg you.
Chorus.
	But you will never raise your father
	out of the lake of Hades, where everyone goes,
	not by crying and not by prayers.
	You go past what can be borne into a grief
	that has no cure, and you groan yourself to death,
	and in all of it there is no release from evil.
	Why do you reach for what cannot be carried?
Electra.
	Only a fool forgets a parent
	who died pitifully.
	No — my heart is set on the bird of grief,
	who cries Itys, always Itys,
	frantic with mourning, Zeus's messenger.
	And you, Niobe, who suffered everything,
	I count you a god,
	weeping for ever in your tomb of rock.
Chorus.
	Not to you alone, child,
	of all people has grief come,
	yet you take it harder than the others in the house,
	your own family, born of the same blood.
	Chrysothemis lives, and Iphianassa,
	and he lives too, hidden away from grief
	in his youth, a fortunate boy,
	whom the famous land of Mycenae
	will one day welcome home as a nobleman's son,
	when Zeus guides his step back to this land —
	Orestes.
Electra.
	Yes, and I wait for him without tiring —
	childless, wretched, unmarried, always walking my road
	soaked in tears, carrying a fate of evils
	that never ends. And he forgets
	what he suffered and what he learned.
	What message comes to me
	that does not turn out a lie?
	He is always longing to come,
	and for all his longing he never thinks it worth appearing.
Chorus.
	Courage, child. Take courage.
	Zeus is still great in the sky;
	he watches everything and rules it.
	Hand your too-painful anger over to him.
	Do not hate the people you hate too much,
	and do not forget them either.
	Time is a god who makes things easy.
	Agamemnon's son, on the cattle-grazed
	shore at Crisa, has not turned his back on you,
	and neither has the god who rules beside Acheron.
Electra.
	But most of my life has gone already
	without hope, and I have no strength left.
	I am wasting away without children.
	No man who loves me stands up for me.
	Like some worthless foreigner in the house
	I keep my father's rooms,
	dressed like this, in rags,
	standing at tables with nothing on them.
Chorus.
	Pitiful was the cry at his homecoming,
	pitiful the cry in your father's banquet hall
	when the blow of the all-bronze axe
	swung straight at him.
	Cunning planned it. Lust did the killing.
	Between them they bred a terrible shape, terribly —
	whether it was a god or a mortal
	who did the thing.
Electra.
	That day — of all days
	the one I hate most.
	That night, the horror of it,
	the feast no one can speak of,
	where my father saw shameful death
	come from two hands —
	the hands that took my life and betrayed it,
	the hands that destroyed me.
	May the great god of Olympus
	give them suffering to pay for it,
	and may they never enjoy their splendour,
	the pair who did such things.
Chorus.
	Take care. Say no more.
	Do you not understand how you are falling,
	so shamefully, from where you stand
	into ruin of your own making?
	You have earned yourself far more trouble than you needed,
	breeding wars, always, in your sullen soul.
	And you cannot fight the powerful
	and expect to win.
Electra.
	I was forced to it by terrible things — terrible things.
	I know. My anger does not escape me.
	But in the middle of terrors I will not hold back
	this ruin, as long as life is in me.
	Who, dear women, who could ever say
	a fitting word to me — who, with any sense of what is right?
	Let me be. Let me be, you comforters.
	This will be called a thing without a cure,
	and I will never rest from my labours,
	my cries beyond counting.
Chorus.
	I say it out of goodwill,
	the way a mother you could trust would say it:
	do not breed ruin on ruin.
Electra.
	And what is the measure of evil? Tell me —
	how can it be right to forget the dead?
	Where in the human race did that grow?
	May I never be honoured by such people,
	and if I ever live with anything good
	may I not live at ease,
	holding back the wings of my shrill grief
	and cheating my father of his honours.
	Because if the dead man lies there, poor wretch,
	dust and nothing,
	and they do not pay back in return
	blood for blood, in justice,
	then shame is gone, and reverence for the gods
	is gone, from the whole human race.

Chorus. Child, I came here as much for your sake as for my own. But if what I say is wrong, you win. We will follow you.

Electra. I am ashamed, women, if you think I go too far with all this grieving. But it is force that makes me do it — forgive me. How could any woman of good birth not do it, seeing what has happened to her father, when I see it night and day, and not fading but growing?

First, my own mother, who gave birth to me, has become my worst enemy. Then, in my own house, I live with my father's murderers. They rule me. It is from them that I get what I get, and it is from them that I go without.

And then what kind of days do you think I have — when I see Aegisthus sitting on my father's throne, when I see him wearing the same clothes my father wore, pouring wine at the hearth where he killed him; and when I see the crowning outrage of it all, the murderer in my father's bed beside my wretched mother — if I must call her mother, when she sleeps with that man. She is so shameless that she lives with the man who stained her, with no fear of any Fury. No — as if she were laughing at what she did, she found the day on which she killed my father by treachery, and on that day she sets up dances, and every month she slaughters sheep to the gods who saved her.

Electra. And I watch it — I, the ill-fated one — and I weep in the house, and waste away, and wail over the accursed feast that is named after my father, alone with myself. I cannot even cry as much as my heart would like. Because this lady — so noble, in words — shouts abuse at me like this: "You godless, hateful thing. Is your father the only one who ever died? Is no one else in the world in mourning? Damn you — and may the gods below never release you from your crying." That is her outrage. Except when she hears from someone that Orestes is coming. Then she goes wild, and stands over me and screams: "Is this not your doing? Is this not your work — you who stole Orestes out of my hands and smuggled him away? You will pay for it, and you will pay in full." She barks like that, and right beside her, egging her on, is her glorious bridegroom, the same words in his mouth — that complete coward, that walking disaster, the man who fights his battles with women at his side.

And I wait, always, for Orestes to come and put an end to it, and I am dying while I wait. He is always about to do something, and it has destroyed every hope I have, the ones I had and the ones I might have had. In a life like this, friends, there is no room for self-control or for reverence. In evil times there is a great need to do evil too.

Chorus. Tell us — is Aegisthus near, while you say this to us, or has he left the house?

Electra. He is gone. Do not imagine I would be out of doors if he were near. He is in the country now.

Chorus. Then I could speak to you more boldly, if that is how things stand?

Electra. He is not here. Ask what you like.

Chorus. Then I ask you: what of your brother? Is he coming, or is he putting it off? I want to know.

Electra. He says he is coming. He says it, and does none of what he says.

Chorus. A man will hesitate, when the thing he is doing is a great one.

Electra. I did not hesitate when I saved him.

Chorus. Take heart. He is of good blood. He will stand by his own.

Electra. I believe it. Otherwise I would not have lived this long.

Chorus. Say no more now. I see your sister coming out of the house — Chrysothemis, your father's daughter and your mother's — with offerings for the grave in her hands, the kind that custom sends down to the dead.

(Chrysothemis comes out of the house.)

Chrysothemis. What is it this time, sister? What speech have you come out to the doors to make in public? Will you never learn, in all this time, to stop feeding a useless temper with empty words? I know this much about myself: I hate what is happening. If I had the strength, I would show them what I think of them. But as things are, in bad times, I think it best to sail with the sail lowered, and not to look as if I am doing something when I cannot hurt them. I wish you would do the same. And yet — the right is on your side, not on mine. But if I am to live as a free woman, then the people in power have to be obeyed in everything.

Electra. It is a terrible thing that you, the daughter of the father you were born to, can forget him and care about your mother. Every lecture you give me was taught to you by her. Not a word of it is your own. Then choose one or the other: be a fool, or be sensible and forget the people you owe. You said just now that if you had the strength you would show your hatred of them — and when I do everything I can to avenge our father, you do not help me. You try to turn me aside from doing it. Is that not cowardice on top of everything else?

Explain to me — or learn it from me — what I would gain by stopping this grieving. Am I not alive? Badly, I know, but well enough for me. And I hurt them, and that is an honour paid to the dead man, if there is any pleasure where he is. But you, who hate them — you hate in words, while in fact you live with our father's murderers. I would never give in to them, not if someone were to bring me the gifts you preen yourself on now. Keep your rich table and your overflowing life. My food is this: not to hurt myself. I have no wish for the honour you have — and neither would you, if you had any self-respect. As it is, when you could be called the daughter of the best father there ever was, be called your mother's. That way most people will see you for what you are — a traitor to your dead father and your own people.

Chorus. Nothing in anger, for the gods' sake. There is gain for both of you in what has been said, if you would learn to use her words, and she yours.

Chrysothemis. Women, I am used to what she says, more or less. I would never have mentioned this at all if I had not heard that the greatest evil of all is coming for her — the thing that will put an end to her long grieving.

Electra. Then say it — this terror. If you can tell me anything worse than what I have now, I will not argue with you any more.

Chrysothemis. I will tell you everything I know. If you do not stop this grieving, they mean to send you where you will never see the light of the sun again — alive, in a covered cell, outside this country, singing your own miseries. Think about that, and do not blame me afterwards when it happens to you. Now is the time to be sensible.

Electra. They have really decided to do that to me?

Chrysothemis. They have. As soon as Aegisthus comes home.

Electra. Then let him come quickly, if that is what he is bringing.

Chrysothemis. Poor girl. What curse are you calling down on yourself?

Electra. That he should come — if he means to do any of that.

Chrysothemis. So that what will happen to you? Where are your wits?

Electra. So that I can get as far away from all of you as I can.

Chrysothemis. Do you give no thought to the life you have?

Electra. My life is a beautiful thing. A wonder to look at.

Chrysothemis. It would be, if you knew how to be sensible.

Electra. Do not teach me to betray my own people.

Chrysothemis. I am not. I am teaching you to give way to the people in power.

Electra. You do the flattering. That is not my way.

Chrysothemis. It is a fine thing, all the same, not to fall through stupidity.

Electra. We will fall, if we must, avenging our father.

Chrysothemis. Our father forgives me for this. I know he does.

Electra. Those are words only cowards would praise.

Chrysothemis. So you will not be persuaded? You will not agree with me?

Electra. No. I hope I am never that empty of sense.

Chrysothemis. Then I will go where I was sent.

Electra. Where are you going? Who are these offerings for?

Chrysothemis. Mother is sending me to pour them on our father's grave.

Electra. What did you say? For the man she hated most in the world?

Chrysothemis. The man she killed herself. That is what you want to say.

Electra. Which of her friends talked her into it? Whose idea was it?

Chrysothemis. Something frightened her in the night, I think.

Electra. Gods of my father — stand with me now, at last.

Chrysothemis. Do you find something to hope for in her fright?

Electra. Tell me the dream, and then I can answer you.

Chrysothemis. I only know a little of it.

Electra. Then tell me that. A few words have often brought people down before now, and set them on their feet.

Chrysothemis. The story is that she saw your father and mine come back to the light, and stand beside her a second time. Then he took the sceptre he used to carry himself — the one Aegisthus carries now — and planted it at the hearth; and from it a branch grew up, thick with leaves, and its shade covered the whole land of Mycenae. That is what I heard from someone who was there when she told her dream to the Sun. More than that I do not know — only that she is sending me because of this fear of hers. So I beg you, by the gods of our family, listen to me, and do not fall through stupidity. If you push me away now, you will come looking for me later, in trouble.

Electra. Dear sister — do not let one thing you are holding touch that tomb. It is not lawful, it is not holy, for you to set grave-gifts or pour offerings to our father from the woman who hated him. Throw them to the winds, or dig a deep hole and bury them in the dust, where none of it can ever come near our father's resting place. Let it be kept as treasure for her underground, when she dies.

If she were not the most shameless woman ever born, she would never have thought of pouring these hateful offerings to the man she killed. Ask yourself whether the dead man in his grave is likely to receive her gifts with any love — she, by whose hand he died dishonoured, hacked about like an enemy, his hands and feet cut off and strung under his armpits, and who wiped the bloodstains off on his head, to wash herself clean. Do you really think what you are carrying will absolve her of the murder? It cannot.

No. Put those things away. Cut a lock from the ends of your hair, and one from mine — I am wretched, and this is little, but it is what I have — and give him this hair, which has no oil on it, and this belt of mine, with no ornament on it. Then kneel and pray that he himself will come up from the ground, kind to us, to help us against our enemies; and that his son Orestes lives, and will set his foot on our enemies with the upper hand, so that from now on we can crown his tomb with richer hands than we bring him now. I think — yes, I think he too had some part in sending her these dreams she did not want to see. Still, sister, do this. Help yourself, and help me, and help the man we love most of all, who lies in Hades — the father we share.

Chorus. The girl speaks with reverence. And you, dear friend, will do as she says, if you have any sense.

Chrysothemis. I will do it. When a thing is right there is no reason for two people to argue about it; the only sense is to hurry and do it. But while I am trying to do this, friends — by the gods, keep silent about it. If my mother hears of it, I think I will pay a bitter price for daring this.

(She goes out towards the tomb.)

Chorus.
	If I am not a prophet gone wrong,
	if my judgement has not lost its wisdom,
	then Justice is coming, who sent this sign ahead of her,
	carrying just victory in her hands.
	She will come after them, child, and not long from now.
	There is courage in me
	since I heard, just now,
	of that dream, and the sweet breath of it.
	Your father never forgets, the lord of the Greeks;
	and the old axe never forgets,
	bronze-struck, two-edged,
	that cut him down in the most shameful outrage.
Chorus.
	She is coming — many feet, many hands,
	hidden in terrible ambush,
	the Fury with feet of bronze.
	Because they went at it, that pair,
	a marriage that was no marriage, no wedding,
	a contest of lust polluted with murder,
	which the gods' law forbade them.
	And so I am sure
	that never, never will this portent come near
	the doers and their helpers
	without blame. For there is no prophecy for mortals
	in terrible dreams or in oracles
	if this vision of the night does not come safely to shore.
Chorus.
	Pelops, that chariot-race of yours long ago,
	so full of pain —
	how it has come down, unending,
	on this land.
	Ever since Myrtilus, the charioteer who let you win,
	was put to sleep in the sea,
	thrown out root and branch
	from the golden chariot,
	a wretched outrage —
	never once since then
	has outrage, full of pain,
	left this house.

(Clytemnestra comes out of the house, with servants.)

Clytemnestra. Loose again, it seems, and roaming about — because Aegisthus is not here, and he at least always kept you from going out of doors and shaming your family. But now that he is away, you take no notice of me. And yet you have told a great many people, often, that I am reckless and rule beyond what justice allows, and that I outrage you and everything that is yours. I do no outrage. I speak ill of you because I hear ill from you, constantly.

Your father — that and nothing else is your excuse, always — died at my hands. At my hands. I know it perfectly well. I do not deny it. Because it was Justice that took him, not I alone — and you should have been on Justice's side, if you had any sense. This father of yours, whom you never stop mourning, was the one man of all the Greeks who could bring himself to sacrifice your sister, your own blood, to the gods — he who had none of the pain in sowing her that I had in giving birth.

Well then. Tell me: for whose sake did he sacrifice her? For the Argives, you will say? They had no right to kill a daughter of mine. Or was it for his brother Menelaus that he killed my child — and was he not going to pay me for that? Did Menelaus not have two children of his own, who ought to have died before mine, seeing that their father and mother were the reason for that voyage in the first place? Did Hades have some special appetite to feast on my children rather than hers? Or had that accursed father lost all love for the children I bore him, and kept it for the children of Menelaus? Is that not the mark of a thoughtless father, and a bad one? I think so, even if you judge it differently. And the dead girl would say so too, if she could find a voice.

So I feel no regret over what was done. And if you think I am wrong, get a just judgement of your own before you blame the people around you.

Electra. You will not say, this time at least, that I started it — that I said something to hurt you first and then got this from you in return. But if you will let me, I would like to say what is right on behalf of the dead man, and of my sister with him.

Clytemnestra. I will let you. And if you always opened with me in that tone, you would not be so painful to listen to.

Electra. Then here it is. You say you killed my father. Is there any admission more shameful than that, whether you were right to do it or not? But I will show you that you had no right. It was persuasion that dragged you into it — the persuasion of a bad man, the one you live with now.

Ask Artemis the huntress what she was punishing when she held all those winds at Aulis. Or I will tell you, since we are not allowed to learn it from her. My father once, as I hear it, was out hunting in the goddess's grove, and his footfall startled a dappled stag with antlers, and he killed it, and over the kill he let some boast fall. Leto's daughter was angry at that, and she held the Greeks there until my father should sacrifice his own daughter to balance the beast. That is how she came to be sacrificed. There was no other release for the army, not homeward and not to Troy. So he was forced to it, and fought against it, and at last, barely, he killed her — and not for the sake of Menelaus.

But suppose — I will make your case for you — suppose he did it because he wanted to help his brother. Did he have to die for that, at your hands? Under what law? Be careful. If you lay that law down for people, you may be laying down pain and regret for yourself. Because if we are going to kill one person for another, you would be the first to die, if you got justice.

So look at whether this excuse of yours is any excuse at all. Tell me, if you will, what you are paying back now by doing the most shameful things of all: sleeping with the man whose hands are bloody, the man you first destroyed my father with, and having children by him — while the children you had before, born in decency from a decent marriage, you have thrown out. How am I supposed to approve of that? Or will you say this too is payment for your daughter? A shameful thing to say, if you do say it. There is nothing fine about marrying an enemy for a daughter's sake.

But there is no arguing with you. You let your whole tongue loose about how I abuse my mother. And I count you less a mother than a master over me, given the miserable life I lead, always in the troubles that come from you and from the man you keep. And the other one, out there, who barely escaped your hand — poor Orestes — grinds out an unhappy life. You have accused me many times of raising him as the avenger against you. And I would have, believe me, if I had had the strength. So on that account, proclaim me to everyone, if you like: bad, loud-mouthed, full of shamelessness. If I was born with a talent for that kind of thing, then I am not far from doing credit to your nature.

Chorus. I see her breathing rage. But whether she has justice on her side — I no longer see her caring about that.

Clytemnestra. And what kind of care do I owe to her, when she has done such outrage to the mother who bore her — and at her age? Do you not think she would go to any length, with no shame at all?

Electra. Understand this: I am ashamed of it, whatever you think. I know that what I do is unfit for my age and unlike me. But your hatred and your actions force me to it, against my will. Shameful behaviour is taught by shameful behaviour.

Clytemnestra. You shameless creature. I and my words and my actions give you far too much to say.

Electra. It is you who say it, not me. You do the deed, and the deeds find their own words.

Clytemnestra. Well, by our lady Artemis, you will not get away with this boldness once Aegisthus comes home.

Electra. You see? You are carried off into a rage. You let me say what I liked, and you have no idea how to listen.

Clytemnestra. Will you not even let me make my sacrifice without your ill-omened shouting, now that I have let you say everything?

Electra. I let you. I urge you. Sacrifice. And do not blame my mouth, because I will not say another word.

Clytemnestra. (to the attendant) You there — lift up the offerings, the fruits of every kind, so that I can raise my prayers to this lord whose statue stands at the door, prayers to be released from the fears I have now.

Apollo, guardian of this house, hear me now, though my words are hidden. I am not among friends when I speak, and it is not fitting to unfold all of it to the light with her standing at my elbow, or she will take her spite and her chattering tongue and sow an idle story through the whole city. So hear me like this. This is how I will say it.

The visions I saw last night, in dreams that could be read two ways — if they came to me for good, Lord Apollo, grant that they are fulfilled; if for harm, throw them back on the people who wish me harm. And if anyone is scheming to throw me out, by trickery, of the wealth I have now, do not allow it. Let me go on living as I do, unharmed, holding the house of the sons of Atreus and this sceptre; living with the people I live with now, in good days, and with those of my children who bear me no ill will and no bitter grudge. Lord Apollo, hear this kindly and give it to us all, just as we ask. And everything else, though I say nothing of it — you are a god, and I take it you know. The children of Zeus, I suppose, see everything.

(The Tutor comes in, as a traveller from Phocis.)

Tutor. Ladies — I am a stranger here — how can I be sure that this is the house of the king, Aegisthus?

Chorus. This is it, stranger. You have guessed right.

Tutor. And am I right again in guessing that this lady is his wife? She has the look of a queen.

Chorus. Right in every way. That is she, in front of you.

Tutor. Greetings, my queen. I come bringing you sweet news, and Aegisthus too, from a friend.

Clytemnestra. I accept the greeting. But first I want to know from you who it was who sent you.

Tutor. Phanoteus of Phocis, on a matter of weight.

Clytemnestra. What matter, stranger? Tell me. You come from a friend, so I know the news you bring will be friendly.

Tutor. Orestes is dead. That is the short of it.

Electra. Oh — no. I am finished. This is the day.

Clytemnestra. What did you say? What did you say, stranger? Do not listen to her.

Tutor. I said it and I say it again. Orestes is dead.

Electra. I am destroyed. I am nothing any more.

Clytemnestra. You — mind your own business. You, stranger, tell me the truth. How did he die?

Tutor. That is what I was sent for, and I will tell you all of it.

He went to the great showplace of Greece, to compete for the prizes at Delphi. When he heard the herald call out in his high voice for the foot race, which is the first event to be judged, he came in shining, and every man there was in awe of him. He ran the course from the start to the finish and came out with the prize of victory, and all the honour that goes with it. To tell you a little where there is a great deal to tell — I never knew a man to do what that man did, or win as he won. But know this one thing: in every contest the judges announced, the sprints, the double course, the pentathlon with its five events, he carried off all the victories and was called a happy man, proclaimed each time as an Argive, Orestes by name, son of Agamemnon, who once gathered the famous army of Greece.

So far, so good. But when a god does harm, not even a strong man can escape it. Another day, at sunrise, came the fast race of the horses, and he entered it with many charioteers. One was an Achaean; one was from Sparta; two were Libyans, masters of yoked cars; and he among them, driving Thessalian mares, was the fifth. The sixth was from Aetolia, with chestnut colts; the seventh was a man from Magnesia; the eighth, with white horses, was of the Aenian people; the ninth was from Athens, the city the gods built; and a Boeotian was another, filling the tenth chariot.

They took their places where the appointed judges had set them by lot and ranged the cars, and at the bronze trumpet they were off. All of them shouted at their horses together and shook the reins in their hands; the whole course filled with the crash of rattling chariots; the dust went up. They were all packed together, none of them sparing the goad, each trying to get past the others' wheel-hubs and their snorting horses; for the breath of the horses was foaming all round their backs and round the rolling wheels, and breaking over them.

Orestes, keeping right under the pillar at the end, was grazing it with his wheel-hub every time, giving rein to the trace-horse on the right and holding in the one on the inside. And up to then every chariot was still upright. But then the Aenian's hard-mouthed colts got away with him by force, and coming out of the turn, finishing the sixth lap and beginning the seventh, they dashed their foreheads against the chariot from Barca. And from there, out of that one accident, one car smashed into another and went over, and the whole plain of Crisa filled up with the wreckage of chariots.

Tutor. The clever driver from Athens saw it and pulled his horses out wide and held back, letting the wave of horses go by that was churning in the middle. Orestes was driving last, holding his colts back, trusting the finish. But when he sees that only one man is left in against him, he sends a sharp cry through the ears of his fast colts and goes after him. They brought their yokes level and drove, first one man, then the other, showing his head in front of the other's car.

And all the other laps the poor man got through safely, upright in an upright chariot. Then, letting out the left rein while the horse was turning, he struck the edge of the pillar without knowing it. He broke the axle-box in the middle and slid over the rail and was tangled in the cut reins; and as he fell to the ground his colts scattered into the middle of the course.

When the crowd saw him thrown from his chariot, they cried out for the young man — what deeds he had done, and what he had drawn instead — dragged along the ground, now with his legs showing against the sky, until the charioteers, with difficulty, got the running horses under control and cut him loose, so bloody that not one of his friends, seeing him, would have known the pitiful body. They burned him on a pyre at once, and chosen men of Phocis are bringing that mighty frame, now a wretched handful of ash, in a small bronze urn, so that he can have his share of a tomb in his father's country.

That is my story. It is painful even as a story; to those who saw it, as we saw it, it was the worst thing my eyes have ever looked on.

Chorus. Oh, oh. So the whole line of our old masters is destroyed, it seems, root and all.

Clytemnestra. Zeus, what is this? Do I call it good luck? Or terrible, but a gain? It is a painful thing, when I save my own life by my own losses.

Tutor. Why so downcast, lady, at what I have told you?

Clytemnestra. There is a terrible power in giving birth. Even when they treat you badly, you cannot hate the ones you bore.

Tutor. Then it seems we came for nothing.

Clytemnestra. Not for nothing. How can you say for nothing, when you came bringing me sure proof of his death? He came out of my own life, and left my breast and my care, and made himself a stranger, an exile. Once he left this country he never saw me again; he charged me with his father's murder and swore he would do terrible things to me. So that neither by night nor by day would sweet sleep cover me; every hour standing over me led me on as a woman about to die. But now — because today I am free of the fear of him, and of her; she was the greater harm, living in my house, drinking my life's blood undiluted, always — now, I think, for all her threats, I will pass my days in peace.

Electra. Oh, no. Now, Orestes, I can cry for what has happened to you, when this is what you have come to and your mother, this woman, outrages you for it. Is it not fine?

Clytemnestra. Not for you. He is fine, as he is.

Electra. Hear her, Nemesis of the newly dead.

Clytemnestra. She heard whom she should, and settled it well.

Electra. Outrage us. Today is your lucky day.

Clytemnestra. Then you and Orestes will not be putting a stop to it.

Electra. We are the ones stopped. We will not be stopping you.

Clytemnestra. Stranger, you would deserve a great reward for your coming, if you have stopped that chattering tongue.

Tutor. Then I will take my leave, if all is well.

Clytemnestra. Certainly not. That would be no welcome worthy of me, or of the friend who sent you. Come inside. Leave her out here to howl her own troubles and the troubles of her friends.

(Clytemnestra and the Tutor go into the house.)

Electra. Did she look to you like a woman in pain and grief, weeping terribly and crying out over her son, poor woman, dead like that? She left laughing. Oh, I am wretched. Orestes, my dearest, your death has destroyed me. You have gone, and torn out of my heart the only hopes I had left — that you would come back one day alive, to avenge our father, and me in my misery. Now where am I to turn? I am alone. Robbed of you, and of my father. Now I must be a slave again among the people I hate most in the world, my father's murderers. Is it not fine for me?

But no. Not for what time is left me. I will not go inside and live under one roof with them. I will lie down here at this gate, with no friend, and let my life wither. And if that weighs on anyone in that house, let them come out and kill me. Killing me is a kindness; living is the pain. I have no wish for life.

Chorus.
	Where are the thunderbolts of Zeus,
	where is the blazing Sun,
	if they look down on this
	and calmly cover it over?
Electra.
	Ah. Aiai.
Chorus.
	Child, why do you weep?
Electra.
	Oh.
Chorus.
	No loud cry. It is ill-omened.
Electra.
	You will kill me.
Chorus.
	How?
Electra.
	If you hold out hope
	for the ones who have plainly gone
	down to Hades, you tread
	the harder on me as I waste away.
Chorus.
	I know of a king, Amphiaraus,
	whom a woman's chain of gold,
	a bribe, trapped and hid underground.
	And now, under the earth —
Electra.
	Ah. Oh.
Chorus.
	he rules, with all his wits alive.
Electra.
	Oh.
Chorus.
	Oh, yes. Because the woman who destroyed him —
Electra.
	was cut down.
Chorus.
	Yes.
Electra.
	I know it, I know it. Someone rose
	to care for the man in his grief.
	For me there is no one now.
	The one who was left
	has been snatched away and gone.
Chorus.
	Wretched, and a wretched lot.
Electra.
	And I know it, too well,
	in a life swept along
	month after month by a flood
	of terrible and hateful things.
Chorus.
	We have seen what you cry for.
Electra.
	Then do not turn me aside
	any longer, since no —
Chorus.
	What do you mean?
Electra.
	— no help is left me
	from the brother I hoped in,
	born of the same noble father.
Chorus.
	Death was born for everyone.
Electra.
	Death like that? Among racing hooves,
	the way that poor man met it,
	tangled in the cutting reins?
Chorus.
	The mangling is past imagining.
Electra.
	How could it not be? In a strange land,
	without my hands to tend him —
Chorus.
	Oh, no.
Electra.
	— he lies buried, given no tomb by me
	and no lament from us.

(Chrysothemis comes in, hurrying.)

Chrysothemis. Dearest sister, joy is driving me. I have dropped all decorum to come at a run. I bring you delight — and rest from the troubles you had before, and groaned under.

Electra. And where would you find help for my sufferings? There is no cure for them anywhere in sight.

Chrysothemis. Orestes is here with us. Know it from my mouth: as plainly as you see me now.

Electra. Have you gone mad, poor girl? Are you laughing at your own troubles and mine?

Chrysothemis. By our father's hearth, I am not saying it to mock you. I am saying he is here, with us two.

Electra. Oh, poor girl. Whose story is this that you have heard and believe so far?

Chrysothemis. Nobody's. I believe it from myself and no one else. I saw the clear signs.

Electra. What proof, poor girl? What did you look at, that warms you with this fever there is no curing?

Chrysothemis. In the gods' name, listen, and then, when you have heard the rest from me, call me sane or a fool.

Electra. Go on, then, if talking gives you pleasure.

Chrysothemis. I will tell you everything I saw. When I came to our father's old tomb, I saw fresh streams of milk running from the top of the mound, and the grave wreathed all round with every flower there is. I was amazed at the sight, and I looked about in case anyone was coming near me. But when I saw that the whole place was quiet, I crept closer to the tomb, and at the edge of the pyre I saw a lock of hair, newly cut.

The moment I saw it — oh, I am wretched — a familiar image struck into my heart, and I felt I was looking at a token of Orestes, of everyone alive the one I love best. I took it up in my hands, and I said nothing ill-omened, but my eyes filled with tears of joy at once. And now, as much as then, I know for certain that this bright gift came from no one but him. Who else has any business there but you and me? I did not do it, that I know. Nor did you — how could you? You cannot even leave this house to worship the gods without paying for it in tears. And our mother's mind does not run to doing such things, and she could not have done them without our knowing. No. These offerings at the tomb are from Orestes.

Take heart, my dear. The same god does not stand by the same people for ever. Ours was hateful to us before; but this day, perhaps, will set the seal on many good things.

Electra. Oh, the folly. How I have been pitying you.

Chrysothemis. What is it? Is what I say not welcome?

Electra. You do not know where on earth you are, or where your mind is wandering.

Chrysothemis. How can I not know what I saw with my own eyes?

Electra. He is dead, poor girl. Your rescue by him is gone. Do not look to him for anything.

Chrysothemis. Oh, no. Who told you that?

Electra. The man who was there beside him when he died.

Chrysothemis. And where is he? I am beginning to feel very strange.

Electra. In the house — a welcome guest, and not at all unpleasant to our mother.

Chrysothemis. Oh, no. Then whose were all those offerings at our father's tomb?

Electra. Most likely, I think, someone left them there as a memorial to the dead Orestes.

Chrysothemis. Oh, this is wretched. And I came hurrying here with news like that, with joy in it, not knowing after all where our ruin stood. And now that I have come, I find the old troubles, and new ones on top of them.

Electra. That is how it stands with you. But if you will listen to me, you will lift the weight of the trouble we have now.

Chrysothemis. Am I going to raise the dead to life some day?

Electra. That is not what I said. I was not born that much of a fool.

Chrysothemis. Then what are you telling me to do, that I am able to do?

Electra. To have the courage to do what I advise.

Chrysothemis. If there is any good in it, I will not refuse.

Electra. Look — nothing succeeds without hard work.

Chrysothemis. I know. I will carry my share of it, as far as I have strength.

Electra. Then hear how I have decided to act. You know as well as I do that we have no friends standing by us: Hades has taken them and robbed us, and we two are left alone. As long as I heard that my brother was alive and thriving, I had hopes that he would come one day to exact payment for our father's murder. But now that he is gone, I look to you. Do not flinch from helping me, your sister, to kill the man who did the murder with his own hand — Aegisthus. I have no need to hide anything from you any more.

Because what are you waiting for, doing nothing? What hope is there still standing that you can look to? You have reason to groan that you have been robbed of your father's wealth; you have reason to grieve that you have come this far in years unmarried, without a bridal song. And do not go on hoping you will ever get those things. Aegisthus is not such a fool as to let a child of yours, or of mine, ever grow up to be a plain danger to him. But if you follow my plan, first you will earn the name of piety from our dead father below, and from our brother with him. And then you will be called free for the rest of your life, as you were born, and you will find a marriage worthy of you; because everyone likes to look at what is good.

And the fame of it. Do you not see how much glory you will bring on yourself and on me by listening to me? What citizen or stranger, seeing us, will not greet us with praise like this: "Look at these two sisters, friends. They saved their father's house. Their enemies were secure, and they took no thought for their own lives and stood up to do the killing.
