Those two should be loved; those two should be honoured by everybody; at the festivals, and wherever the whole city is gathered, everyone must honour them for their courage." That is what everyone will say of us, alive or dead, so that our glory never fails.

My dear, do as I say. Work alongside our father; share the labour with our brother; put an end to my troubles and to your own. And know this: for people born well, living shamefully is a shame.

Chorus. In matters like this, forethought is an ally to the one who speaks and the one who listens.

Chrysothemis. Yes — and before she opened her mouth, ladies, if she had had a sound mind she would have kept hold of caution, as she has not. Where were your eyes looking, that you arm yourself with boldness like this and call me to serve under you? Can you not see? You were born a woman, not a man, and your arm is weaker than your enemies'. Their fortune prospers day by day; ours is draining away and coming to nothing. Who could plot to bring down a man like that and get clear of ruin unhurt? Take care that, badly as we are doing now, we do not do worse, if anyone hears this talk of yours. It is no release for us and no help to win a fine name and die a disgraceful death. And death is not the worst thing; the worst is to want to die and not be able to get even that.

I beg you, before we are utterly destroyed, both of us, and the family is wiped out — hold back your rage. What you have said I will keep for you unspoken and undone. But you, get some sense at last, and since you have no strength, give way to the people who have the power.

Chorus. Listen to her. There is no better gain a human being can get than foresight and a sensible mind.

Electra. You have said nothing I did not expect. I knew very well you would throw away what I offered. Then the job must be done by my own hand, and by me alone. I will not leave it undone.

Chrysothemis. Oh, if only you had thought like this on the day our father died. You would have carried the whole thing off.

Electra. I was the same by nature. My mind was younger, that is all.

Chrysothemis. Then work at keeping your mind that young for the rest of your life.

Electra. You lecture me like somebody who is not going to help.

Chrysothemis. No. Because the one who puts her hand to a thing like this is likely to come to grief by it.

Electra. I envy you your good sense. Your cowardice I loathe.

Chrysothemis. I will listen just as calmly the day you have something good to say about me.

Electra. You will never have to put up with that from me.

Chrysothemis. There is a long time still to come to decide that in.

Electra. Go away. There is no help in you.

Chrysothemis. There is. It is you who cannot be taught.

Electra. Go and tell your mother all of this.

Chrysothemis. No. I do not hate you as much as that.

Electra. Then at least understand what dishonour you are dragging me down into.

Chrysothemis. Not dishonour. I am thinking ahead for you.

Electra. So I have to follow your idea of what is right?

Chrysothemis. Yes. When you have some sense, you can lead the two of us.

Electra. Terrible, that somebody can talk so well and be so wrong.

Chrysothemis. You have just described exactly the fault you cling to.

Electra. What? You do not think justice is on my side in this?

Chrysothemis. There are times when even justice does harm.

Electra. I do not want to live by those laws.

Chrysothemis. Well, if you go through with it, you will end up agreeing with me.

Electra. I will go through with it. You have not frightened me at all.

Chrysothemis. Is that really true? You will not think it over again?

Electra. No. There is nothing I hate more than bad advice.

Chrysothemis. You seem to take in nothing of what I am saying.

Electra. I made up my mind about this long ago, not today.

Chrysothemis. Then I am going. You cannot bring yourself to approve of what I say, and I cannot approve of the way you behave.

Electra. Go on inside. I will never come after you, however much you may wish I would. It is a great foolishness even to go chasing what is empty.

Chrysothemis. Well, if you think you are being sensible, go on thinking it. When you are already standing in the wreckage, you will agree with what I said.

(Chrysothemis goes into the house.)

Chorus.
	We look up at the birds,
	the wisest of all creatures,
	and see how they take care to feed
	the ones who gave them life and the good of it.
	Why do we not pay the same debt?
	No. By the lightning of Zeus,
	by Themis who sits in the sky,
	the punishment is not long in coming.
	Voice that goes down under the earth to the dead,
	carry a pitiful cry for me
	to the sons of Atreus below,
	a shame no dancing goes with.
Chorus.
	Tell them that the house is sick now, sick to the root;
	that between the children
	a double quarrel has broken out
	and nothing loving levels it any more.
	Betrayed, Electra rides the storm alone,
	miserable, mourning her father without end,
	like the nightingale that cries for everything.
	She takes no thought for dying.
	She is ready to go into the dark
	if she could only bring down the pair
	who are the Fury of this house.
	Who was ever so much her father's child?
Chorus.
	No one born good is willing
	to live badly and shame a fair name,
	and go into the dark without one, my child, my child.
	So you too chose a life
	of nothing but weeping, shared with the dead,
	and armed yourself against dishonour,
	so as to carry two names in a single breath:
	the clever one, and the best of daughters.
Chorus.
	May I see you live as far above your enemies,
	in strength and in wealth,
	as you now live under their hand.
	Because I have found you walking in no good fortune,
	and yet, of the greatest laws there are,
	you carry off the prize,
	by your reverence for Zeus.

(Orestes comes in with Pylades and two attendants. One of them carries a small bronze urn.)

Orestes. Ladies, were we told the way correctly? Is this the right road to where we want to be?

Chorus. What are you looking for? What brings you here?

Orestes. I have been asking for a while where Aegisthus lives.

Chorus. Then you have found it, and whoever told you did nothing wrong.

Orestes. Which of you, then, would let the people inside know that we have arrived — a party they have been waiting for?

Chorus. She will, if it is the nearest of kin who should announce it.

Orestes. Then go in, lady, and tell them that some men from Phocis are asking for Aegisthus.

Electra. Oh, no. No. You are not bringing plain proof of the story we heard?

Orestes. I know nothing of your story. Old Strophius sent me with news about Orestes.

Electra. What news, stranger? The fear is creeping up on me.

Orestes. He is dead. We are carrying what little is left of him, in a small urn, as you see.

Electra. Oh. Then this is it. It is certain now, it seems, and I am looking at it: the weight in your hand.

Orestes. If you are weeping for anything that happened to Orestes, know that this vessel holds his body.

Electra. Stranger, if this urn really holds him, then in the gods' name give it to me, let me take it in my hands, so that I can weep over these ashes — for myself, and for our whole family with me.

Orestes. Bring it and give it to her, whoever she is. She is not asking for it out of any ill will. She is one of his own, or of the same blood.

(The urn is put into Electra's hands.)

Electra. The one thing left to remember him by — the man I loved best in the world — all that is left of Orestes. How far this is from the hopes I had when I sent you away. Now I am lifting you in my hands and you are nothing; and you were shining, child, when I sent you out of this house. I wish I had left this life first, before I stole you away with these two hands and sent you into a strange country and saved you from being murdered — because then you would have been dead that same day, and had your share of our father's tomb beside him. Instead, an exile, far from home, on somebody else's soil, you died miserably, with your sister nowhere near you. These hands that loved you never washed you or laid you out; I never lifted your poor weight, as I should have, out of the fire that burns everything. Strangers tended you, poor boy, and that is how you come back to us: a little heap in a little jar.

Oh, all the care I gave you long ago, and it was for nothing — the work I did for you so often, and it was sweet work. You were never your mother's darling. You were mine. Nobody else in the house nursed you. I did, and you always called me sister. And now in a single day all of it is gone, because you are dead. You have gone like a whirlwind and swept everything away with you. Our father is gone. I am dead, because of you. You yourself are dead and gone. Our enemies are laughing. And our mother, who is no mother, is out of her mind with joy — the woman you used to send me secret messages about, so many times, saying that you would come yourself to punish her. But the bad luck that is yours and mine has snatched all of that away, and sent you back to me like this: instead of the face I loved best, ash and a useless shadow.

Electra. Oh, oh. Poor body. Oh, oh. You were sent on the most terrible road of all, my dearest, and it has destroyed me. It has destroyed me, brother. So take me in with you, into this house of yours — me, nothing, into your nothing — and let me live down there with you from now on. When you were up here I had an equal share in everything with you. Now I want to die and not be shut out of your grave. The dead, as far as I can see, are not in pain.

Chorus. Electra, remember: you are a mortal father's daughter, and Orestes was mortal. So do not grieve too much. Every one of us has this debt to pay.

Orestes. Oh — what am I to say? I am lost. Where do I go for words? I have no strength left to hold my tongue.

Electra. What is hurting you? Why do you say that?

Orestes. Is this the famous Electra? Is this her?

Electra. This is her. And in a very wretched state.

Orestes. Then this is a miserable thing to have happened.

Electra. Stranger, surely it is not me you are groaning over?

Orestes. A body wasted away — dishonoured, godlessly.

Electra. Those ugly words fit nobody but me, stranger.

Orestes. Oh — no husband, and a life as unlucky as this.

Electra. Stranger, why are you looking at me like that, and groaning?

Orestes. Because it seems I knew nothing of my own sorrows.

Electra. What was said that made you see that?

Orestes. The sight of you, marked out by so much pain.

Electra. And you are seeing only a little of what I suffer.

Orestes. How could there be anything uglier to look at than this?

Electra. Because I live in the same house as the murderers.

Orestes. Whose murderers? Where does this evil you are pointing at come from?

Electra. My father's. And on top of that I am their slave, by force.

Orestes. Who is it that forces this on you?

Electra. She is called my mother. She is nothing like a mother.

Orestes. What does she do? Use her hands, or make your life a misery?

Electra. Her hands, and misery, and every kind of evil.

Orestes. And there is nobody to help you, nobody to stop it?

Electra. No. There was one. You have set his ashes in front of me.

Orestes. Poor girl. I have pitied you for a while now, looking at you.

Electra. Then you are the only human being who ever has.

Orestes. Yes, because I am the only one who came here and feels the same pain.

Electra. You are not — you cannot be some kinsman of ours, from somewhere?

Orestes. I would tell you, if these women are on your side.

Electra. They are. You can speak in front of them; they can be trusted.

Orestes. Then put down that urn, and you will learn everything.

Electra. No. In the gods' name, stranger, do not do that to me.

Orestes. Do as I say, and you will not go wrong.

Electra. No, please, by your beard — do not take from me the dearest thing I have.

Orestes. I will not let you keep it.

Electra. Oh, Orestes — this is misery, if I am robbed even of burying you.

Orestes. No ill-omened words. You have no right to grieve.

Electra. No right to grieve for my dead brother?

Orestes. It is not for you to talk about him that way.

Electra. Have I so little claim on the dead?

Orestes. You have every claim. But this is not your business.

Electra. It is, if this is Orestes' body that I am holding.

Orestes. It is not Orestes'. Only a story has dressed it up as his.

(He takes the urn out of her hands.)

Electra. Then where is that poor man's grave?

Orestes. There is none. The living have no grave.

Electra. What did you say, boy?

Orestes. Nothing I am saying is a lie.

Electra. The man is alive?

Orestes. If I am breathing.

Electra. You — are you him?

Orestes. Look at this. Our father's ring. Then tell me whether I speak the truth.

Electra. Oh, light. Oh, dearest light.

Orestes. Dearest. I will bear witness to that.

Electra. Your voice — you have come?

Orestes. Do not ask anyone else about it again.

Electra. I am holding you? In my arms?

Orestes. And may you hold me so for the rest of our lives.

Electra. Dearest women, women of my own city, look — this is Orestes. He was dead by a trick, and now by a trick he is safe and home.

Chorus. We see him, child, and at this turn of your fortune a tear of joy is running out of my eyes.

Electra.
	Oh, child of the man I loved best,
	child of his body, born of him,
	you have come at last,
	you have found, you have reached, you have seen
	the ones you longed for.
Orestes.
	I am here. But keep quiet, and wait.
Electra.
	Why?
Orestes.
	Better to be silent. Someone inside may hear.
Electra.
	No. By Artemis, who is a virgin for ever,
	I will never stoop to fear that —
	the useless weight of women
	shut up in that house all their lives.
Orestes.
	Take care, though. There is a war god in women too.
	You know that well enough, I think. You have felt it.
Electra.
	Oh — oh, oh, oh.
	You have laid it open again,
	the evil that no cloud can cover,
	that can never be undone,
	that can never be forgotten. It is ours.
Orestes.
	I know it too. But when the moment tells us to,
	then is the time to remember what was done.
Electra.
	All of it, all the time there is,
	every hour of it, would be right
	for me to say these things in, and justly.
	It was hard enough to get my mouth free now.
Orestes.
	I agree. So keep it free.
Electra.
	By doing what?
Orestes.
	When it is not the moment, do not want to talk at length.
Electra.
	But who could fairly change
	speech for silence like this,
	when you have appeared?
	I have seen you now,
	past all thought, past all hope.
Orestes.
	You saw me when the gods urged me to come.
Electra.
	Then you have told me of a grace
	higher still than the first,
	if a god brought you to our house.
	I count it the work of heaven.
Orestes.
	I hate to hold back your gladness,
	and I am afraid of too much joy getting the better of you.
Electra.
	Oh — after so long a time
	you thought this road the dearest one to take,
	and came to me. Do not, having seen me in all this pain —
Orestes.
	Do not do what?
Electra.
	Do not rob me of this,
	do not make me give up the joy of your face.
Orestes.
	I would be very angry if I saw anyone else try it.
Electra.
	You agree?
Orestes.
	How could I not?
Electra.
	My friends, I heard a voice
	I could never have hoped to hear,
	and hearing it I could not hold in
	what rose in me — not silent, not without a cry,
	wretch that I am. But now I have you.
	You have come into the light, with the dearest face,
	the face that even in my misery I could never forget.

Orestes. Leave out everything that does not need saying. Do not teach me how wicked our mother is, or how Aegisthus drains our father's property out of this house — pouring some of it away and scattering the rest for nothing. The story would only eat up the time we have. Tell me what fits the moment we are in: where we can show ourselves, or stay hidden, and stop our enemies laughing by this journey of ours. And see to it that our mother does not read your face, all lit up, when the two of us have gone into the house. Keep on mourning, for the disaster in the story that was never true. When we have won, then there will be time to be glad, and to laugh out loud, free.

Electra. Brother, whatever pleases you will please me. The joy I have I took from you; none of it is mine. And I would not accept the smallest hurt to you to win a great gain for myself. That would be no way to serve the power that is with us now.

You know how things stand here — how could you not? You will have heard that Aegisthus is away from the house and our mother is inside. And you need never be afraid that she will see my face light up with laughter. My old hatred of her has melted into me, and now that I have seen you I will never stop weeping — for joy. How could I stop? On one road, on one day, I have seen you dead and seen you alive. What you have done to me is past all reckoning. If my father came walking back to me alive I would no longer call it a miracle; I would believe that I was seeing him. So, since you have come to us by such a road, take the lead yourself, as your heart tells you. Left on my own, I would not have failed at one of two things: I would have saved myself well, or died well.

Orestes. Quiet. I hear someone inside, coming towards the door.

Electra. Go in, strangers — all the more because what you are carrying is a thing nobody could turn away from this door, and nobody could be glad to receive.

(The Tutor comes out of the house.)

Tutor. You utter fools, with no sense left in your heads. Have you stopped caring whether you live at all, or were you born without any wits, that you cannot see you are not near the worst danger but right in the middle of it? If I had not been standing at these doors this long while, keeping watch, what you are planning would have been inside the house before you were. As it is, I took care of that myself. Now stop these long speeches and this shouting for joy that never has enough of itself, and get inside. In business like this, delay is the danger; the moment is to have it finished.

Orestes. Then how do things stand for me inside, when I go in?

Tutor. Well. It helps that nobody will know you.

Orestes. You told them I was dead, I take it.

Tutor. Understand that in this house you are one of the men in Hades.

Orestes. And are they glad about it? What are they saying?

Tutor. I will tell you when it is all over. As things stand now, everything on their side is going well for us — even what is not going well.

Electra. Who is this man, brother? In the gods' name, tell me.

Orestes. You do not understand?

Electra. No. I cannot even guess.

Orestes. You do not know the man you once put me into the hands of?

Electra. What man? What are you saying?

Orestes. The one whose hands carried me out in secret to Phocis, because you thought ahead.

Electra. This is him? The one man out of so many I found faithful, when our father was murdered?

Orestes. This is him. Do not put me through any more questions.

Electra. Oh, dearest light. Only saviour of Agamemnon's house — how did you come here? Are you really the man who saved him, and me, out of so much suffering? Dearest hands — and feet that ran the sweetest errand — how could you be beside me so long and not know you, and give no sign, but kill me with stories while you had the sweetest truth in your keeping? Welcome, father — because it is a father I seem to be looking at. Welcome. And know this: in one day I have hated you and loved you more than any man alive.

Tutor. That is enough, I think. As for the story of everything in between, there are many nights circling round and as many days, and they will make it all clear to you, Electra. But I say this to you two, standing there: now is the moment to act. Now Clytemnestra is alone. Now there is not a man inside. If you hold back, think what you will be fighting then — the people in there, and others cleverer than they are, and more of them.

Orestes. Pylades, this work no longer needs any long words from us. It needs us inside, as fast as we can go — after we have bowed to the seats of my father's gods, all of them that live at these gates.

(Orestes and Pylades go into the house, and the Tutor after them. Electra stays outside.)

Electra. Lord Apollo, hear them kindly, and hear me with them — I who have stood before you so often, with my hands full of what I could find to give you. Now, Apollo of the wolves, with what I have, I ask you, I fall before you, I beg you: be our willing helper in these plans, and show mankind what the gods give back to people for their irreverence.

(She goes into the house.)

Chorus.
	Look where the war god is stalking forward,
	breathing the blood that no one can fight.
	They have just gone under the roof of the house,
	the hounds no one escapes,
	hunting down the crimes and the wickedness.
	So the dream in my mind
	will not hang there in the air much longer.
Chorus.
	The champion of the dead
	is led inside on stealthy feet,
	into the old rich seat of his father,
	with new-whetted death in his hands.
	And Hermes, the son of Maia,
	who hid the trick in darkness,
	leads him straight to the finishing line,
	and waits no longer.

(Electra comes back out of the house.)

Electra.
	Dearest women, the men are about to finish
	the work now. Wait, and keep silent.
Chorus.
	How? What are they doing now?
Electra.
	She is dressing the urn for burial.
	The two of them are standing close beside her.
Chorus.
	And why did you rush out?
Electra.
	To keep watch. So Aegisthus
	does not get in past us without our seeing.
Clytemnestra.
	Aiai. Oh, house —
	empty of friends, and full of the ones who kill.
Electra.
	Somebody is shouting inside. Can you not hear it, friends?
Chorus.
	I heard — what should never be heard.
	It makes me shudder.
Clytemnestra.
	Oh, no. Aegisthus, where are you? Where?
Electra.
	Listen. Someone is crying out again.
Clytemnestra.
	My son, my son — pity the one who bore you.
Electra.
	But you had none for him,
	or for the father who fathered him.
Chorus.
	City, wretched family, now
	the fate that held you day by day
	is dying, dying.
Clytemnestra.
	Oh — I am struck.
Electra.
	Strike her again, if you have the strength.
Clytemnestra.
	Oh — again.
Electra.
	If only Aegisthus were under it too.
Chorus.
	The curses are being carried out.
	The ones who lie under the ground are alive.
	The blood is flowing back the other way:
	the men killed long ago
	are draining it out of their killers.

(Orestes and Pylades come out of the house.)

Chorus.
	And here they are. The red hand
	drips with the war god's sacrifice,
	and I cannot find it in me to blame.
Electra.
	Orestes, how is it with you?
Orestes.
	Inside the house it is well —
	if Apollo's oracle spoke well.
Electra.
	Is the wretched woman dead?
Orestes.
	Never be afraid again
	that your mother's pride will trample you.
Chorus.
	Stop. I can see Aegisthus, out in the open.
Electra.
	Boys — back inside, now.
Orestes.
	Where do you see the man?
Electra.
	There, coming up from the edge of town.
	He is ours. He is full of joy.
Chorus.
	Go, as fast as you can, to the doorway,
	so that having done the first thing well
	you do this one well again.
Orestes.
	Do not worry. We will finish it.
Electra.
	Then hurry, wherever you mean to go.
Orestes.
	I am gone.
Electra.
	What happens here I will see to.

(Orestes and Pylades go back into the house.)

Chorus.
	It would be as well to say a few words
	into this man's ear, softly,
	so that he rushes blind
	into the trial where justice is waiting.

(Aegisthus comes in.)

Aegisthus. Which of you knows where those strangers from Phocis are? I am told they have brought us news that Orestes lost his life in a wreck of chariots. You — yes, you, I am asking you — the one who used to be so bold. I should think it concerns you most, and you know most about it, and can tell me best.

Electra. I know. How could I not? Otherwise I would be a stranger to what happened to the nearest of my own.

Aegisthus. Then where might the strangers be? Tell me.

Electra. Inside. They have found their way to the heart of the lady of the house.

Aegisthus. And did they report him dead — really dead?

Electra. Not just reported it. They have shown him.

Aegisthus. Then I can see him for myself, in plain view?

Electra. You can. It is not a sight anybody would envy.

Aegisthus. You have given me a great deal of joy — which is not like you.

Electra. Enjoy it, if there is anything in this for you to enjoy.

Aegisthus. Silence, I say. And open the gates wide, for every man of Mycenae and Argos to see — so that if any of them was buoyed up before on empty hopes of this man, he can look at the corpse now, and take my bit in his mouth, instead of waiting until I punish him and beat sense into him by force.

Electra. It is being done, on my side. Time has taught me sense: to go along with the stronger.

(The doors are opened. A body lies under a covering. Orestes and Pylades stand beside it.)

Aegisthus. Zeus — what I see is a thing that could not have fallen without the gods' envy. If there is a nemesis in saying so, I unsay it.

(He turns to Orestes and speaks to him.)

Take all the covering off the face, so that his kinsman, at least, can give him the mourning he is owed from me as well.

Orestes. Lift it yourself. It is not my place but yours to look at this, and to greet it with love.

Aegisthus. Good advice. I will take it.

(He turns to Electra and speaks to her.)

You — call Clytemnestra for me, if she is anywhere in the house.

Orestes. She is close by you. Do not look anywhere else.

(He lifts the covering.)

Aegisthus. Oh — what am I looking at?

Orestes. Who are you afraid of? Who is it you do not know?

Aegisthus. Whose nets have I walked into, wretch that I am? Who are these men?

Orestes. Have you not noticed yet that for some time now you have been talking to the living as if they were dead?

Aegisthus. Oh — I understand you. There is no way that this can be anyone but Orestes speaking to me.

Orestes. And such a fine prophet, and wrong for so long.

Aegisthus. Then I am finished, wretch that I am. But let me say a little. Only a little.

Electra. In the gods' name, brother, do not let him say another word, do not let him spin out speeches. When a man is tangled in trouble and is going to die, what does he gain by a little more time? Kill him as fast as you can, and when you have killed him, throw him out to the buriers he deserves — the birds and the dogs — far out of our sight. That is the only thing that could ever set me free from all these years of evil.

Orestes. Get inside, quickly. This is not a contest of words now. It is your life.

Aegisthus. Why take me into the house? If what you are doing is right, why does it need the dark? Why is your hand not ready to strike now?

Orestes. Do not give orders. Go to the place where you killed my father, so that you die on the same spot.

Aegisthus. Must this house see everything, then — all the evils of the sons of Pelops, now and still to come?

Orestes. Yours, at any rate. In that I am a prophet of the first rank.

Aegisthus. The skill you are boasting of was not your father's.

Orestes. You answer back and back, and the walk gets no shorter. Move.

Aegisthus. You lead the way.

Orestes. You go first.

Aegisthus. In case I escape you?

Orestes. No. In case you die the way you would like. I have to make sure this is bitter for you. This justice ought to come at once to everyone who wants to go beyond the laws: death. Then there would not be so much wickedness.

(He drives Aegisthus into the house. Electra follows.)

Chorus.
	Seed of Atreus, after so much suffering,
	you have come out at last, into freedom,
	made whole by what was done today.

Philoctetes (409 BC)

Philoctetes

(A rocky shore on Lemnos. Odysseus and Neoptolemus have just landed, with one sailor. Odysseus keeps back from the rocks.)

Odysseus. This is the shore of Lemnos. The sea goes all the way round it, and no one walks here and no one lives here. It was here, Neoptolemus — son of Achilles, raised by the strongest father any Greek ever had — that I once put ashore the man from Malis, the son of Poeas, and left him. I was ordered to do it by the men in command. His foot was dripping with a disease that was eating it away, and we could not so much as touch a drink-offering or a sacrifice in peace: he kept the whole camp in an uproar with his wild, unlucky cries, howling and groaning. But why go over that? This is no moment for long speeches. If he finds out I am here, I throw away the whole trick I mean to catch him with, and I mean to catch him fast.

Now the work is yours: help me with what is left. Look for a rock somewhere here with two mouths — a cave shaped so that in cold weather there are two places to sit in the sun, and in summer a breeze blows through the tunnel from end to end and brings sleep with it. A little below it, on the left, you may see water running from a spring, if it is still there. Go up quietly and signal to me whether he is still in the same place or somewhere else, so that you can hear the rest of the plan, and I can lay it out, and the two of us can carry it together.

Neoptolemus. King Odysseus, the job you describe is not far off. I think I can see a cave like the one you mean.

Odysseus. Above you or below? I cannot make it out.

Neoptolemus. Here, up above. And there is no sound of footsteps.

Odysseus. Make sure he is not lying in there asleep.

Neoptolemus. I see an empty dwelling. There is nobody inside.

Odysseus. And nothing in there to make it a home?

Neoptolemus. A bed of leaves, trodden down, as if someone sleeps here.

Odysseus. The rest bare? Nothing else under the roof?

Neoptolemus. A cup made out of a single piece of wood — some bad craftsman's work — and things for lighting a fire beside it.

Odysseus. That is his treasure you are describing.

Neoptolemus. Ugh. And here are more of them: rags drying in the sun, full of some heavy sickness.

Odysseus. The man lives in this place, that is clear, and he is not far off. How could a sick man walk far, with that old ruin in his leg? Either he has gone out to find food, or he knows of some leaf somewhere that dulls the pain. Send the man you have with you to keep watch, so that he does not come on me unawares. He would rather have me than all the Greeks together.

Neoptolemus. He is going, and the path will be watched. Now, if you need anything more, tell me the rest.

(The sailor goes.)

Odysseus. Son of Achilles, you must be true to your birth for the job you came on — and not just with your body. If you hear something new, something you have not been told before, you must serve it. You are here to serve.

Neoptolemus. What are you ordering me to do, then?

Odysseus. The mind of Philoctetes. You have to steal it out of him with a story, by cunning, as you talk to him. When he asks who you are and where you come from, say: the son of Achilles. There is nothing to hide in that. But say you are sailing home. You have left the Greek fleet and the army; you hate them with a great hatred. They sent for you, begging you to come from home, because you were their only hope of taking Troy — and when you came and claimed the arms of Achilles, which were yours by right, they judged you not worth them and handed them to Odysseus. Say what you like about me: the worst of the worst. You will not hurt me with any of it. But if you do not do this, you will bring grief on every Greek there is. If this man's bow is not taken, you cannot sack the land of Dardanus.

And let me explain why you can go up to him safely and be trusted, and I cannot. You sailed to Troy under oath to nobody, under no compulsion, and not with the first expedition. I can deny none of those things. So if he sees me while he is still master of his bow, I am dead, and I will take you down with me for being at my side. No — this is the very thing we have to be clever about: how you are to become the thief of those arms that cannot be beaten.

I know, boy. You were not born to say things like this or to scheme harm. But it is a sweet thing to take the prize of victory. Bring yourself to it. We will show ourselves honest men another time. For now, give yourself to me for one small, shameless part of one day, and then, for the rest of time, be called the most righteous man alive.

Neoptolemus. Son of Laertes, the things I hate hearing said, I hate doing. It is not my nature to get anything by a dirty trick — not mine, and not, they tell me, my father's, who begot me. But I am ready to take the man by force, not by cunning. He has one foot to stand on; he is not going to overpower this many of us by force. Still, I was sent here to work with you, and I do not like the thought of being called a traitor. But I would rather, my lord, do the right thing and fail than win by doing wrong.

Odysseus. Son of a good father — I was young once too, and I had a lazy tongue and a busy hand. Now I have come out and been tested, and I see that with men it is the tongue, not the deed, that leads everything.

Neoptolemus. What are you ordering me to do, then, other than lie?

Odysseus. I am telling you to take Philoctetes by cunning.

Neoptolemus. Why cunning? Why not persuade him and bring him?

Odysseus. He will never be persuaded. And you cannot take him by force.

Neoptolemus. Is he really so terrifyingly strong that he dares anything?

Odysseus. Arrows you cannot escape, and death goes ahead of them.

Neoptolemus. So a man cannot even go near him?

Odysseus. No. Not unless you take him by cunning, as I say.

Neoptolemus. You do not think it is shameful, then, to tell lies?

Odysseus. Not if the lie brings us through safe.

Neoptolemus. How does a man look him in the face and dare to say it?

Odysseus. When what you do is for gain, there is no place for hanging back.

Neoptolemus. And what gain is it to me that he should come to Troy?

Odysseus. That bow, and nothing else, takes Troy.

Neoptolemus. Then I am not the one who will sack it, as you told me?

Odysseus. Not you without the bow, and not the bow without you.

Neoptolemus. Then it has to be hunted down, if that is how things stand.

Odysseus. Do this, and you carry off two prizes.

Neoptolemus. What are they? Tell me, and I will not refuse to do it.

Odysseus. You will be called clever and a good man, both at once.

Neoptolemus. All right. I will do it, and throw off all shame.

Odysseus. You remember what I told you to say?

Neoptolemus. Be sure of it. I agreed, once and for all.

Odysseus. You stay here, then, and wait for him. I am going, so that I am not seen here with you, and I will send the lookout back to the ship. And if the two of you seem to me to be wasting time, I will send that same man back here again, disguised as the captain of a merchant ship, so that nobody knows him. He will talk in a roundabout way; take from what he says whatever is useful at the moment, my boy. I am going to the ship, and I leave all this to you. May Hermes the Deceiver, who sends us on this errand, be our guide — and Victory, Athena of the City, who keeps me safe, always.

(He goes. The Chorus comes in: sailors from Scyros, Neoptolemus's own men.)

Chorus.
	What must I, what must I do, master —
	a stranger in a strange land —
	what must I hide, what say
	to a man who will suspect us?
	Tell me. One man's skill
	outreaches another's skill,
	and so does his judgment, when he holds
	the sceptre of Zeus that comes from the god.
	And to you, my boy, all that power
	has come down from the beginning.
	So tell me
	what I must do to serve you.
Neoptolemus.
	For now — since you may want to see
	the place at the island's edge where he lies —
	look, and do not be afraid.
	But when the terrible traveller
	comes back to this house of his,
	keep close to my hand, watch it,
	and try to do what the moment needs.
Chorus.
	That care has been my care a long time, my lord:
	to keep my eye, before everything, on your good.
	Now tell me. What sort of house
	does he live in, sitting there,
	and what ground does he hold?
	It is not out of season for me to know,
	or he may fall on me from somewhere unseen.
	What is the place? Where does he sit?
	Where does he walk — inside, or out of doors?
Neoptolemus.
	You see his house, with its two doors —
	a bed in the rock.
Chorus.
	And the poor wretch himself — where has he gone?
Neoptolemus.
	It is plain to me that he is dragging
	a track along somewhere near here, after food.
	That, they say, is the way he lives:
	shooting animals with feathered arrows,
	a wretch, and wretchedly,
	and nobody comes near him
	with a cure for his troubles.
Chorus.
	I pity him, for my part.
	No one to care for him,
	no companion's face to look at,
	miserable, always alone,
	he is sick with a savage sickness
	and at a loss at every need
	as it comes. How, how does the unlucky man hold out?
	Oh, the contrivances of the gods.
	Oh, the unhappy race of men,
	whose life is never measured right.
Chorus.
	This man — second, it may be,
	to no one of the oldest houses —
	lies with no share in anything life gives,
	alone, apart from other men,
	among dappled beasts and shaggy ones,
	pitiful in his pain and in his hunger both,
	with troubles nothing can cure. And Echo
	of the mountains, her mouth never shut,
	heard from far off, answers back
	his bitter cries.
Neoptolemus.
	None of this is a wonder to me.
	It was gods' work, if I understand anything —
	the suffering that came at him
	from cruel-hearted Chryse,
	and what he suffers now, with no one to tend him.
	It cannot be but some god is seeing to it
	that he should not aim at Troy
	the god's arrows nobody can fight
	before the time is out
	when, they say, Troy has to fall to them.
Chorus.
	Quiet, boy.
Neoptolemus.
	What is it?
Chorus.
	A sound came up —
	the kind that lives with a man being worn down —
	from over there, or there.
	It strikes me, strikes me true,
	the voice of someone crawling
	along his track because he must.
	I cannot miss it, far off as it is,
	that heavy voice of a man worn out.
	He cries out, and it is clear.
Chorus.
	But listen, my boy —
Neoptolemus.
	Say it.
Chorus.
	— to a new thought.
	The man is not away from home. He is here,
	and not with the music of a pipe,
	like a shepherd with his flocks in the fields —
	no: stumbling, it may be, because he must,
	he cries a cry that carries far,
	or he is looking out at the harbour
	no ship comes into. He is calling out something terrible.

(Philoctetes comes in, from the cave side, dragging his foot.)

Philoctetes. You — strangers. Who are you? What country have you put in from, to this land with no harbour and no one living in it? What homeland, what people, should I guess you belong to? The cut of your clothes is Greek, and nothing is dearer to my eyes. But I want to hear your voices. Do not shrink back from me in fear, do not be frightened because I have gone wild to look at. Pity a man who is miserable and alone, cast away like this, with no one of his own, ill-used. Speak to me — if you have come here as friends. Answer me. It is not right that I should fail to get that much from you, or you from me.

Neoptolemus. Then know this first, stranger: we are Greeks. That is what you want to learn.

Philoctetes. Oh, the dearest sound there is. To be greeted by such a man — after so long! What need brought you in to shore, my boy, what carried you here? What errand? Which wind, the kindest of them all? Tell me all of it, so that I know who you are.

Neoptolemus. My people are from Scyros, the island the sea runs round. I am sailing home. I am called the son of Achilles — Neoptolemus. Now you know all of it.

Philoctetes. Son of the father I loved best, son of a country I loved — the old man Lycomedes' fosterling. On what voyage did you touch this land? Where have you sailed from?

Neoptolemus. From Troy, as it happens. That is where I am sailing from now.

Philoctetes. What? You were never on board with us at the start, when the fleet went out to Troy.

Neoptolemus. Were you part of that labour too?

Philoctetes. My boy — do you not know who you are looking at?

Neoptolemus. How could I know a man I have never once seen?

Philoctetes. Not even my name? You never heard a word of what happened to me, the troubles I have been dying of?

Neoptolemus. Take it that I know nothing of what you are asking.

Philoctetes. Oh, I am wretched many times over; the gods have turned bitter against me. Not a rumour of the state I am in has gone home, or reached any corner of Greece. The men who threw me out — an unholy act — keep quiet and laugh, and my sickness flourishes and keeps on growing.

My boy, son of Achilles your father: I am the man — you may have heard of him — who is master of the bow of Heracles. Philoctetes, the son of Poeas. The two commanders and the king of the Cephallenians, Odysseus, threw me out here in disgrace, alone as you see me, wasting away with a savage disease, bitten with the savage bite of the snake that kills men. With that for company, boy, they set me down here and went away and left me alone, when they had put in here with the fleet from Chryse out in the sea. They were glad, then, when they saw me asleep on the shore under the roof of the rock, after all the tossing at sea, and they left me and went — setting out a few rags first, as you would for a beggar who has come down in the world, and a little something to eat as well. A small charity. I hope they get the same.

Boy, can you picture what it was to get up out of sleep that day, when they were gone? What tears I cried, what evils I howled over — seeing the ships I had sailed with all gone, and not a man in the place, not one who could help me, not one who would take a hand with me in my sickness. I looked everywhere and found nothing there but pain — and plenty of that, my boy.

(He stops for a moment, then goes on.)

Philoctetes. So time went by for me, season after season, and in this narrow house I had to be my own servant, alone. For the belly, this bow found what was needed; it brought down the doves on the wing. And whatever the arrow struck, off the bowstring, I would crawl to it myself, in misery, dragging my wretched foot behind me. If I had to get a drink, or break a bit of wood when the frost was spread on the ground, as it is in winter, I would creep out in pain and manage it somehow. And then there would be no fire; but by grinding stone on stone I would at last bring out the hidden spark, and that is what keeps me alive, always. A roof to live under, and fire — that supplies everything except an end to being sick.

Now, my boy, let me tell you about the island. No sailor comes near it by choice. There is no anchorage, nowhere he can sail to and sell a cargo at a profit or be put up by a host. Men with any sense do not make their voyages here. But suppose someone is driven in against his will — in the long course of a man's life such things happen often enough. When they come, my boy, they have pitying words for me, and perhaps in their pity they have given me a share of food, or some clothing. But the one thing nobody will do, when I bring it up, is take me safely home. So I am dying, wretched, in this the tenth year now, of hunger and of hardship, feeding this sickness that never stops eating. That is what the sons of Atreus and the brute strength of Odysseus have done to me, boy. May the gods on Olympus give them the same, some day, in return for what I have suffered.

Chorus. I think I pity you too, son of Poeas, as much as the strangers who came before us.

Neoptolemus. And I can be a witness to what you say myself. I know it is true. I have met the wickedness of the sons of Atreus for myself, and the brute strength of Odysseus.

Philoctetes. What — do you have a complaint against those damned sons of Atreus as well? Some wrong they did you, to be angry about?

Neoptolemus. If only my hand could fill up my anger one day, so that Mycenae would learn, and Sparta, that Scyros too is a mother of fighting men.

Philoctetes. Well said, boy. What is it? Why have you come here with this great anger, laying a charge against them?

Neoptolemus. Son of Poeas, I will tell you — though it is hard to tell — how they insulted me when I came. When fate decreed that Achilles should die —

Philoctetes. Oh — say no more. Tell me this first: the son of Peleus is dead?

Neoptolemus. Dead. Not by any man's hand, but a god's. Shot, they say — brought down by Apollo.

Philoctetes. Well. The one who killed and the one who died were both of good birth. I do not know, boy, whether to ask about your wrong first or mourn for him.

Neoptolemus. I think your own pains are enough for you, poor man, without grieving over your neighbour's.

Philoctetes. You are right. So tell me your business again from the start — how they outraged you.

Neoptolemus. They came for me in a ship dressed out in colours, the great Odysseus and the man who raised my father, and they said — whether it was true or an empty story I do not know — that since my father was dead, it was not lawful for anyone but me to take the towers of Troy. That was what they told me, stranger, and it did not keep me long from sailing, and fast. Mostly it was longing for the dead man: to see him before he was buried, because I had never seen him. And then there was the fine story on top of it, that if I went I would take the towers of Troy.

It was the second day of my voyage when a fair wind and the oars brought me in to Sigeum — bitter Sigeum. As soon as I landed the whole army came round me in a ring to welcome me, swearing they were looking at Achilles alive again, the man who was gone. But he lay there dead. And I, unlucky, when I had wept for him, went not long afterwards to the sons of Atreus — my friends, as I had every reason to think — and asked for my father's arms, and everything else that had been his. And they said — oh, the most shameless thing anyone ever said: "Seed of Achilles, everything else your father had, you may take. But another man is master of those arms now: the son of Laertes." The tears came at once and I jumped up in a heavy rage and said, in pain, "You wretch, did you dare give my arms to somebody else instead of me, without asking me?" And Odysseus — he happened to be standing near — said, "Yes, boy. They gave them, and rightly. I was there and I saved them, and saved him too." And I was furious, and at once I started lashing at him with every insult there is, leaving nothing out, if he was going to rob me of my arms. He had come to that point, and he is not a man who angers easily, but stung by what he heard he answered like this: "You were not where we were. You were away, when you were needed. And since you have such a bold mouth, you will never sail back to Scyros with those arms." That is what I heard, that is how I was abused, and I am sailing home, robbed of what is mine by Odysseus himself, the worst man of a bad line.

And I do not blame him as much as the men in charge. A city belongs wholly to its leaders, and so does an army; and when men break the rules, it is their teachers' words that made them bad. There. The whole story is told. Whoever hates the sons of Atreus — may he be as dear to the gods as he is to me.

Chorus.
	Earth of the mountains, feeder of all things,
	mother of Zeus himself,
	you who hold the great Pactolus and its gold —
	there too, mother, lady, I called on you
	when all the outrage of the sons of Atreus
	came down on this man,
	when they handed over his father's arms,
	the thing most to be honoured,
	to the son of Laertes.
	Oh blessed one, who rides
	on lions that kill bulls.

Philoctetes. You have come to me, strangers, carrying a clear token of grief, it seems — a grief that matches mine. You sing the same tune I do, and I recognise the work: this is the sons of Atreus and Odysseus. I know that man would set his tongue to any dirty story and any villainy that would let him get to something wicked in the end. None of that surprises me. What does is that the greater Ajax, if he was there, could stand and watch it.

Neoptolemus. He was no longer alive, friend. I would never have been robbed like that while he lived.

Philoctetes. What are you saying? He is dead and gone too?

Neoptolemus. Think of him as one who no longer sees the light.

Philoctetes. Oh, no. But the son of Tydeus, Diomedes, and the son of Sisyphus that Laertes bought — Odysseus — they will never die. They are the ones who should not be alive.

Neoptolemus. No, they will not. Be sure of that. They are flourishing now, and greatly, in the Greek army.

Philoctetes. And what of my good old friend Nestor of Pylos — is he not alive? He used to hold back their wickedness, with his wise advice.

Neoptolemus. He is doing badly now. Antilochus, the son he had at his side, is dead and gone from him.

Philoctetes. Oh — two more men you have named that I would least have wanted to hear were dead. Oh, no. What is a man to look for, when these are dead and Odysseus is alive — here, again — when he should have been the one reported dead in their place?

Neoptolemus. He is a clever wrestler, that man. But even clever schemes, Philoctetes, get tripped up often enough.

Philoctetes. Tell me, in the gods' name — where was Patroclus when you needed him? Your father loved him more than anyone.

Neoptolemus. He was dead too. And in one short lesson I will teach you this: war never takes a bad man if it can help it. It always takes the good ones.

Philoctetes. I will be your witness on that. And on that exact point I will ask you about a man of no worth — but terrible with his tongue, and clever. How is he doing now?

Neoptolemus. Who could you mean, but Odysseus?

Philoctetes. I did not mean him. There was a man called Thersites, who would never choose to speak once when he could speak again, even where nobody let him speak at all. Do you know if he is alive?

Neoptolemus. I never saw him. But I heard he is still alive.

Philoctetes. He would be. Nothing bad has ever died yet. The gods wrap those up carefully; somehow they take pleasure in turning the villains and the twisted ones back from Hades, and the just and the good they are for ever sending down. How am I to reckon this? How praise it, when I praise the gods' ways and find the gods are bad?

Neoptolemus. For my part, son of a father from Oeta, from now on I will keep a long way off from Troy and from the sons of Atreus, and look at them from there. Where the worse man is stronger than the good, where what is decent goes to ruin and the coward wins — men like that I will never love. Rocky Scyros will be enough for me from now on; I will enjoy my home. Now I go to the ship. And you, son of Poeas — goodbye, all the good there is, goodbye. May the gods take the sickness away from you, as you wish. We must go, so that whenever the god lets us sail, we can be on our way.

Philoctetes. You are leaving already, boy?

Neoptolemus. The right moment calls us to watch for the wind near the ship, not far from it.

Philoctetes. By your father, then, and by your mother, boy, and by everything at home you love, I beg you as a suppliant: do not leave me alone like this, deserted in these troubles — you see what they are like; you have heard how many there are. Make me a side-job. There is a lot of disgust, I know, in a cargo like me. Put up with it all the same. To a man of good birth what is shameful is hateful and what is decent brings a good name. Leave this undone and you carry a blot on your name that is not pretty; do it, boy, and you win the greatest prize of a good name there is, if I get home alive to the land of Oeta.

Come. It is not one whole day's trouble. Bring yourself to it. Take me and throw me wherever you like — in the bilge, in the bow, in the stern, wherever I will trouble the men on board least. Nod yes, by Zeus himself, who watches over suppliants, boy; be persuaded. I fall at your knees, for all that I am a helpless wretch, and lame. Do not leave me deserted like this, far from any path men walk. Bring me safe to your own home, or to Euboea, where Chalcodon rules. From there it is no long voyage for me to Oeta, and the ridge of Trachis, and the fine stream of Spercheius, so that you can show me to my father, whom I love — though for a long time now I have been afraid he may be gone. I sent him many messages by the men who came here, prayers and begging, that he would send a ship himself and bring me safely home. But either he is dead, or — as I think likely — the messengers made little of my business and hurried on their voyage home.

Now, though, in you I have come to a man who can be my escort and my messenger both. You save me. You pity me. See how everything for men is full of fear and danger: to do well, and then to have the opposite happen. When a man is out of trouble he should watch for the terrible things, and when he is living well, that is the time to look hardest at his life, in case it is destroyed without his noticing.

Chorus.
	Pity him, my lord. He has told of many struggles,
	of pains hard to bear. May no one I love
	ever meet the like.
	And if you hate the sons of Atreus, my lord,
	if you hate them bitterly, then I would turn
	their wrong into this man's gain
	and carry him where he longs to go,
	home, in your fast, well-fitted ship,
	and get clear of the anger of the gods.

Neoptolemus. Take care. You are easy about it now, standing here looking on. When you have had your fill of the sickness, living alongside it, you may find you are no longer the man who said these things.

Chorus. Never. You will never have a fair reason to throw that in my face.

Neoptolemus. Well then, it would be a disgrace for me to be found less ready than you to work for a stranger when the moment calls for it. If you all agree, let us sail. Let him set out, and quickly. The ship will carry him, and will not say no. Only let the gods bring us safe out of this land, and on from here to wherever we choose to sail.

Philoctetes. Oh, dearest of days. Oh, sweetest of men — and you, sailors, my friends. How can I show you in deeds what a friend you have made of me? Let us go, my boy. But first the two of us will go in and say goodbye to my home that is no home, so you can learn what I lived on and how strong a heart I was born with. I think nobody else could have stood so much as the sight of it, let alone lived with it. Necessity was my teacher, and it taught me to put up with what is bad.

Chorus. Wait, both of you. Let us hear this. Two men are coming: one of them a sailor off your ship, the other a foreigner. Hear what they have to say, and then go in.

(A merchant comes in, with one of Neoptolemus's sailors.)

Merchant. Son of Achilles, this man here, my fellow traveller, was one of three keeping guard on your ship, and I asked him to tell me where you were to be found, since I had run into you — not by design, but by some chance that brought me to anchor on this same shore. I am a shipmaster, sailing home from Troy with no great company, to Peparethus, where the grapes grow thick; and when I heard that the sailors were all men who had sailed out with you, I decided not to go on my way in silence without first telling you what I know, and getting the same in return. You know nothing, I suspect, about your own affairs — what new plans the Greeks have about you. And not plans only: things being done, no longer put off.

Neoptolemus. I will remember the kindness of your thinking ahead, stranger, unless I was born bad. Tell me what you spoke of, so I can know what new plan of the Greeks you are carrying to me.

Merchant. They have set out after you with ships — old Phoenix, and the sons of Theseus.

Neoptolemus. To bring me back by force, or by talking?

Merchant. I do not know. I heard it, and I have come to tell you.

Neoptolemus. Phoenix and his shipmates are that keen to do this for the sons of Atreus?

Merchant. Take it as being done. Not about to be done. Done.

Neoptolemus. Then why was Odysseus not ready to sail and bring the message himself? Was some fear holding him back?

Merchant. He and the son of Tydeus were fitting out to go after another man, when I put to sea.

Neoptolemus. Who was this other man, that Odysseus himself was sailing after?

Merchant. There was a man — but first tell me who that is over there. And whatever you say, keep your voice down.

Neoptolemus. This, stranger, is the famous Philoctetes.

Merchant. Then ask me nothing more. Gather yourself up and get out of this land, as fast as you can, and sail.

Philoctetes. What is he saying, boy? What is this sailor selling of me to you, in whispers, in the dark?

Neoptolemus. I do not know yet what he means. Whatever he has to say, he must say it in the light — to you and me and these men.

Merchant. Seed of Achilles, do not get me in trouble with the army for saying what I should not. I do a good deal for them, and I get a good deal back, as much as a poor man can.

Neoptolemus. I am the enemy of the sons of Atreus, and this man is my greatest friend, because he hates them. You came to me as a friend. Then you must hide from us nothing of what you have heard.

Merchant. Watch what you are doing, boy.

Neoptolemus. I have been watching it a long time.

Merchant. I will hold you responsible.

Neoptolemus. Do. But talk.

Merchant. I am talking. It is this man that those two are sailing after — the two you heard me name, the son of Tydeus and the brute strength of Odysseus. They have sworn to bring him, either by talking him round or by main force. And all the Greeks heard it plainly, from Odysseus's own mouth; he had more confidence than the other that he could do it.

Neoptolemus. And what for? Why should the sons of Atreus, after so long a time, turn their minds so hard to a man they threw out years ago? What longing came over them? Or was it the force of the gods, and their anger — the gods who pay back evil deeds?

Merchant. I will teach you the whole of it, since it seems you have not heard. There was a seer of good family, a son of Priam, Helenus by name. That man went out alone one night — the man everybody calls foul and disgraceful names, Odysseus the trickster — and caught him, and led him in chains into the middle of the Greeks and showed him off: a fine catch. And Helenus prophesied everything else they asked him, and this about Troy: that they would never take its towers unless they talked this man round and brought him from the island where he lives now. And as soon as the son of Laertes heard the seer say it, he promised on the spot to bring the man and show him to the Greeks. He thought he would most likely get him willing; and if he would not come, then unwilling; and if he failed, anybody who liked could cut his head off. You have heard it all, boy. I advise speed — for you, and for anyone you care about.

Philoctetes. Oh, misery. He — that man, that walking harm — has sworn to talk me round and bring me to the Greeks? Then I will be talked round into coming up out of Hades, dead, into the light — the way his father did, Sisyphus, who talked his way back up once.

Merchant. I know nothing about that. I am going back to my ship. May a god bring the two of you whatever is best.

(He goes.)

Philoctetes. Is it not astonishing, my boy, that the son of Laertes should ever expect to lead me off his ship with soft words and show me in the middle of the Greeks? No. I would sooner listen to the thing I hate most in the world — the viper that made me the cripple I am. But there is nothing he would not say, nothing he would not dare. And I know now that he is coming here. Come, son, let us move, so that a wide sea can lie between us and Odysseus's ship. Let us go. Hurry at the right moment, and when the work is done there is sleep, and rest.

Neoptolemus. Then as soon as the wind off the bow drops, we sail. It is dead against us now.

Philoctetes. The sailing is always good when you are running from trouble.

Neoptolemus. No. It is against them too.

Philoctetes. No wind is ever against pirates, when there is a chance to steal something and grab it by force.

Neoptolemus. Well, if that is what you want, let us go — once you have taken from inside whatever you most need and most want.

Philoctetes. There are things I need. Not that there is much to choose from.

Neoptolemus. What is there that is not on board my ship?

Philoctetes. I have a leaf, a herb, that I use more than anything to put this wound to sleep, till it is quite tamed.

Neoptolemus. Bring it out, then. What else do you want to take?

Philoctetes. Any of these arrows that may have been forgotten and slipped out of my sight — so I do not leave one for someone else to pick up.

Neoptolemus. Is that really the famous bow — the one in your hands now?

Philoctetes. This one, and no other. The one I carry in my two hands.

Neoptolemus. Could I look at it closer? Hold it — and bow to it, as if it were a god?

Philoctetes. For you, my boy, that and anything else of mine that would do you good.

Neoptolemus. I do want to. But my wanting goes like this: if it is allowed, I would like it. If not, let it go.

Philoctetes. What you say is holy, son, and it is allowed. You alone have given me this light of the sun to look at, and the land of Oeta to see again, and my old father, and my own people; you alone, when I was down under my enemies' feet, have lifted me clear of them. Take heart. You may touch it, and give it back to the hand that gave it, and you can boast to the world that for your goodness you alone among men have laid a hand on it. It was by doing good that I got it myself.

Neoptolemus. I am not sorry I found you, and made a friend of you. A man who knows how to do good when good has been done to him is a friend worth more than any possession. Go in, please.

Philoctetes. And I will bring you in with me. The sickness in me wants you standing by.

(They go into the cave.)

Chorus.
	I have heard the story, though I never saw it:
	the man who once went near the bed of Zeus —
	Ixion — and how the all-powerful son of Cronus
	bound him to the running wheel.
	But of no other man, by hearsay or by sight,
	do I know one who met a fate more hateful than this man's.
	He did no one wrong. He robbed no one.
	He dealt fairly with fair men,
	and he was left to die like this, for nothing.
	It is a wonder to me,
	how — how in the world — alone,
	hearing the surf beat on both sides of him,
	he kept hold of a life so full of tears.
Chorus.
	Here he was his own only neighbour,
	with no way to walk,
	no one in the country to be near him in his misery,
	no one to answer his groans
	when he cried out over the heavy gnawing bloody thing,
	no one to gather soft leaves from the feeding earth
	and put to sleep the hot blood oozing
	from the sores of his poisoned foot,
	whenever the fit came on.
	He would crawl, this way and that,
	dragging himself like a child without its nurse,
	to wherever something could be had,
	when the ruin that bites the heart let go.
Chorus.
	Not the seed of the holy earth for food,
	nor anything else we eat,
	we men who work for our bread —
	only what his fast-shooting bow
	and its winged arrows won for his belly.
	Poor wretched soul.
	Ten years, and not one taste of poured wine;
	looking round for standing water, wherever he could find it,
	and always making his way towards it.
Chorus.
	But now he has met the son of good men,
	and out of all that he will come to happiness, and to greatness.
	After the fullness of many months
	the boy carries him on the sea-going timbers home,
	to his father's hall,
	the haunt of the Malian nymphs, the banks of the Spercheius,
	where the man of the bronze shield, Heracles,
	went up to the gods, all in a blaze
	of his father's divine fire, above the heights of Oeta.

(Neoptolemus comes out of the cave, carrying the bow. Philoctetes follows, and stops.)

Neoptolemus. Come on, if you are willing. Why are you silent like this, for no reason? Why are you standing there struck dumb?

Philoctetes. Aah. Aah.

Neoptolemus. What is it?

Philoctetes. Nothing terrible. Go on, son.

Neoptolemus. Is it the pain of the sickness? Has it come on you?

Philoctetes. No, no. I think it is easing just now. Oh, gods.

Neoptolemus. Why do you groan like that and call on the gods?

Philoctetes. For them to come to us, to save and to soothe. Aah. Aah.

Neoptolemus. What has happened to you? Will you not say, but stay silent like this? It is plain that you are in some trouble.

Philoctetes. I am finished, son. I cannot hide it from you. Attatai. It goes through me — it goes right through. Oh, I am wretched. I am finished, son. It is eating me, son. Papai. Apappapai. Papappapappapappapai. By the gods, if you have a sword ready to your hand, son, strike at the end of my foot. Cut it off, quickly. Do not spare my life. Go on, boy.

Neoptolemus. What is this new thing that has come on you so suddenly, that makes you howl and groan over yourself like this?

Philoctetes. You know, son.

Neoptolemus. What is it?

Philoctetes. You know, boy.

Neoptolemus. What is wrong with you? I do not know.

Philoctetes. How can you not know? Papappapappapai.

Neoptolemus. It is terrible, the weight of this sickness.

Philoctetes. Terrible past telling. Pity me.

Neoptolemus. What am I to do?

Philoctetes. Do not take fright at me and betray me. She comes now and then, this thing — perhaps when she has had her fill of wandering somewhere else.

Neoptolemus. Oh, oh — poor man. Poor, poor man, as all this pain has shown you to be. Do you want me to take hold of you? To touch you somewhere?

Philoctetes. No, not that. But take this bow of mine, as you asked me to just now, and keep it, and guard it, until this present fit of the sickness lets go. Sleep takes me as soon as the evil passes off, and it cannot stop before then. You must let me sleep in peace. And if those men come in the meantime, by the gods I charge you: not willingly, not unwillingly, not by any trick, let this bow go to them — or you will be the death of yourself and of me together, me, who am your suppliant.

Neoptolemus. Do not worry about my care. It will go into no hands but yours and mine. Give it to me, and good luck go with it.

Philoctetes. There. Take it, boy. And bow to the jealousy of the gods, so that it does not bring you pain on pain, as it did me, and the man who had it before me.

Neoptolemus. Gods, grant us that, both of us. And grant us a voyage with a fair wind, and a quick one, to wherever the god thinks right and our errand is bound.

Philoctetes. I am afraid your prayers are empty, son. Look — again the dark blood is dripping from deep down, oozing, and I expect something new. Papai. Oh. Papai — again — foot, what you do to me. It is creeping up. It is coming — here, close. Oh, I am wretched. Now you have the whole thing. Do not run away, whatever you do. Attatai. Odysseus — you stranger from Cephallenia — I wish this pain could go right through your chest and stay there. Oh. Papai. Papai again. You two generals, Agamemnon, Menelaus — if only you could feed this sickness in my place, for as long as I have. Oh, oh. Death. Death. I call you every day, day after day — why can you never come? Son. My noble boy. Take me up and burn me in this fire they call Lemnian, the fire of this island. Burn me, noble boy. I did that once myself, for the son of Zeus, and it was for that I got these weapons, that you are keeping safe now. What do you say, boy? What do you say? Why are you silent? Where are you, son?

Neoptolemus. I have been in pain a long while, groaning over your suffering.

Philoctetes. Then take heart too, son. She comes sharp and she goes quickly. Only I beg you — do not leave me alone.

Neoptolemus. Take heart. We will stay.

Philoctetes. You will stay?

Neoptolemus. Be sure of it.

Philoctetes. I do not think it right to put you on oath, son.

Neoptolemus. It is not allowed me to leave without you.

Philoctetes. Give me your hand on it.

Neoptolemus. I give it. To stay.

Philoctetes. Now take me there — over there —

Neoptolemus. Where do you mean?

Philoctetes. Up —

Neoptolemus. What is this? Are you raving again? Why are you staring at the sky's circle above us?

Philoctetes. Let me go. Let me go.

Neoptolemus. Let you go where?

Philoctetes. Let me go, will you.

Neoptolemus. I will not.

Philoctetes. You will kill me if you touch me.

Neoptolemus. There. I am letting you go, since you seem to know better.

Philoctetes. Earth, take me, dying, as I am. This evil will not let me stand up any more.

(He sinks down.)

Neoptolemus. Sleep will have the man before long, I think. His head is falling back. Yes — sweat is running down his whole body, and a dark vein has burst at the end of his foot, and the blood is pouring out. Let us leave him quiet, friends, so he can fall asleep.

Chorus.
	Sleep, who knows nothing of anguish, Sleep, who knows nothing of pain,
	come to us with a kind breath,
	come happy, come happy, and bring happiness, lord.
	Hold over his eyes
	this light that is spread across them now.
	Come, come to me, healer.
	My son, look where you are going to stand,
	where you will step from here,
	in your thinking. You see already.
	What are we waiting for?
	The right moment is the judge of everything,
	and wins a great, great victory
	at a single step.
Neoptolemus.
	No. He hears nothing. But I see this: we have hunted this bow for nothing
	if we sail without him.
	His is the crown. He is the one the god said to bring.
	To boast of a thing half done, and done with lies, is a foul disgrace.
Chorus.
	That, my son, a god will see to.
	But when you answer me again,
	softly, my son, softly,
	send me your words.
	In sickness every man's sleep is sleepless;
	it sees sharply.
	But as far as you can, as far as ever you can,
	that — that is what to look to, secretly:
	how you will bring it off.
	You know whom I mean.
	If you keep the mind you have now towards him,
	a shrewd man can see troubles ahead with no way out.
Chorus.
	A wind, my son, a fair wind.
	The man is eyeless, helpless,
	stretched out in his night —
	sleep in the sun is a good sleep —
	master of no hand, no foot, of nothing,
	like a man lying beside Hades.
	Look. See if what you say
	is timely. What my mind can take hold of, boy,
	is this: the work that frightens no one
	is the best work.

Neoptolemus. Quiet, I tell you, and keep your wits. The man is moving his eyes and lifting his head.

Philoctetes. Light, after sleep. And you — watchers, keeping guard by me, past anything I could have hoped for from strangers. I could never have boasted of this, boy: that you would bear to stay by my sufferings, pitying, standing by me and helping. The sons of Atreus certainly never had the stomach to bear it so lightly, those fine generals. But your nature is good, my son, and comes from good men, and so you made light of all of it, though you were full of my screaming and my stench. And now, since the evil seems to be giving me some forgetting and rest, son, you lift me up yourself, you set me on my feet, so that when the weakness lets me go we can start for the ship and not hold back from sailing.

Neoptolemus. I am glad to see you, past what I hoped, looking about you with no pain and still breathing. From your symptoms, set against the trouble you were in, you looked like a man already gone. Now lift yourself up. Or if you would rather, these men will carry you. They will not shirk the labour, since you and I have decided to do this.

Philoctetes. I thank you for that, boy. Lift me yourself, as you meant to. Leave them alone, so they are not weighed down with the foul smell before they have to be. It will be trouble enough for them, living on board with me.

Neoptolemus. So be it. Stand up, then, and hold on to me.

Philoctetes. Do not worry. My old habit will get me upright.

Neoptolemus. Papai. What am I to do now, from here?

Philoctetes. What is it, boy? Where has your talk gone wandering?

Neoptolemus. I do not know where to turn. There is no way through what I have to say.

Philoctetes. No way through — for you? Do not say that, son.

Neoptolemus. But that is where I am now. This is what it has come to.

Philoctetes. It is not the foulness of my sickness that has talked you out of taking me on board?

Neoptolemus. Everything is foul, when a man leaves his own nature and does what does not fit him.

Philoctetes. But you are doing nothing, saying nothing, that goes beyond the man who fathered you, when you help a good man.

Neoptolemus. I will be shown up for a disgrace. That is what has been eating me a long while.

Philoctetes. Not for anything you do. But what you say — that is starting to frighten me.

Neoptolemus. Zeus, what am I to do? Am I to be caught out twice over as bad — hiding what I should not hide, and saying the foulest words there are?

Philoctetes. This man, unless I am a poor judge, means to betray me: leave me here, and sail.

Neoptolemus. Leave you? Not I. Take you — and hurt you by it. That is what has been eating me.

Philoctetes. What are you saying, son? I do not understand.

Neoptolemus. I will hide nothing. You must sail to Troy. To the Greeks, and the sons of Atreus and their fleet.

Philoctetes. Oh. What did you say?

Neoptolemus. Do not groan before you understand.

Philoctetes. Understand what? What do you mean to do to me?

Neoptolemus. Save you from this misery first. Then go with you and lay waste the plains of Troy.

Philoctetes. And you truly mean to do this?

Neoptolemus. A great necessity governs it. Do not be angry to hear it.

Philoctetes. I am finished. Wretched — betrayed. What have you done to me, stranger? Give me back my bow. Now.

Neoptolemus. It cannot be done. Justice and my own interest, both of them, make me obey the men in command.

Philoctetes. You fire — you thing of pure terror — you hateful masterpiece of terrible cunning. What you have done to me. How you have cheated me. Are you not ashamed to look at me — the suppliant who turned to you, the man who begged you, you brute? You took the bow, and you took my life with it. Give it back, I beg you. Give it back, I implore you, son. By the gods of your fathers, do not take my life from me. Oh, I am wretched. He does not even speak to me any more. He looks away, like a man who will never let go.

Harbours. Headlands. You mountain beasts who lived here with me. You broken rocks. To you — I know no one else to say it to — to you who are here, my old companions, I cry out what the son of Achilles has done to me. He swore to take me home, and he takes me to Troy. He gave me his right hand on it, and he has taken my bow, the holy bow of Heracles, son of Zeus, and keeps it, and wants to show it to the Greeks. He drags me off by force as if he had caught a strong man, and does not see that he is killing a corpse, a shadow of smoke, a phantom and nothing more. He could never have taken me with my strength in me. Not even as I am, except by a trick. Now I have been cheated, damned as I am. What am I to do?

Give it back. Even now — come back to yourself. What do you say? Silence. I am nothing, I am damned. Cave, you rock with two doors, back I come to you, back again, stripped, with nothing to live on. I will wither in that den alone, bringing down no winged bird with this bow, no beast that walks the hills; I will die there myself, wretched, and make a meal for the creatures I fed on, and the ones I hunted will hunt me now. Blood for blood — I will pay it, wretched, at the hands of a man who seemed to know nothing of evil. Damn you — no. Not yet. Not till I learn whether you will change your mind again. If you will not, die a bad death.

Chorus. What do we do? It is with you, my lord, whether we sail now, or give way to what this man says.

Neoptolemus. A terrible pity for this man has fallen on me — not now for the first time, but long since.

Philoctetes. Have mercy, boy, by the gods. Do not let men call you a disgrace for cheating me.

Neoptolemus. Oh, what am I to do? I wish I had never left Scyros. That is how sick I am at what is happening.

Philoctetes. You are not bad. You came here, it seems, having learned bad things from bad men. Leave that to the others — the ones it suits — and sail. Give me my weapons, and go.

Neoptolemus. What do we do, men?

(Odysseus comes out from where he has been hiding, with two of his men behind him.)

Odysseus. You worst of men, what are you doing? Get back here and hand that bow to me.

Philoctetes. Oh. Who is that? Is that Odysseus I hear?

Odysseus. Odysseus. Be sure of it. Me, the man you are looking at.

Philoctetes. Oh. I am sold, and I am finished. So this was the man who trapped me and stripped me of my weapons.

Odysseus. I did. Be sure of that too. Nobody else. I admit it freely.

Philoctetes. Give it back. Boy, let me have the bow.

Odysseus. That he will never do, not even if he wanted to. And what is more, you are coming with it. Or these men will take you by force.

Philoctetes. Me? You worst of the bad, you most shameless of them all, these men will take me by force?

Odysseus. If you will not crawl there on your own.

Philoctetes. Land of Lemnos. Fire that Hephaestus made, that masters everything. Are you going to put up with this, this man dragging me off your ground by force?

Odysseus. It is Zeus, understand that. Zeus, who rules this land. Zeus, who has decided it. I am only his servant.

Philoctetes. You hateful thing. What things you find to say. You hold the gods up in front of you, and you make liars of the gods.

Odysseus. No. Truth-tellers. And the road has to be walked.

Philoctetes. I say no.

Odysseus. And I say yes. You will do as you are told.

Philoctetes. Oh, I am wretched. So my father bred me a slave, plainly, and not a free man.

Odysseus. No. The equal of the best men there are, the men you are going to take Troy with, and pull it down by force.

Philoctetes. Never. Not if I have to suffer every evil there is. Not while this island's steep rock is under my feet.

Odysseus. What are you going to do?

Philoctetes. Throw myself off. This head of mine, I will bloody it on the rocks below, right now.

Odysseus. Hold him, both of you. Do not let him have that.

Philoctetes. My hands. Look what is done to you, for want of the bowstring you love, hunted down, both of you, and roped by this man.

And you, you who have never had one healthy thought or one free one, how you crept up on me again. How you hunted me. You took this boy for your shield, a boy I did not know, who was too good for you and good enough for me; who knew nothing except to do what he was ordered, and who is plainly in pain now, even now, over what he did wrong himself and over what I have suffered. But that bad soul of yours, always peering out from some corner, taught him well. He had no gift for it and no wish for it, and still you taught him to be clever at evil.

And now, you wretch, you mean to tie me up and take me from this shore, the same shore where you threw me out: no friend, no company, no city, a corpse among the living. Ah. Damn you. I have prayed that for you often enough. But the gods deal me out nothing sweet, so you are alive and glad of it, and I have the pain, this exact pain, that I am alive at all, with all these evils on me, and laughed at by you and by the two sons of Atreus, the pair of generals you do these errands for. And yet you sailed with them only when they yoked you by a trick and forced you. I sailed of my own free will, with seven ships, to my utter ruin, and they threw me out with no honour. So you say. They say it was you.

And now what are you taking me for? Why haul me off? What for? I am nothing. To you I have been dead for years.
