How is it, you thing the gods hate most, that I am not lame to you any more, and not stinking? How will you burn your offerings to the gods if I sail with you? How will you pour them a drink? That was your excuse for throwing me out in the first place.

A bad death to you. And you will die badly, for the wrong you have done this man, if the gods care anything for justice. And I know they care. You would never have sailed on this errand for one miserable man if some goad from the gods had not driven you here after me.

Land of my fathers. Gods who look down and see. Pay them back. Pay them back, all of them, in time, at last, if there is any pity in you for me. My life is a pitiful thing. But if I saw those men destroyed, I would think I had got clear of my sickness.

Chorus. That is a hard man, Odysseus, and hard words he has said. His troubles have not bent him.

Odysseus. I could say a good deal in answer to that, if I had the time. As it is, I have one thing to say. Where a certain kind of man is needed, I am that kind of man. And where the contest is one of just and good men, you would not find anyone more god-fearing than me. It is my nature to want to win, everywhere, except against you. To you, this once, I will gladly give way.

Let him go. Take your hands off him. Let him stay. We have no further need of you, not now we have these weapons. Teucer is with us, and he has the skill; and so have I. I think I could handle this bow no worse than you, and point it no worse. So what do we need you for? Goodbye. Enjoy walking about on Lemnos. We are leaving. And perhaps this prize of yours will bring me the honour that should have been yours.

Philoctetes. Oh, what am I to do, in my bad luck? Are you going to show yourself to the Greeks decked out in my weapons?

Odysseus. Not another word to me. I am leaving.

Philoctetes. Seed of Achilles, will you not even speak to me? Are you going to walk off like that?

Odysseus. Move. Do not look at him. You are a decent man, and you will wreck our luck.

Philoctetes. And you, strangers, will you leave me here too, alone like this? Have you no pity for me?

Chorus. The boy here commands our ship. Whatever he says to you, we say too.

Neoptolemus. I am going to be told by this man that I am too full of pity. All the same, stay, if he agrees to it, for as long as it takes the sailors to get the ship ready and for us to make our prayers to the gods. In that time perhaps he will come to think better of us. We two are going. And you, when we call, come quickly.

(Odysseus and Neoptolemus go out towards the ships. Philoctetes is left with the Chorus.)

Philoctetes.
	Hollow cave in the rock,
	hot, and then freezing. So I was never
	going to leave you after all. Poor fool.
	You will be with me while I die.
	Oh. Oh, oh.
	Wretched shelter, filled to the brim
	with the pain that came out of me,
	what will there be for me now, day by day?
	Where will I find any hope of food?
	Who will feed me?
	Up there the timid doves
	will go by on the shrill wind.
	I cannot stop them any more.
Chorus.
	You did this. You, I say. You chose it,
	you and your heavy luck.
	This did not come from anywhere else,
	from some power stronger than you.
	You could have seen sense,
	and you chose to praise the worse fate
	over the better.
Philoctetes.
	Wretched, then. Wretched,
	and broken by suffering. From now on
	I live here with no man after this,
	and I die here.
	Aiai. Aiai.
	No more bringing home food,
	no more winged arrows in my strong hands
	to take it with.
	Words I could not see through, words with a lie inside,
	from a crooked mind, crept under me.
	Let me see him,
	the man who planned it,
	allotted my pain for exactly as long as I have had it.
Chorus.
	Fate. Fate from the gods did this to you.
	No trick from my hand caught you.
	Turn that hateful, ill-fated curse of yours
	onto other men.
	Because I care about this too:
	do not push away my friendship.
Philoctetes.
	Oh, oh. Somewhere on the grey shore
	he is sitting, laughing at me,
	tossing in his hand the thing that fed me,
	my poor life's food, the bow nobody else ever carried.
	Dear bow, forced out of the hands that loved you,
	if you have any feeling
	you must look with pity
	at the friend of Heracles, who will never use you again.
	Now you are handled in another man's turn,
	a man of many devices;
	you are looking at shameful tricks,
	at a hateful face, my enemy's,
	and at the countless evils
	that rose out of shameful things,
	the evils he planned against me. Oh, Zeus.
Chorus.
	A man should always say what is right.
	But once he has said it,
	let him not spit out spiteful, stinging words.
	That man was one man, picked from many,
	and at their command he did
	what was a service to all his friends.
Philoctetes.
	Winged things I hunted,
	and tribes of bright-eyed beasts
	that this ground feeds on the hills,
	you need not leap from your dens
	and run from me any more. My hands
	do not hold the strength of the arrows they held before.
	I am wretched now.
	Come freely. The place is not fearful
	to you any more. Crawl in.
	Now it is a fine time to fill your mouths
	with blood for blood,
	on my discoloured flesh, all you want.
	I will be leaving life soon.
	Where would food come from?
	Who feeds on the winds like this,
	with no power any more over anything
	the life-giving earth sends up?
Chorus.
	By the gods, if you have any respect for a stranger,
	come near to one who comes near you with nothing but good will.
	But know this. Know it well:
	it is in your own hands to escape this doom.
	It is a pitiful thing to feed it,
	and to have no way of learning to carry
	the endless weight that lives with it.
Philoctetes.
	Again. Again you have brought the old pain back to me,
	you, the best of all who ever came here.
	Why have you destroyed me? What have you done to me?
Chorus.
	What do you mean?
Philoctetes.
	If you hoped to take me
	to the land of Troy, which I hate.
Chorus.
	I think that is the best thing.
Philoctetes.
	Then leave me. Now.
Chorus.
	Gladly. Gladly. That is an order I am happy to obey.
	Let us go, let us go,
	each to his place in the ship.
Philoctetes.
	No. By Zeus who hears a curse, do not go. I beg you.
Chorus.
	Be reasonable.
Philoctetes.
	Strangers. Stay. By the gods.
Chorus.
	Why are you shouting?
Philoctetes.
	Aiai. Aiai. My god, my god.
	I am finished. Foot, my foot,
	what am I going to do with you
	for the rest of my life?
	Strangers, come back to me. Come back.
Chorus.
	To do what? With what different mind
	from what you showed us before?
Philoctetes.
	There is no call for anger
	when a man is out of his wits
	with a storm of pain, and talks nonsense.
Chorus.
	Then come, poor man, the way I tell you.
Philoctetes.
	Never. Never. Understand that. It is fixed.
	Not even if the lord of the fire and the lightning
	comes to burn me in the blaze of his thunder.
	Damn Troy, and every man under its walls
	who could bear to send this foot of mine away.
	But, strangers, give me one thing I ask.
Chorus.
	What thing is that?
Philoctetes.
	A sword, if you have one anywhere,
	or an axe, or any weapon. Hand it to me.
Chorus.
	So you can do what with it?
Philoctetes.
	Cut off all this flesh, cut off my limbs, with my own hand.
	My mind is on killing. Killing, now.
Chorus.
	Why?
Philoctetes.
	Looking for my father.
Chorus.
	Where on earth?
Philoctetes.
	In Hades. He is not in the light any more.
	My city. My father's country.
	How could I look at you, wretched man that I am,
	who left your holy river
	and went to help the Greeks, my enemies.
	I am nothing now. Nothing.

(He goes into the cave.)

Chorus. I would have been on my way to my ship long ago, if I had not seen Odysseus coming this way, and the son of Achilles with him, heading for us.

(Neoptolemus comes in fast, carrying the bow. Odysseus is behind him.)

Odysseus. Will you tell me why you have turned round and come back up this road in such a hurry?

Neoptolemus. To undo the wrong I did before.

Odysseus. That is an alarming thing to say. What wrong?

Neoptolemus. The one I did when I obeyed you and the whole army —

Odysseus. What did you do that was beneath you?

Neoptolemus. I took a man with shameful tricks and lies.

Odysseus. Which man? Oh no. You are not planning something new?

Neoptolemus. Nothing new. But to the son of Poeas —

Odysseus. What are you going to do? Something is frightening me.

Neoptolemus. — from whom I took this bow, back again —

Odysseus. Zeus. What are you going to say? You do not mean to give it back?

Neoptolemus. I do. I got it shamefully, and with no right to it.

Odysseus. By the gods, are you saying this to mock me?

Neoptolemus. If telling the truth is mockery.

Odysseus. What are you saying, son of Achilles? What are these words?

Neoptolemus. Do you want me to go over the same thing twice and three times?

Odysseus. I would rather not have heard it once.

Neoptolemus. Then you have heard all I have to say. Be sure of that.

Odysseus. There is someone, there is, who will stop you doing it.

Neoptolemus. What? Who is going to stop me?

Odysseus. The whole people of the Greeks. And I am one of them.

Neoptolemus. Clever as you were born, there is nothing clever in what you are saying.

Odysseus. And nothing clever in what you say, or in what you want to do.

Neoptolemus. But if it is just, it is better than clever.

Odysseus. And how is it just, to let go of what you won by my planning?

Neoptolemus. I made a shameful mistake. I am going to try to take it back.

Odysseus. And the Greek army does not frighten you, doing this?

Neoptolemus. With justice on my side, I am not afraid of the fear you are offering me.

Odysseus. With justice on my side, my hand will make you.

Neoptolemus. Not your hand either. I am not going to be made to do it.

Odysseus. Then it is not the Trojans we fight. It is you.

Neoptolemus. Let what is coming come.

Odysseus. Do you see my right hand, on the hilt of my sword?

Neoptolemus. You will see mine do the same. And not slowly.

Odysseus. Still, I will leave you be. I am going to tell all this to the whole army, and it will punish you.

Neoptolemus. Now you are being sensible. Go on thinking like that and perhaps you will keep your feet out of trouble.

(Odysseus goes, but not far.)

Son of Poeas. Philoctetes, I mean you. Come out. Leave that stone roof of yours.

(Philoctetes appears at the mouth of the cave.)

Philoctetes. What is this shouting again at my cave? Why are you calling me out? What do you want, strangers? Oh. This is bad. Have you come to bring me new evils on top of the old?

Neoptolemus. Take heart. Listen to what I have come to tell you.

Philoctetes. I am afraid to. Fine words did me harm before, when I believed what you said.

Neoptolemus. Is there no room for a man to change his mind?

Philoctetes. You talked like this when you were stealing my bow. To be trusted, and ruin underneath.

Neoptolemus. Not now. Not this time. I want to hear from you: have you decided to stay here and stick it out, or to sail with us?

Philoctetes. Stop. Not another word. Anything you say will be said for nothing.

Neoptolemus. That is decided?

Philoctetes. More firmly than I can say.

Neoptolemus. Well. I would have liked you to be persuaded by what I say. But if nothing I say is any use, I am finished.

Philoctetes. Everything you say is for nothing. You will never have my good will, you who took my life away with tricks, robbed me of it, and then come here to lecture me. The worst son of the best father. Damn all of you: the sons of Atreus first, then the son of Laertes, then you.

Neoptolemus. No more curses. Take these weapons from my hand.

Philoctetes. What did you say? Am I being tricked a second time?

Neoptolemus. No. I swear it by the holy majesty of Zeus most high.

Philoctetes. The dearest words I ever heard, if they are true.

Neoptolemus. What I do will make it plain. Hold out your right hand. Be master of your weapons.

(He gives him the bow. Odysseus steps out.)

Odysseus. And I forbid it, with the gods as my witnesses, in the name of the sons of Atreus and the whole army.

Philoctetes. Boy, whose voice was that? Was that Odysseus I heard?

Odysseus. It was. And you can see him, close by: the man who is going to ship you to the plains of Troy by force, whether the son of Achilles likes it or not.

Philoctetes. Not gladly, you will not. Not if this arrow flies straight.

(He fits an arrow to the string. Odysseus runs.)

Neoptolemus. No. No, by the gods, do not let it go.

Philoctetes. Let go of my hand, by the gods. Dear boy, let go.

Neoptolemus. I will not.

Philoctetes. Ah. Why did you stop me killing him? A man who hates me, an enemy, with my own bow.

Neoptolemus. Because it would be no credit to me, and none to you.

Philoctetes. Well, know this much anyway: the first men of the army, the Greeks' lying heralds, are cowards with a spear and bold with words.

Neoptolemus. Good. You have the bow, and there is nothing left for you to be angry with me about, or to blame me for.

Philoctetes. Agreed. You have shown the stock you sprang from, boy. Not Sisyphus for a father. Achilles, who had the best name of all men while he was alive, and has it now among the dead.

Neoptolemus. I am glad to hear you speak well of my father, and of me. Now hear what I want from you. Men have to bear what the gods send them. But when men sit inside a harm they chose for themselves, as you do, there is no justice in forgiving them, and no justice in pitying them either. You have gone wild. You will take no advice; if someone tries to set you right, meaning nothing but good, you hate him and count him an enemy who wishes you ill.

I am going to say it anyway, and I call on Zeus who watches over oaths. Understand this, and write it inside your mind. You are sick with this pain because a god sent it, because you came too close to the guardian of Chryse, the snake that keeps hidden watch over her open-air shrine. And know that you will never find rest from this heavy sickness, as long as this same sun rises on one side of the sky and sets on the other, until you come of your own free will to the plains of Troy and meet the two sons of Asclepius, the healers, who are with us, and are eased of this sickness, and are seen to take the towers of Troy, with this bow and with me.

How I know this is so, I will tell you. We have a man taken from Troy, Helenus, the best of the prophets, who says plainly that this must happen. And more: that this very summer all Troy must fall. If he is lying, he offers himself up freely to be killed.

So now that you know it, give way, and willingly. It is a fine thing to gain: to be judged the one best man of the Greeks, and first to come into hands that heal you, and then to take Troy, which has cost so many tears, and win the highest fame there is.

Philoctetes. Hateful life. Why, why do you keep me up here looking at the light, and not let me go down to Hades? Oh, what am I to do? How can I refuse to believe what this man says, when he meant well by me in saying it? Then give way? And then how do I come out into the light, in my bad luck, having done that? Who would I speak to?

You eyes that have watched everything that was done around me, how could you bear to see it: me, keeping company with the sons of Atreus, who destroyed me? Or with that son of Laertes, ruin to everything he touches? It is not the pain of what is past that bites me. It is what I think I can see coming, what I will have to suffer at their hands still. When a man's mind has once given birth to wrongdoing, it teaches him to be a wrongdoer in everything after.

And you. I am amazed at you in this. You ought never to go back to Troy yourself, and you ought to keep me from it. They did you outrage. They stripped you of your father's prize, when they judged poor Ajax a lesser man than Odysseus in the contest for the arms. And after that you are going to fight beside them, and force me to as well? No, boy. Not that. Do what you swore to me: send me home. Stay on Scyros yourself, and let bad men die badly. Do that, and you will have thanks from me twice over, and twice over from your father, and you will not look like a man born the same as the bad men because you helped them.

Neoptolemus. What you say is reasonable. But all the same I want you to trust the gods, and trust what I say, and sail out of this land with me, a friend.

Philoctetes. To the plains of Troy? To the hateful son of Atreus? On this wretched foot?

Neoptolemus. To the men who will stop the pain in that foot, with the pus in it, and save you from your sickness.

Philoctetes. That is a terrible thing to recommend. What are you saying?

Neoptolemus. What I see is best for you and for me, once it is done.

Philoctetes. You say that, and feel no shame before the gods?

Neoptolemus. How could a man be ashamed of helping his own?

Philoctetes. Helping the sons of Atreus, do you mean, or me?

Neoptolemus. You, I should think. I am your friend, and that is a friend talking.

Philoctetes. How, when you want to hand me to my enemies?

Neoptolemus. Sir. Learn not to be so defiant when things are bad.

Philoctetes. You will destroy me with this talk. I know you.

Neoptolemus. I will not. I say you do not understand.

Philoctetes. Do I not know the sons of Atreus threw me out?

Neoptolemus. They threw you out. Now see whether they will not save you.

Philoctetes. Never. Not if it means agreeing to look at Troy.

Neoptolemus. Then what are we to do, if nothing I say can persuade you of anything? The easiest thing is for me to stop talking, and for you to go on living as you live now, with no way out.

Philoctetes. Let me suffer what I have to suffer. But what you promised me with your right hand in mine, to take me home, do that for me, boy. Do not delay, and do not mention Troy to me again. I have had enough of weeping and lamenting.

Neoptolemus. If that is what you want, let us go.

Philoctetes. That is a noble thing to say.

Neoptolemus. Now set your feet firmly against mine.

Philoctetes. As far as I have the strength.

Neoptolemus. But how do I escape the blame of the Greeks?

Philoctetes. Do not think about it.

Neoptolemus. And if they come and lay waste my country?

Philoctetes. I will be there —

Neoptolemus. And what help will you be?

Philoctetes. — with the arrows of Heracles —

Neoptolemus. Meaning what?

Philoctetes. — I will keep them off.

Neoptolemus. Then kiss the ground goodbye, and come.

(Heracles appears above them.)

Heracles.
	Not yet. Not until you have heard
	what I have to say, son of Poeas.
	Know that it is the voice of Heracles
	your ears are hearing, and his face your eyes see.
	For your sake I have come,
	leaving my seat in the sky,
	to tell you the plans of Zeus
	and to hold you back from the road you are taking.
	Listen to my words.
	First I will tell you my own fortunes:
	how many labours I laboured through to the end,
	and won undying excellence, as you can see.
	For you too, be sure of it, this is what is owed:
	out of these sufferings, to make your life famous.
	You will go with this man to the city of Troy.
	First you will be cured of your cruel sickness.
	Then, judged first of the whole army in excellence,
	you will take Paris, the cause of all this evil,
	and with my bow you will part him from his life.
	You will sack Troy. You will send the spoils home,
	the prize for the bravest, taken from the army,
	to your father Poeas, on the high ground of Oeta.
	And whatever spoils you take from that army,
	carry to my pyre, in memory of my bow.
	And this is for you as well, son of Achilles:
	you cannot take the plain of Troy without him,
	and he cannot without you.
	Guard each other, like two lions who hunt together,
	he you, and you him.
	I will send Asclepius to Troy to end your sickness.
	It is fated to fall a second time to my bow.
	But remember this, when you sack the land:
	show reverence to the gods.
	Father Zeus counts everything else second.
	Reverence does not die when men die.
	Alive or dead, they do not lose it.
Philoctetes.
	Voice I longed for. You sent it to me
	and showed yourself at last.
	I will not disobey what you say.
Neoptolemus.
	And I set my judgement the same way.
Heracles.
	Then do not wait long. Act.
	The moment is right, and the wind
	is pushing at your stern.
Philoctetes.
	Then let me call to the land as I go.
	Goodbye, house that kept watch with me.
	Goodbye, nymphs of the water-meadows,
	and you, deep pounding of the sea on the headland,
	where so often my head was wet
	far inside the cave from the blows of the south wind,
	and often the hill of Hermes sent my own voice back,
	groan for groan, when I was in the storm.
	Now, springs, and you Lycian water,
	I am leaving you, leaving you at last.
	I never thought I would stand on this hope.
	Goodbye, Lemnos, ground with the sea all round you.
	Send me off with a fair wind, and no blame,
	to where great Fate is carrying me,
	and the judgement of my friends, and the god
	who tames everything, who brought this to pass.
Chorus.
	Let us all go now, together,
	after a prayer to the nymphs of the sea
	to come and see us safely home.

Oedipus at Colonus (401 BC)

Oedipus at Colonus

(The edge of a grove at Colonus, a mile or so outside Athens. Oedipus, blind and old, comes in led by Antigone.)

Oedipus. Antigone, child of a blind old man — where have we come to? Whose city is this? Who is going to take in the wanderer Oedipus today, with a little something to spare for him? I ask for little, and I get less than that, and it is enough for me. Suffering has taught me to be content, and the long years I have lived with it, and thirdly the good blood in me. Now, child, if you see a place to sit, on open ground or in some grove of the gods, stop me and settle me there, so we can find out where we are. We are strangers here. We have come to learn from the townspeople, and to do whatever we are told.

Antigone. Father — poor worn-out Oedipus — the towers that shut in the city are a long way off, as far as I can see. And this place is holy, by the look of it: it is thick with laurel and olive and vine, and inside it the nightingales are singing, a whole crowd of them. Bend your legs and sit here, on this rough stone. It has been a long road for an old man.

Oedipus. Then sit me down, and keep watch over the blind man.

Antigone. I have been doing that long enough not to need telling.

Oedipus. Can you tell me where we have stopped?

Antigone. I know it is Athens. I do not know this place.

Oedipus. Yes — every traveller on the road told us that much.

Antigone. Shall I go somewhere and find out what the place is called?

Oedipus. Yes, child, if anyone lives here.

Antigone. Someone lives here. But there is no need, I think. I can see a man, close by.

Oedipus. Coming this way? Heading for us?

(A man of Colonus has come up to them.)

Antigone. He is here already. Whatever seems right to you to say, say it. The man is in front of you.

Oedipus. Stranger — this girl sees for both of us, and she tells me you have come at a lucky moment, a scout to explain what we do not know —

Stranger. Before you ask me anything else, get up off that seat. You are on ground it is not holy to walk on.

Oedipus. What ground is it? Which god does it belong to?

Stranger. Nobody touches it and nobody lives on it. The goddesses of fear hold it — the daughters of Earth and Darkness.

Oedipus. What holy name do they have? If I hear it I can pray to them.

Stranger. The people here would call them the Eumenides, the Kindly Ones, who see everything. Other places like other names.

Oedipus. Then may they be kind, and take in the man who kneels to them. I will not leave this seat, in this land, ever again.

Stranger. What is that supposed to mean?

Oedipus. It is the sign I was told to wait for. The password of my fate.

Stranger. Well, I would not dare move you myself without the city behind me, before I report what I am doing.

Oedipus. Then in the gods' name, stranger, do not refuse me — a wanderer, look at me — the answers I am begging you for.

Stranger. Ask. You will get no refusal from me.

Oedipus. What is this place, then, that we have come into?

Stranger. Everything I know, you will hear. This whole place is holy. Poseidon the revered holds it, and the fire-carrying god is in it too, the Titan Prometheus. And the spot you are standing on is called the bronze threshold of this land, the thing that props Athens up. The fields round about claim the horseman Colonus as their founder — that is his statue there — and all the people take his name together and call themselves by it. That is what this place is, stranger. It is not honoured in stories. It is honoured by living with it.

Oedipus. So people do live in these parts?

Stranger. Certainly. They are named for that god there, Colonus.

Oedipus. Does someone rule them? Or does the decision lie with the people?

Stranger. The king in the city rules here.

Oedipus. And who is he, this man with the power of word and of force?

Stranger. His name is Theseus, son of Aegeus who was king before him.

Oedipus. Could one of you go to him with a message?

Stranger. To say what? To get him to come?

Oedipus. To tell him that a small service will win him a great gain.

Stranger. And what help can there be from a man who cannot see?

Oedipus. Everything I say will have eyes in it.

Stranger. Listen, stranger — I do not want you coming to harm. You have good blood in you, anyone can see it, whatever your luck has been. Stay where I found you, right here, while I go and tell the people of this district — here, not in the city. They will decide for you whether you stay or go back the way you came.

(The stranger goes off the way he came.)

Oedipus. Child, has the stranger gone?

Antigone. He has gone. You can say anything you like now, father, in peace. There is nobody near you but me.

Oedipus. Ladies with the terrible eyes — since yours is the first seat in this land where I have bent my knees, do not be hard on Apollo, or on me. When he foretold all those evils for me, he told me this too: that after a long time I would find rest here. I would come to a land at the end of my journey, where I would find a seat of the Revered Goddesses and a place to shelter a stranger, and there I would round off my miserable life — a profit to the people who took me in, if I settled among them, and a ruin to the ones who sent me away, who drove me out. And he promised that signs would come to warn me: an earthquake, or thunder, or the lightning of Zeus.

I know now that it must have been some trustworthy sign from you that guided me on this road into this grove. Otherwise I would never have come across you first of all in my wandering — I who drink no wine, meeting you who take none — or sat down on this holy seat that no axe has shaped. So, goddesses, according to the word of Apollo, give me now some way to reach the end and close my life — unless you think me beneath your notice, a slave for ever to the worst troubles any man on earth has known. Come, sweet daughters of ancient Darkness. Come, Athens, called the city of great Athena, honoured above every city: pity this wretched ghost of the man Oedipus. It is not the body he once had.

Antigone. Quiet. Some men are coming — old men, by their years — to see who is sitting here.

Oedipus. I will be quiet. Hide me in the grove, off the road, until I hear what they say. Knowing is the safe way to act.

(They hide. The Chorus comes in, old men of Colonus, searching.)

Chorus.
	Look. Who was he? Where is he lodged?
	Where has he run to, out of the place,
	the most shameless man alive,
	of all men the most shameless?
	Search. Look for him. Hunt for him everywhere.
	A wanderer, that old man, a wanderer,
	not one of ours. He would never have walked
	into the untrodden grove
	of these girls no one can fight,
	whose name we tremble to speak,
	whom we pass without a look,
	without a sound, without a word,
	only the lips moving in silent prayer.
	And now they say someone has come
	with no fear of them at all,
	and I look across the whole holy ground
	and still I cannot find
	where he is hiding.
Oedipus.
	Here is the man. I am the one.
	I see by the voice, as people say.
Chorus.
	Oh. Oh.
	Terrible to look at. Terrible to hear.
Oedipus.
	I beg you — do not look at me as a lawbreaker.
Chorus.
	Zeus, keep us safe. Who is this old man?
Oedipus.
	Not one whose lot you would call happy,
	not the best of luck, guardians of this land.
	That is plain. Or I would not creep along
	on somebody else's eyes,
	a big man moored to a small one.
Chorus.
	Ah. Those blind eyes —
	were you born with them? A hard life,
	and a long one, by the look of you.
	But not with my help
	will you add this curse to it.
	You are trespassing. Trespassing.
	Do not stumble on into that silent glade
	deep in grass, where the bowl of water
	runs together with the honeyed drink.
	Stranger, cursed in everything, take care.
	Move. Come away. Come out.
	There is a long way between us.
	Do you hear, worn-out wanderer?
	If you have anything to say to us,
	come off the forbidden ground
	to where everyone may go, and speak there.
	Until then, keep off.
Oedipus.
	Daughter, what are we to think?
Antigone.
	Father, we must do as the townspeople do,
	giving way where we must, and listening.
Oedipus.
	Then take hold of me.
Antigone.
	I have you.
Oedipus.
	Strangers, do not let me be wronged
	for trusting you and moving from my place.
Chorus.
	Never, old man. Nobody will take you
	from this resting place against your will.
Oedipus.
	Further?
Chorus.
	Come further still.
Oedipus.
	Further?
Chorus.
	Lead him on, girl, further.
	You can hear what we say.
Antigone.
	Come, father, come this way
	with your dark steps, where I am leading you.
Chorus.
	Stranger in a strange land,
	poor man, bring yourself to hate
	what the city has learned to hate,
	and to honour what it loves.
Oedipus.
	Then lead me, child,
	to where we can stand on holy ground
	and speak, and listen,
	and stop fighting what has to be.
Chorus.
	There. Do not lean your foot
	past that ledge of natural rock.
Oedipus.
	Like this?
Chorus.
	Enough. You heard me.
Oedipus.
	Shall I sit?
Chorus.
	Yes — sideways, on the edge of the stone,
	crouch down low.
Antigone.
	Father, this is my job. Gently —
Oedipus.
	Oh. Oh no.
Antigone.
	— one step fitted to the next,
	and lean your old body
	on my arm, that loves you.
Oedipus.
	Oh, the ruin, the madness of it.
Chorus.
	Poor man, now that you are at ease,
	tell us: who are you, what man?
	Who is led here in such pain?
	What country can you name as yours?
Oedipus.
	Strangers, I have no city. But do not —
Chorus.
	Do not what? What are you forbidding, old man?
Oedipus.
	Do not, do not, do not ask me who I am.
	Do not question me. Do not dig further.
Chorus.
	Why?
Oedipus.
	A terrible birth.
Chorus.
	Speak.
Oedipus.
	Child — oh — what am I to say?
Chorus.
	What seed are you from, stranger? Speak. Who was your father?
Oedipus.
	Oh no. What is going to happen to me, my child?
Antigone.
	Say it. You are at the very edge now.
Oedipus.
	Then I will say it. There is no hiding it.
Chorus.
	You are both taking too long. Quickly.
Oedipus.
	You know of a son of Laius?
Chorus.
	Oh. Oh.
Oedipus.
	And the house of Labdacus?
Chorus.
	Oh, Zeus.
Oedipus.
	The wretched Oedipus?
Chorus.
	You are that man?
Oedipus.
	Do not be afraid of anything I say —
Chorus.
	Oh. Oh, no.
Oedipus.
	I am cursed.
Chorus.
	Oh. Oh.
Oedipus.
	Daughter, what happens now?
Chorus.
	Out. Get out of this country, far away.
Oedipus.
	And your promise? What will you do with that?
Chorus.
	No fate punishes a man
	who pays back what was first done to him.
	Trick set against trick
	is paid in pain, not thanks.
	You — up from this seat, out again,
	off my land, away with you, fast,
	before you fasten some heavier debt
	on my city.
Antigone.
	Strangers, you have respect in you.
	You will not put up with my old father,
	because you have heard the story
	of things he did without meaning to.
	Then pity me, I beg you, strangers,
	pity a wretched girl, who begs you
	for her father alone,
	who looks at you with eyes not blind,
	eyes meeting eyes, as if I were
	one of your own blood,
	and asks respect for a broken man.
	We lie in your hands as in a god's,
	we poor sufferers. Come, grant us
	the kindness we hardly dared to hope for.
	I beg you by whatever is dear to you at home,
	by a child, by a wife, by property, by a god.
	Look where you like, you will not find
	the man alive who could escape
	if a god were leading him.

Chorus. Daughter of Oedipus, be sure of this: we pity you, and we pity him as much, for what has happened to you. But we are afraid of what comes from the gods, and we cannot say anything beyond what we have said to you already.

Oedipus. Then what is the good of a reputation? What use is a fine name that runs away like water, if Athens, as they say, is the most god-fearing city there is, and the only one able to save a stranger who has been badly treated, and the only one able to help him? Where is any of that for me, when you have raised me up off this seat only to drive me out of the country — and all for fear of a name? Not of my body, surely, and not of my acts. My acts were things done to me, far more than things I did — if I have to speak of my mother and my father, which is what makes you afraid of me. I know that perfectly well. And yet how am I evil by nature? I was struck, and I struck back. Even if I had known what I was doing, I could not have been called evil for that. As it was, I knew nothing, and I went where I went — while the ones who wronged me knew exactly what they were doing, and were out to destroy me.

So I beg you by the gods, strangers: you made me stand up; now keep me safe. Do not honour the gods and then, in the same breath, treat the gods as if they were fools. Reckon instead that they look at the man who respects them, and they look at the man who does not, and that no godless man has ever yet escaped them. With those gods behind you, do not cloud over the good fortune of Athens by making yourselves the servants of an unholy act. You took the suppliant in on your word; now rescue me and guard me to the end. Do not look at this face — I know it is hard to look at — and dishonour me. I come here holy, a man who respects the gods, and I bring a benefit to the people of this city. When your master comes, whoever is your leader, you will hear all of it and understand. Until then, do not be bad to me.

Chorus. Old man, what you have laid before us has to be respected. You have not put it in a few words. But I am content that the lords of this land should judge the matter.

Oedipus. And where is the ruler of this country, strangers?

Chorus. He is in his father's city, in our land. The man who spotted you and sent us here has gone to fetch him.

Oedipus. And do you think he will have any regard for a blind man, or care enough to come himself?

Chorus. Certainly — the moment he hears your name.

Oedipus. And who is going to carry him that word?

Chorus. It is a long road, and travellers' talk gets around. He will hear it, and he will come; do not worry. Your name, old man, has reached everyone, and even if he is slow and half asleep, once he hears it is you he will come quickly enough.

Oedipus. Then let him come, and good fortune with him, for his city and for me. What good man is not a friend to himself?

Antigone. Zeus — what am I to say? What am I to think, father?

Oedipus. What is it, Antigone, child?

Antigone. I see a woman coming towards us, riding a pony from Sicily, and a wide Thessalian hat on her head shading her face from the sun. What can I say? Is it her? Is it not? Is my mind playing tricks? Yes — no — I cannot tell. Oh, I am wretched. It is no one else. Her eyes are bright, she is greeting me as she comes close. She is waving to me. Clearly it is Ismene, and nobody else.

Oedipus. What did you say, child?

Antigone. That I see your daughter, my own sister. In a moment you can know her by her voice.

(Ismene comes in, with one attendant.)

Ismene. Father — sister — the two sweetest words I have to say. It was hard to find you, and now I have found you, it is hard to see you through my tears.

Oedipus. Child, you have come?

Ismene. Father — what a sight you are.

Oedipus. Child, you are really here?

Ismene. It was not easy getting here.

Oedipus. Touch me, child.

Ismene. I have you both — one hand for each.

Oedipus. Oh, my own blood.

Ismene. Oh, what a wretched way to live.

Oedipus. Her life and mine?

Ismene. And mine, wretched as I am, makes three.

Oedipus. Child, why have you come?

Ismene. Out of concern for you, father.

Oedipus. Because you missed me?

Ismene. Yes, and to bring you news with my own mouth. I had one servant I could trust, and I brought him.

Oedipus. And the young men, your brothers — where are they, when there is work to be done?

Ismene. They are where they are. It is terrible between them now.

Oedipus. Those two! In their nature and the way they live they are copies of the Egyptians. Over there the men sit at home at the loom, and the wives go out and earn the daily bread. And you two, children — the ones who should be doing this work sit at home keeping house like girls, while you, in their place, carry your wretched father's troubles for him. This one, from the time she stopped being a child and her body grew strong, has wandered with me always, poor girl, an old man's guide, often going hungry and barefoot through wild woodland, often drenched by rain and burned by sun — hard going, and she puts the comforts of home second, so long as her father is looked after. And you, child — before this you came out to your father, without the Thebans knowing, bringing every oracle that had been given about this body of mine, and you stood guard for me faithfully when I was being driven from the land. And now what story have you brought your father, Ismene? What errand has sent you from home? You have not come empty-handed; I know that much for certain. You are bringing me something to fear.

Ismene. What I went through, father, hunting for the place where you were living — I will let that go. I do not want to suffer it twice, once in the doing and again in the telling. It is the trouble that is on your two unlucky sons now that I have come to tell you about.

At first they wanted to leave the throne to Creon and not stain the city, when they looked with cool heads at the old blight on our family and how it has clung to your wretched house. But now some god, and their own guilty minds, have put a wicked rivalry into the two of them — three times wretched — to seize the rule and the power of a tyrant. The younger one, the one born second, has stripped Polyneices, the elder, of the throne and driven him out of the country. And Polyneices — this is the story everywhere among us — has gone as an exile down to the valley of Argos, and there he is taking a new marriage and new shield-companions for allies, meaning that Argos will soon either hold the land of Thebes in honour, or lift Thebes' name to the sky. These are not just words piled up, father. They are terrible deeds. And where the gods will take pity on your sufferings, I cannot tell.

Oedipus. So you actually had hope that the gods would ever give me a thought, and that I would be saved one day?

Ismene. I do, father. From the new oracles.

Oedipus. What oracles? What has been prophesied, child?

Ismene. That the people there will want you one day, dead and alive, for their own safety's sake.

Oedipus. And who could be helped by a man like me?

Ismene. Their power, they say, comes to rest in you.

Oedipus. When I no longer exist, then I am a man?

Ismene. Yes. The gods are raising you up now. Before, they were destroying you.

Oedipus. It is a poor thing, to prop up an old man who fell when he was young.

Ismene. Well, know this: Creon is coming to you because of it — and soon, not in some far-off time.

Oedipus. To do what, daughter? Explain it to me.

Ismene. To settle you near the Theban border, so they have control of you, but you never set foot inside the land.

Oedipus. And what good am I to them, lying outside their gates?

Ismene. Your tomb, if it is neglected, weighs heavily on them.

Oedipus. A man could work that out without a god's help.

Ismene. That is why they want you close to the country — and not somewhere you would be your own master.

Oedipus. Will they at least cover me with Theban earth?

Ismene. No, father. The blood of your own family forbids it.

Oedipus. Then they will never get their hands on me.

Ismene. One day that will be a heavy thing for Thebes.

Oedipus. How? What will bring it about, child?

Ismene. Your anger, when they stand at your tomb.

Oedipus. And who told you what you are telling me?

Ismene. The sacred envoys, coming back from the hearth at Delphi.

Oedipus. And Apollo really said this about me?

Ismene. So say the men who came back to Thebes.

Oedipus. And has either of my sons heard it?

Ismene. Both of them, and they understand it well.

Oedipus. And then those two — the worst of men — heard this, and still put the throne before wanting me back?

Ismene. It hurts to hear it, but I bear it.

Oedipus. Then may the gods never put out the quarrel that is fated between them, and may the end of the fight they are set on — with spears already lifted — rest with me. Then the one who holds the sceptre and the throne now would not keep it, and the one who has gone out would never come back. When I, their father, was being shoved out of my own country in that disgrace, they did not stop it, they did not defend me; I was uprooted and sent out by them, and proclaimed an exile.

You might say the city gave me what I wanted then, and gave it reasonably. No. On that first day, when my heart was boiling and the sweetest thing would have been to die, to be stoned to death — nobody came forward to help me to what I wanted. Later, when the whole pain had softened and I had learned that my anger had run out ahead of me, a bigger punishment than my old mistakes deserved — then, after all that time, the city drove me out of the land by force, and they, the sons of their father, who could have helped their father, would not lift a finger. For the sake of one small word from them I was thrown out, a beggar, and I have wandered ever since.

From these two girls, as far as their nature allows, I get the food that keeps me alive, and safety on the road, and the support of my own family. Those two chose the throne over the father who made them — the sceptre, and ruling the land as tyrants. Well, they will not get me for an ally, and they will never have any joy of ruling Thebes. I know that, hearing this girl's oracles and thinking over the old prophecies Apollo made to me long ago, which he has now brought to pass. So let them send Creon to hunt me, and anyone else with power in the city. Strangers, if you are willing — together with the Revered Goddesses who stand guard over your people — to make a defence of me, you will win a great saviour for this city, and pains for my enemies.

Chorus. You deserve pity, Oedipus, you and these two girls with you. And since you add to that plea the claim that you will be this country's saviour, we want to give you some advice that is in your interest.

Oedipus. Dearest friends, be my sponsors here, and I will carry out all of it.

Chorus. Then make an act of cleansing to these goddesses, whose ground you walked on when you first came in.

Oedipus. What is the way of doing it? Teach me, strangers.

Chorus. First bring holy water for the offering from a spring that never runs dry, and touch it only with hands that are clean.

Oedipus. And when I have got this pure water?

Chorus. There are bowls, the work of a skilled hand. Wreathe the rims of them, and the handles on both sides.

Oedipus. With leafy twigs, or strips of wool, or in what way?

Chorus. With the new-shorn fleece of a young ewe.

Oedipus. Very well. And after that, how do I finish it?

Chorus. Pour the offering out, standing with your face to the first light of dawn.

Oedipus. Do I pour it from those jars you spoke of?

Chorus. Yes, three streams; and empty the last jar to the bottom.

Oedipus. And what do I fill that one with before I set it down? Teach me this too.

Chorus. With water and with honey. Bring no wine near it.

Oedipus. And when the earth under its dark leaves has taken all this?

Chorus. Lay three times nine sprigs of olive on it with both your hands, and say this prayer over them.

Oedipus. I want to hear the prayer. That is the most important part.

Chorus. We call them the Kindly Ones. So ask them, out of hearts that are kindly, to receive the man who begs their protection and to keep him safe. Ask it yourself, or let someone else ask it for you, speaking so low that no one can hear and never raising your voice. Then walk away, and do not turn round. Do all that and we would stand beside you with a good heart. Otherwise, stranger, we would be afraid for you.

Oedipus. Daughters, do you hear what these men of the place are saying?

Antigone. We heard. Tell us what has to be done.

Oedipus. I cannot make the walk. Two things are against me: I have no strength and I have no eyes. One of you must go and do it in my place. I think one soul can pay this debt for ten thousand, if it comes with a good will. But do it quickly. Only do not leave me alone. This body of mine has not the strength to crawl about with no one there and no hand to guide it.

Ismene. I will go and do it. But I want to be told where I am to find the place.

Chorus. On the far side of this grove, young woman. And if you find you need anything, there is a keeper of the place who will show you.

Ismene. Then I will go. Antigone, you stay here and watch over our father. When it is for a parent, whatever the labour is, it is not to be counted as labour.

(Ismene goes into the grove.)

Chorus.
	It is a terrible thing, stranger, to wake an old evil
	that has lain quiet so long. And still I long to ask.
Oedipus.
	Ask what?
Chorus.
	About that misery with no way out of it,
	the pain you have wrestled with.
Oedipus.
	By the welcome you have given me, do not open up
	the shameful things that were done to me.
Chorus.
	The story is everywhere and it never dies down.
	I want to hear it, friend, told straight.
Oedipus.
	Oh.
Chorus.
	Give way. I beg you.
Oedipus.
	Oh. Oh.
Chorus.
	Do as I ask. I did all that you asked of me.
Oedipus.
	Then I have borne the worst of evils, strangers.
	I bore it, but not by my own will.
	Let a god be witness.
	Not one part of it was chosen.
Chorus.
	But what was it?
Oedipus.
	A wicked marriage. The city tied me,
	knowing nothing, to a bed that was my ruin.
Chorus.
	Is it true, as I hear, that you filled
	your mother's bed, a bed with an evil name?
Oedipus.
	Oh. It is death to hear it said, stranger.
	And these two girls of mine —
Chorus.
	What are you saying?
Oedipus.
	Two children. Two curses —
Chorus.
	Oh, Zeus.
Oedipus.
	— born from the same womb that bore me.
Chorus.
	Then they are your children, and also —
Oedipus.
	Yes. Their own father's sisters.
Chorus.
	Oh.
Oedipus.
	Oh, yes. Ten thousand evils, wheeling back on me.
Chorus.
	You suffered —
Oedipus.
	I suffered what can never be forgotten.
Chorus.
	You did it —
Oedipus.
	I did not do it.
Chorus.
	Then what?
Oedipus.
	I was given a gift.
	I wish to my aching heart I had never
	earned that gift from the city I had served.
Chorus.
	Wretched man. And then? The killing —
Oedipus.
	What is this? What do you want to know?
Chorus.
	— of your father?
Oedipus.
	Ah. You strike a second time. Wound on top of wound.
Chorus.
	You killed —
Oedipus.
	I killed. But there is something in it for me —
Chorus.
	What?
Oedipus.
	— on the side of justice.
Chorus.
	How?
Oedipus.
	I will tell you.
	The men I killed would have killed me.
	Before the law I am clean. I came to it not knowing.

Chorus. Look, here is our king, Theseus, son of Aegeus. Your message reached him, and he has come to do what it asked.

(Theseus comes in.)

Theseus. I have heard from so many people over the years about the bloody ruin of your eyes that I knew who you were, son of Laius; and what I have been told on the road just now makes me more certain still. Your clothes and that unhappy face tell us plainly who you are. I pity you, and I want to ask you, ill-starred Oedipus, what it is you have come to beg from my city and from me, you and the poor girl who stands beside you. Tell me. It would have to be some terrible business you described before I would turn my back on it. I know what it is to be brought up in exile, as you were, and in foreign lands I have wrestled with more dangers to my own life than any man alive. So I would never turn away from a stranger like you and refuse to help him to safety. I know very well that I am a man, and that I have no larger share in tomorrow than you have.

Oedipus. Theseus, your generosity in a few words has left me needing only a few in reply. You have said yourself who I am, who my father was and what land I come from. So nothing is left for me but to say what I want, and then the talking is over.

Theseus. Then tell me exactly that, so that I understand it.

Oedipus. I have come to give you my wretched body as a gift. It is not much to look at. But what comes of it is worth more than a handsome shape.

Theseus. And what is this gain you claim to be bringing?

Oedipus. In time you will learn it. Not now, I think.

Theseus. Then when will your offering show itself?

Oedipus. When I am dead, and you have buried me.

Theseus. You are asking for the last service of a life. What lies in between, you have either forgotten or you count as nothing.

Oedipus. Yes. Because in that one thing all the rest is gathered up for me.

Theseus. Well, then, this favour you ask of me is a small one.

Oedipus. Be careful, though. This is not a small struggle. No, it is not.

Theseus. Do you mean between your sons and me?

Oedipus. They will want to carry me back there, my lord. To Thebes.

Theseus. But if you are willing to go, exile is no fit thing even for you.

Oedipus. No. When I was willing, they would not allow it.

Theseus. Fool. Anger is no use to a man in trouble.

Oedipus. When you have heard me out, lecture me. Until then, let it be.

Theseus. Speak. I should not say anything without understanding.

Oedipus. I have suffered, Theseus. Terrible things, evil on top of evil.

Theseus. Do you mean the old disaster of your family?

Oedipus. No. Every Greek talks about that.

Theseus. Then what is this sickness of yours that is bigger than a man's?

Oedipus. It is this. I was driven out of my own country by my own sons. And I can never go back, because I killed my father.

Theseus. Then why would they send for you, if you must live apart from them?

Oedipus. The voice of the god will force them to it.

Theseus. What do the oracles say they should be afraid of?

Oedipus. That they must be struck down in this land.

Theseus. And how could there be bitterness between them and me?

Oedipus. Dearest son of Aegeus, only to the gods does old age never come, and death never. Everything else all-mastering time throws into confusion. The strength of the earth decays, and the strength of the body; trust dies, and distrust grows up in its place; and the same wind never blows for long between one man and his friends, or between city and city. For some now, for others later, what was sweet turns bitter, and then turns back to love. If it is all fine weather now between Thebes and you, time in its endless going gives birth to endless nights and days, and in one of them the pledges that join hands today will be scattered by the spear over some small word. And then, one day, my corpse, sleeping and hidden and cold, will drink their warm blood — if Zeus is still Zeus, and Apollo, the son of Zeus, speaks true.

But there is no pleasure in saying words that should stay unmoved. Let me stop where I began. Only keep your own word good, and you will never say that you took Oedipus in as a useless resident of this place — unless the gods are lying to me.

Chorus. My lord, from the first this man has shown that he means to carry out these promises, or promises like them, for our land.

Theseus. Who would throw away the goodwill of a man like this? First, the hearth of an ally in war is always open to him with us, by a right that goes both ways. Then, he has come here to beg protection from our gods, and he pays this land and me no small tribute for it. I respect those claims. I will never throw away what he offers, and I will settle him in this country as one of our own. If it pleases the stranger to stay here, I will order you to guard him. Or, if he prefers, he can come with me. Choose whichever you like, Oedipus, and take it. I will go along with you either way.

Oedipus. Zeus, be good to men like this one.

Theseus. Then what do you want? To come to my house?

Oedipus. If it were allowed me. But this is the place —

Theseus. Where you will do what? I will not stand in your way.

Oedipus. — where I will get the better of the men who threw me out.

Theseus. Then the gift of your staying here would be a great one.

Oedipus. Yes, if you hold to what you have said and do it for me.

Theseus. Have no fear on that man's account. I will not betray you.

Oedipus. I will not bind you with an oath, as if you were a bad man.

Theseus. You would get nothing more by it than you have by my word.

Oedipus. Then how will you manage it?

Theseus. What is it that frightens you most?

Oedipus. Men will come —

Theseus. These men here will see to that.

Oedipus. Take care, if you leave me —

Theseus. Do not tell me what I have to do.

Oedipus. Fear makes me —

Theseus. My heart feels no fear.

Oedipus. You do not know the threats —

Theseus. I know that no man will take you out of here by force against my will. Threats have blustered many times, many loud and empty words out of a man's temper; but when the mind comes back to itself, the threats are gone. And as for those people — even if they have talked themselves up to some bold plan of carrying you off, I know that the sea between here and there will turn out long, and hard to sail. Whatever I may decide, I tell you to take heart, if it really was Apollo who sent you here. And even when I am not with you, I know my name will guard you from harm.

(Theseus goes.)

Chorus.
	Stranger, in this land of fine horses
	you have come to the fairest farmstead on earth,
	white Colonus, where the nightingale,
	clear-voiced, sings and sings,
	a constant guest in the green hollows,
	keeping to the wine-dark ivy
	and the god's untrodden leaves
	heavy with ten thousand berries,
	where no sun comes
	and no wind out of any storm;
	where Dionysus, drunk with his own joy,
	walks always on the ground,
	going about with the goddesses who nursed him.
Chorus.
	And under the dew of heaven
	the narcissus with its lovely clusters
	blooms new every day,
	the ancient crown of the two great goddesses,
	Demeter and her daughter;
	and the crocus, with its gleam of gold.
	The springs never sleep and never dwindle,
	the springs that feed the wandering streams of Cephisus,
	but every day it comes down over the plain
	with its clean water, quick to bring the crops,
	over the broad breast of the land.
	The dancing Muses have never turned away from this place,
	nor has
Chorus.
	Aphrodite with her golden reins.
	And there is a thing here I have not heard of
	in the land of Asia, nor ever yet
	in the great Dorian island of Pelops:
	a tree no hand has tamed, that plants itself,
	a terror to the spears of the enemy,
	and it grows best of all in this land —
	the grey-leaved olive, the nurse of children.
	No man young or old
	will lay a hand on it and wreck it and get away,
	for the eye of Zeus who guards the olives
	looks on it always and never closes,
	and so does grey-eyed Athena.
Chorus.
	And I have another praise for this mother city,
	the strongest of all, the gift of a great god,
	the land's proudest boast:
	good horses, good foals, and a good sea.
	Son of Cronus, Lord Poseidon,
	it was you who set her on that height of pride,
	when on these roads, the first of any,
	you made the bit that cures the rage of horses.
	And the oar, well shaped for the hand,
	that goes flashing past the land on the salt water,
	leaps on the sea to follow
	the Nereids with their hundred feet.

Antigone. Land that is praised above all lands, now is the time to show these bright words in what you do.

Oedipus. What is it, child? What has happened?

Antigone. Creon is coming towards us, father. He is close, and he has men with him.

Oedipus. Dearest old men, now let me have from you the last proof that I am safe.

Chorus. Take heart. You will have it. I may be old, but the strength of this country has not grown old.

(Creon comes in, with armed men behind him.)

Creon. Gentlemen, well-born people of this land, I can see in your eyes that my coming has put a sudden fear into you. Do not shrink from me, and do not let a hard word slip out. I have not come here meaning to do anything. I am an old man, and I know that the city I have come to is strong — as strong as any in Greece. No. I have been sent, old as I am, to persuade this man to come with me back to the land of Cadmus. Not sent by any one man: the whole people gave me the order, because it falls to me, as his kinsman, more than to anyone in Thebes, to grieve for what has happened to him.

Poor Oedipus, listen to me and come home. All the people of Cadmus call you, and they have the right to; and I most of all, because — unless I am the worst man ever born — I hurt most for your troubles, old man, when I see you, wretched as you are, a stranger and a wanderer for ever, going about with nothing to live on and one girl to lean on. I never thought, poor fool that I am, that she could sink as low as she has sunk, this unlucky child, looking after you and that head of yours for ever on a beggar's diet, at her age, with no husband, and there for the first passer-by to seize. Is that not a miserable reproach — oh, it is misery to say it — that I have thrown at you and at me and at our whole family? But what is out in the open cannot be hidden. So, Oedipus, by the gods of your fathers, listen to me and hide it: agree to come to the city and the house of your fathers. Say a kind goodbye to this city, which has deserved it. But the city at home has the older claim on your respect, because it was Thebes that raised you, long ago.

Oedipus. You who will dare anything, who can spin a cunning device out of any honest argument — why are you trying this on me? Why do you want to catch me a second time, in the trap where being caught would hurt me most? Before, when I was sick with the evils of my own house and it would have been a joy to me to be thrown out of the country, you would not do me that favour, though I wanted it. But when my anger had had its fill at last and living quietly in the house had become sweet to me — that was when you shoved me out and threw me away. And this kinship you talk about was nothing to you then. And now, again, when you see that this city and its whole people have taken me in and wish me well, you try to drag me away, saying hard things in a soft voice.

And what pleasure is there in being loved against your will? It is as if a man begged you and begged you for something and you gave him nothing and would not help him, and then, when his heart was full of all he had wanted, you handed it over — when the favour was no favour at all. Would that not be an empty kind of pleasure? Yet that is exactly what you are offering me: good in the words, evil in the deeds. And I will say it in front of these men too, to show them what you are. You have come to fetch me — not to take me home, but to plant me on your border, so that your city may get clear of this land without harm. That is not what you will get. What you will get is this: my avenging spirit living in your country for ever. And my sons will get just so much of my land as will serve them to die in.

Do I not understand the affairs of Thebes better than you do? Far better — because the voices I hear them from are truer: Apollo's, and Zeus's himself, who is Apollo's father. But your mouth has come here with a lie in it, and a very sharp edge; and with all your talking you are more likely to reap harm than safety. Still, I know I will not persuade you of that. Go. And let us live here. Even as we are, our life would not be a bad one, if we were content with it.

Creon. Which of us do you think comes off worse in this exchange — me, by what you have said, or you, by your own words?

Oedipus. The sweetest thing for me is that you cannot persuade me, and cannot persuade these men beside me either.

Creon. You poor wretch. Will you show everyone that not even time has grown you a mind, and that you feed your old age on what disgraces it?

Oedipus. You have a clever tongue. But I never knew an honest man who could make a fine speech out of any case at all.

Creon. Saying a great deal and saying what fits the moment are two different things.

Oedipus. As if what you say were short, and fitted the moment.

Creon. Not to anyone with a mind like yours, it does not.

Oedipus. Get out. I say it for these men too. And stop standing guard over me, watching the place where I am meant to stay.

Creon. I call these men to witness, not you, how you answer your own people. And if I ever get my hands on you —

Oedipus. And who is going to lay hands on me, against all these allies of mine?

Creon. I promise you, you will be hurt soon enough without that.

Oedipus. What have you done, that lets you make that threat?

Creon. Of your two daughters, one I have just now seized and sent away. The other I will take with me shortly.

Oedipus. Oh.

Creon. You will soon have more to cry about than that.

Oedipus. You have my child?

Creon. And this one too, before long.

Oedipus. Oh, strangers, what will you do? Will you betray me? Will you not drive this godless man out of your land?

Chorus. Out, stranger. Quickly. There is no justice in what you are doing now, and none in what you did before.

Creon. (to his men) It is time you took this girl away, whether she likes it or not, since she will not come willingly.

Antigone. Oh, no. Where can I run to? What help can I find, from gods or from men?

Chorus. What are you doing, stranger?

Creon. I will not touch this man. Only what is mine.

Oedipus. Lords of this land.

Chorus. Stranger, what you are doing is not just.

Creon. It is just.

Chorus. How is it just?

Creon. I am taking my own.

Oedipus. Oh, city.

Chorus. What are you doing, stranger? Let her go. It will come to a trial of strength between us soon.

Creon. Stand off.

Chorus. Not from you. Not while this is what you mean to do.

Creon. You will be at war with Thebes if you do me any harm.

Oedipus. Did I not say so?

Chorus. Take your hands off the girl. Now.

Creon. Do not give orders where you are not the master.

Chorus. Let her go, I tell you.

Creon. And I tell you: be on your way.

Chorus. Here, men of Colonus — come here, come here. The city is being taken by force. My city. Come here to me.

Antigone. They are dragging me away. Strangers. Strangers.

Oedipus. Child, where are you?

Antigone. They are taking me by force.

Oedipus. Reach out your hands to me, child.

Antigone. I cannot. I have no strength.

Creon. (to his men) Take her. Go.

Oedipus. Oh. I am wretched, wretched.

(The men go off with Antigone.)

Creon. So. You will never go walking on those two sticks again. And since you are set on beating your own country and your own people — whose orders I am carrying out, though I am the ruler there too — go on and beat them. In time, I know, you will understand this: that you are doing yourself no good now, and did none before, when you went against your own people and gave your anger its way. Your anger has always been the ruin of you.

(He moves towards Oedipus. The Chorus closes in on him.)

Chorus. Stop where you are, stranger.

Creon. Keep your hands off me, I tell you.

Chorus. I will not let you go until you give back those girls.

Creon. Then you will soon be paying the city a bigger ransom. I will lay hold of more than those two.

Chorus. What will you turn to next?

Creon. I will take this man, and carry him off.

Chorus. That is a terrible thing to say.

Creon. It will be done, now.

Chorus. Not unless the ruler of this land stops you.

Oedipus. Voice without shame. You are going to lay hands on me?

Creon. Be quiet, I tell you.

Oedipus. No. May the goddesses of this place not take my voice from me before I have said this one curse more. You, worst of men, when these eyes of mine were already dark, you tore from me the helpless girl who was my eyes, and you are making off with her by force. And so may the Sun, the god who sees everything, give you and your family a life like mine — an old age exactly like this.

Creon. Do you see this, people of the land?

Oedipus. They see you and they see me. And they understand that what was done to me was done with hands, and I fight back with words.

Creon. I will not hold my temper any longer. I will take this man by force, alone as I am and slow with age.

Oedipus. Oh. I am wretched.

Chorus. What arrogance you brought here with you, stranger, if you think you can carry this off.

Creon. I think so.

Chorus. Then I no longer count this a city at all.

Creon. When a man has justice on his side, the small man beats the big one.

Oedipus. Do you hear what he is saying?

Chorus. He will not do it. Great Zeus be my witness.

Creon. Zeus would know that. You do not.

Chorus. Is this not outrage?

Creon. Outrage. And you will have to put up with it.

Chorus. All of you, everyone, leaders of this land, come here, come quickly! These men are over the line already.

(Theseus comes in, with his men behind him.)

Theseus. What is that shouting? What has happened? What were you so afraid of, that you stopped me in the middle of a sacrifice at the altar of the sea god, the god who watches over this Colonus? Tell me all of it. I ran here faster than my legs enjoyed, and I want to know why.

Oedipus. Dearest of friends. I know your voice. This man here has just done me a terrible wrong.

Theseus. What wrong? Who has hurt you? Speak.

Oedipus. Creon. The man you are looking at. He has torn my children away from me, the only pair I had, and he is taking them off.

Theseus. What did you say?

Oedipus. You have heard what has been done to me.

Theseus. Then one of you men, quick, to the altars over there. Order the whole people to leave the sacrifice and race, on foot and on horseback, with the reins loose, to the place where the two roads the traders use come together, so that the girls do not get past, and I am not made a laughing-stock to this stranger, beaten by force. Go. Do as I have ordered, and fast.

As for this man: if I let my anger go as far as he has earned, he would not get out of my hands unhurt. As it is, he will be judged by the very laws he brought in here with him, and by no others. You will never leave this country until you have brought those girls back and set them here in front of me, where I can see them. What you have done is a disgrace to me, and to your own ancestors, and to your own land. You came into a city that practises justice and settles nothing without law; you brushed aside the authority of this country, burst in, took what you wanted and made it yours by force. You must have decided that my city was empty of men, or a city of slaves, and that I counted for exactly nothing.

And yet it was not Thebes that trained you to be bad. Thebes does not like to breed lawless men, and she would not praise you if she heard you were robbing me, and robbing the gods, dragging off by force the unhappy people who came to them for protection. If I had set foot in your country, even with the justest claim in the world, I would not have dragged anybody off or led anybody away without the consent of whoever ruled there. I would have known how a stranger ought to behave among citizens. But you are disgracing a city that does not deserve it, your own city; and the years piling up on you have made you old and empty of sense at the same time.

I said it before and I say it again now: somebody bring those girls here, fast, unless you want to become a resident of this country yourself, by force and not by choice. That is what I am telling you, and what is in my mind is the same as what is on my tongue.

Chorus. Do you see where you have landed, stranger? By where you come from, you look like a just man. By what you do, you turn out to be evil.

Creon. Son of Aegeus, I did not do this because I took this city for a place empty of men, or empty of counsel, as you say. I did it because I judged that nobody here would ever feel such a passion for my relatives that they would keep them against my will. And I knew this people would not take in a man who killed his father, a polluted man, a man found living in a marriage that was unholy, with children in it. That much I knew: this country has a council on the hill of Ares that judges well, and it does not allow wanderers like that to settle in this city. I trusted in that, and I laid hands on my prize.

And I would not have done it, if he had not called down bitter curses on me and on my family. That was the wrong done to me, and I thought I had the right to pay it back. Anger has no old age except death; only the dead feel no pain.

So do what you like. Even if I am in the right, having nobody on my side makes me small. Still, old as I am, I will try to answer what you do with what I do.

Oedipus. You shameless thing. Whose old age do you think you are insulting with this? Mine, or your own? Killings, marriages, disasters: you have let all of that out of your mouth at me, and I bore all of it, poor wretch, against my will. That was what the gods wanted; perhaps they had some old anger against my family. Take me by myself and you could not find one fault in me to throw in my face, no crime to explain why I did these wrongs to myself and to my own people.

Tell me this. If an oracle came to my father from the gods, saying he would die at the hands of his children, how can you justly hold that against me, when I had not been born yet, when no father had begotten me and no mother conceived me? I did not exist. And if, having been born unlucky, as I was, I came to blows with my father and killed him, understanding nothing of what I was doing or whom I was doing it to, how can you reasonably blame an act nobody meant?

And my mother. You wretch. Have you no shame, forcing me to speak of her marriage, when she was your own sister, and when it was the kind of marriage I am now going to describe? Because I will not keep quiet, when you have gone that far in unholy talk. She bore me. Yes, she bore me, oh, the misery of it, not knowing, and I not knowing; and having borne me, she gave me children, to her own shame. But one thing I know for certain: you foul-mouth her and me on purpose. I married her without meaning to, and I speak of it now without wanting to. No. I will not be called evil for that marriage, nor for my father's killing, which you keep flinging at me over and over, bitterly, as a taunt.

Answer me one question, only one. If someone came up to you, here and now, and tried to kill you, you, the just man, would you stop to ask whether the killer was your father, or would you pay him back on the spot? I think, if you are fond of living, you would pay back the man who did it, and not look round for a justification. Well, those are the evils I walked into, with the gods leading me. And I do not think my father's soul, if it came back to life, would say a word against me on that.

But you are not just. You think it is fine to say everything, what may be said and what may not, and so you taunt me like this in front of these men. And you find it fine to flatter Theseus's name, and Athens, and how well she is governed; and in the middle of all that praise you forget one thing: that if any land knows how to worship the gods with honour, this one comes first at it. And it is from this land that you tried to steal me, an old man and a suppliant. You laid hands on me, and you have taken my girls and gone. For that, I call on the goddesses of this place, I beg them, I lay it on them with my prayers: come as my helpers and my allies, so that you find out what kind of men stand guard over this city.

Chorus. The stranger is a good man, my lord. What has happened to him is utter ruin, and it deserves our help.

Theseus. Enough talk. The men who did this are running, and we, who were wronged, are standing still.

Creon. Then what are your orders for a man with no strength left?

Theseus. Lead the way on the road out there, and I will be your escort. If you are keeping the girls somewhere in these parts, you can show me where yourself. If your men are running off with them in their grip, there is no need for us to trouble: others are after them already, and they will never get out of this country to thank their gods. Go on. Lead. And understand this: the man who held has been held. Luck caught you in the middle of your hunt. What is got by dishonest trickery does not stay got.

And you will have nobody else to help you in this. I know very well you did not come to this pitch of outrage, this daring you have shown just now, unarmed and unprepared. There is somebody you trusted when you did it. That I must look into; I must not let this city be weaker than one man. Do you take any of that in? Or does it seem as empty to you now as it did when you were planning this?

Creon. Say what you like while I am here; I will not object. At home, we too shall know what has to be done.

Theseus. Threaten as you go. Oedipus, you stay here quietly, and trust me for this: unless I die first, I will not stop until I have put your children back in your hands.

Oedipus. Bless you, Theseus, for your nobility, and for the just care you have taken of us.

(Theseus goes out with his men, taking Creon with him.)

Chorus.
	Oh, to be there, where the enemy
	turn and stand, and soon
	the bronze cry of battle breaks:
	on Apollo's shore, perhaps,
	or the shore of the torches,
	where the great goddesses keep
	their holy rites for mortals,
	and their priests, the Eumolpids,
	carry a golden key upon the tongue.
	There, I think, Theseus,
	the man who wakes the fight,
	and the two unwed sisters
	will soon meet, in a shout that settles it,
	somewhere in these fields.
Chorus.
	Or maybe they are nearing
	the pastures west of Oea's snowy rock,
	on colts, or in chariots
	racing away at full stretch.
	He will be caught. Terrible
	is the war god of these parts,
	terrible the strength of Theseus's men.
	Every bridle flashes,
	every rider lets the reins go loose
	and comes on: all of them
	who honour Athena of the horses
	and the sea god who holds the earth,
	Rhea's own son.
Chorus.
	Are they at it, or still to start?
	Something tells me,
	my mind is a prophet,
	that soon I shall meet the girls
	who suffered terribly,
	who found terrible things at a kinsman's hands.
	Zeus will finish something, finish it today.
	I foretell a good fight.
	Oh, to be a dove, storm-fast, storm-strong,
	and reach a cloud high in the air,
	and hang my eye above the battle.
Chorus.
	Zeus, all-ruler of the gods,
	all-seeing Zeus, grant
	the guardians of this land
	the strength that wins,
	and bring the ambush to a good catch;
	and you, holy Pallas Athena, his child.
	And Apollo the hunter,
	and his sister, who follows
	the dappled deer that run so fast:
	I want them to come, two helpers,
	for this land and for its people.
	Wandering stranger, you will not say
	your watchman was a false prophet.
	I see the girls, there,
	with their escort, coming close again.

Oedipus. Where? Where? What are you saying? What did you say?

(Antigone and Ismene come in, with Theseus and his men.)

Antigone. Father. Father. If only some god would let you see this best of men, who has brought us back here to you.

Oedipus. Child, are you both really here?

Antigone. Yes. These arms saved us: Theseus's, and his dearest men's.

Oedipus. Come here to your father, children. Let me hold you. I never hoped you would come back.

Antigone. You will get what you ask for. We want the gift as much as you do.

Oedipus. Where are you, then? Where?

Antigone. Here. We are coming to you together.

Oedipus. My dearest branches.

Antigone. Everything is dear to the one who made it.

Oedipus. The props a man leans on.

Antigone. Unlucky props of an unlucky man.

Oedipus. I have what I love most. Now if I died I would not be wholly wretched, with the two of you standing beside me. Press against me, children, one on each side, grown to the father who grew you, and give me rest from that wandering, so lonely and so wretched, before this. And tell me what happened, as briefly as you can. For girls of your age a short account is enough.

Antigone. Here is the man who saved us. Hear it from him, father; the deed was his. That way my part will be short.

Oedipus. Stranger, do not wonder if I go on at length, and stubbornly, with children who have come back when I had given up hope. I know very well that this joy in them has come to me from you and from nobody else. You saved them; no other man alive did. And may the gods give you what I wish for you, you yourself and this land; because it is among you alone, of all people, that I have found reverence, and decency, and mouths that do not lie. I know it, and these words are how I pay it back. What I have, I have through you and through no other man.

Give me your right hand, my lord, so I can touch it, and kiss your face, if that is allowed. But what am I saying? How could a man like me, born to misery, want you to touch a man in whom every stain of evil has come to live? I will not do it. I would not let you, if you offered. Only people who have been through this can share the weight of it. Take my greeting from where you stand, and go on caring for me justly, as you have up to this hour.

Theseus. I am not surprised that you spun your words out at some length in your delight at these children, or that you put their words ahead of mine. There is nothing in that to weigh on me. It is not with words that I try to make my life shine, but with things done. And here is the proof: I have broken none of what I swore to you, old man. I am here, bringing the girls alive, untouched by all those threats. As for how the fight was won, why should I boast about it for nothing, when you will hear it yourself from these two once you are together?

But there is something that came my way just now, as I was walking here. Give me your opinion on it. It is a small thing to tell, but it is worth wondering about; and a man should treat nothing as beneath his notice.

Oedipus. What is it, son of Aegeus? Tell me. I know nothing of what you are asking about.

Theseus. They say a man, not from your city, but of your blood, has thrown himself down somehow at the altar of Poseidon and is sitting there as a suppliant. The altar where I was sacrificing when I set out to come here.

Oedipus. Where is he from? What does he want by sitting there?

Theseus. I know only one thing. They tell me he asks for a short word with you. Nothing heavy.

Oedipus. What kind of word? A seat at an altar is not the seat for a small matter.

Theseus. They say he asks only to come and speak with you, and to go back the way he came in safety.

Oedipus. Who could it be, sitting at that altar?

Theseus. Think. Is there anybody of your family in Argos who might want that favour from you?
