Oedipus. Dearest friend, stop there.

Theseus. What is wrong?

Oedipus. Do not ask me to —

Theseus. To what? Say it.

Oedipus. From what I have heard, I know who this suppliant is.

Theseus. And who is he, that I should have anything against him?

Oedipus. My son, my lord. The hated one. Of all men alive, his are the words it would hurt me most to hear.

Theseus. Why? Can you not listen, and still not do what you do not want to do? What is painful to you in hearing him?

Oedipus. That voice, my lord, has become the most hateful thing there is to his father. Do not force me to give way in this.

Theseus. But consider whether his sitting there compels you. Whether you have a duty to the god that you must keep.

Antigone. Father, listen to me, even if I am young to be giving advice. Let this man do what his own heart and the god want; let him give that favour. And give way to the two of us, and let our brother come. He will not drag you off your resolve by force, do not be afraid of that, with words that are not to your good. What harm is there in hearing words? Things badly planned show themselves in the telling. You fathered him. So even if he is doing you the wickedest of the wickedest wrongs, father, it is not right for you to wrong him back. Let him come. Other men have bad children and a sharp temper too, and still they are talked round; their friends' words work on their nature like a spell.

Look back at those things, not at now: at what you suffered through your father and your mother. If you look at that, I know you will see how bad an end comes of a bad temper. You have no small reason to think about it, robbed as you are of the eyes that cannot see. Give way to us. It is not right that people asking for what is just should have to keep on asking; nor that a man should be treated well and then not know how to pay it back.

Oedipus. Child, you win, and the pleasure you win from me weighs heavily. Let it be as you two want. Only, stranger, if that man is to come here, let nobody ever get power over my life.

Theseus. I need to hear that once, old man, not twice. I do not want to boast; but know that you are safe, so long as any god keeps me safe.

(Theseus goes out.)

Chorus.
	Whoever wants the larger share of life,
	and lets the fitting measure go,
	is keeping company with folly:
	that is plain to me.
	Because the long days lay down
	a great deal that is nearer to pain,
	and where the pleasures are you will not find,
	once a man falls past
	what he needs. And the helper
	comes to all alike in the end,
	when the doom of Hades shows itself,
	no wedding song, no lyre, no dance:
	death at the last.
Chorus.
	Not to be born beats every argument.
	And once a man has appeared,
	to go back where he came from
	as fast as he can is next best by far.
	For when youth goes by,
	with its light-headed follies in its hands,
	what heavy blow stays away? What labour is not in it?
	Envy, faction, strife, battles,
	and killings. And last of all, allotted,
	comes despised old age,
	weak, unsociable, friendless,
	where every evil of evils lives together.
Chorus.
	That is where he is, this poor man,
	and not I alone.
	Like some headland facing north,
	beaten by the waves on every side in winter,
	so the terrible ruins break on him too,
	wave after wave, from crown to base,
	and never leave him:
	some from the setting of the sun,
	some from its rising,
	some from the beam of noon,
	some from the Rhipaean hills of night.

Antigone. And look, here comes the stranger, it seems, and alone, father, nobody with him. He is walking this way with the tears pouring from his eyes.

Oedipus. Who is it?

Antigone. The man we have had in mind all this while. Polyneices has come to us.

(Polyneices comes in.)

Polyneices. Oh, what am I to do? Do I weep first for my own troubles, sisters, or for what I see of his, my father's, an old man's? I have found him here, in a strange country, cast out, with the two of you, in clothes like these, whose filth, old and loathsome, has settled on the old man's body and is wasting his sides; and on his eyeless head the hair blows uncombed in the wind. And the food he carries for his poor belly, it seems, is a match for all the rest.

I am wretched, utterly, and I learn it all far too late. And I say it against myself: I have come here the worst of men, in the matter of feeding you. Do not learn what I am from anybody else. But Shame sits beside Zeus on his throne, over everything men do; let her stand beside you too, father. There are cures for the wrongs I have done; there is no adding to them any more.

Why are you silent? Say something, father. Do not turn away from me. Will you not answer me at all? Will you send me off in dishonour, without a word, without even telling me what you are angry about?

Seed of this man, my sisters, you at least try to move our father's mouth, which will not be approached and will not speak, so that he does not send me off like this, dishonoured, when I am the god's suppliant, without one word in answer.

Antigone. Say it yourself, poor man: what you have come needing. A flood of words, by pleasing somehow, or angering, or stirring pity, has given a voice before now to people who had none.

Polyneices. Then I will say it. You guide me well. First I make the god himself my helper, the god from whose altar the ruler of this land raised me up to come here, granting me the right to speak and to hear and to leave in safety. And I will want the same from you, strangers, and from these two sisters, and from my father.

Now what I came for, father. I want to tell you. I have been driven out of my own country as an exile, because I claimed, as the elder-born, the right to sit on your throne, the throne of sole power. For that Eteocles, my younger brother, thrust me out of the land, not by beating me in argument, not by coming to any test of hand or deed, but by persuading the city. And the chief cause of that, I say, is your Fury; and I hear the same from the prophets.

When I came to Dorian Argos, and took Adrastus as my father-in-law, I bound to myself by oath every man in the land of Apia who is called first and honoured with the spear, so that with them I could gather the army of seven spears against Thebes, and either die in a wholly just cause or throw the men who did this out of the land.

Well. Why have I come now? To you, father, with a suppliant's prayers, on my own account and on my allies', who now with seven companies and seven spears stand round the whole plain of Thebes. There is Amphiaraus, the spear-shaker, first with the spear and first in the paths of birds; second, the Aetolian, Tydeus, son of Oeneus; third, Eteoclus, Argive born; fourth, Hippomedon, sent by his father Talaus; the fifth, Capaneus, swears he will raze the town of Thebes and burn it to the ground; sixth, Parthenopaeus the Arcadian comes hurrying, named for the mother who was long a virgin before she bore him, the true son of Atalanta; and I, your son, or if not yours, then the child of an evil fate, but called yours at least, lead the fearless army of Argos against Thebes.

And all of us, father, beg you by these children and by your own life, and we ask you to let go of your heavy anger against this man, as he sets out to punish his brother, who thrust him out and robbed him of his country. Because if there is anything to trust in oracles, they said the power would be with whichever side you joined.

So now, by our springs and by the gods of our blood, I ask you to listen and give way. We are beggars and strangers, and you are a stranger; you and I live by flattering other men, both of us, with the same fate allotted to us. And he, the tyrant at home, oh, the misery of it, laughs at us both and grows soft on it. If you stand with my purpose, I will scatter him with little trouble and in little time. And then I will bring you home and set you in your own house, and set myself there too, once I have thrown him out by force. That is a boast I can make, with you on my side. Without you, I have not the strength even to survive.

Chorus. Oedipus — for the sake of the man who sent him, say whatever is right to say, and then send him back.

Oedipus. Men who hold this land: if it had not been Theseus who sent him here to me — Theseus, who thought it fair that he should hear my answer — he would never have heard my voice at all. As it is, he has been granted that much, and he will go away having heard from me things that will never once make his life any happier.

You. Worst of men. When you held the sceptre and the throne that your brother holds in Thebes now, you drove out your own father — this man here — and made me a man with no city, and made me wear these rags, the rags that bring tears to your eyes now you look at them, now that you have walked into the same misery I am in. This is nothing to weep over. It is something I have to carry, for as long as I live, remembering you as my murderer. You are the one who put me here, to live on hardship. You threw me out. It is because of you that I wander and beg my daily bread from strangers. And if these girls had not been born to me to look after me, I would be dead, for anything you did. As it is they keep me alive. They are the ones who look after me. They are men, not women, when it comes to sharing the work. You two are some other man's sons, not mine.

So the god's eye is on you — not yet as it will be, very soon, if those companies of yours are really marching on the city of Thebes. There is no way you will ever tear that city down. Before that, you will fall, fouled with blood, and your brother with you, the two of you alike. Those are the curses I sent up against the pair of you before, and I call on them now to come and fight on my side, so that you two learn to respect the parents who made you, and not to hold your father in contempt because the father who fathered such sons is blind. These girls did not do that. So my curses have your seat of supplication in their power, and your throne with it — if Justice, spoken of from of old, still sits beside Zeus under the ancient laws.

Now get out. I spit you out. You have no father in me. Worst of all bad men, take these curses with you that I call down on you: never to conquer the land of your own blood with the spear, never to come home to the valley of Argos, but to die by a kinsman's hand, and to kill the man who drove you out. That is my prayer. And I call on the hateful darkness of Tartarus, where my fathers are, to take you away and house you there. I call on the goddesses of this place. I call on Ares, who threw that terrible hatred into the two of you. You have heard it. Now go. Go and tell all the people of Cadmus, and your loyal allies with them, what kind of inheritance Oedipus has divided among his sons.

Chorus. Polyneices, I take no pleasure in the road that brought you here. Now go back, as fast as you can.

Polyneices. Oh, my journey. Oh, the failure of it — and my companions. So that is the end of the road we set out on from Argos. I am wretched. An end I cannot even speak of to a single one of my companions, and cannot turn them back from. I have to go and meet it without a word.

Sisters — his daughters, my own blood — you have heard the hard things our father is praying for. If his curses are fulfilled, and you somehow find a way home, then by the gods do not dishonour me. Put me in a grave, with the rites the dead are owed. The praise you get from this man now, for the work you do for him, will bring you another praise no smaller, for what you do for me.

Antigone. Polyneices, I beg you. Listen to me about one thing.

Polyneices. What is it, dearest Antigone? Say it.

Antigone. Turn your army back to Argos, as fast as you can. Do not destroy yourself and the city both.

Polyneices. It cannot be done. How could I ever lead the same army out again, once I had shown fear?

Antigone. But why do you need to be angry a second time, brother? What do you gain by tearing down your own homeland?

Polyneices. It is shameful to be in exile — I am the elder — and to be laughed at like this by my brother.

Antigone. Then do you see how you are carrying his prophecies straight to their mark? He says the two of you will die at each other's hands.

Polyneices. Because that is what he wants. I cannot give way to it.

Antigone. Oh, I am wretched. But who will dare to follow you, once they hear what this man has foretold?

Polyneices. I will not report bad news. A good commander tells the better side and keeps the worse to himself.

Antigone. So that is what you have decided, brother?

Polyneices. Yes. And do not hold me back. This road is mine to take now, doomed and evil, thanks to my father here and his Furies. As for you two — may Zeus be good to you, if you do this for me when I am dead. Alive, you will not have me again. Let go of me now. Goodbye. You will never see me living again.

Antigone. Oh, I am wretched.

Polyneices. Do not grieve for me.

Antigone. Who could help grieving for you, brother, when you are hurrying towards a death you can see in front of you?

Polyneices. If I have to, I will die.

Antigone. No. Listen to me instead.

Polyneices. Do not urge what must not be.

Antigone. Then I am wretched past all bearing, if I lose you.

Polyneices. That lies with the god — this way, or the other. As for you two, I pray to the gods that you never meet with harm. Everyone knows you do not deserve to suffer.

(Polyneices goes.)

Chorus.
	New troubles — here they come, new,
	heavy with fate, from the blind stranger,
	unless what is allotted is finding its mark.
	I cannot say that any purpose of the gods is empty.
	Time sees these things. Time always sees them,
	throwing some of them down,
	and the next day raising others up again.
	The sky cracked. Zeus!
Oedipus.
	Children, children — is there anyone here
	who could bring Theseus, the best of men, to me?
Antigone.
	Father, what is it you want him for?
Oedipus.
	This winged thunder of Zeus is going to take me
	to Hades, now. Send for him. Quickly.

(Thunder.)

Chorus.
	Look — a great one. It comes crashing down,
	the unspeakable noise, thrown by Zeus.
	Terror has run up into the ends of my hair.
	My heart is cowering. The lightning is burning
	across the sky again.
	What end will it let loose? I am afraid.
	It never flies out for nothing, never
	without something coming after it.
	Great sky! Zeus!
Oedipus.
	Children, the end of my life that was foretold
	has come for me. There is no turning from it now.
Antigone.
	How do you know? What tells you so?
Oedipus.
	I know it well. Somebody go, as fast as he can,
	and bring the lord of this land to me.

(Thunder.)

Chorus.
	Ah — ah — there! Once again
	the piercing crash is all round us.
	Be kind, god, be kind, if you are bringing
	something dark for the earth, our mother.
	Let me find you gracious. Let me not,
	because I looked on a man under a curse,
	come in for a favour that brings no gain.
	Lord Zeus, I am calling to you.
Oedipus.
	Is the man near? Will he find me still breathing, children,
	and my mind still steady?
Antigone.
	What is the promise you want fixed in his mind?
Oedipus.
	To give them, for the good they did me,
	the favour I promised when I received it — in full.
Chorus.
	Come, my son, come! Even if you are out on the headland,
	in the hollow, making the altar holy
	with an ox for Poseidon, god of the sea — come.
	The stranger thinks you and your city and your people
	deserve a just return for what he was given.
	Hurry. Run, my lord.

(Theseus comes in.)

Theseus. What is this noise again, all of you together — my own people plainly, and the stranger as clear as they are? Was it a thunderbolt from Zeus, or a burst of hail out of the rain? When a god sends a storm like this, you can guess at anything.

Oedipus. My lord, you have appeared just when I wanted you. Some god has made this journey of yours a lucky one.

Theseus. What is it now, son of Laius? What is new?

Oedipus. My life is tipping on the scale. I want to die without cheating you and this city of what I agreed to.

Theseus. What proof of your death are you relying on?

Oedipus. The gods themselves are the heralds. They are announcing it to me, and not one of the signs that were laid down is false.

Theseus. How do you mean, old man? What makes it plain?

Oedipus. The thunder, crash after crash, and the many bolts of lightning flashing from the hand that nobody has ever beaten.

Theseus. You convince me. I have seen you prophesy a good deal, and none of it false. Tell me what has to be done.

Oedipus. I will teach you, son of Aegeus, what will be laid up for this city and will never grow old or bring you pain. I myself, now, with no hand to guide me, will lead the way to the place where I must die. Never tell any human being where it is — neither where it is hidden nor what part of the country it lies in — so that it may stand for you always as a defence, better than many shields, better than allied spears brought in from your neighbours. The things too holy to be stirred by speech you will learn for yourself when you come to that place alone. I cannot speak them to any of these citizens, nor to my own children, though I love them. Keep them yourself, always; and when you come to the end of your life, tell them to your eldest son alone, and let him show them in turn to the one who comes after him, for ever. That way you will keep this city safe from the men who grew from the dragon's teeth. Countless cities, however well they are governed, slip easily into outrage. The gods see well, but they see late, when a man throws away what is theirs and turns to madness. Do not want that to happen to you, son of Aegeus. But I am teaching a man what he knows already.

Now, the place — the god's presence is pressing me — let us go, and not hold back any longer. Children, follow me, this way. It is my turn now. I have come out as a new guide for the two of you, as you were for your father. Come. Do not touch me. Let me find the holy tomb by myself, where it is allotted that this man be hidden in this earth. This way. Here — this way. Hermes the guide is leading me this way, and the goddess of the dead. Light — no light to me — once, somewhere, you were mine; now my body feels you for the last time. I am going now to hide the end of my life in the house of Hades. Dearest of hosts — you, and this land, and your people — may you be happy; and in your good fortune remember me when I am dead, and be fortunate for ever.

(He goes into the grove, leading. His daughters, Theseus and his men follow him.)

Chorus.
	If it is right for me to honour with my prayers
	the goddess no one sees,
	and you, lord of the things of the night,
	Aidoneus, Aidoneus — I beg you:
	let the stranger come to the end
	without pain, without a death
	of heavy groaning,
	to the plain of the dead below that hides everything,
	and the house of the Styx.
	Many were the sufferings that came to him
	for nothing. Let a just god
	raise him up again.
Chorus.
	Goddesses under the earth,
	and you, the body of the beast nobody has beaten,
	who lie at the gates where so many come,
	and snarl out of the cave —
	the untamed guard beside Hades,
	so the story has always gone:
	I pray to you, Death, child of Earth and Tartarus,
	that the beast leave the way clear
	for the stranger as he sets out
	for the plain of the dead below.
	You are the one I call on,
	the sleep that does not end.

(A Messenger comes out of the grove.)

Messenger. Citizens — the shortest way to tell it is to say that Oedipus is dead. But what happened cannot be told in a few words, and what was done there was not small either.

Chorus. He is dead, then — the poor man?

Messenger. You can be sure that he has left this everyday life behind him.

Chorus. How? By some god's doing, and without pain, poor man?

Messenger. That is exactly the part worth wondering at. How he set out from here you know yourself; you were here. No one of his friends leading him — he himself leading all of us. When he had come to the steep path down, the one rooted into the earth with steps of bronze, he stopped on one of the many branching tracks, near the hollow basin in the rock where the pact of Theseus and Peirithous is kept, faithful for ever. He stood midway between that basin and the Thorician rock, between the hollow wild pear and the tomb of stone, and there he sat down, and undid his filthy clothes. Then he called his daughters and told them to bring water from somewhere running, for washing and for an offering. The two of them went to the hill in sight of us, the hill of Demeter of the green shoots, and did what their father asked in a short time; they washed him and dressed him the way it is done.

When he had had everything he wanted done, and nothing he had asked for was left undone, Zeus under the earth thundered. The girls shivered when they heard it. They fell at their father's knees and wept, and did not stop beating their breasts and wailing at full length. And when he heard that sudden bitter sound he folded his arms round them and said: "Children, on this day you have no father any more. All that was mine is gone now, and you will not have the hard work of looking after me any longer. It was hard, I know, children. But one word alone undoes all that labour. Love — no one ever gave you more of that than this man did; and now you will be without him for the rest of your lives."

Messenger. Like that, clinging to one another, all of them sobbed and wept. But when they came to the end of their weeping and the sound had died away, there was silence. And suddenly a voice called out to him, so that every one of us stood with our hair on end in a sudden terror. The god called him, again and again, from every side: "You — you there, Oedipus. Why are we waiting to go? You have kept us too long already." When he understood it was a god calling him, he asked for the lord of the land to come to him, Theseus himself. And when Theseus came, he said: "Dear friend, give me the pledge of your right hand, the one an oath is sworn on, for my children — and you, children, give yours to him. Promise never to betray these girls of your own free will, and always to do whatever, in your good judgement, is for their good." And he, like the fine man he is, without any show of pity, swore to the stranger that he would do it.

When that was done, Oedipus at once felt for his children with his blind hands, and said: "Children, you must bear it bravely in your hearts and go away from this place. Do not think it right to look at what must not be seen, or to hear people saying what must not be heard. Go, as fast as you can. Only Theseus, who has the right, must stay and learn what is done here." That much we all heard him say, and we went with the girls, groaning, our tears running unchecked. When we had gone a little way, in a short time we turned and looked back — and the man was nowhere. Not there at all any more. Only the king, alone, holding his hand up in front of his face to shade his eyes, as if something terrible had appeared, something not to be looked at. And then, in a very short while, we saw him bowing down to the earth and to Olympus of the gods in one and the same prayer.

By what death that man died, no mortal could tell you, except Theseus alone. No fiery thunderbolt of the god took him off; no whirlwind rose from the sea in that hour. Either some escort came from the gods, or the dark floor of the underworld opened kindly for him, without pain. The man was sent on his way with no grieving, no sickness, no suffering — and if any mortal ever was, he was a wonder. If I seem to anyone to be talking nonsense, I would not try to win over a man who thinks I am a fool.

Chorus. Where are his daughters, and the friends who went with them?

Messenger. Not far. The sound of their grief is plain enough. It tells you they are coming this way.

(Antigone and Ismene come in.)

Antigone.
	Aiai. Oh — now, now it is ours,
	the two of us, and not one thing only but all of it:
	to weep for the cursed blood in our father,
	planted in us, doomed as we are —
	the father for whom we carried the long labour,
	steady, all that time,
	and now at the last we bring back
	what cannot be reckoned:
	what we saw and what we suffered.
Chorus.
	What is it?
Antigone.
	You can guess it, friends.
Chorus.
	He is gone?
Antigone.
	Just as you would most wish, if you were wishing.
	How not? No war god met him,
	and no sea;
	the plains that no eye can see took him,
	carried off in some death nobody witnessed.
	Wretched — and on our eyes
	a night of destruction has come down.
	How are we to find the hard bread of life,
	wandering some far land,
	or the swell of the sea?
Ismene.
	I do not know. Let Hades, the killer,
	take me down to die with my old father.
	I am wretched. The life ahead of me
	is not a life to be lived.
Chorus.
	Best pair of daughters —
	what a god brings, you must bear.
	Do not burn too hot with grief.
	You have come through nothing to be blamed for.
Antigone.
	So there was a longing even for troubles.
	What was in no way dear was dear,
	all the while I held him in my arms.
	Father — dear one —
	you who wear the dark under the earth for ever,
	even down there you will never be
	unloved by me and by her.
Chorus.
	He did it?
Antigone.
	He did what he wanted.
Chorus.
	What was that?
Antigone.
	He died on the foreign ground he wanted.
	He has his bed under the earth,
	well shaded, for ever,
	and he did not leave a grief behind him unwept.
	This eye of mine, father, weeps for you
	and goes on weeping. I do not know
	how I am to make a pain this size
	disappear. Oh — you wanted
	to die on foreign ground,
	but you died like this, with no one by you. Not me.
Ismene.
	Wretched — what lot is waiting
	for you and me now, dear one,
	left like this without a father?
Chorus.
	But since he untied the end of his life
	in blessedness, friends,
	stop this grieving. Nobody
	is out of the reach of harm.
Antigone.
	Let us hurry back, dear one.
Ismene.
	To do what?
Antigone.
	A longing has hold of me.
Ismene.
	For what?
Antigone.
	To see the hearth under the earth —
Ismene.
	Whose?
Antigone.
	Our father's. Oh, I am wretched.
Ismene.
	How can that be allowed? Surely you see —
Antigone.
	Why do you scold me?
Ismene.
	And this too —
Antigone.
	What now? What is it this time?
Ismene.
	He fell without a tomb, apart from everyone.
Antigone.
	Take me there, and then kill me too.
Ismene.
	Aiai. Wretched past bearing — where, now,
	alone like this, with no way out,
	am I to live my hard life?
Chorus.
	Girls — do not be afraid.
Antigone.
	But where am I to run?
Chorus.
	Before this, something escaped —
Antigone.
	What?
Chorus.
	Your affairs. They escaped falling out badly.
Antigone.
	I know.
Chorus.
	Then what is it you are thinking?
Antigone.
	How we are to get home — I do not know.
Chorus.
	Do not even look for it.
Antigone.
	Hardship has hold of us.
Chorus.
	It had before, too.
Antigone.
	Then it was past bearing. Now it is worse than that.
Chorus.
	A great sea of it is what you two were allotted.
Antigone.
	Yes. Yes.
Chorus.
	I say so too.
Antigone.
	Aiai — where are we to go, Zeus?
	To what hope, of all that are left,
	is the god driving me now?

(Theseus comes in.)

Theseus. Stop your wailing, children. Where the favour of the night below is stored up as a treasure shared by all, grieving is not right. It brings retribution.

Antigone. Son of Aegeus, we fall at your feet.

Theseus. To have what done, children?

Antigone. We want to see our father's tomb ourselves, with our own eyes.

Theseus. That is not allowed.

Antigone. What do you mean, my lord — ruler of Athens?

Theseus.
	Children, he forbade me
	to let anyone come near that place,
	or any mortal call out over
	the holy tomb that holds him.
	If I did that well, he said,
	I would keep this country free of pain for ever.
	The god heard me say it,
	and the Oath of Zeus, that hears all things.
Antigone.
	If that is what he had in mind,
	then it must be enough for us.
	But send us to Thebes, old Thebes,
	in case we can somehow stop
	the killing that is coming
	for our brothers.
Theseus.
	I will do that, and everything else
	I can do that is of use to you
	and pleasing to the man under the earth
	who has just gone from us.
	I must not tire of it.
Chorus.
	Stop now. Do not raise
	the lament any higher.
	All this stands settled. It holds.
